Chapter 1

Introduction

†
†
margin:

134

This thesis is a mathematical study of quantum computing, concentrating
on two related, but independent topics.
First up are
dilations
, covered in chapter
2
.
In chapter
3

“diamond, andthen, dagger”
we turn to the second topic:
effectus theory
.
Both chapters, or rather parts, can be read separately
and feature a comprehensive introduction of their own.
For now, we focus on what they have in common.

†
†
margin:

II

Both parts study the category of von Neumann algebras
(with ncpsu-maps
*
*

*

An abbreviation for
n
ormal
c
ompletely
p
ositive contractive (i.e.
s
ub
u
nital) linear maps.
between them).
A von Neumann algebra is a model for a (possibly infinite-dimensional)
quantum datatype, for instance:

M_{2}

a qubit

M_{3}\otimes M_{3}

an (ordered) pair of qutrits

M_{3}

a qutrit

\mathbb{C}^{2}

a bit

M_{2}\oplus M_{3}

a qubit or a qutrit

\mathscr{B}(\ell^{2})

quantum integers

The ncpsu-maps between these algebras represent physical
(but not necessarily computable) operations
in the opposite direction
(as in the ‘Heisenberg picture’):

1.

Measure a qubit in the computational basis
(
\mathrm{qubit}\to\mathrm{bit}
)

\mathbb{C}_{2}\rightarrow M_{2},

(\lambda,\mu)\ \mapsto\ \lambda\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|+\mu\left|1\right>\!\left<1\right|
,
using braket notation
†
†

†

See e.g.
[
91
, fig. 2.1]
,
so here

\ket{0}\equiv\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

\bra{0}\equiv\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

\ket{1}\equiv\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and

\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

2.

Discard the second qubit
(
\mathrm{qubit}\times\mathrm{qubit}\to\mathrm{qubit}
)

M_{2}\rightarrow M_{2}\otimes M_{2}
,
a\ \mapsto\ a\otimes 1

3.

Apply a Hadamard-gate to a qubit
(
\mathrm{qubit}\to\mathrm{qubit}
)

M_{2}\rightarrow M_{2}
,
a\ \mapsto\ H^{*}aH
,  where
H=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

4.

Initialize a qutrit as 0
(
\mathrm{empty}\to\mathrm{qutrit}
)

M_{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
a\ \mapsto\ \bra{0}a\ket{0}

†
†
margin:

III

The first part of this thesis starts with a concrete question:
does the famous Stinespring dilation theorem
[
106
]
,
a cornerstone of quantum theory,
extend to every ncpsu-map between von Neumann algebras?
Roughly, this means that every ncpsu-map splits
in two maps of a particularly nice form.
We will see that indeed it does.
The insights attained pondering this question lead naturally,
as they so often do in mathematics, to other new results:
for instance, we learn more about the tensor product of
dilations (
2.5.1
)
and the pure maps associated to them (
2.6
).
But perhaps the mathematically
most interesting by-catch of pondering the question
are the contributions (see
2
) to the theory
of self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-modules over a von Neumann algebra,
which underlie these dilations.

†
†
margin:

IV

In contrast to the first part of this thesis,
the second part starts
with the abstract (or rather: vague) question:
what characterizes the category of von Neumann algebras?
The goal is to find a set of reasonable axioms
which pick out the category of von Neumann algebras
among all other categories.
In other words: a reconstruction of quantum theory with categorical axioms
(presupposing von Neumann algebras are the right model of quantum theory).
Unfortunately, we do not reach this goal here.
‡
‡

‡

Building on this thesis,
van de Wetering recently found a reconstruction
of finite-dimensional quantum theory
[
118
,
117
]
.

However, by studying von Neumann algebras
with this self-imposed restriction,
one is led to, as again is often the case in mathematics,
upon several new notions and results,
which are of independent interest,
such as, for instance,
\diamond
-adjointness,
pure maps and the existence of their dagger.

†
†
margin:

V

Twin theses
A substantial part of the research presented here
has been performed in close collaboration with
my twin brother Abraham Westerbaan.
Our results are interwoven to such a degree
that separating them is difficult
and, more importantly, detrimental to the clarity of presentation.
Therefore we decided to write our theses as two consecutive volumes
(with consecutive numbering, more on that later).
Abraham’s thesis
[
124
]
is the first volume
and covers the preliminaries on von Neumann
algebras and, among other results, an axiomatization of
the sequential product
b\mapsto\sqrt{a}b\sqrt{a}
.
The present thesis is the second volume and builds upon
the results of Abraham’s thesis,
in its study of dilations and effectus theory.
Because of this clear division,
a significant amount of Abraham’s original work appears in my thesis
and, vice versa, my work appears in his.
However, the large majority of the
new results in each of our theses is our own.

†
†
margin:

VI

Preliminaries
For the first part of this thesis,
only knowledge is presumed of, what is considered to be,
the basic theory of von Neumann algebras.
Unfortunately, this ‘basic theory’ takes most introductory texts
close to a thousand pages to develop.
This situation has led my twin brother to ambitiously
redevelop the basic theory of von Neumann algebras
tailored to the needs of our theses (and hopefully others
in the field).
In his thesis, Abraham presents
[
124
]

a clear, comprehensive and self-contained development of the basic
theory of von Neumann algebras in less than 200 pages.
For a traditional treatment,
I can wholeheartedly recommend the books of Kadison and Ringrose
[
73
]
.
For the second part of this thesis,
only elementary category theory is required
as covered in any introductory text, like e.g.
[
9
]
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Writing style
This thesis is divided into points,
each a few paragraphs long.
Points are numbered with Roman numerals in the margin:
this is point
1
.
Points are organized into groups,
which are assigned Arabic numerals,
that appear at the start of the group in the margin.
The group of this point is
1
.
Group numbers below
1

appear in my twin brother’s thesis
[
124
]
.
Page numbers have been replaced by the
group numbers that appear on the spread.
§
§

§

A spread are two pages that are visible at the same time.

This unusual system allows for
very short references:
instead of writing ‘see Theorem 2.3.4 on page 123’,
we simply write ‘see
2.3
’.
Even better: ‘see the middle of the proof of Theorem 2.3.4 on page 123’
becomes the more precise ‘see
2.3
’.

Somewhat unconventionally, this thesis includes exercises.
Most exercises replace straight-forward, but repetitive, proofs.
Other exercises, marked with an asterisk *,
are harder and explore tangents.
In both cases, these exercises are not meant to force the reader into the
role of a pupil, but rather to aid her, by reducing clutter.
Solutions to these exercises (page
7
)
and errata to the printed version of this text (page
8
),
can be found at the end of this PDF.
Possibly further errata will be made available
on the arXiv under
1803.01911
.

†
†
margin:

Advertisements
This thesis is based on the publications
[

,

,

,

]
.
During my doctoral research,
I also published the following papers,
which although somewhat related,
would’ve made this thesis too broad in scope if included.

1.

A Kochen–Specker System has at least 22 Vectors

[

]
,
Uijlen and I justify the title building upon the work
of Arends, Ouaknine and Wampler.

2.

In
Unordered Tuples in Quantum Computation

[
35
]

Furber and I argue that
M_{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}
is the right algebra
modeling an unordered pair of qubits. Then we compute
the algebra for an unordered
n
-tuple of
d
-level systems.

3.

In
Picture-perfect QKD

[
76
]

Kissinger, Tull and I show how to graphically derive a security
bound on quantum key distribution using the continuity of Stinespring’s dilation.

4.

In
[
100
]
, Schwabe and I
work out the details of a quantum circuit
to break binary
\mathcal{MQ}
using Grover’s algorithm.

In the second part of this thesis,
we
use the notion of effectus (introduced by Jacobs
[
57
]
),
solely for studying quantum theory.
Effectus theory, however, has more breadth to it,
see e.g.

,

,

[

]
.

†
†
margin:

IX

Next, I would like to mention some other recent approaches
to the mathematical study of quantum theory
which are related in spirit and of which I am aware.
First, there are colleagues who also study particular categories
related to quantum theory.
Spearheaded by Coecke and Abramsky
[
2
]
,
dozens of authors around Oxford
have developed insightful graphical calculi
by studying the category of Hilbert spaces
categorically
[
25
]
.
Not explicitly categorically (but certainly in spirit)
is the work around operational probabilistic theories,
see e.g.
[
27
]
.
Besides these approaches (and that of effectus theory),
there are many other varied categorical forages
into quantum theory, e.g.
[
78
,
96
,
105
,
34
]
.
Then there are authors who pick out different structures from quantum theory
to study.
Most impressive is the work of Alfsen and Shultz,
who studied and axiomatized the state
spaces of many operator algebras, including von Neumann algebras
[
4
]
.
Recently, the poset of commutative subalgebras of a fixed operator algebra
has received attention,
see e.g.
[
52
,
85
,
51
]
.
Finally,
tracing back to von Neumann himself,
the lattice of projections (representing sharp predicates)
and the poset of effects (fuzzy predicates),
has have been studied by many, see e.g.
[
29
]
.

†
†
margin:

IXa

About the cover
One of the most familiar structures
in operator algebras (the mathematical field of this thesis)
is perhaps the infinite-dimensional
Hilbert space
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
.
What would its unit sphere look like?
The cover depicts an approximation:
a 12-dimensional spherical shell split into two.
On the outside the pieces are completely reflective —
on the inside they are dark blue and only mostly reflective.
The pieces are sat between two (11-dimensional) hyperplanes
with a somewhat reflective checkerboard pattern.
The planes are cropped at a certain radius from the origin
turning them into
hyperdisks
.
The picture is rendered using a homebrew
¶
¶

¶

Source code is available at
https://github.com/westerbaan/ndpt
.

path tracer
,
which simulates how rays of light would bounce to end up
in the camera.
The calculations involved (such as the one to determine how a ray bounces
from an
n
-dimensional sphere)
are all conveniently expressed and performed using
inner products.
The primary rays cast by the camera have the biggest presence
in the first three dimensions, only ten percent in the fourth,
five percent in the fifth et cetera.
This causes more higher-dimensional oddities to appear in the image
where rays have travelled further or bounced farther in the
higher dimensions.

Progress in theoretical fields
is often dramatized
as the fruit of personal struggles of
einzelgängers

grown in deep focus and isolation.
But what is missing in this depiction is that
often the seeds were sown
in casual conversation among colleagues.

for instruments, which led to quotients and comprehension.

against my initial skepticism,

universal property.

Chapter 2

Dilations

†
†
margin:

135

In this chapter we will study dilations.
The common theme is that a complicated map
is actually the composition of a simpler map
after a representation into a larger algebra.
The famous Gel’fand–Naimark–Segal construction (see

in
[
124
]
)
is a dilation theorem in disguise:
it shows that every state is a vector state on a larger algebra:

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem (GNS’)
For each pu-map
*
*

*

pu-map is an abbreviation for positive unital linear map.
See
and

for other abbreviations, like nmiu-map
and ncpsu-map.

\omega\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}

from a C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{A}
,
there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
,
an miu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

and a vector
x\in\mathscr{H}

such that
\omega=h\mathop{\circ}\varrho

where
h\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathbb{C}

is given by
h(T)\equiv\left<x,Tx\right>
.

†
†
margin:

III

Probably the most famous dilation theorem is that
of Stinespring
[
106
, thm. 1]
.
(We will see a detailed proof later, in
2.1
.)

†
†
margin:

IV

Theorem (Stinespring)
For every cp-map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

between a C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{A}

and the C
∗
-algebra of
bounded operators on a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
,
there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}
,
an miu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

and a bounded operator
V\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{K}

such that
\varphi=\mathrm{ad}_{V}\mathop{\circ}\varrho
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\mathrm{ad}_{V}}{\@index{\@indexphrase[ad]{$\mathrm{ad}_{V}$}\@indexphrase{Hilbert spaces}}}\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

is given by
\mathrm{ad}_{V}(T)\equiv V^{*}TV
.
Furthermore

1.

if
\varphi
is unital, then
V
is an isometry
and

2.

if
\mathscr{A}
is a von Neumann algebra
and
\varphi
is normal, then
\varrho
is normal as well.

†
†
margin:

V

Stinespring’s theorem
is fundamental in the study
of quantum information and quantum computing:
it is used to prove entropy inequalities (e.g.
[
84
]
),
bounds on optimal cloners (e.g.
[
123
]
),
full completeness of quantum programming languages (e.g.
[
105
]
),
security of quantum key distribution (e.g.
[
80
,
76
]
),
to analyze quantum alternation (e.g.
[
13
]
),
to categorify quantum processes (e.g.
[
102
]
)
and

as an axiom to single out
quantum theory among information processing theories
[
18
]
.
These are only a few examples
of the many thousands building on Stinespring’s discovery.

Stinespring’s theorem only applies
to maps of the form
\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

and so do most of its useful consequences.
One wonders:
is there an extension of Stinespring’s theorem
to arbitrary ncp-maps
\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
?
A different and perhaps less frequently asked question is whether
Stinespring’s dilation is categorical in some way?
That is: does it have a defining universal property?
Both questions turn out to have an affirmative answer:
Paschke’s generalization of GNS for Hilbert C
∗
-modules
[
93
]

turns out to have the same universal property
as Stinespring’s dilation and so it extends Stinespring
to arbitrary ncp-maps.

;

\mathscr{A}
;

)

,

(Hilbert spaces
\mathscr{H}

Table 2.1:
Various dilation theorems.
Maps
\varphi
of the given type
split as
\varphi=h\mathop{\circ}\varrho
,
where
\varrho
is as in LHS
and
h
is as in RHS.
The first three appear prominently in this thesis;
the last, KSGNS, is only briefly touched upon in
2.3
.

†
†
margin:

VI

We start this chapter with a detailed proof of Stinespring’s theorem.
Then we show that it obeys a universal property.
Before we move on to Paschke’s GNS,
we need to develop Paschke’s theory of self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-modules.
Paschke’s work builds on Sakai’s characterization of von Neumann algebras,
which would take considerable effort to develop in detail.
Thus to be as self-contained as possible,
we avoid Sakai’s characterization (in contrast to our
article
[
127
]
)
and give new proofs
of Paschke’s results where required.
One major difference is that we will use
a completion (see
2.2.3
) of a uniform space
instead of considering the dual space of a Hilbert C
∗
-module.
This uniformity (which we call the
ultranorm uniformity
)
plays a central rôle
in our development of the theory
and many of the new results.
We finish the first part of this chapter
by constructing Paschke’s dilation
and establishing that it indeed extends Stinespring’s dilation.
We start the second part of this chapter
by characterizing when the representation in the Paschke dilation
of an ncp-map is injective (see
2.3
),
which is a generalization of our answer
[
125
]

to the same question about the Stinespring dilation.
Then we allow for an intermezzo
to prove several new results on (self-dual) Hilbert C
∗
-modules,
notably:

1.

we generalize the Kaplansky density theorem
to Hilbert C
∗
-modules, see
2.4
;

2.

we prove that every self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-module over
a von Neumann algebra factor
is of a particularly nice form,
see
2.5
, and

3.

we greatly simplify the construction of the exterior tensor product
for Hilbert C
∗
-modules over von Neumann algebras
in
2.5.1

and show that its self-dual completion
is particularly well-behaved, see e.g.
2.5.1
.

We use this new insight into the exterior tensor product
to compute the tensor product
of dilations, see
2.5.1
.
In the final sections of this chapter we turn our attention to the maps
that occur on the right-hand side of the Paschke dilation.
In Stinespring’s dilation these maps are always of the form
\mathrm{ad}_{V}
,
but in Paschke’s dilation different maps may appear —
in any case, they are pure in the sense of
.
The main result of these last sections
is an equivalence between this and another
seemingly unrelated notion of purity:
we show that an ncp-map
\varphi
is pure in the sense of

if and only if the map on the left-hand side of
the Paschke dilation of
\varphi
is surjective.
This is a bridge to the next chapter
in which we prove in greater generality
that these pure maps have a
\dagger
-structure,
see
3.7
.

2.1

Stinespring’s theorem

†
†
margin:

136

In the proof of Stinespring’s theorem
(
2.1
),
it will be necessary to
‘‘complete’’
†
†

†

Note that
we do not require an inner product to be definite.
The inner product on a Hilbert space
is
definite.
Thus the completion will quotient out those vectors with
zero norm with respect to the inner product.

a (complex) vector space with inner product into a Hilbert space.
The details of this classical result were only
sketched
,
where it’s used in the GNS-construction (
).
Here we will work through the details
as the corresponding completion (
2.2.3
) required
in Paschke’s dilation
is more complex and its exposition will benefit
from this familiar analog.

†
†
margin:

II

Proposition
Let
V
be a (complex) vector space with inner
product
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
.

There is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}

together with a bounded linear map
\eta\colon V\to\mathscr{H}

such that

1.

[v,w]=\left<\eta(v),\eta(w)\right>
for all
v,w\in V
and

2.

the image of
\eta
is dense in
\mathscr{H}
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
We will form
\mathscr{H}
from the set of Cauchy sequences from
V

with a little twist.
Recall that two Cauchy
sequences
(v_{n})_{n}
and
(w_{n})_{n}
in
V

are said to be equivalent
if for every
\varepsilon>0

there is a
n_{0}

such that
\|v_{n}-w_{n}\|\leqslant\varepsilon

for all
n\geqslant n_{0}
,
where as usual
\|v\|\equiv\sqrt{[v,v]}
.
Call a Cauchy sequence
(v_{n})_{n}

fast

if for each
n_{0}

we have
\|v_{n}-v_{m}\|\leqslant\frac{1}{2^{n_{0}}}

for all
n,m\geqslant n_{0}
.
Clearly every Cauchy sequence has a fast subsequence
which is (as all subsequences are) equivalent with it.

†
†
margin:

IV

Define
\mathscr{H}
to be the set of fast Cauchy sequences modulo
equivalence.
‡
‡

‡

We use fast Cauchy sequences
in the definition of
\mathscr{H}
only to shorten the text:
any Cauchy sequence is equivalent to a fast one anyway.

For brevity we will denote an element of
\mathscr{H}
,
which is an equivalence class of fast Cauchy sequences, simply by
a single representative.
Also, we often tersely write
v
for the Cauchy sequence
(v_{n})_{n}
.
The set
\mathscr{H}
is a metric space
with the standard
distance
d(v,w)\equiv\lim_{n\to\infty}\|v_{n}-w_{n}\|
.
It is independent of representatives
as by the reverse and regular triangle inequality combined,
we get

\bigl|\|v_{n}-w_{n}\|\,-\,\|v_{n}^{\prime}-w_{n}^{\prime}\|\bigr|\ \leqslant\ \|v_{n}-v_{n}^{\prime}\|\,+\,\|w_{n}-w_{n}^{\prime}\|\ \rightarrow\ 0

for
v,w
equivalent with a Cauchy sequence
v^{\prime}
, respectively
w^{\prime}
.
To show
\mathscr{H}
is a complete metric space, assume

v^{1},v^{2},\ldots

is a fast Cauchy sequence of fast Cauchy sequences.
We will show
(v_{n}^{n})_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence.
Here the restriction to fast sequences bears fruit:
without it, the limit sequence is (notationally) harder to construct.
Assume
n,m,k\geqslant N
for some
N
. We have

\|v^{n}_{n}-v^{m}_{m}\|\ \leqslant\ \|v^{n}_{n}-v^{n}_{k}\|\,+\,\|v^{n}_{k}-v^{m}_{k}\|\,+\,\|v^{m}_{k}-v^{m}_{m}\|\ \leqslant\ \|v^{n}_{k}-v^{m}_{k}\|+\frac{2}{2^{N}}.

Because
\lim_{k\to\infty}\|v_{k}^{n}-v_{k}^{m}\|=d(v^{n},v^{m})\leqslant 2^{-N}

we can find a
k\geqslant N

such that
\|v^{n}_{k}-v^{m}_{k}\|\leqslant\frac{2}{2^{N}}

and so
\|v^{n}_{n}-v^{m}_{m}\|\leqslant\frac{4}{2^{N}}
.
Thus
(v^{n}_{n})_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence.
It’s easily checked
v^{1},v^{2},\ldots

converges to
(v^{n}_{n})_{n}
with respect to
d
,
with one caveat: the sequence
(v^{n}_{n})_{n}
might not be fast
and so, might not be in
\mathscr{H}
.
Like any other Cauchy sequence,
(v^{n}_{n})_{n}

has a fast subsequence.
The equivalence class
of this subsequence is the limit of
v^{1},v^{2},\ldots
in
\mathscr{H}
.
We have shown
\mathscr{H}
is a complete metric space.

†
†
margin:

V

Define
\eta\colon V\to\mathscr{H}

to be the map which sends
v
to the equivalence class of the
constant sequence
(v)_{n}
.
By construction of
\eta
and
\mathscr{H}
,
the image of
\eta
is dense in
\mathscr{H}
.
Let
f\colon V\to X

be any uniformly continuous map to a complete metric space
X
.
We will show there is a unique continuous
g\colon\mathscr{H}\to X

such that
g\mathop{\circ}\eta=f
.
From the uniform continuity it easily follows
that
f
maps Cauchy sequences to Cauchy sequences
and preserves equivalence between them.
Together with the completeness of
X

there is a unique
g\colon\mathscr{H}\to X

fixed by
g((v_{n})_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}f(v_{n})
.
Clearly
g\mathop{\circ}\eta=f
.
Finally, uniqueness of
g
follows readily from
the fact
g
is fixed on the image of
\eta
,
which is dense in
\mathscr{H}
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Thus we directly get a scalar multiplication
on
\mathscr{H}
by extending
\eta\mathop{\circ}r_{z}\colon V\to\mathscr{H}

where
r_{z}(v)=zv
is scalar multiplication by
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
which is uniformly continuous.
In fact
z(v_{n})_{n}\equiv(zv_{n})_{n}
.
Extending addition is more involved, but straightforward:
given representatives
v,w\in\mathscr{H}

we know
(v_{n}+w_{n})_{n}
is Cauchy in
V

by uniform continuity of addition and so
picking a fast subsequence
v+w
of
(v_{n}+w_{n})_{n}

fixes an addition on
\mathscr{H}
.
With this expression for addition, and the similar one for
scalar multiplication, it is easy to see
they turn
\mathscr{H}
into a complex vector space with zero
\eta(0)

for which
\eta
is linear.
Extending the inner product is bit trickier.

†
†
margin:

VII

To define the inner product on
\mathscr{H}
,
first note that for Cauchy sequences
v
and
w
in
V

we have (using Cauchy–Schwarz,
, on the second line):

\displaystyle\bigl|[v_{n},w_{n}]-[v_{m},w_{m}]\bigr|

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl|[v_{n},w_{n}-w_{m}]+[v_{n}-v_{m},w_{m}]\bigr|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|v_{n}\|\|w_{n}-w_{m}\|+\|v_{n}-v_{m}\|\|w_{m}\|.

Thus as
(\|v_{n}\|)_{n}
and
(\|w_{n}\|)_{n}
are bounded
we see
([v_{n},w_{n}])_{n}
is Cauchy.
In a similar fashion we see
\bigl|[v_{n},w_{n}]-[v^{\prime}_{n},w^{\prime}_{n}]\bigr|\leqslant\|v_{n}\|\|w_{n}-w_{n}^{\prime}\|+\|v_{n}-v_{n}^{\prime}\|\|w^{\prime}_{n}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty

for
v^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}
Cauchy sequences equivalent
to
v
respectively
w
.
Thus

([v_{n},w_{n}])_{n}
is equivalent to

([v^{\prime}_{n},w^{\prime}_{n}])_{n}

and so
we define
\left<v,w\right>\equiv\lim_{n\to\infty}[v_{n},w_{n}]
on
\mathscr{H}
.
With vector space structure from
2.1

we easily see this is an inner product
\mathscr{H}
.
The metric induced by the inner product coincides with
d
:

\left<v-w,v-w\right>\ =\ \lim_{n}[v_{n}-w_{n},v_{n}-w_{n}]\ =\ \lim_{n}\|v_{n}-w_{n}\|^{2}\ =\ d(v,w)^{2}.

And so
\mathscr{H}
is a Hilbert space.
Finally,
\left<\eta(v),\eta(w)\right>=[v,w]
is direct.
\square

†
†
margin:

137

We are ready to prove Stinespring’s dilation theorem.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof of Stinespring’s theorem,
2
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

be any cp-map.
Write
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}
for the tensor product of
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{H}

as vector spaces.

We will define
\mathscr{K}
as the Hilbert space completion
of
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}
with respect to the inner
product
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]

fixed by

[a\otimes x,\,b\otimes y]\ \equiv\ \left<x,\,\varphi(a^{*}b)\,y\right>_{\mathscr{H}}.

(2.1)

To proceed, we have to show that
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]

is indeed an inner product.
By linear extension, (
2.1
)
fixes a sesquilinear form
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
.
As
\varphi
preserves involution as a positive map,
see
,
the sesquilinear form
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]

is also symmetric, i.e.
[t,s]=\overline{[s,t]}
.
Assume
\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\otimes x_{i}

is an arbitrary element of
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}
.
To see
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
is positive,
we need to show

0\ \leqslant\ \bigl[\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes x_{i},\sum_{j}a_{j}\otimes x_{j}\bigr]\ \equiv\ \sum_{i,j}\left<x_{i},\varphi(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})x_{j}\right>.

The trick is to consider the matrix algebra
M_{n}\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

acting on
\mathscr{H}^{\oplus n}
in the obvious way:

(A(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{\mathrm{t}})_{i}\equiv\sum_{j}A_{ij}x_{j}
.
(In fact, this yields an
isomorphism
M_{n}\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\cong\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}^{\oplus n})
.)
Writing
x
for
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{\mathrm{t}}
in
\mathscr{H}^{\oplus n}
,
we get

\sum_{i,j}\left<x_{i},\,\varphi(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})\,x_{j}\right>\ =\ \bigl<x,(M_{n}\varphi)\,\bigl(\,(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})_{ij}\,\bigr)\,x\bigr>_{\mathscr{H}^{\oplus n}}.

(2.2)

The matrix
(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})_{ij}

is positive as
(C^{*}C)_{ij}=a_{i}^{*}a_{j}

with
C_{ij}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}a_{j}
.
By complete positivity
M_{n}\varphi(\,(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})_{ij}\,)
is positive
and so is (
2.2
),
hence
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
is an inner product.
Write
\eta\colon\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{K}
for the Hilbert space completion
of
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}
with the inner product
described in
2.1
.

†
†
margin:

III

We need a lemma before we continue.
Let
T\colon\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}

be a bounded linear map.
We show there is an extension
\hat{T}\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{K}
.
The operator
T
is
uniformly continuous and so is
\eta\mathop{\circ}T\colon\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{K}
.
Thus by
2.1

there is a unique continuous extension
\hat{T}\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{K}

with
\hat{T}(\eta(t))=\eta(T(t))

for all
t\in\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}
.
Clearly
\hat{T}
is linear on the image of
\eta
,
which is dense and so
\hat{T}
is linear everywhere.
As
\hat{T}
is linear and continuous, it must be
bounded, so
\hat{T}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
.
It is easy to see
\widehat{T+S}=\hat{T}+\hat{S}
and

\widehat{\lambda T}=\lambda\hat{T}

for operators
S,T
on
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.
Also
\widehat{TS}=\hat{T}\hat{S}
:
indeed
\hat{T}\hat{S}\eta(t)=\hat{T}\eta(St)=\eta(TSt)

and so by uniqueness
\hat{T}\hat{S}=\widehat{TS}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Assume
b\in\mathscr{A}
.
Let
\varrho_{0}(b)
be the operator on
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}

fixed by

\varrho_{0}(b)\,a\otimes x\,\ \equiv\ (ba)\otimes x.

Clearly
\varrho_{0}
is linear, unital and multiplicative.
We want to show
\varrho_{0}(b)
is bounded for fixed
b\in\mathscr{A}
,
so that we can define
\varrho(b)\equiv\widehat{\varrho_{0}(b)}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
using
2.1
.
To show
\varrho_{0}(b)
is bounded, we claim that in
M_{n}\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

we have

(a_{i}^{*}b^{*}ba_{j})_{ij}\ \leqslant\ \|b\|^{2}(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})_{ij}.

(2.3)

Indeed, as
b^{*}b\leqslant\|b\|^{2}
,
there is some
c
with
c^{*}c=\|b\|^{2}-b^{*}b
.
Define
C_{ij}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}ca_{j}
.
We compute:

(C^{*}C)_{ij}=a_{i}^{*}c^{*}ca_{j}=a_{i}^{*}(\|b\|^{2}-b^{*}b)a_{j}

and so (
2.3
) holds.
Hence

\displaystyle\bigl\|\varrho_{0}(b)\,\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes x_{i}\bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{i}(ba_{i})\otimes x_{i}\bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl<x,(M_{n}\varphi)(\,(a_{i}^{*}b^{*}ba_{j})_{ij}\,)\,x\bigr>

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|b\|^{2}\,\bigl<x,(M_{n}\varphi)(\,(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})_{ij}\,)\,x\bigr>

by (
2.3
)

\displaystyle\ =\ \|b\|^{2}\,\bigl\|\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes x_{i}\bigr\|^{2}

and so
\varrho_{0}(b)
is bounded.
Define
\varrho(b)\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

using
2.1

by
\varrho(b)\equiv\widehat{\varrho_{0}(b)}
.

†
†
margin:

V

We already know
\varrho
is a mu-map.
We want to show it preserves involution:
\varrho(c^{*})=\varrho(c)^{*}

for all
c\in\mathscr{A}
.
Indeed, for every
a,b\in\mathscr{A}
and
x,y\in\mathscr{H}

we have

[\varrho_{0}(c^{*})\,a\otimes x,b\otimes y]\ =\ [(c^{*}a)\otimes x,b\otimes y]\ =\ \left<x,\varphi(a^{*}cb)y\right>\ =\ [a\otimes x,\varrho_{0}(c)\,b\otimes y].

Hence
\left<\varrho(c^{*})\eta(a\otimes x),\eta(b\otimes y)\right>=\left<\eta(a\otimes x),\varrho(c)\eta(b\otimes y)\right>
.
As the linear span of vectors of the form
\eta(a\otimes x)

is dense in
\mathscr{K}
,
we see
\varrho(c^{*})
is adjoint to
\varrho(c)

and so
\varrho(c^{*})=\varrho(c)^{*}
, as desired.

†
†
margin:

VI

Let
V_{0}\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{H}

be given by
V_{0}x=1\otimes x
.
This operator
V_{0}
is bounded:

\|V_{0}x\|\ =\ \left<x,\varphi(1^{*}1)x\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}\ =\ \|\sqrt{\varphi(1)}x\|\ \leqslant\ \|\sqrt{\varphi(1)}\|\|x\|.

Define
V\equiv\eta\mathop{\circ}V_{0}
.
For all
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
x,y\in\mathscr{H}
, we have

[a\otimes x,V_{0}y]\ =\ \left<x,\varphi(a^{*})y\right>\ =\ \left<x,\varphi(a)^{*}y\right>\ =\ \left<\varphi(a)x,y\right>

and so
V^{*}
satisfies
V^{*}\eta(a\otimes x)=\varphi(a)x
.
Hence for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
x\in\mathscr{H}

V^{*}\varrho(a)Vx\ =\ V^{*}\varrho(a)\eta(1\otimes x)\ =\ V^{*}\eta(a\otimes x)\ =\ \varphi(a)x.

We have shown
\mathrm{ad}_{V}\mathop{\circ}\varrho=\varphi
.
If
\varphi
is unital,
then
V^{*}Vx=V^{*}(1\otimes x)=\varphi(1)x=x

for all
x\in\mathscr{H}
and so
V
is an isometry.
(Or equivalently
\mathrm{ad}_{V}
is unital.)

†
†
margin:

VII

Assume
\mathscr{A}
is a von Neumann algebra.
It remains to be shown that
\varrho

is normal when
\varphi
is.
So assume
\varphi
is normal.
To start, note that
the vector functionals
\left<a\otimes x,(\,\cdot\,)a\otimes x\right>

are together a faithful set of np-functionals.
Thus, by
,
it is sufficient
to show that

b\ \mapsto\ \left<a\otimes x,\varrho(b)\,a\otimes x\right>\ \equiv\ \left<x,\varphi(a^{*}ba)\,x\right>

is normal for every
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
x\in\mathscr{H}
.
This is indeed the case, as normal
maps are closed under composition and
\left<x,(\,\cdot\,)x\right>

is normal by
,
a^{*}(\,\cdot\,)a
by

and
\varphi
by assumption.

\square

VIII

Remark
Stinespring’s theorem generalizes GNS.
It seems tempting to directly prove Stinespring instead of GNS —
however, for our version with ncp-maps,
we used GNS itself in the proof of Stinespring’s theorem to show
that
\varrho
is normal.

†
†
margin:

138

In the following series of exercises, the reader will develop
a few consequences of Stinespring’s theorem
which are in some fields better-known than
Stinespring’s theorem itself.
As preparation we study the nmiu-maps between type I von Neumann algebras.
The following result can be derived
from the more general
[
110
, thm. 5.5]
,
but we will give a direct proof,
which seems to be new.
[
130
]

†
†
margin:

II

Proposition
Let
\varrho\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

be any non-zero
§
§

§

If
\mathscr{K}
is 0-dimensional,
then there zero-map
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

is an nmiu-map.
nmiu-map for Hilbert spaces
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{K}
.
There is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and a unitary
U\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

(see
)
such that
\varrho(a)=U^{*}(a\otimes 1)U

for all
a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
To start, we will show that
\varrho
is injective
by proving
\ker\varrho=\{0\}
.
Clearly
\ker\varrho
is a two-sided ideal
— indeed, if
\varrho(a)=0
for some
a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
,
then
\varrho(ab)=\varrho(a)\varrho(b)=0

and
\varrho(ba)=0

for any
b\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
.
As
\varrho
is normal,
we have
\varrho(\sup D)=\sup_{d\in D}\varrho(d)=0

for any bounded directed
D\subseteq\ker\varrho

of self-adjoint elements.
So by
,
we know
\ker\varrho=z\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

for some central projection
z\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
.
Either
z=0
or
z=1
.
Clearly
z\neq 1
, for otherwise
0=\varrho\neq 0
, which is absurd.
So
z=0
and
\ker\varrho=\{0\}
, thus
\varrho
is injective.

†
†
margin:

IV

Pick an orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathscr{H}
.
Write
p_{i}
for the projection onto
\mathbb{C}e_{i}
,
i.e.
p_{i}\equiv\left|e_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i}\right|
.
Choose any
i_{0}\in I

and write
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\equiv\varrho(p_{i_{0}})\mathscr{K}
.
Pick any orthonormal basis
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
of
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and write
q_{j}
for the projection onto
\mathbb{C}d_{j}
,
i.e.
q_{j}\equiv\left|d_{j}\right>\!\left<d_{j}\right|
.
Let
u_{i}
denote the unique partial
isometry fixed by
u_{i}e_{i}=e_{i_{0}}
and

u_{i}e_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
—
viz.
u_{i}\equiv\left|e_{i_{0}}\right>\!\left<e_{i}\right|
.
Define
r_{ij}\equiv\varrho(u_{i}^{*})d_{j}
.
We will show
(r_{ij})_{(i,j)\in I\times J}
is an orthonormal
basis of
\mathscr{K}
.
For this, it is sufficient to
show
r_{ij}
are unit vectors
and
\sum_{i,j}\left|r_{ij}\right>\!\left<r_{ij}\right|=1

(where the sum is defined as supremum over the partial sums),
using that then
\left|r_{ij}\right>\!\left<r_{ij}\right|

are pairwise orthogonal projections
by
.
Clearly these
r_{ij}
are unit vectors,
as by
u_{i}u_{i}^{*}=p_{i_{0}}

and
\varrho(p_{i_{0}})d_{j}=d_{j}
,
we have
\|r_{ij}\|^{2}=\langle\varrho(u_{i}u_{i}^{*})d_{j},d_{j}\rangle=1
.
To show
1=\sum_{i,j}\left|r_{ij}\right>\!\left<r_{ij}\right|
, we compute

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\ =\ \varrho(1)

as
\varrho
is unital

\displaystyle\ =\ \varrho\bigl(\,\sum_{i}p_{i}\,\bigr)

as
e_{i}
is an orthn. basis

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i}\varrho(p_{i})

as
\varrho
is normal

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i}\varrho(u_{i}^{*}p_{i_{0}}u_{i})

by dfn.
u_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i}\varrho(u_{i})^{*}\varrho(p_{i_{0}})\varrho(u_{i})

as
\varrho
is mi-map

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i}\varrho(u_{i})^{*}\,\bigl(\sum_{j}q_{j}\bigr)\,\varrho(u_{i})

as
d_{j}
is an orthn. basis of
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\varrho(u_{i})^{*}\,q_{j}\,\varrho(u_{i})

by

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\left|r_{ij}\right>\!\left<r_{ij}\right|.

†
†
margin:

V

Let
U\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

denote the unitary fixed by
Ur_{ij}=e_{i}\otimes d_{j}
.
For any
i\in I
,
j\in J
and
a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
,
it is straightforward to show that
\left|ae_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i_{0}}\right|=\sum_{k}\langle e_{k},ae_{i}\rangle\left|e_{k}\right>\!\left<e_{i_{0}}\right|
,
where the sum converges ultrastrongly
and consequently

\displaystyle\varrho(a)\,r_{ij}

\displaystyle\ =\ \varrho(a)\varrho(\left|e_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i_{0}}\right|)\,d_{j}

by dfn.
r_{ij}
and
u_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \varrho(\left|ae_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i_{0}}\right|)\,d_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ \varrho\bigl(\,\sum_{k}\langle e_{k},ae_{i}\rangle\left|e_{k}\right>\!\left<e_{i_{0}}\right|\,\bigr)\,d_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl(\sum_{k}\langle e_{k},ae_{i}\rangle\,\varrho(\left|e_{k}\right>\!\left<e_{i_{0}}\right|)\bigr)\,d_{j}

as
\varrho
is us-cont. by

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{k}\langle e_{k},ae_{i}\rangle\,\varrho(\left|e_{k}\right>\!\left<e_{i_{0}}\right|)\,d_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{k}\langle e_{k},ae_{i}\rangle\,U^{*}\,e_{k}\otimes d_{j}

by dfn.
U
and
u_{k}

\displaystyle\ =\ U^{*}\,\bigl(\,\sum_{k}e_{k}\langle e_{k},ae_{i}\rangle\,\bigr)\otimes d_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ U^{*}\,(ae_{i})\otimes d_{j}

as
e_{k}
is an orthn. basis

\displaystyle\ =\ U^{*}\,(a\otimes 1)\,U\,r_{ij}

by dfn.
r_{ij}
.

We have shown
\varrho(a)=U^{*}(a\otimes 1)U
.
\square

†
†
margin:

VI

Corollary
The nmiu-isomorphisms
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

are precisely of the form
\mathrm{ad}_{U}

for some unitary
U\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Exercise*
Use
2.1
and
2
to show
that for every ncp-map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

there are a Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and a bounded operator
V\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

such that
\varphi(a)=V^{*}(a\otimes 1)V
.

Conclude that any quantum channel

\Phi
from
\mathscr{H}
to itself
(i.e. completely positive trace-preserving
linear map between trace-class operators over
\mathscr{H}
,
see e.g.
[
112
, §2.6.2]
)
is of the
form
\Phi(\varrho)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{\mathscr{K}^{\prime}}[U^{*}\varrho\otimes\left|v_{0}\right>\!\left<v_{0}\right|U]

for some unitary
U
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Exercise* (Kraus’ decomposition)
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

be any ncp-map.
By
2.1

there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and bounded operator
V\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

such that
\varphi(a)=V^{*}(a\otimes 1)V
.
Choose an orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
.
Show
\varphi(a)=\sum_{i\in I}V^{*}(a\otimes\left|e_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i}\right|)V
,
where the sum converges ultraweakly.
Deduce there are projections
P_{i}\colon\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{H}

with
\varphi(a)=\sum_{i\in I}V^{*}P_{i}^{*}aP_{i}V
.
Conclude all ncp-maps
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

are of the form
\varphi(a)=\sum_{i\in I}V_{i}^{*}aV_{i}

for some bounded operators
V_{i}\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}
,
for which the partial sums of
\sum_{i}V_{i}^{*}V_{i}
are bounded.
These
V_{i}
are called
Kraus operators

for
\varphi
. (For a different approach,
see e.g.
[
28
, thm. 2.3]
.)

Now assume
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{K}
are finite dimensional.
¶
¶

¶

For arbitrary
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{K}
,
the cardinality of the set of Kraus operators
can be chosen to be less than or equal
to
(\dim\mathscr{H})\cdot(\dim\mathscr{K})
,
with dimension understood as the cardinality of
an orthonormal basis and the usual product on cardinals.

Show that in this case
the total number of Kraus operators can be chosen
to be less than or equal to
(\dim\mathscr{H})\cdot(\dim\mathscr{K})
.
(There is a similar fact for infinite-dimensional
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{K}
,
where the product of numbers is replaced by a maximum of cardinals.)

†
†
margin:

139

Definition
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

be any ncp-map
for some Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
and von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{A}
.
A
normal Stinespring dilation

(cf.
[
94
, ch. 4]
)
is a triple
(\mathscr{K},\varrho,V)

with
\mathscr{K}
a Hilbert space,

\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
an nmiu-map
(i.e. normal representation)
and
V\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{K}
a bounded operator
such that
\varphi
decomposes as
\varphi=\mathrm{ad}_{V}\mathop{\circ}\varrho
.
We just saw every
\varphi
has (at least one)
normal Stinespring dilation.
A (normal) Stinespring dilation

(\mathscr{K},\varrho,V)
is said to be
minimal

if the linear span of
\varrho(\mathscr{A})V\mathscr{H}\ \equiv\ \{\,\varrho(a)Vx;\ a\in\mathscr{A},\ x\in\mathscr{H}\,\}

is dense in
\mathscr{K}
.
By construction the (normal) Stinespring dilation
from
2.1
is minimal.
Every (normal) Stinespring dilation
(\mathscr{K},\varrho,V)

of
\varphi

restricts to a minimal dilation
(\mathscr{K}^{\prime},\varrho,V)

for
\varphi
, where
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{K}

is the norm-closure of the linear span of
\varrho(\mathscr{A})V\mathscr{H}
.

†
†
margin:

II

It is a well-known fact that all minimal normal Stinespring dilations
for a fixed
\varphi
are unitarily equivalent,
see for instance
[
94
, prop. 4.2]
.
We will adapt its proof to show that a minimal normal Stinespring
dilation admits a universal property.
Later we will use this to prove a second universal property
2.1

that allows us to generalize Stinespring’s dilation to
arbitrary ncp-maps.
The modification of the familiar argument
is mostly straightforward, except for the following lemma,
which we published earlier as
[
127
, lem. 11]
.

†
†
margin:

III

Lemma
Let
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
and
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}

be nmiu-maps between von Neumann algebras,
and let
\sigma\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{B}
be an ncp-map
such that
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
. Then

\par\sigma\bigl(\varrho^{\prime}(a_{1})\,c\,\varrho^{\prime}(a_{2})\bigr)\ =\ \varrho(a_{1})\,\sigma(c)\,\varrho(a_{2})

for all
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathscr{A}
and
c\in\mathscr{C}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
By Theorem 3.1 of
[
24
]
(see
),
we know that for all
c,d\in\mathscr{C}
:

\par\sigma(d^{*}d)=\sigma(d)^{*}\sigma(d)\quad\implies\quad\sigma(cd)=\sigma(c)\sigma(d).

(2.4)

We can apply this to
\varrho^{\prime}(a)^{*}\varrho^{\prime}(a)

with
a\in\mathscr{A}
— indeed we have

\par\sigma(\varrho^{\prime}(a)^{*}\varrho^{\prime}(a))\ =\ \sigma(\varrho^{\prime}(a^{*}a))\ =\ \varrho(a^{*}a)\ =\ \varrho(a)^{*}\varrho(a)\ =\ \sigma(\varrho^{\prime}(a))^{*}\sigma(\varrho^{\prime}(a))

and so by (
2.4
),
we have
\sigma(c\varrho^{\prime}(a))=\sigma(c)\sigma(\varrho^{\prime}(a))=\sigma(c)\varrho(a)

for all
c\in\mathscr{C}
.
Thus also (taking adjoints):

\sigma(\varrho^{\prime}(a)c)=\varrho(a)\sigma(c)
for all
c\in\mathscr{C}
.
Hence

\par\sigma(\varrho^{\prime}(a_{1})c\varrho^{\prime}(a_{2}))\ =\ \varrho(a_{1})\sigma(c\varrho^{\prime}(a_{2}))\ =\ \varrho(a_{1})\sigma(c)\varrho(a_{2})

for all
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathscr{A}
and
c\in\mathscr{C}
as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

V

Proposition
Assume
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

is any ncp-map with normal
Stinespring dilations
(\mathscr{K},\varrho,V)

and
(\mathscr{K}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},V^{\prime})
.
If
(\mathscr{K},\varrho,V)
is minimal,
then there is a unique isometry
S\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

such that
SV=V^{\prime}
and
\varrho=\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
(This is the same proof we gave in
[
127
]
.)
First we will deal with a pathological case.
If
V=0
, then
\varphi=0
,
V^{\prime}=0
,
\mathscr{K}=\{0\}

and
\varrho=0
. Thus the unique linear map
S\colon\{0\}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

satisfies the requirements. Assume
V\neq 0
.

†
†
margin:

VII

(Uniqueness)
Let
S_{1},S_{2}\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

be any isometries for which
S_{k}V=V^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{S_{k}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

for
k=1,2
.
We want to show
S_{1}=S_{2}
.
First we will prove that
\mathrm{ad}_{S_{1}}=\mathrm{ad}_{S_{2}}
.
For
k=1,2
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}\in\mathscr{A}
,

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\in\mathscr{H}

and

c\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K}^{\prime})
,
we have

\displaystyle\bigl<\mathrm{ad}_{S_{k}}(c)\sum_{i}\varrho(a_{i})Vx_{i},\sum_{j}\varrho(\alpha_{j})Vy_{j}\bigr>

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\bigl<V^{*}\varrho(\alpha_{j}^{*})\,\mathrm{ad}_{S_{k}}(c)\,\varrho(a_{i})Vx_{i},y_{i}\bigr>

\displaystyle\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsf{\ref{dils-univlemma}}}}{{=}}\ \sum_{i,j}\bigl<V^{*}\mathrm{ad}_{S_{k}}\bigl(\varrho^{\prime}(\alpha_{j}^{*})\,c\,\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i})\bigr)Vx_{i},y_{j}\bigr>

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\bigl<(V^{\prime})^{*}\varrho^{\prime}(\alpha_{j}^{*})\,c\,\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i})V^{\prime}x_{i},y_{j}\bigr>.

As the linear span of
\varrho(\mathscr{A})V\mathscr{H}
is dense in
\mathscr{K}
,
we see
\mathrm{ad}_{S_{1}}(c)=\mathrm{ad}_{S_{2}}(c)
for all
c\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K}^{\prime})
.
So
\mathrm{ad}_{S_{1}}=\mathrm{ad}_{S_{2}}

and thus
\lambda S_{1}=S_{2}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,
cf.
[
126
, lem. 9]
.
As
V\neq 0
, there is an
x\in\mathscr{H}
with
Vx\neq 0
.
Also
S_{1}Vx\neq 0
as
S_{1}
is an isometry and thus injective.
From this and
S_{1}Vx=V^{\prime}x=S_{2}Vx=\lambda S_{1}Vx
, we get
\lambda=1
.
Hence
S_{1}=S_{2}
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

(Existence)
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}

and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathscr{H}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sum_{i}\varrho(a_{i})Vx_{i}\bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\bigl<V^{*}\varrho(a_{j}^{*}a_{i})Vx_{i},x_{j}\bigr>

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\bigl<\varphi(a_{j}^{*}a_{i})x_{i},x_{j}\bigr>

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{i}\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i})V^{\prime}x_{i}\bigr\|^{2}.

It follows (again
using denseness of the linear span of
\varrho(\mathscr{A})V\mathscr{H}
),
that there is a unique isometry
S\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

such that
S\varrho(a)Vx=\varrho^{\prime}(a)V^{\prime}x

for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
x\in\mathscr{H}
.
As
SVx=S\varrho(1)Vx=\varrho^{\prime}(1)V^{\prime}x=V^{\prime}x
for all
x\in\mathscr{H}
,
we have
SV=V^{\prime}
. Furthermore
we have

\displaystyle S\varrho(a)\sum_{i}\varrho(a_{i})Vx_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i}S\varrho(aa_{i})Vx_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i}\varrho^{\prime}(aa_{i})V^{\prime}x_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \varrho^{\prime}(a)\sum_{i}\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i})V^{\prime}x_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \varrho^{\prime}(a)S\sum_{i}\varrho(a_{i})Vx_{i}.

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
and

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathscr{H}
.
So
S\varrho(a)=\varrho^{\prime}(a)S
.
Thus
S^{*}\varrho^{\prime}(a)S=S^{*}S\varrho(a)=\varrho(a)
for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
,
whence
\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
.
\square

†
†
margin:

IX

Exercise*
The statement of the universal property

2.1

for Stinespring
was proposed by Chris Heunen in an unpublished note.
In this note he shows it is equivalent to the existence
of a left-adjoint, as we will see in this exercise.

Let
\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathrm{cp}}

denote the category with as objects
ncpu-maps
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

and as arrows between
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}^{\prime})

pairs
(m,S)

where
m\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
is an nmiu-map
and
S\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{H}^{\prime}
is an isometry
such that
\varphi=\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}m
.
Write
\mathsf{Rep}

for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathrm{cp}}

consisting of objects
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

that are nmiu.

Show using
2.1

that the inclusion functor
U\colon\mathsf{Rep}\to\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathrm{cp}}
has a left adjoint.

†
†
margin:

IXa

Remarks
Recently there have been two interesting categorical
discoveries concerning Stinespring’s dilation theorem,
which deserve a mention.
Parzygnat found a rather different characterization
of Stinespring’s dilation
theorem as an adjunction
[
92
]
.
Secondly, Huot and Staton discovered
that the category of completely positive maps
between matrix algebras
is the universal monoidal category with terminal unit
with a functor from the category of matrix algebras
with isometries between them.
[
54
]

This fact is surprisingly closely related
to Stinespring’s dilation theorem for matrix algebras.

†
†
margin:

X

In the special case of ncp-maps
of the form
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
,
there is a different and noteworthy property
concerning dilations ‘of the same dimension’
called
essential uniqueness of purification
.
This property is the cornerstone of several publications,
notably
[
18
]
, which popularized it.

†
†
margin:

XI

Exercise* (Essential uniqueness of purification)
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

be any ncp-map.
By
2.1

here is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and bounded operator
V\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

such that
V^{*}(a\otimes 1)V=\varphi(a)
.
Assume there is a bounded
operator
W\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
W^{*}(a\otimes 1)W=\varphi(a)
as well.
We will show there is a unitary
U\colon\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

such that
V=(1\otimes U)W
.

First show that in the special case the dilations are minimal
(i.e. the linear span of
\{(a\otimes 1)Vx;\ a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}),x\in\mathscr{K}\}
is dense
in
\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
),
there is a (unique) unitary
U_{0}
on
\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
V=U_{0}W
and
(a\otimes 1)U_{0}=U_{0}(a\otimes 1)

for all
a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
.
Derive from the latter
that for each
y\in\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
and unit-vector
e\in\mathscr{H}
,
there is a
y^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
U_{0}(e\otimes y)=e\otimes y^{\prime}
.
Conclude
U_{0}=1\otimes U
for some unitary
U
on
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
.
Show that the general case follows from the special case
in which the dilations are minimal.

†
†
margin:

140

We are ready to state the more general universal property
that Stinespring’s dilation admits,
which will allow us to generalize it to arbitrary ncp-maps.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

be any ncp-map between von Neumann algebras.
A
Paschke dilation
of
\varphi

is a triple
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)

with a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{P}
;
an nmiu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}

and ncp-map
h\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{B}

with
\varphi=h\mathop{\circ}\varrho

and the following universal property holds.

For every triple
(\mathscr{P}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},h^{\prime})

with a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
;
an nmiu-map
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}

and ncp-map
h^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}

with
\varphi=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}
,
there is a unique ncp-map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}

(the “mediating map”)
with
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

and
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime}
.

-1

†
†
margin:

III

Theorem
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

be any ncp-map
together with a minimal normal Stinespring dilation
(\mathscr{K},\varrho,V)
.
Then
(\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K}),\varrho,\mathrm{ad}_{V})

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
(This is essentially the same proof
we gave in
[
127
]
.)

Let
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
be any von Neumann algebra
together with nmiu-map
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}

and ncp-map
h^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

for which it holds that
h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varphi
.
We must show that there is a unique
ncp-map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

such that
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

and
\mathrm{ad}_{V}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime}
.
First we will prove uniqueness of this map
\sigma
.
Then we will show that such a
\sigma
exists
by appealing to the universal property
2.1
.

†
†
margin:

V

(Uniqueness)
Let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

be two such ncp-maps.
Similarly to the uniqueness proof in
2.1
,
we compute
that for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,

a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}\in\mathscr{A}
,

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1}\ldots,y_{m}\in\mathscr{H}

and
c\in\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl<\sigma_{k}(c)\sum_{i}\varrho(a_{i})Vx_{i},\sum_{j}\varrho(a_{j})Vx_{j}\bigr>

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\bigl<V^{*}\varrho(\alpha_{j}^{*})\sigma_{k}(c)\varrho(a_{i})Vx_{i},y_{i}\bigr>

\displaystyle\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{dils-univlemma}}}}}{{=}}\ \sum_{i,j}\bigl<V^{*}\sigma_{k}\bigl(\varrho^{\prime}(\alpha_{j}^{*})c\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i})\bigr)Vx_{i},y_{j}\bigr>

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\bigl<h^{\prime}(\varrho^{\prime}(\alpha_{j}^{*})c\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i}))x_{i},y_{j}\bigr>.

By definition of minimality of a Stinespring dilation,
the set of vectors of the form
\sum_{i}\varrho(a_{i})Vx_{i}

is norm dense in
\mathscr{K}

and so
\sigma_{1}(c)=\sigma_{2}(c)
as desired.

†
†
margin:

VI

(Existence)
Let
(\tilde{\mathscr{K}},\tilde{\varrho},\tilde{V})

be a minimal normal Stinespring dilation of
h^{\prime}
.
The triple
(\tilde{\mathscr{K}},\tilde{\varrho}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime},\tilde{V})

is a normal Stinespring dilation of
\varphi
.
Thus by
2.1

there is a (unique) isometry
S\colon\mathscr{K}\to\tilde{\mathscr{K}}

with
SV=\tilde{V}

and
\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\tilde{\varrho}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
.
Define
\sigma=\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\tilde{\varrho}
.
Clearly
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\tilde{\varrho}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

and
\mathrm{ad}_{V}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=\mathrm{ad}_{V}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\tilde{\varrho}=\mathrm{ad}_{SV}\mathop{\circ}\tilde{\varrho}=\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{V}}\mathop{\circ}\tilde{\varrho}=h^{\prime}

as desired.
\square

-1

†
†
margin:

VII

The converse holds as well:
by
2.1

every Paschke dilation of a map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

is up to a unique nmiu-isomorphism a minimal Stinespring dilation.
Later we will show that
every ncp-map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

has a Paschke dilation:
it turns out that Paschke’s generalization of
GNS to Hilbert C
∗
-modules fits the bill.
Before we show this,
we look at some other examples and basic properties
of Paschke dilations.
First, as Paschke dilations are defined by a simple universal property,
they are unique up to a unique nmiu-isomorphism:

-1

†
†
margin:

VIII

Lemma
If
(\mathscr{P}_{1},\varrho_{1},h_{1})

and
(\mathscr{P}_{2},\varrho_{2},h_{2})

are two Paschke dilations for
the same ncp-map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
,
then there is a unique (nmiu)
isomorphism
\vartheta\colon\mathscr{P}_{1}\to\mathscr{P}_{2}

with
\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}

and
h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta=h_{1}
.

†
†
margin:

IX

Proof
There are unique mediating maps
\sigma_{1}\colon\mathscr{P}_{1}\to\mathscr{P}_{2}

and
\sigma_{2}\colon\mathscr{P}_{2}\to\mathscr{P}_{1}
.
It is easy to see
\sigma_{1}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{2}

satisfies the same defining property
as the unique mediating map
\mathrm{id}\colon\mathscr{P}_{2}\to\mathscr{P}_{2}

and so
\sigma_{1}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{2}=\mathrm{id}
.
Similarly
\sigma_{2}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{1}=\mathrm{id}
.
Define
\vartheta=\sigma_{1}
.
We just saw
\vartheta
is an ncp-isomorphism.
Note
\vartheta(1)=\vartheta(\varrho_{1}(1))=\varrho_{2}(1)=1

and so
\vartheta
is unital.
But then

\vartheta
is an nmiu-isomorphism
by
.
\square

-1

†
†
margin:

X

Exercise
The following can be shown using only the defining universal property.

1.

Show
(\mathscr{A},\varrho,\mathrm{id})
is a Paschke dilation of
an nmiu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
.

2.

Let
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
be any Paschke dilation.
Prove
(\mathscr{P},\mathrm{id},h)
is a Paschke dilation for
h
.
(Hint: use
\mathrm{id}\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}

is the unique ncp-map with
\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}\varrho=\varrho

and
h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=h
.)

3.

Let
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}_{1}\oplus\mathscr{B}_{2}

be an ncp-map.
Show
(\mathscr{P}_{1}\oplus\mathscr{P}_{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varrho_{1}\\
\varrho_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),h_{1}\oplus h_{2})

is a Paschke dilation of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

if
(\mathscr{P}_{i},\varrho_{i},h_{i})

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

4.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

be any ncp-map with Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)

and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
with
\lambda>0
.
Prove
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,\lambda h)

is a Paschke dilation of
\lambda\varphi
.

margin:

XI

Examples
Later we will be able to compute more dilations.

1.

In
2.5.1
, we will see
that if
(\mathscr{P}_{i},\varrho_{i},h_{i})

is a Paschke dilation of an ncp-map
\varphi_{i}\colon\mathscr{A}_{i}\to\mathscr{B}_{i}

for
i=1,2
,
then
(\mathscr{P}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{2},\varrho_{1}\otimes\varrho_{2},h_{1}\otimes h_{2})

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}
.

2.

In
2.6
, we will
show that
(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A},h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil},h_{p})

is a Paschke dilation of the
ncp-map
h\colon\mathscr{A}\to p\mathscr{A}p

given by
h(a)=pap
for some projection
p\in\mathscr{A}
.

3.

In
2.6
, it’s proven
that a map
\varphi

with Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)

is pure (in the sense of
)
if and only if
\varrho
is surjective.

4.

In
2.6

the Paschke dilations of
\varphi

and
c\mathop{\circ}\varphi
for a filter
c
are related.

-1

2.2

Hilbert C
∗
-modules

†
†
margin:

141

Recall that by GNS
every state (pu-map)
\omega\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}

on a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{A}

splits as
h\mathop{\circ}\varrho

for some nmiu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
,
Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}

and vector state
h(T)=\left<x,Tx\right>

for some
x\in\mathscr{H}
.
It is easy to see
(\mathscr{H},\varrho,h)
is a Paschke dilation of
\omega
.

Paschke showed that GNS can be generalized by replacing the scalars
\mathbb{C}

with an arbitrary von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
:
every ‘state’ ncp-map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

splits as
h\mathop{\circ}\varrho

for some nmiu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
,
and ‘vector state’
h(T)=\left<x,Tx\right>
,
where
X
is a self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-module over
\mathscr{B}
,
x\in X

and
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
are the
\mathscr{B}
-linear bounded operators on
X
.
It turns out
(\mathscr{B}^{a}(X),\varrho,h)

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi
.
Before we will prove this,
we first develop the theory of (self-dual) Hilbert C
∗
-modules.

We already encountered Hilbert C
∗
-modules
in the development of the basics of completely positive maps
in my twin brother’s thesis
[
124
]
.
(They appear in
.)
As Hilbert C
∗
-modules are central to the present topic,
we will prove some basic results again, which were already
covered by my twin.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a C
∗
-algebra and
X

be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module.
A
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product

on
X

is a map
\left<\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right>\colon X\times X\to\mathscr{B}

such that

1.

\left<x,\,\cdot\,\right>\colon X\to\mathscr{B}

is
\mathscr{B}
-linear for all
x\in X

— viz.
\left<x,yb\right>=\left<x,y\right>b
;

2.

\left<x,y\right>^{*}=\left<y,x\right>

for all
x,y\in X

and

3.

\left<x,x\right>\geqslant 0
for all
x\in X
.

A
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product is called
definite

provided
x=0
whenever
\left<x,x\right>=0
.
A
pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module

X

is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

together with a definite
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product.

It will turn out
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\|x\|}=\|\left<x,x\right>\|^{\frac{1}{2}}

defines a norm on a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X
.
A
Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module

(also called a
Hilbert C
∗
-module over
\mathscr{B}
)
is a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
which is complete in this norm.

†
†
margin:

IIa

A pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module is called
self dual

if for each bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear
\tau\colon X\to\mathscr{B}
,
there is a
t\in X
such that
\tau(x)=\left<t,x\right>

for all
x\in X
.

†
†
margin:

IIb

Beware
In his original definition
[
93
, §2.1]
, Paschke
chose to make his
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
\mathscr{B}
-linear
on the left instead of on the right.
He also assumes his inner products to be definite.

†
†
margin:

III

Examples
Several examples
[
93
]
of (pre-)Hilbert C
∗
-modules follow,
to each of which we will return in the remainder.

•

Hilbert
\mathbb{C}
-modules are the same as Hilbert spaces.
They are all self dual.

•

Any C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
is a self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-module over itself
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
\left<a,b\right>=a^{*}b
.
By the C
∗
-identity, the Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
norm on
\mathscr{B}
coincides with
the norm of the C
∗
-algebra.

•

More generally: if
J\subseteq\mathscr{B}

is any right ideal of
\mathscr{B}
,
then
J
is a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\left<a,b\right>=a^{*}b
.
If
J
is closed with respect to the norm, then
J
is a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
It might not be self dual.

•

Principal right-ideals are self dual:
the right ideal
e\mathscr{B}
for a projection
e\in\mathscr{B}

is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module with
\left<ea,eb\right>=a^{*}eb
.
Later we will see that every self-dual
Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module over a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}

is built up from such
e\mathscr{B}
.

•

If
X
and
Y
are (pre-)Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules,
then their direct sum
X\oplus Y
(as right
\mathscr{B}
-modules)
is a (pre-)Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
with the familiar inner product
\left<(x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\right>=\left<x,x^{\prime}\right>+\left<y,y^{\prime}\right>
.
If
X
and
Y
are self dual, then so is
X\oplus Y
.

2.2.1

The basics of Hilbert C
∗
-modules

†
†
margin:

142

Perhaps the most important stones in the foundation
of the theory of operator algebras
are the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality

|\langle x,y\rangle|^{2}\leqslant\|x\|^{2}\|y\|^{2}

and the polarization
identity
\langle x,y\rangle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k=0}^{3}i^{k}\|i^{k}x+y\|^{2}
,
see
.
Also in the development of Hilbert C
∗
-modules,
their generalizations support the bulk of the structure.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
X
be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner
product
\left<\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right>
for some
C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
For any positive functional
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
,
write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\left<x,y\right>_{f}}=f(\left<x,y\right>)

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\|x\|_{f}}=\smash{\langle x,x\rangle^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{f}}
.
This
\left<\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right>_{f}

is a complex-valued inner product on
X
.
Hence, by
,
we know that
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}
is a seminorm
(i.e. a not-necessarily-positive-definite norm.)

†
†
margin:

IIa

We start with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
for
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner products.
The proof is the same as Paschke gives
in
[
93
, prop. 2.3 (ii)]
,
but we use it to prove a slightly more general result.

†
†
margin:

III

Proposition (Cauchy–Schwarz)
Let
X
be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
\left<\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right>

for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Then
\left<x,y\right>\left<y,x\right>\ \leqslant\ \|\left<y,y\right>\|\left<x,x\right>
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
Let
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
be any state (pu-map).
Since the states on
\mathscr{B}
are order separating,
see
,
it suffices to show
f(\left<x,y\right>\left<y,x\right>)\leqslant\|\left<y,y\right>\|f(\left<x,x\right>)
.
If
f(\left<x,y\right>\left<y,x\right>)=0

the inequality holds trivially,
so assume
f(\left<x,y\right>\left<y,x\right>)\neq 0
.
Using Cauchy–Schwarz for
\left<\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right>_{f}
,
we derive

\displaystyle f(\left<x,y\right>\left<y,x\right>)^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \left<x,y\left<y,x\right>\right>_{f}^{2}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|x\|_{f}^{2}\,\|y\left<y,x\right>\|_{f}^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \|x\|_{f}^{2}\,f(\left<x,y\right>\left<y,y\right>\left<y,x\right>)

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|x\|_{f}^{2}\,\|\left<y,y\right>\|\,f(\left<x,y\right>\left<y,x\right>),

which yields the inequality by dividing by

f(\left<x,y\right>\left<y,x\right>)
.
\square

†
†
margin:

V

Exercise
Let
X
be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
\left<\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right>

for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
First use Cauchy–Schwarz (
2.2.1
)
to show
\|\left<x,y\right>\|\leqslant\|x\|\|y\|
.
With this, derive
\|x\|=\|\left<x,x\right>\|^{\frac{1}{2}}
is a seminorm on
X
with

\|x\cdot b\|\leqslant\|x\|\|b\|

for all
b\in\mathscr{B}
.
(See
[
93
, prop. 2.3]
.)

†
†
margin:

VI

From this, it follows easily that
that the addition and the module action
are jointly uniformly continuous
and that the inner product is uniformly continuous in each argument
separately.
(The inner product is also jointly continuous,
but not uniformly.)

†
†
margin:

VII

Definition
Let
V
be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
A
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form
on
V

is a map
B\colon V\times V\to\mathscr{B}

such that for each
y\in V

the maps
x\mapsto B(y,x)

and
x\mapsto B(x,y)^{*}
are
\mathscr{B}
-linear — that is:

\beta^{*}\,\langle x,y\rangle\,b\ =\ \langle\,x\beta\,,\,yb\,\rangle\qquad\text{for all $x,y\in X$ and~$b,\beta\in\mathscr{B}$}.

†
†
margin:

VIIa

Remark
It seems that
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear forms do not explicitly appear
in the established literature,
even though its associated polarization identity
is often used implicitly.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Example
Let
X
be a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
For every
\mathscr{B}
-linear
T\colon X\to X

the map
\left<(\,\cdot\,),T(\,\cdot\,)\right>

is a
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form.
In
2.2.3

we will see that on a self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-module
all
bounded

\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear forms
are of this form.

†
†
margin:

IX

Exercise
Let
B
be a
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form.
Prove the
polarization identity
:

B(x,y)\ =\ \frac{1}{4}\sum_{k=0}^{3}i^{k}B(i^{k}x+y,i^{k}x+y).

†
†
margin:

143

Definition
Let
X
,
Y
be pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules
for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
A (
\mathbb{C}
-)linear map
T\colon X\to Y

is said to be
adjointable

if there is a linear map
S\colon Y\to X

such that
\left<y,Tx\right>_{Y}=\left<Sy,x\right>_{X}

for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.
Adjoints are unique:
if
S
and
S^{\prime}
are both adjoints of
T

an easy computation shows
\left<(S-S^{\prime})y,(S-S)^{\prime}y\right>=0

for all
y\in Y
.
We write
T^{*}
for the adjoint of
T
(if it exists)
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)}

for the subset of (norm-)bounded operators on
X

which are adjointable. (See
[
93
, §2]
.)

†
†
margin:

Ia

The second part of the following lemma is well-known,
but is proven in a new way using
the equivalence which is inspired
by
[
93
, thm. 2.8 (ii)]
.

†
†
margin:

II

Lemma
For a linear map
T\colon X\to Y

between pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules
X
,
Y

and

real number
B>0
,
the following are equivalent.

1.

\|Tx\|\leqslant B\|x\|
for all
x\in X
.

2.

\|\left<y,Tx\right>\|\leqslant B\|y\|\|x\|

for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.

If
T
is bounded and adjointable, then

\|T^{*}\|=\|T\|
and
\|T^{*}T\|=\|T\|^{2}
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
Assume
\|Tx\|\leqslant B\|x\|
for all
x\in X
.
Then by
2.2.1

we find that for all
y\in X
we have
\|\left<y,Tx\right>\|\leqslant\|y\|\|Tx\|\leqslant B\|y\|\|x\|
, as desired.

For the converse, pick
x\in X

and assume
\|\left<y,Tx\right>\|\leqslant B\|y\|\|x\|

for all
y\in Y
.
Then
\|Tx\|^{2}=\|\left<Tx,Tx\right>\|\leqslant B\|Tx\|\|x\|
.
So
\|Tx\|\leqslant B\|x\|

by dividing
\|Tx\|
if
\|Tx\|\neq 0

and trivially otherwise.

Now assume
T
is adjointable and bounded.
Then
\|\left<x,T^{*}y\right>\|=\|\left<y,Tx\right>\|\leqslant\|T\|\|y\|\|x\|

for all
x\in X
,
y\in Y

and so by the previous
\|T^{*}\|\leqslant\|T\|
.
As adjoints are unique and
T
is an adjoint of
T^{*}

we get
T^{**}=T

and so
\|T\|=\|T^{**}\|\leqslant\|T^{*}\|\leqslant\|T\|
, as desired.

For the final identity, note that for any
x\in X
we have

\|Tx\|^{2}\ =\ \|\left<Tx,Tx\right>\|\ =\ \|\left<x,T^{*}Tx\right>\|\ \leqslant\ \|x\|\|T^{*}Tx\|\ \leqslant\ \|x\|^{2}\|T^{*}T\|

and so
\|T\|\leqslant\|T^{*}T\|^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Hence
\|T\|^{2}\leqslant\|T^{*}T\|\leqslant\|T^{*}\|\|T\|=\|T\|^{2}
.

\square

†
†
margin:

IV

Proposition
Let
X
be a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
With composition as multiplication,
adjoint as involution
and operator norm,
the set of adjointable operators

\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
is a C
∗
-algebra.
[
93
, §2]

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
It is easy to see
T^{*}+S^{*}
is an adjoint of
T+S

for
T,S\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

and so
T+S
is adjointable with
(T+S)^{*}=T^{*}+S^{*}
.
Similarly it is easy to see
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

is closed under scalar multiplication, composition and involution
with
(\lambda T)^{*}=\overline{\lambda}T^{*}
,

(TS)^{*}=S^{*}T^{*}
and

T^{**}=T
.
Also the identity
1
is clearly bounded and self-adjoint.
So
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
is a unital
*
-algebra.
By
2.2.1

the C
∗
-identity holds.
It only remains to be shown that
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
is complete.
Let
T_{1},T_{2},\ldots
be a Cauchy sequence in
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
.
As
X
is a Banach space
\mathscr{B}(X)
is complete
and so
T_{n}\to T
for some
T\in\mathscr{B}(X)
.
We have to show
T
is adjointable.
For any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\|T_{n}^{*}-T_{m}^{*}\|=\|(T_{n}-T_{m})^{*}\|=\|T_{n}-T_{m}\|

and so
T^{*}_{1},T^{*}_{2},\ldots

is also Cauchy
and converges to, say,
S\in\mathscr{B}(X)
.
So

\left<Sx,y\right>=\langle\lim_{n}T^{*}_{n}x,y\rangle=\lim_{n}\left<T_{n}^{*}x,y\right>=\lim_{n}\left<x,T_{n}y\right>=\left<x,Ty\right>,

for any
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.
Thus
S=T^{*}
and
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

144

Proposition
Let
X
be a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
The vector states on
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
are order separating —
that is:
T\geqslant 0
if and only if
\left<x,Tx\right>\geqslant 0

for all
x\in X
.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
(Proof from
[
81
, lem. 4.1]
.)
Assume
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
with
T\geqslant 0
.
Then
T=S^{*}S
for some
S\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

and so
\left<x,Tx\right>=\left<Sx,Sx\right>\geqslant 0

for all
x\in X
.

For the converse, assume
\left<x,Tx\right>\geqslant 0

for all
x\in X
.
We claim
T
is self-adjoint.
By the polarization identity

\left<x,Ty\right>=\frac{1}{4}\sum^{3}_{k=0}i^{k}\left<i^{k}x+y,T(i^{k}x+y)\right>
,
see
2.2.1
,
we have
\left<x,Ty\right>=\left<Tx,y\right>
for all
x,y\in X

and so
T^{*}=T
.
There are positive
T_{+},T_{-}\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

with
T=T_{+}-T_{-}
and
T_{+}T_{-}=0
, see
.
By assumption
0\leqslant\left<T_{-}x,TT_{-}x\right>

from which
-\left<x,T_{-}^{3}x\right>\geqslant 0
,
but
T_{-}^{3}\geqslant 0
so
T_{-}^{3}=0
.
Thus
T_{-}=0
(by the functional calculus,
)
and so
T\geqslant 0
.
\square

†
†
margin:

III

Lemma
Let
T\colon X\to Y

be an adjointable linear map
between pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules
X,Y
.
Then
T
is
\mathscr{B}
-linear.
[
93
, §2]

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
We have

\left<y,(Tx)b\right>=\left<T^{*}y,x\right>b=\left<y,T(xb)\right>

for any
x\in X
,
y\in Y
and
b\in\mathscr{B}
.
In particular we
get
\left<(Tx)b-T(xb),(Tx)b-T(xb)\right>=0

taking
y=(Tx)b-T(xb)

and so
T(x)b=T(xb)
.
\square

†
†
margin:

V

Proposition
Let
X
and
Y
be right
\mathscr{B}
-modules with
\mathscr{B}
-valued
inner products for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
If
T\colon X\to Y
is a bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear map,
then we have the
inequality
\left<Tx,Tx\right>\leqslant\|T\|^{2}\left<x,x\right>

for any
x\in X
. (cf.
[
93
, rem. 2.9]
.)

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
(The proof is a slight variation on the first part
of
[
93
, thm. 2.8]
.)
Pick
\varepsilon>0
.
By

we know
\left<x,x\right>+\varepsilon
is invertible.
Define
h=(\left<x,x\right>+\varepsilon)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.
Clearly

0\ \leqslant\ \left<xh,xh\right>\ =\ h(\left<x,x\right>+\varepsilon)h-h^{2}\varepsilon\ =\ 1-h^{2}\varepsilon\ \leqslant\ 1.

Thus
\|xh\|\leqslant 1

and so
\|h\left<Tx,Tx\right>h\|=\|T(xh)\|^{2}\leqslant\|T\|^{2}
.
From this and
0\leqslant h\left<Tx,Tx\right>h

we get
h\left<Tx,Tx\right>h\leqslant\|T\|^{2}
. Hence, by dividing by
h

on both sides,

\left<Tx,Tx\right>\ \leqslant\ \|T\|h^{-2}\ =\ \|T\|^{2}(\left<x,x\right>+\varepsilon)

for all
\varepsilon>0

and so
\left<Tx,Tx\right>\leqslant\|T\|^{2}\left<x,x\right>
as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

145

Proposition
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra,

X
be a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
and
x\in X
.
Then the map
h\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)\to\mathscr{B}

defined by
h(T)=\left<x,Tx\right>

is completely positive.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
Pick any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in\mathscr{B}

and compute

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}h(T_{i}^{*}T_{j})b_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\left<x,T_{i}^{*}T_{j}x\right>b_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\left<T_{i}xb_{i},T_{j}xb_{j}\right>

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl\langle\sum_{i}T_{i}xb_{i},\sum_{i}T_{i}xb_{i}\bigr\rangle\ \geqslant\ 0,

which shows
h
is indeed completely positive.
\square

2.2.2

Ultranorm uniformity

†
†
margin:

146

In
2.1

we saw how to complete a complex vector space with inner product
to a Hilbert space.
Now we will see how to complete a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
to a
self-dual
Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
under the assumption
\mathscr{B}
is a von Neumann algebra.
We will use a different construction than Paschke used
[
93
]
.
Paschke showed that for a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

the set of functionals
X^{\prime}

(i.e.
\mathscr{B}
-module homomorphisms into
\mathscr{B}
)
turns out to be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module,
which he uses as the completion of
X
.
A considerable part of his paper
[
93
]

is devoted to this construction.
It requires Sakai’s characterization of von Neumann algebras,
which we have not covered.
To avoid developing Sakai’s theory,
we give a different construction.
Instead of embedding
X
into a dual space,
we will stay closer to the similar fact for Hilbert spaces
and use a topological completion.
A simple metric completion will not do,
we will need to complete
X
as a
uniform space
.
Uniformities are structures that sit between topological spaces
and metric spaces: every metric space is a uniformity
(see
2.2.2
)
and every uniformity is a topological space.
We will refresh the basics of uniformities —
for a thorough treatment, see for instance
[
133
, ch. 9]
.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
A
uniform space
is a set
X

together with a family of relations

\Phi\subseteq\wp(X\times X)
called
entourages

satisfying the following conditions.

1.

The set of entourages
\Phi
is a filter.
That is:

(a)

if
\varepsilon,\delta\in\Phi
,
then
\varepsilon\cap\delta\in\Phi

and

(b)

if
\varepsilon\subseteq\delta
and
\varepsilon\in\Phi
,
then
\delta\in\Phi
.

2.

For every
\varepsilon\in\Phi

and
x\in X
we have
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}x
.

3.

For each
\varepsilon\in\Phi
,
there is a
\delta\in\Phi

such that
\delta^{2}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
So, if
x\mathrel{\delta}y
and
y\mathrel{\delta}z

then
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}z
for any
x,y,z\in X
.

4.

For every
\varepsilon\in\Phi
,
there is a
\delta\in\Phi

with
\delta^{-1}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
So, if
x\mathrel{\delta}y
then
y\mathrel{\varepsilon}x

for any
x,y\in X
.

A uniform space is
Hausdorff

whenever
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}y

for all
\varepsilon\in\Phi

implies that
x=y
.

†
†
margin:

III

The elements of
X
are the points of the uniform space.
The entourages
\varepsilon\in\Phi

are generalized distances:
one reads
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}y
as
‘the point
x
is at most
\varepsilon
far from to
y
’.
With this in mind, the second axiom states every point is arbitrarily close
to itself.
The third axiom requires that for every entourage
\varepsilon

there is an entourage which acts like
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}
.
There might be several
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}

which fit the bill.
Whenever we write
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}

we implicitly pick some entourage that
satisfies
(\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}})^{2}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
Also we will use the obvious shorthand
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}}=\nicefrac{{(\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}})}}{{2}}
.

†
†
margin:

IIIa

Definition
Let
X
be a set together with
a family of relations
B\subseteq\wp(X\times X)
.
The set
B
is said to be a
subbase (for a uniformity on
X
)

if it satisfies axioms 2, 3 and 4
of
2.2.2
. (Cf.
[
133
, dfn. 5.5]
.)

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Let
X
be a set together with a subbase
B
.
Write
\Phi
for the filter generated by
B

(that is:
\delta\in\Phi

iff
\varepsilon_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\varepsilon_{n}\subseteq\delta

for some
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}\in B
).
Show that
(X,\Phi)
is a uniform space.

†
†
margin:

V

Examples
Using the definition of a subbase, it is easy
to describe the entourages of some common uniformities.

1.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space.
Define
\hat{\varepsilon}\equiv\{(x,y);\ d(x,y)\leqslant\varepsilon\}

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
The set
B\equiv\{\hat{\varepsilon};\ \varepsilon>0\}

is a subbase and so fixes a uniformity
\Phi
on
X
.
In this sense every metric space is a uniformity.

2.

Let
X
be a set together with a (infinite)
family
(d_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}

of pseudometrics.
∥
∥

∥

Like a metric,
a pseudometric
d
is symmetric,
obeys the triangle inequality
and
d(x,x)=0
,
but unlike a metric
d(x,y)=0

is not required to entail
x=y
.

Define
E_{\alpha,\varepsilon}=\{(x,y);\ d_{\alpha}(x,y)\leqslant\varepsilon\}
.
Then
B\equiv\{E_{\alpha,\varepsilon};\ \varepsilon>0,\ \alpha\in I\}

is a subbase and fixes
a uniformity
\Phi
on
X
.
(Every uniform space is of this form, see e.g.
[
133
, thm. 39.11]
.)

3.

Let
V
be a vector space with a
family of seminorms
(\|\ \|_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}
.
As a special case of the previous,

V
has a uniformity fixed by
pseudometrics
d_{\alpha}(x,y)=\|x-y\|_{\alpha}
.
(If additionally
x=0
provided
\|x\|_{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha
,
then
V
is called a locally convex space.)

4.

Assume
\mathscr{B}
is a von Neumann algebra.
There are several uniformities on
\mathscr{B}
— two of which
are of particular interest to us.
The
ultrastrong uniformity
on
\mathscr{B}

is fixed by the seminorms
\|b\|_{f}=f(b^{*}b)^{\frac{1}{2}}

where
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
is an np-map.
The
ultraweak uniformity
on
\mathscr{B}

is given by the seminorms
|f(b)|

for np-maps
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
.

†
†
margin:

VI

We are ready to define a uniformity for Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules.
Later, we will complete
X
with respect to this uniformity.

†
†
margin:

VII

Definition
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra.
Assume
X
is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
.

We call the uniformity on
X

given by seminorms
\|x\|_{f}=f([x,x])^{\frac{1}{2}}

for np-maps
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}

the
ultranorm uniformity
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

The ultranorm uniformity will play a very similar rôle
to the norm for Hilbert spaces.
If
\mathscr{B}=\mathbb{C}
, then the ultranorm uniformity is
the same as the uniformity induced by the norm.
If
X=\mathscr{B}
with
[a,b]=a^{*}b
,
then the ultranorm uniformity coincides with the ultrastrong uniformity.

†
†
margin:

IX

Beware
The ultranorm uniformity is (in general) not given by a single norm.
Furthermore the ultranorm uniformity is weaker than the norm uniformity
(that is: norm convergence implies ultranorm convergence,
but not necessarily the other way around),
even though on von Neumann algebras the
ultraweak uniformity
is stronger than the weak uniformity.

†
†
margin:

X

Remarks
The ultranorm uniformity has appeared earlier in the literature
under various different names; for instance as the
\tau_{1}
-topology
in
[
88
, thm. 3.5.1]
and as the s-topology
in
[
38
, 2.9]
.
In previous work the ultranorm uniformity only plays a minor rôle,
often amongst other topologies.
In this thesis we put the ultranorm uniformity center-stage
as the right generalization of the norm
and give it the ‘ultranorm’ name to match this view.

†
†
margin:

147

Definition
Let
X
be a uniform space with entourages
\Phi
.
It is easy to translate the familiar notions for
metric spaces to uniform spaces.

1.

A net
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is said to

converge
to
x

if for each
\varepsilon\in\Phi

there is an
\alpha_{0}

such that for all
\alpha>\alpha_{0}

we have
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}x_{\alpha}
.
Note that a net can converge to two different points.
