Indeed, this is the case if we start of with a pseudometric
that is not a metric.

2.

A net
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is called
Cauchy

if for each
\varepsilon\in\Phi

there is an
\alpha_{0}

such that for all
\alpha,\beta>\alpha_{0}

we have
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x_{\beta}
.
The uniform space
X
is
complete

when every Cauchy net converges.

3.

Let
(Y,\Psi)
be a second uniform space.
A map
f\colon X\to Y

is said to be
uniformly continuous

if for each
\varepsilon\in\Psi

there is a
\delta\in\Phi

such that for all
x\mathrel{\delta}y

we have
f(x)\mathrel{\varepsilon}f(y)
.
The map
f
is merely
continuous

if for each
x
and
\varepsilon\in\Psi
,
there is a
\delta\in\Phi

such that for all
x\mathrel{\delta}y

we know
f(x)\mathrel{\varepsilon}f(y)
.

4.

We say two Cauchy nets

(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}

and
(y_{\beta})_{\beta\in J}
are
equivalent
,
in symbols:
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(y_{\beta})_{\beta}
,
when for every
\varepsilon\in\Phi

there are
\alpha_{0}\in I
and
\beta_{0}\in J

such that for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
and
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}

we have
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x_{\beta}
.

5.

A subset
D\subseteq X
is said to be
dense

if for each
\varepsilon\in\Phi
and
x\in X
,
there is a
y\in D

with
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}y
.

†
†
margin:

II

Exercise
In the same setting as the previous definition.

1.

Show that equivalence of Cauchy nets is an equivalence relation.
Show that if
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is a subnet of
a Cauchy net
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
,
that then
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is equivalent to
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.

2.

Prove that if
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
and
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

are equivalent Cauchy nets and
x_{\alpha}\to x
,
then also
y_{\alpha}\to x
.

3.

Assume
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is a net with
x_{\alpha}\to x

and
x_{\alpha}\to y
. Prove
x=y
whenever
X
is Hausdorff.

4.

Show that if
f\colon X\to Y
is a continuous map
between uniform spaces
and we have
x_{\alpha}\to x
in
X
,
then
f(x_{\alpha})\to f(x)
in
Y
.

5.

Assume
f
is a uniformly continuous map between uniform spaces.
Show that
f
maps Cauchy nets to Cauchy nets
and furthermore that
f
maps equivalent Cauchy nets to equivalent
Cauchy nets.

6.

Suppose
D\subseteq X
is a dense subset.
Show that for each
x\in X

there is a Cauchy net
(d_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}

in
D
with
d_{\alpha}\to x
in
X
.

7.

Assume
f,g\colon X\to Y
are continuous maps between uniform spaces
where
Y
is Hausdorff.
Conclude from
4
and

6

that
f
and
g
are equal whenever they agree on a dense subset of
X
.

†
†
margin:

III

Exercise
Let
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of sets with
uniformities
(\Phi_{i})_{i\in I}
.
For each
i_{0}\in I

and
\varepsilon\in\Phi_{i_{0}}
,
define a relation on

\Pi_{i\in I}X_{i}
by

(x_{i})_{i\in I}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}}(y_{i})_{i\in I}\iff x_{i_{0}}\mathrel{\varepsilon}y_{i_{0}}
.
Show
\{\hat{\varepsilon};\ \varepsilon\in\Phi_{i},\ i\in I\}

is a subbase for
\Pi_{i\in I}X_{i}
;
that the projections
\pi_{i}\colon\Pi_{i\in I}X_{i}\to X_{i}

are uniformly continuous with respect to them

and
that they make
\Pi_{i\in I}X_{i}

into the product of
(X_{i})_{i\in I}

in the category of uniform spaces with uniformly continuous maps.

†
†
margin:

148

Proposition
Let
X
and
Y
be right
\mathscr{B}
-modules
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner products.
A bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear map
T\colon X\to Y

is uniformly ultranorm continuous.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
Let
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
be an np-map
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Assume
\|x-y\|_{f}\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{\|T\|}
.
By
2.2.1

we have
\left<T(x-y),T(x-y)\right>\leqslant\|T\|^{2}\left<x-y,x-y\right>

and so

\|Tx-Ty\|_{f}\ =\ f(\left<T(x-y),T(x-y)\right>)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \leqslant\ \|T\|\|x-y\|_{f}\leqslant\varepsilon.

Thus
T
is uniformly continuous w.r.t. the ultranorm uniformity.
\square

†
†
margin:

III

Corollary
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra
and
X
a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product.
For
x_{0}\in X
the maps

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto x+y

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left<x_{0},x\right>

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{0}b

\displaystyle X\times X

\displaystyle\to X

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\to\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle\to X

are all uniformly continuous w.r.t. the ultranorm uniformity.

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra and
X
a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product.
Show that
x\mapsto xb

is ultranorm continuous for any
b\in\mathscr{B}
.

†
†
margin:

V

Proposition
Let
X
be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]

for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
If
x_{\alpha}\to x
and
y_{\alpha}\to y

in the ultranorm uniformity,
for some nets
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}
,
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}
,
then
[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}]\to[x,y]
ultraweakly.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
Let
\varepsilon>0
and np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
be given.
Note that
\|y_{\alpha}\|_{f}\to\|y\|_{f}
;
indeed by the reverse triangle inequality

|\|y_{\alpha}\|_{f}-\|y\|_{f}|\leqslant\|y_{\alpha}-y\|_{f}\to 0
.
Thus we can find
\alpha_{1}
such that
\|y_{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant\|y\|_{f}+1

for
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{1}
.

We can find
\alpha_{2}

such that for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{2}

we have
\|x-x_{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon(\|y\|_{f}+1)^{-1}

and

\|y-y_{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon(\|x\|_{f}+1)^{-1}
.
Pick
\alpha_{0}\geqslant\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
.
Then for
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
we have

\displaystyle|f([x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}]-[x,y])|

\displaystyle\ =\ |[x_{\alpha}-x,y_{\alpha}]_{f}+[x,y_{\alpha}-y]_{f}|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|x_{\alpha}-x\|_{f}\|y_{\alpha}\|_{f}+\|x\|_{f}\|y_{\alpha}-y\|_{f}

by
2.2.1

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|x_{\alpha}-x\|_{f}(\|y\|_{f}+1)+(\|x\|_{f}+1)\|y_{\alpha}-y\|_{f}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \varepsilon.

Thus
[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}]\to[x,y]
ultraweakly.
\square

†
†
margin:

VII

Corollary
Let
X
be a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
together with an ultranorm-dense subset
D\subseteq X
.
The vector states from
D
are order separating:
for each
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
we have

T\geqslant 0
if and only if
\left<x,Tx\right>\geqslant 0

for all
x\in D
. Cf.
2.2.1
.

†
†
margin:

149

Definition
Let
X
be pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Let
E\subseteq X
be some subset.

1.

E
is
orthogonal
if
\left<e,d\right>=0

for
e,d\in E
with
e\neq d
.

2.

E
is
orthonormal
if additionally

\left<e,e\right>
is a non-zero projection
for
e\in E
.

3.

A family
(b_{e})_{e\in E}
in
\mathscr{B}

is said to be
\ell^{2}
-summable

if the partial sums of
\sum_{e\in E}b_{e}^{*}b_{e}

are bounded.

4.

E
is an
(orthonormal) basis
if
it is orthonormal and additionally

(a)

for each
x\in X
we have

x\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>,

where the sum converges in the ultranorm uniformity

and

(b)

the sum
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}

converges in the ultranorm uniformity
for any
\ell^{2}
-summable
(b_{e})_{e\in E}

in
\mathscr{B}
.

†
†
margin:

II

Notation
As with Hilbert spaces, it will sometimes be more convenient
to view an orthonormal basis as a sequence/family
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
,
instead of a subset. This should cause no confusion.

†
†
margin:

IIa

Remark
The first assumption ‘(a)’ in the definition of an orthonormal basis
E

requires that every element
x
can be reconstructed
from its coefficients
(\left<e,x\right>)_{e\in E}
.
This assumption fails, for instance, if there is a non-zero element
orthogonal to all basis elements.
The second assumption ‘(b)’ requires that behind
any a reasonable family of coefficients,
there is a corresponding element.
This second assumption fails if the module is not ultranorm complete,
see
2.2.2
.

†
†
margin:

IIb

Beware
In the literature, there are several different definitions of an
orthonormal basis
for a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X
.

1.

Some
[
82
,
88
]

define an orthonormal basis as an orthonormal subset
E\subseteq X

such that
x=\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>

for all
x\in X

where the sum converges in the
norm
.
This notion of basis is independent of ours:
there is a basis in our sense
that is not a basis
in their sense (
\{\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|,\left|1\right>\!\left<1\right|,\ldots\}

in
\mathscr{B}(\ell^{2})
)
and vice versa (the standard Hilbert module
H_{\mathscr{B}(\ell^{2})}

defined in
[
82
, §3]
has a basis
in their sense, but not a basis in ours
as it is not self dual, cf.
2.2.2
.)

2.

Others such as
[
12
, lemma 8.5.23]

define an orthonormal basis
only when
X
is a self-dual (
2.2.2
)
Hilbert C
∗
-module
over a von Neumann algebra.
In this case an orthonormal basis
is defined
as an orthonormal subset
E\subseteq X

such that
x=\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>

for all
x\in X

where the sum converges in the
w^{*}
-topology of
X

(with respect to the unique Banach space predual
of
X
for which the inner product is
separately
w^{*}
-continuous,
see
[
12
, lemma 8.5.4]
.)
This notion is equivalent to ours, but only defined
for self-dual modules over von Neumann algebras.

†
†
margin:

III

Exercise
Let
X
be a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for a C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Show that we have
e\langle e,e\rangle=e

for any
e\in X

where
\langle e,e\rangle
is a projection.

(Hint: consider
\|e(1-\langle e,e\rangle)\|^{2}
;
cf.
[
93
, thm. 3.12]
.)

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Let
X
be a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}

with an orthonormal basis
E\subseteq X
.
Show
Parseval’s identity
holds, that is:

\langle x,x\rangle\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}\langle x,e\rangle\langle e,x\rangle,

where the sum converges ultraweakly.
[
93
]

(Hint: use
2.2.2
.)

†
†
margin:

V

Theorem
Assume
\mathscr{B}
is a von Neumann algebra.
For any pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X
,
the following conditions are equivalent.

1.

X
is self dual.

2.

X
is ultranorm complete.

3.

Every norm-bounded ultranorm-Cauchy net in
X
converges.

4.

X
has an orthonormal basis.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
We will prove
1\Rightarrow 3\Rightarrow 4\Rightarrow 2\Rightarrow 4\Rightarrow 1
.
The equivalence
1\Leftrightarrow 4

was already shown by Paschke in
[
93
, thm. 3.12]
,
but we give a different proof.
The equivalence
1\Leftrightarrow 3
turns out
proven by Frank already
[
32
, thm. 3.2]

in a different way.
The final equivalence
1\Leftrightarrow 2
is new, as far as we know.

†
†
margin:

VII

(1
\Rightarrow
3, self-dual
X
are bounded complete)
Let
X
be a self-dual pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
To show norm-bounded ultranorm completeness,
assume
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is an ultranorm-Cauchy net in
X

with
\|x_{\alpha}\|\leqslant B
for some
B\geqslant 0
.
For the moment pick an
y\in X
.
By
2.2.1
we
have
f(\left<x_{\alpha},y\right>\left<y,x_{\alpha}\right>)\leqslant\|y\|^{2}f(\left<x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}\right>)
for any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}

and so
\left<y,x_{\alpha}\right>
is an ultrastrong-Cauchy net in
\mathscr{B}
.
Von Neumann algebras are ultrastrongly complete, see
,
so we can define
\tau(y)=(\mathop{uslim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<y,x_{\alpha}\right>)^{*}
,
where
\mathop{uslim}\displaylimits
denotes
the ultrastrong limit.
As addition and multiplication by a fixed element
are ultrastrongly continuous (see
)

\tau
is
\mathscr{B}
-linear.
We will show
\tau
is bounded.
For each
\alpha
we have
\left<x_{\alpha},y\right>\left<y,x_{\alpha}\right>\leqslant\|y\|^{2}\left<x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}\right>\leqslant\|y\|^{2}B^{2}
and so

\tau(y)\tau(y)^{*}\ \overset{\textsf{\ref{usconv}}}{=}\ \mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<x_{\alpha},y\right>\left<y,x_{\alpha}\right>\ \leqslant\ \|y\|^{2}B^{2},

where
\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits

denotes the ultraweak limit.
Thus
\|\tau(y)\|^{2}=\|\tau(y)\tau(y)^{*}\|\leqslant\|y\|^{2}B^{2}
.
So
\tau
is bounded.
By self-duality, there is a
t\in X
such that
\tau(y)=\left<t,y\right>

for all
y\in X
.
And so for each np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
we have

\|\left<y,t-x_{\alpha}\right>\|_{f}\ =\ \|\left<y,t\right>-\left<y,x_{\alpha}\right>\|_{f}\ =\ \|\tau(y)^{*}-\left<y,x_{\alpha}\right>\|_{f}\ \rightarrow\ 0.

(2.5)

In
2.2.1

we introduced the inner product
\left<\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right>_{f}

defined by
\left<x,y\right>_{f}\equiv f(\left<x,y\right>)
.
From Kadison’s inequality

it follows
|f(a)|^{2}\leqslant f(a^{*}a)f(1)

and so

|\left<y,t-x_{\alpha}\right>_{f}|^{2}\ \leqslant\ f(\left<t-x_{\alpha},y\right>\left<y,t-x_{\alpha}\right>)f(1)\ =\ \|\left<y,t-x_{\alpha}\right>\|_{f}^{2}f(1).

(2.6)

Combining (
2.5
) and (
2.6
),
we see
|\left<y,t-x_{\alpha}\right>_{f}|\rightarrow 0
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be given.
As
\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}
is norm bounded
we can find
B>0
such that
\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant B

for all
\alpha
.
As
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is ultranorm Cauchy,
we can pick
\alpha
such that
\|x_{\beta}-x_{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant(\|t\|_{f}+B)^{-1}\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
for all
\beta\geqslant\alpha
.
Find
\beta
such that
|\left<t-x_{\alpha},t-x_{\beta}\right>_{f}|\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
.
Putting it all together:

\displaystyle\left<t-x_{\alpha},t-x_{\alpha}\right>_{f}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ |\left<t-x_{\alpha},t-x_{\beta}\right>_{f}|\,+\,|\left<t-x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}-x_{\alpha}\right>_{f}|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\|t-x_{\alpha}\|_{f}\|x_{\beta}-x_{\alpha}\|_{f}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \frac{\varepsilon}{2}+(\|t\|_{f}+B)\,\|x_{\beta}-x_{\alpha}\|_{f}\ \leqslant\ \varepsilon.

Thus
x_{\alpha}
converges ultranorm to
t
.
So
X
is ultranorm bounded complete.

†
†
margin:

VIII

(3

\Rightarrow
4, bounded complete
X
has an orthonormal basis)
Assume norm-bounded ultranorm-Cauchy nets converge in
X
.
By Zorn’s lemma, there is a maximal orthonormal subset
E\subseteq X
.
We will show
E
is a basis.

Let
(b_{e})_{e\in E}
be an
E
-tuple in
\mathscr{B}

such that the partial sums of
\sum_{e\in E}b_{e}^{*}b_{e}
are bounded.
We want to show
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}
converges in the ultranorm
uniformity.
As the summands of
\sum_{e\in E}b_{e}^{*}b_{e}
are positive,
the partial sums form a directed bounded net
that converges ultrastrongly
to its supremum
\sum_{e\in E}b_{e}^{*}b_{e}
.
By
2.2.2
,
we have
e\left<e,e\right>=e
for each
e\in E

and so
\left<x,e\right>\left<e,e\right>=\left<x,e\right>

for every
x\in X
.
Thus for any finite subset
S\subseteq E

\Bigl<\sum_{e\in S}eb_{e},\sum_{e\in S}eb_{e}\Bigr>\ =\ \sum_{e\in S}b_{e}^{*}\left<e,e\right>b_{e}\ \leqslant\ \sum_{e\in S}b_{e}^{*}b_{e}.

To show
\sum_{e}eb_{e}
converges ultranorm,
pick any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
.
As
\sum_{e\in E}b^{*}_{e}b_{e}
converges ultraweakly,
we have
\sum_{e\in E}f(b^{*}_{e}b_{e})<\infty
.
Thus the tails of the series
\sum_{e\in E-S}f(b_{e}^{*}b_{e})
tend to zero, hence for finite
S,T\subseteq E
we know

\bigl\|\sum_{e\in S}eb_{e}-\sum_{e\in T}eb_{e}\bigr\|_{f}^{2}\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{e\in S\Delta T}eb_{e}\bigr\|_{f}^{2}\ \leqslant\ \sum_{e\in S\Delta T}f(b_{e}^{*}b_{e}).

The latter vanishes as
S\cap T
grows.
Thus the partial sums of
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}
are ultranorm Cauchy
and norm-bounded by
\|\sum_{e\in E}b_{e}^{*}b_{e}\|
,
so
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}
converges in the ultranorm uniformity.

Pick
x\in X
.
We have to show
\sum_{e\in E}x\left<e,x\right>

converges in the ultranorm uniformity to
x
.
For any finite subset
S\subseteq E
we have

0\ \leqslant\ \Bigl<x-\sum_{e\in S}e\left<e,x\right>,x-\sum_{e\in S}e\left<e,x\right>\Bigr>\ =\ \left<x,x\right>-\sum_{e\in S}\left<x,e\right>\left<e,x\right>.

Rearranging we find
Bessel’s inequality
:

\sum_{e\in S}\left<x,e\right>\left<e,x\right>\ \leqslant\ \left<x,x\right>.

Hence the
E
-tuple
(\left<e,x\right>)_{e}

is
\ell^{2}
-summable
and so
\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>

converges in the ultranorm uniformity.
Consider
x^{\prime}=x-\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>
.
We are done if we can show
x^{\prime}=0
.

To see
x^{\prime}=0
, we
first show that
X
has
polar decomposition
:
for each
y\in X
, there is an
u\in X

with
\left<u,u\right>=\left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil

and
y=u\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
By

the positive element
\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}

has an approximate pseudoinverse
h_{1},h_{2},\ldots

— that is:

h_{n}\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}h_{n}=\left\lceil h_{n}\right\rceil

and
\sum_{n}h_{n}\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sum_{n}\left\lceil h_{n}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil
. Note

\left<yh_{n},yh_{m}\right>\ =\ (h_{n}\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}})(\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}h_{m})\ =\ \left\lceil h_{n}\right\rceil\left\lceil h_{m}\right\rceil

and so using that
\left\lceil h_{1}\right\rceil,\left\lceil h_{2}\right\rceil,\ldots
are pairwise orthogonal,
we see

\bigl<\sum_{n=1}^{N}yh_{n},\sum_{n=1}^{N}yh_{n}\bigr>\ =\ \sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\left\lceil h_{n}\right\rceil\left\lceil h_{m}\right\rceil\ =\ \sum_{n=1}^{N}\left\lceil h_{n}\right\rceil\ \leqslant\ \left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil.

Thus
(\sum_{n=1}^{N}yh_{n})_{N}
forms an ultranorm-Cauchy net
norm bounded by
1
.
By assumption this bounded net converges:
write
u\equiv\sum_{n}yh_{n}
.
(So in the special case
X=\mathscr{B}
,
we have
u=y/\langle y,y\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
see
.)
We derive

\displaystyle u\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{n}yh_{n}\left<y,y\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}

by dfn.
u
and
2.2.2

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{n}y\left\lceil h_{n}\right\rceil

by dfn.
h_{n}

\displaystyle\ =\ y\sum_{n}\left\lceil h_{n}\right\rceil

by
2.2.2

\displaystyle\ =\ y\left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil

by dfn.
h_{n}

\displaystyle\ =\ y,

where the last equality
follows from
y(1-\left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil)=0
,
which is justified by

\bigl<y(1-\left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil),y(1-\left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil)\bigr>\ =\ (1-\left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil)\left<y,y\right>(1-\left\lceil\left<y,y\right>\right\rceil)\ =\ 0.

This finishes the proof of polar decomposition.

Recall we want to
show
x^{\prime}\equiv x-\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>=0
.
Reasoning towards contradiction, assume
x^{\prime}\neq 0
.
By polar decomposition
x^{\prime}=u\left<x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}

for some
u\in X
with
\left<u,u\right>=\left\lceil\left<x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\right>\right\rceil
.
Note
\left<u,u\right>\neq 0

for otherwise
x^{\prime}=0
, quod non.
For any
e_{0}\in E
, we have

\left<e_{0},x^{\prime}\right>\ =\ \bigl<e_{0},x-\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>\bigr>\ \overset{\textsf{\ref{ultranormcontstruct}}}{=}\ \left<e_{0},x\right>-\sum_{e\in E}\left<e_{0},e\right>\left<e,x\right>\ =\ 0

and so
0=\left<e,x^{\prime}\right>=\left<e,u\right>\left<x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\right>^{\frac{1}{2}}
for any
e\in E
.
Thus

\left<e,u\right>\ =\ \left<e,u\right>\left<u,u\right>\ =\ \left<e,u\right>\left\lceil\left<x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\right>\right\rceil\ \overset{\textsf{\ref{mult-cancellation}}}{=}\ 0.

Thus
E\cup\{u\}
contradicts the maximality of
E
.
Hence
x^{\prime}=0
and so
x=\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>
as desired.

†
†
margin:

IX

(4
\Rightarrow
2, basis implies completeness)
Assume
X
has an orthonormal basis
E
.
Let
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
be an ultranorm-Cauchy net.
Pick
e\in E
.
By
2.2.2

(\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>)_{\alpha}
is an ultrastrong-Cauchy net.
As von Neumann algebras are ultrastrongly complete
(see
)
there is a
b_{e}\in\mathscr{B}

to which
\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>
converges ultrastrongly.
We will show
x_{\alpha}
converges in the ultranorm uniformity
to
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}
.

We will first show that
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}
converges.
As before, it is sufficient to show
\sum_{e\in E}b_{e}^{*}b_{e}
is bounded.
For this, it suffices to show
\sum_{e\in E}f(b_{e}^{*}b_{e})
is bounded
for each np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
,
as ultraweakly-bounded nets are norm bounded,
see
.
For any
e\in E

we have
\|b_{e}\|_{f}=\lim_{\alpha}\|\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>\|_{f}

and so
f(b_{e}^{*}b_{e})=\lim_{\alpha}f(\left<x_{\alpha},e\right>\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>)
.
Pick a finite subset
S\subseteq E
. Then we have

\displaystyle f\bigl(\sum_{e\in S}b_{e}^{*}b_{e}\bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{e\in S}\lim_{\alpha}f(\left<x_{\alpha},e\right>\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>)

\displaystyle\ =\ \lim_{\alpha}f\bigl(\sum_{e\in S}\left<x_{\alpha},e\right>\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>\bigr)

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ f(\left<x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}\right>)

by Bessel’s inequality.

As
x_{\alpha}
is ultranorm Cauchy,
the net
f(\left<x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}\right>)

must be Cauchy and so bounded.
Hence
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}
converges in the ultranorm uniformity.

To prove
x_{\alpha}
converges to
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}
,
pick any
\varepsilon>0

and np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
.
We want to find
\alpha_{0}

such that
\|x_{\alpha}-\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}\|_{f}\leqslant\varepsilon

for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
.
As
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is ultranorm Cauchy,
we can pick an
\alpha_{0}

such that
\|x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\|_{f}\leqslant\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon

for all
\alpha,\beta\geqslant\alpha_{0}
.
As
x_{\alpha}=\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>

we find with Parseval’s identity (
2.2.2
) that

\bigl\|x_{\alpha}-\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}\bigr\|_{f}^{2}\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{e\in E}e(\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>-b_{e})\bigr\|_{f}^{2}\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}\|\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>-b_{e}\|_{f}^{2}.

Take a finite subset
S\subseteq E

such that

\sum_{e\in E-S}\|\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>-b_{e}\|_{f}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}
.
If we can also show

\sum_{e\in S}\|\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>-b_{e}\|_{f}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}
,
then we are done.
For any
\beta\geqslant\alpha

we have using the triangle inequality of
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}

on
X

\displaystyle\Bigl(\sum_{e\in S}\|\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>-b_{e}\|_{f}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{e\in S}e\left<e,x_{\alpha}\right>-eb_{e}\bigr\|_{f}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \bigl\|\sum_{e\in S}e\left<e,x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\right>\bigr\|_{f}\ +\bigl\|\sum_{e\in S}e\left<e,x_{\beta}\right>-eb_{e}\bigr\|_{f}

\displaystyle\ =\ \Bigl(\sum_{e\in S}\|\left<e,x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\right>\|_{f}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Bigl(\sum_{e\in S}\|\left<e,x_{\beta}\right>-b_{e}\|_{f}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We want to show the previous is bounded by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon
.
We bound the two terms separately.
For the first term using Bessel’s
inequality and our choice of
\alpha_{0}

we have

\sum_{e\in S}\|\left<e,x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\right>\|_{f}^{2}\leqslant\|x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\|^{2}_{f}\leqslant\bigl(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon\bigr)^{2}.

As
\|\left<e,x_{\beta}\right>-b_{e}\|_{f}

vanishes for each
e\in S
and
S
is finite,
we can find sufficiently large
\beta

such that the right term is also bounded by
\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon
.
Thus
x_{\alpha}
converges in the ultranorm uniformity
to
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{e}
, as desired.

†
†
margin:

X

(2
\Rightarrow
4, completeness implies basis)
Follows from 3
\Rightarrow
4
as 2
\Rightarrow
3 is trivial.

†
†
margin:

XI

(4
\Rightarrow
1, basis implies self-duality)
Assume
X
has an orthonormal basis
E
.
Suppose
\tau\colon X\to\mathscr{B}
is a bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear map.
We want to show there is some
t\in X

such that
\tau(x)=\left<t,x\right>
for all
x\in X
.
Pick any
x\in X
.
By assumption
\sum_{e\in E}e\left<e,x\right>

converges in the ultranorm uniformity to
x
.
As by
2.2.2

\tau
is ultranorm continuous
we see

\tau(x)\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}\tau(e)\left<e,x\right>\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}\left<e\tau(e)^{*},x\right>.

Thus if we can show
\sum_{e}e\tau(e)^{*}
converges in the ultranorm,
we are done. By assumption this is
the case if
(\tau(e)^{*})_{e\in E}
is
\ell^{2}
-summable
— that is: the partial sums of
\sum_{e\in E}\tau(e)\tau(e)^{*}

must be bounded.
We will show
that for finite
S\subseteq E

we have

\|\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}\|\leqslant\|\tau\|
,
which is sufficient as then
\|\sum_{e\in S}\tau(e)\tau(e)^{*}\|=\|\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}\|^{2}\leqslant\|\tau\|^{2}
.
For the moment pick an arbitrary
x\in X
.
By Bessel’s inequality
we have
\|\sum_{e\in S}e\left<e,x\right>\|^{2}=\sum_{e\in S}\|\left<x,e\right>\left<e,x\right>\|\leqslant\|x\|^{2}
.
Thus

\bigl\|\bigl<\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*},x\bigr>\bigr\|\ =\ \bigl\|\tau\bigl(\sum_{e\in S}e\left<e,x\right>\bigr)\bigr\|\leqslant\|\tau\|\|x\|.

Substituting
\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}
for
x
, we find

\|\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}\|^{2}\ =\ \bigl\|\bigl<\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*},\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}\bigr>\bigr\|\ \leqslant\ \|\tau\|\bigl\|\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}\bigr\|.

If
\|\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}\|=0
,
then we are done.
Otherwise, divide both sides of the previous equation
by
\|\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}\|

to find
\|\sum_{e\in S}e\tau(e)^{*}\|\leqslant\|\tau\|
as desired.
\square

2.2.3

Self-dual completion

†
†
margin:

150

In
2.1

we saw that every vector space with inner product
can be completed into a Hilbert space.
We continue with the generalization to Hilbert C
∗
-modules.
Paschke proves a similar result
[
93
, thm. 3.2]

in a completely different way.

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra
and
V
a right
\mathscr{B}
-module with
\mathscr{B}
-valued
inner product
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
.

There is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

together with a bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear
\eta\colon V\to X

such that

1.

[v,w]=\left<\eta(v),\eta(w)\right>
and

2.

the image of
\eta
is ultranorm dense in
X
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
Before we start the proof proper,
we give an overview.

†
†
margin:

IV

(Overview)
By
2.2.2
and
2.2.2

one would expect that
one can simply extend the inner product of
V

to its ultranorm completion
\overline{V}

by

[(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha},(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}]\ =\ \mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}],

(2.7)

which will turn
\overline{V}
into a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
It will turn out that
\overline{V}

is indeed a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
with this inner product,
but it isn’t clear at all how to show directly that the ultraweak limit
in (
2.7
) converges.

We will sketch an indirect construction,
before delving into the details.
As with a metric completion,

\overline{V}
consists of equivalence classes of ultranorm
Cauchy nets in
V
.
Let
V_{0}\subseteq\overline{V}

denote the equivalence classes of constant Cauchy nets.

V_{0}
is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
using the operations of
V
.
Let
V_{1}\subseteq\overline{V}
denote
the limits of norm-bounded ultranorm Cauchy nets over
V_{0}
.
Using the norm boundedness we can extend
the module structure and inner product of
V_{0}
to
V_{1}
.
If all norm-bounded ultranorm Cauchy-nets over
V_{1}
would
converge in
V_{1}
,
then
V_{1}
would be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
and
V_{1}
would even be ultranorm complete.
Later, when we have established that
\overline{V}
is a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module,
we will find out that in fact
\overline{V}\subseteq V_{1}

due to
2.4

—
i.e.
V_{1}
is already ultranorm complete.
However, in a most frustrating state of affairs,
we do not know how to prove this directly
without first proving that
\overline{V}
is a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
Instead, we repeat: let
V_{2}\subseteq\overline{V}

denote the limits of norm-bounded ultranorm Cauchy nets over
V_{1}
.
We have to go further:
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

define
V_{n+1}=\sigma(V_{n})

where
\sigma(U)\subseteq\overline{V}

denotes the limits of norm-bounded ultranorm Cauchy over
U
.

V_{0}\ \subseteq\ V_{1}\ \subseteq\ V_{2}\ \subseteq\ V_{3}\ \subseteq\ \cdots\ \subseteq\ \bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{n}.

Will we be able to show that
\bigcup_{n}V_{n}
is finally ultranorm complete?
Not yet.
We have to go even further.
Define
V_{\omega}=\sigma(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{n})
,

V_{\omega+1}=\sigma(V_{\omega})

and so on:
for any ordinal number
\alpha>0
,
define
V_{\alpha}=\sigma(\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}V_{\beta})
.
Now
V_{\alpha}

is an ascending chain of subsets of
\overline{V}
.
Thus
V_{\alpha_{0}}=V_{{\alpha_{0}}+1}

for some
\alpha_{0}\leqslant|2^{\overline{V}}|

(for otherwise
|2^{\overline{V}}|\leqslant|\overline{V}|
).
Thus norm-bounded ultranorm Cauchy nets in
V_{\alpha_{0}}

must converge.
We will see that at each step we can extend the module structure
and inner product and with those
V_{\alpha_{0}}
is self-dual.
Thus
V_{\alpha_{0}}
is ultranorm complete
and actually
V_{\alpha_{0}}=\overline{V}
.

V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V_{\omega}\subseteq V_{\omega+1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V_{2\omega}\subseteq\ \cdots\ \ \cdots\ \subseteq V_{\alpha_{0}}=\overline{V}

To avoid requiring familiarity with transfinite induction,
we will phrase the proof using Zorn’s lemma instead.

†
†
margin:

V

(
\overline{V}
: fast nets)
As we need some details in its construction,
we will explicitly define
\overline{V}
,
the completion of
V
in the ultranorm uniformity
using Cauchy nets.
There are other ways to construct a completion of a uniform space,
see for instance
[
133
, thm. 39.12]
.

Let
\Phi
denote the set of entourages of the ultranorm
uniformity on
V
; for its definition see
2.2.2
.

\Phi
is a filter and thus can be used as index set for a net
using reverse inclusion as order.
(Thus
\varepsilon\geqslant\delta\ \Leftrightarrow\ \varepsilon\subseteq\delta
.)
We say a net
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
indexed by entourages
is
fast
if for every
\varepsilon\in\Phi

and
\alpha,\beta\geqslant\varepsilon

we have
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon^{2}}x_{\beta}
.

Every Cauchy net is equivalent to a fast one, but this is not as
evident as in the metric case.
Let
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}

be an arbitrary Cauchy net in
V
.
By definition we can find
\alpha_{\varepsilon}\in I

such that for each
\varepsilon\in\Phi

and
\alpha,\beta\geqslant\alpha_{\varepsilon}

we have
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x_{\beta}
.
Unfortunately
\alpha_{(\,\cdot\,)}
need not be order preserving
and so
(x_{\alpha_{\varepsilon}})_{\varepsilon\in\Phi}

need not be a subnet of
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}
.
However, we claim the net
(x_{\alpha_{\varepsilon}})_{\varepsilon\in\Phi}

is Cauchy, fast and equivalent to
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I}
.
To show it’s Cauchy and fast, pick any
\varepsilon\in\Phi

and
\zeta,\xi\geqslant\varepsilon

(that is:
\zeta,\xi\subseteq\varepsilon
).
As
I
is directed, we can find
\beta\in I

with
\beta\geqslant\alpha_{\zeta},\alpha_{\xi}
.
By definition of
\alpha_{(\,\cdot\,)}

we have
x_{\alpha_{\zeta}}\mathrel{\zeta}x_{\beta}\mathrel{\xi}x_{\alpha_{\xi}}

and so
x_{\alpha_{\zeta}}\mathrel{\varepsilon^{2}}x_{\alpha_{\xi}}
, as desired.
To show equivalence, assume
\varepsilon\in\Phi
is given.
Assume
\delta\geqslant\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}

and
\beta\geqslant\alpha_{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}}
.
There is a
\gamma\in I
with
\gamma\geqslant\alpha_{\delta},\beta
.
Then
x_{\beta}\mathrel{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}}x_{\gamma}\mathrel{\delta}x_{\alpha_{\delta}}

and so as
\delta\subseteq\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}

we get
x_{\beta}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x_{\alpha_{\delta}}
.
We have shown equivalence.

A different fact about fast nets will be useful later on:
if fast Cauchy nets
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}

and
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
are equivalent,
then we can find for every
\varepsilon\in\Phi

some
\beta\in\Phi

such that for all
\gamma\geqslant\beta
,
we have
x_{\gamma}\mathrel{\varepsilon}y_{\gamma}
.

†
†
margin:

VI

(
\overline{V}
: the uniform space
N
)
Write
N
for the set of fast Cauchy nets over
V
.
Later we will define
\overline{V}
as
N
modulo equivalence.
Because of a subtlety with the definition of the uniformity
on
\overline{V}
later,
it is helpful to consider
N
separately.
Let
\varepsilon\in\Phi
be given.
For nets
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

and
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
in
N
,
define

(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\hat{\varepsilon}}{\@index{\@indexphrase[*epsi]{$\hat{\varepsilon}$}}}}(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\beta\in\Phi\,\forall\gamma\geqslant\beta.\ x_{\gamma}\mathrel{\varepsilon}y_{\gamma}.

If
\varepsilon\subseteq\delta
,
then
\hat{\varepsilon}\subseteq\hat{\delta}

and

\hat{\varepsilon}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\hat{\varepsilon}_{2}\subseteq\widehat{\varepsilon_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varepsilon_{2}}
.
So
\widehat{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}}\mathop{\circ}\widehat{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}}\subseteq\widehat{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}\mathop{\circ}\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{2}}}\subseteq\widehat{\varepsilon}
,
which is one requirement for
\{\hat{\varepsilon};\ \varepsilon\in\Phi\}

to be a subbase for
N
.
The others are easy as well.
Also
\widehat{\varepsilon_{1}\cap\varepsilon_{2}}=\hat{\varepsilon}_{1}\cap\hat{\varepsilon}_{2}

and so each entourage of
N
has some
\hat{\varepsilon}
as subset.
For the following,
it is helpful to remark now
that Cauchy nets
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
and
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
over
V

are equivalent if and only if
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}}(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
for all
\varepsilon\in\Phi
.

†
†
margin:

VII

(
\overline{V}
:
N
is complete)
As every Cauchy net is equivalent to a fast one,
it is sufficient to show convergence of fast Cauchy nets.
Thus let
((x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha})_{\gamma}

be a fast Cauchy net in
N
.
First we will show
(x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

is a Cauchy net.
It might not be fast, so
formally
((x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha})_{\gamma}

cannot converge to it,
but it will converge to an equivalent fast Cauchy net.
As
((x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha})_{\gamma}

is fast,
we know that for all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\geqslant\hat{\varepsilon}

we have
(x^{\gamma_{1}}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}^{2}}(x^{\gamma_{2}}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
So there is a
\zeta_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}\in\Phi

such that for
\alpha\geqslant\zeta_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}

we have
x^{\gamma_{1}}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon^{2}}x^{\gamma_{2}}_{\alpha}
.
Suppose
\alpha,\beta\geqslant\varepsilon
.
Pick
\xi\geqslant\alpha,\beta,\zeta_{\hat{\alpha},\hat{\beta}}
.
Then we have
x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon^{2}}x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\xi}\mathrel{\varepsilon^{2}}x^{\hat{\beta}}_{\xi}\mathrel{\varepsilon^{2}}x^{\hat{\beta}}_{\beta}
and so
(x_{\alpha}^{\hat{\alpha}})_{\alpha}

is Cauchy.
Let
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
be a fast Cauchy net equivalent
to
(x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
We want to prove
(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\to(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
As
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is equivalent to
(x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

we can find
\alpha_{0}

such that
y_{\alpha}\mathrel{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}}}x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\alpha}

whenever
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
.
As
((x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha})_{\gamma}

is fast Cauchy,
we know that
(x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\beta})_{\beta}\mathrel{\widehat{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}}}}(x^{\gamma}_{\beta})_{\beta}

if
\hat{\alpha},\gamma\geqslant\widehat{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{8}}}
.
Thus there is some
\beta_{0}
such
that
x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\beta}\mathrel{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}}}x^{\gamma}_{\beta}

for
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}

and
\hat{\alpha},\gamma\geqslant\widehat{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{8}}}
.
Thus for
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}\cap\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{8}}
,

\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}\cap\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{8}}

and
\gamma\geqslant\widehat{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{8}}}

we get
y_{\alpha}\mathrel{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}}}x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}}}x^{\hat{\alpha}}_{\beta}\mathrel{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}}}x^{\gamma}_{\beta}\mathrel{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}}}x^{\gamma}_{\alpha}
.
Thus
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}}(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

whenever
\gamma\geqslant\widehat{\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{8}}}
,
so
(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\to(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
We have shown
N
is complete.

†
†
margin:

VIII

(
\overline{V}
: uniformity)
Let
\overline{V}
denote the set of fast Cauchy nets over
V

modulo equivalence.
Write
\eta\colon V\to\overline{V}

for the map that sends
x\in V

to the equivalence class of the constant Cauchy net
(x)_{\alpha\in\Phi}
.
For brevity, we may write
\hat{x}=\eta(x)
.
As announced before, define
V_{0}\equiv\eta(V)
.

What uniformity to put on
\overline{V}
?
Unfortunately the relations
\hat{\varepsilon}
do not necessarily preserve
equivalence (
\sim
) of Cauchy nets:
there might be
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

and
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(y^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
in
N

such that
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}}(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
,
but not
(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}}(y^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

for some
\varepsilon\in\Phi
.
Instead we define for any
\varepsilon\in\Phi

the following entourages on
\overline{V}
:

(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\tilde{\varepsilon}}{\@index{\@indexphrase[*epsiti]{$\tilde{\varepsilon}$}}}}(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}}(y^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\quad\text{for all
$(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}$
and $(y^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}$.}

By definition
\tilde{\varepsilon}

respects equivalence and can be considered as a relation on
\overline{V}
.
It is not hard to verify
\{\tilde{\varepsilon};\ \varepsilon\in\Phi\}

is a subbase for
\overline{V}

and
\widetilde{\varepsilon_{1}\cap\varepsilon_{2}}=\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\cap\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}
.
It is easy to see
\overline{V}
is a Hausdorff uniform space.
Write
\tilde{\Phi}
for the generated set of entourages on
\overline{V}
.
The entourages of
\overline{V}
are exactly those relations
which have some
\tilde{\varepsilon}

as a subset.

Furthermore, if
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}}(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
,
then
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\mathrel{\tilde{\varepsilon}^{3}}(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
Thus if a net
(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

converges to
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
in
N
,
then so do their equivalence classes in
\overline{V}
.
Hence
\overline{V}
is complete.
(The inclusion
\hat{\varepsilon}\subseteq\tilde{\varepsilon}^{3}\subseteq\hat{\varepsilon}^{3}

implies that the
\hat{\varepsilon}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}

generate the same uniformity on
N
.)

†
†
margin:

IX

(
\overline{V}
is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module)
First we will define an addition on
\overline{V}
.
The addition on
V
is uniformly continuous
(as the uniformity on
V
is given by seminorms,
see
2.2.2
for the uniformity on
V^{2}
.)
Thus for any
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

and
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
in
N
,
the net
(x_{\alpha}+y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is again Cauchy,
see
2.2.2
.
Also because of the uniform continuity of addition:
if
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

and
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(y^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
,
then
(x_{\alpha}+y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(x^{\prime}_{\alpha}+y^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
There is a net in
N
equivalent to
(x_{\alpha}+y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
This fixes an addition on
\overline{V}
,
which turns it into an Abelian group.
By construction
\eta(x+y)=\eta(x)+\eta(y)
.

Pick any
b\in\mathscr{B}
. We show the map
r_{b}\colon V\to V

given by
r_{b}(x)=xb
is also uniformly continuous,
which requires us to unfold the definitions further
than with addition.
Assume we are given an entourage in
V
,
that is: np-maps
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}

and
\varepsilon>0
.
For any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
,
the map
b*f
given by
(b*f)(x)\equiv f(b^{*}xb)
is also an np-map,
see
.
Clearly
\|xb\|_{f}=f([xb,xb])^{\frac{1}{2}}=f(b^{*}[x,x]b)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\|x\|_{b*f}

for any
x\in V
.
Thus if
\|x-y\|_{b*f_{i}}\leqslant\varepsilon
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
,
then
\|xb-yb\|_{f_{i}}=\|x-y\|_{b*f_{i}}\leqslant\varepsilon

as well. Hence
r_{b}
is uniformly continuous.
As before, this uniform continuity
allows us to define a right
\mathscr{B}
-action on
\overline{V}

by sending the equivalence class of
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

to
(x_{\alpha}b)_{\alpha}
. By definition
\eta(x)b=\eta(xb)
.
It is straightforward to check this turns
\overline{V}

into a right
\mathscr{B}
-module.

†
†
margin:

X

(Extending
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}
to
\overline{V}
)
Let
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
be a Cauchy net over
V

and
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
be any np-map.
From the reverse triangle inequality
|\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}-\|x_{\beta}\|_{f}|\leqslant\|x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\|_{f}
,
it follows
(\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f})_{\alpha}
is Cauchy.
Define
\|(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\|_{f}=\lim_{\alpha}\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}
.
Again using the reverse triangle
inequality
|\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}-\|x^{\prime}_{\alpha}\|_{f}|\leqslant\|x_{\alpha}-x^{\prime}_{\alpha}\|_{f}

we see
\|(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\|_{f}=\|(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\|_{f}

whenever
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
Thus
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}
lifts to
\overline{V}
.

These extended norms
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}
also induce a uniformity
on
\overline{V}
. We will now show it is the same uniformity.
Let
((x_{\alpha}^{\gamma})_{\alpha})_{\gamma}
be a net in
\overline{V}
.
It is sufficient to show that
(x_{\alpha}^{\gamma})_{\alpha}\to 0

in the original uniformity
if and only if
\|(x_{\alpha}^{\gamma})_{\alpha}\|_{f}\to 0

for all np-maps
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
.

Assume
(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\to 0
in
\overline{V}
.
That is: for each
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
and
\varepsilon>0

there is a
\gamma_{0}

such that for each
\gamma\geqslant\gamma_{0}

there is an
\alpha_{0}

such that
\|x^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant\varepsilon

when
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
.
Let any
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
and
\varepsilon>0
be given.
Find such
\gamma_{0}
for
f
and
\varepsilon
.
Then
\|(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\|_{f}=\lim_{\alpha}\|x^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\|\leqslant\varepsilon

whenever
\gamma\geqslant\gamma_{0}

and so
\|(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\|_{f}\to 0
as desired.

For the converse, assume
\|(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\|_{f}\to 0

for all np-maps
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
be given.
There is some
\gamma_{0}
such that

\lim_{\alpha}\|x^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\|_{f}=\|(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon

for
\gamma\geqslant\gamma_{0}
.
Hence
\|x^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant\varepsilon

for sufficiently large
\alpha
,
which is to say
(x^{\gamma}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\to 0
in
\overline{V}
.

†
†
margin:

XI

(Induction set-up)
The majority of the remaining work is to show
we can define an inner product on
\overline{V}
.
As sketched earlier,
we will extend the inner product
from
V_{0}
step-by-step.
Call a subset
W\subseteq\overline{V}

together with a
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
a

compatible extension
if

1.

V_{0}\subseteq W
;

2.

W
is a
\mathscr{B}
-submodule of
\overline{V}
;

3.

the inner product turns
W
into a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
and

4.

\|x\|_{f}^{\overline{V}}=\|x\|_{f}^{W}

for all
x\in W

and np-maps
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
,
where
\|x\|_{f}^{W}=f([x,x]_{W})^{\frac{1}{2}}

and
\|\,\cdot\,\|^{\overline{V}}_{f}

is the extension of
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}

on
V
to
\overline{V}

as in
2.2.3
.

As
\|\,\cdot\,\|^{\overline{V}}_{f}
also
generate the uniformity on
\overline{V}
,
the last requirement implies
that on a compatible extension
W

the ultranorm uniformity and
the uniformity induced by
\overline{V}

coincide.

For compatible extensions
W_{1},W_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{V}

we say
W_{1}\leqslant W_{2}

iff
W_{1}\subseteq W_{2}

and
[v,w]_{W_{1}}=[v,w]_{W_{2}}

for all
v,w\in W_{1}
.
Later, we will apply Zorn’s lemma to the compatible extensions
ordered in this way.

†
†
margin:

XII

(Induction base case)
The inner product of
V
lifts to
V_{0}
.
Indeed,
\eta(x)=0
if and only if
\|x\|=0
.
So by Cauchy–Schwarz
[x,y]=0
whenever
\eta(x)=0
.
Thus
[x,y]=[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]

if
\eta(x)=\eta(x^{\prime})

and
\eta(y)=\eta(y^{\prime})
.
Define
[\eta(x),\eta(y)]=[x,y]
.
It’s easy to see
V_{0}
is a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-submodule of
\overline{V}
.
Finally, for any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}

we have
\|\eta(x)\|_{f}^{\overline{V}}=\|x\|_{f}^{V}=\|\eta(x)\|_{f}^{V_{0}}
, as desired.

†
†
margin:

XIII

(Induction step)
Assume
W\subseteq\overline{V}
with
[\,\cdot\,,\,\,\cdot\,]

is a compatible extension.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\sigma(W)}
denote the limits of norm-bounded Cauchy nets over
W
.
We will define a
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product on
\sigma(W)

that turns it into a compatible extension.
In fact
W\leqslant\sigma(W)
.
By assumption the induced uniformity on
W

is the ultranorm uniformity
and so addition and the
\mathscr{B}
-action
are uniformly continuous.
Hence
\sigma(W)
is again a submodule.

To define the inner product,
assume
x,y\in\sigma(W)
.
There are
\Phi
-indexed norm-bounded Cauchy
nets
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
and
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

over
W

such that
x_{\alpha}\to x
and
y_{\alpha}\to y
.
For any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
we have
by Cauchy–Schwarz

\displaystyle\bigl|f([x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}]-[x_{\beta},y_{\beta}])\bigr|

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl|[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}-y_{\beta}]_{f}+[x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta},y_{\beta}]_{f}\bigr|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}\|y_{\alpha}-y_{\beta}\|_{f}+\|x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\|_{f}\|y_{\beta}\|_{f}.

(2.8)

(If not otherwise specified
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}=\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}^{W}
.)
As
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is norm bounded and

\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}^{2}=f([x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}])\leqslant\|f\|\|x_{\alpha}\|^{2}
,
we see
(\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f})_{\alpha}
is bounded.
Similarly
(\|y_{\beta}\|_{f})_{\beta}
is bounded.
Thus from (
2.2.3
)
it follows
([x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}])_{\alpha}

is a Cauchy net in the ultraweak uniformity of
\mathscr{B}
,
see
2.2.2
.
As
\|[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}]\|\leqslant\|x_{\alpha}\|\|y_{\alpha}\|
,
see
2.2.1
, it is also a norm-bounded net.
By
norm-bounded Cauchy nets in the ultraweak uniformity
converge, so
\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}]
exists.

Assume we are given any
\Phi
-indexed
norm-bounded Cauchy nets
(x^{\prime})_{\alpha}
and
(y^{\prime})_{\alpha}
over
W

with
(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\to x

and
(y^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\to y
.
Then, like (
2.2.3
):

\displaystyle\bigl|f([x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}]-[x^{\prime}_{\alpha},y^{\prime}_{\alpha}])\bigr|

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl|[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}-y^{\prime}_{\alpha}]_{f}+[x_{\alpha}-x^{\prime}_{\alpha},y^{\prime}_{\alpha}]_{f}\bigr|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}\|y_{\alpha}-y^{\prime}_{\alpha}\|_{f}+\|x_{\alpha}-x^{\prime}_{\alpha}\|_{f}\|y^{\prime}_{\alpha}\|_{f}.

From this it follows
\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}]=\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[x^{\prime}_{\alpha},y^{\prime}_{\alpha}]
.
We are justified to define

[x,y]\ \equiv\ \mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\,[x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}].

We will show
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
is a
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product.

Pick any
x,y,z\in\sigma(W)

and
\Phi
-indexed
norm-bounded Cauchy nets

(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
,
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
and
(z_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
over
W

with
x_{\alpha}\to x
,
y_{\alpha}\to y
and
z_{\alpha}\to z
in
\overline{V}
.
So
x_{\alpha}+y_{\alpha}\to x+y.

With ultraweak continuity of addition

[x+y,z]=\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[x_{\alpha}+y_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}]=\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[x_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}]+\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[y_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}]=[x,z]+[y,z].

In a similar fashion one proves the other axioms of
a
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product:

[x,y]=[y,x]^{*}
follows from the ultraweak continuity of
(\,\cdot\,)^{*}
;

\mathscr{B}
-homogeneity follows from
ultraweak continuity of
c\mapsto cb
(see
)

and

[x,x]\geqslant 0
follows from ultraweak-closedness of the
positive cone of
\mathscr{B}
.

To prove definiteness of
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
, assume
[x,x]=0

for some
x\in\sigma(W)
.
Pick some
\Phi
-indexed norm-bounded
Cauchy net
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
over
W

with
x_{\alpha}\to x
.
For any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}

we have
\lim_{\alpha}f([x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}])=f(\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}])=f([x,x])=0
.
Thus
x_{\alpha}
converges in the ultranorm uniformity on
W
to
0
.
Hence
x=0
.

Thus
\sigma(W)
is a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
It remains to be shown that
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}^{\sigma(W)}

and
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{f}^{\overline{V}}

agree.
Pick any
x\in\sigma(W)
and
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
.
By definition there is a norm-bounded Cauchy net
x_{\alpha}
over
W

for which
x_{\alpha}\to x
in
\overline{V}
.
Then

\|x\|^{\sigma(W)}_{f}\ =\ f(\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}[x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}])^{\frac{1}{2}}\ =\ \lim_{\alpha}\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}^{W}\ =\ \lim_{\alpha}\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}^{\overline{V}}\ =\ \|x\|_{f}^{\overline{V}},

as desired.
We have shown that
\sigma(W)
is a compatible extension.

†
†
margin:

XIV

(Induction limit step)
Assume
\mathscr{V}

is a non-empty chain of compatible extensions.
See
2.2.3
for the order on compatible extensions.
Write
W=\bigcup\mathscr{V}
.
We will turn
W
into a compatible extension.
Clearly
W
is a submodule of
\overline{V}
.
As
\mathscr{V}
is not empty,
V_{0}\subseteq W
.
For any
v,w\in W

there is some
W^{\prime}\in\mathscr{V}
with
v,w\in W^{\prime}
.
Define
[v,w]_{W}=[v,w]_{W^{\prime}}
.
This turns
W
into a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
as all its axioms only involve finitely many elements,
which are contained in some single compatible extension far enough up
in the chain.
Finally, to see the seminorms agree,
pick any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}

and
x\in W
.
Then
x\in W^{\prime}
for some
W^{\prime}\in\mathscr{V}
.
Consequently

\|x\|^{\overline{V}}_{f}\ =\ \|x\|^{W^{\prime}}_{f}\ =\ f([x,x]_{W^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\ =\ f([x,x]_{W})^{\frac{1}{2}}\ =\ \|x\|^{W}_{f},

as desired. We have shown
W
is a compatible extension.

†
†
margin:

XV

(Self-duality)
We have seen
that every non-empty chain of compatible extensions
has an upper bound.
Thus by Zorn’s lemma there is a maximal compatible
extension
W\subseteq\overline{V}
.
By maximality
\sigma(W)=W

and so in
W
norm-bounded ultranorm-Cauchy nets converge.
Thus by
2.2.2

W
must be self-dual and ultranorm complete.
By completeness
W=\overline{V}
.
Define
X=\overline{V}

and
\left<x,y\right>=[x,y]_{W}
.
Clearly
[v,w]=\left<\eta(v),\eta(w)\right>

and the image of
\eta
is norm dense.
\square

†
†
margin:

151

The completion
2.2.3
we just constructed
has a universal property,
which can be seen as a generalization of
[
93
, prop. 3.6]
.

†
†
margin:

Ia

Lemma
Assume
\mathscr{B}
is a von Neumann algebra
and
V
is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product.
Let
X
be any self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
together with inner product preserving

\mathscr{B}
-linear map
\eta\colon V\to X

such that the image of
\eta
is ultranorm dense in
X
.
(E.g. the completion into a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
from
2.2.3
.)
Then
\eta
has the following universal property.

Let
Y
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
For each bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear
map
T\colon V\to Y
,
there is a unique bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear
map
\hat{T}\colon X\to Y

such that
\hat{T}\mathop{\circ}\eta=T
.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
By
2.2.2
bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear
maps are ultranorm uniformly continuous.
As
\eta(V)
is ultranorm dense in
X
,
the extension
\hat{T}
is unique if it exists.
Pick any
x\in X
.
There is a net
x_{\alpha}
in
V
such
that
\eta(x_{\alpha})\to x
ultranorm.
So
\eta(x_{\alpha})
is ultranorm Cauchy.
As
\eta
preserves inner product,

x_{\alpha}
is ultranorm Cauchy as well.
As
T
is ultranorm uniformly continuous

T(x_{\alpha})
is ultranorm Cauchy.
With the same motions we see
T(x_{\alpha})\sim T(x^{\prime}_{\alpha})

when
x_{\alpha}\sim x^{\prime}_{\alpha}
.
Using this and that
Y
is ultranorm complete, see
2.2.2
,
we may define
\hat{T}x=\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}
,
where
\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits
denotes the limit in the ultranorm uniformity.
Clearly
\hat{T}\mathop{\circ}\eta=T
.
It is straightforward to check
that
\mathscr{B}
-linearity
of
\hat{T}

follows from
\mathscr{B}
-linearity of
T

and ultranorm continuity of addition and
the
\mathscr{B}
-module action.

It remains to be shown
\hat{T}
is bounded.
By
2.2.1
we have

\left<Tx_{\alpha},Tx_{\alpha}\right>\ \leqslant\ \|T\|^{2}[x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}]\ =\ \|T\|^{2}\left<\eta(x_{\alpha}),\eta(x_{\alpha})\right>

and so using
2.2.2
we see that

\langle\hat{T}x,\hat{T}x\rangle\ =\ \mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<Tx_{\alpha},Tx_{\alpha}\right>\ \leqslant\ \|T\|^{2}\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<\eta(x_{\alpha}),\eta(x_{\alpha})\right>\ =\ \|T\|^{2}\left<x,x\right>,

which implies that
\|\hat{T}\|\leqslant\|T\|
as desired.
(In fact
\|\hat{T}\|=\|T\|
.)
\square

†
†
margin:

152

Before we show that every ncp-map has a Paschke dilation,
we study
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
a bit more for self-dual
X
.
We start with
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear forms.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
V
be a normed right
\mathscr{B}
-module.
A sesquilinear form
B\colon V\times V\to\mathscr{B}

is said to be
bounded

if there is an
r\geqslant 0
such that

\|B(x,y)\|\leqslant r\|x\|\|y\|

for all
x,y\in V
.

†
†
margin:

III

Example
Let
X
be a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
For every
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

the map
\left<(\,\cdot\,),T(\,\cdot\,)\right>

is a bounded
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form.

†
†
margin:

IV

For self-dual
X
every bounded
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form
arises in this way.

†
†
margin:

V

Proposition
Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
For every bounded
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form
B
on
X
,
there is a unique
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

with
B(x,y)=\left<x,Ty\right>

for all
x,y\in X
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
For each
y\in X

the map
B(\,\cdot\,,y)^{*}
is
\mathscr{B}
-linear and bounded.
Thus by self-duality of
X

there is a unique
t_{y}

for each
y\in X

such that
\left<t_{y},x\right>=B(x,y)^{*}

for all
x\in X
.
It is easy to see
\left<t_{y+y^{\prime}},x\right>=\left<t_{y}+t_{y^{\prime}},x\right>

and
\left<t_{yb},x\right>=\left<t_{y}b,x\right>
,
and so by uniqueness
y\mapsto t_{y}
is
\mathscr{B}
-linear.
Define
Ty=t_{y}
.
Clearly
\left<x,Ty\right>=\left<t_{y},x\right>^{*}=B(x,y)

and
T
must be the unique such
\mathscr{B}
-linear map.
Reasoning in the same way,
we see there is a unique
S

with
B(y,x)^{*}=\langle x,Sy\rangle
.
Hence
\langle x,Ty\rangle=B(x,y)=\langle y,Sx\rangle^{*}=\langle Sx,y\rangle
,
so
T
is adjointable.
It remains to be shown
T
is bounded.
There is an
r\geqslant 0
such that for all
x\in X
:

\|Tx\|^{2}\ =\ \|\left<Tx,Tx\right>\|\ =\ \|B(Tx,x)\|\ \leqslant\ r\|Tx\|\|x\|.

So
r
is a bound
by dividing out
\|Tx\|

if
\|Tx\|\neq 0

and trivially otherwise.
\square

†
†
margin:

VII

Remark
In
a more general result was shown.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Exercise
Suppose
T\colon X\to Y
is a bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear map between
Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules.
Show that if
X
is self dual,
then
T
is adjointable.
[
93
, prop. 3.4]

†
†
margin:

IX

Exercise
Let
V
be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
for some von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
and

\eta\colon V\to X
be the ultranorm completion of
V

from
2.2.3
.
Show that

\{\ \langle\hat{x},(\,\cdot\,)\,\hat{x}\rangle\colon X\to\mathscr{B}\ ;\ x\in V\ \}\qquad\text{(where $\hat{x}\equiv\eta(x)$)}

is an order separating set of ncp-maps, see
.
(That is:
T\geqslant 0
iff
\langle\hat{x},T\hat{x}\rangle\geqslant 0

for all
x\in V
.
Consequently
S=T
iff
\langle\hat{x},T\hat{x}\rangle=\langle\hat{x},S\hat{x}\rangle
for all
x\in V
.)

†
†
margin:

X

Theorem
Suppose
X
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Then
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
is a von Neumann algebra.

†
†
margin:

XI

Proof
The theorem is due to Paschke
[
93
, prop. 3.10]
;
we give a new proof, which also appeared as
.
We already know that
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
is a C
∗
-algebra, see
2.2.1
.

†
†
margin:

XII

(bounded order completeness)
Let
(T_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
be a norm-bounded net of self-adjoint elements
of
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
. We have to show it has a supremum.
Pick
x\in X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
r\geqslant 0

such that
\|T_{\alpha}\|\leqslant r
for all
\alpha
.
By
2.2.1

(\left<x,T_{\alpha}x\right>)_{\alpha}

is a norm-bounded net of self-adjoint elements of
\mathscr{B}

and so it has a supremum to which
it converges ultrastrongly by
.
In particular

\frac{1}{4}\sum^{3}_{k=0}i^{k}\left<i^{k}x+y,T_{\alpha}(i^{k}x+y)\right>\ =\ \left<x,T_{\alpha}y\right>

converges ultrastrongly for all
x,y\in X
.
Define
B(x,y)=\mathop{uslim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<x,T_{\alpha}y\right>
.
As addition and multiplication by a fixed element are
ultrastrongly continuous (see
),
we see that
B
is a
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form.
By
2.2.1
and
2.2.1

we have
\left<T_{\alpha}y,x\right>\left<x,T_{\alpha}y\right>\leqslant r^{2}\|x\|^{2}\left<y,y\right>

for each
\alpha
, hence

\displaystyle\|B(x,y)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ =\ \ \|B(x,y)^{*}B(x,y)\|

\displaystyle\ \ \overset{\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{usconv}}}}{=}\ \ \|\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<T_{\alpha}y,x\right>\left<x,T_{\alpha}y\right>\|

\displaystyle\ \ \leqslant\ \ r^{2}\|x\|^{2}\|y\|^{2}.

So
B
is a bounded
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form.
By
2.2.3

there is a unique
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

with
\left<x,Ty\right>=B(x,y)=\mathop{uslim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<x,T_{\alpha}y\right>
.
Noting
B(x,y)=\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\langle x,T_{\alpha}y\rangle
,
it is easy to see
T
is self-adjoint.
Using
2.2.1
and
the fact that ultraweak limits respect the order (
),
we see
\left<x,T_{\alpha}x\right>\leqslant\left<x,Tx\right>

for all
x\in X
and so
T_{\alpha}\leqslant T
.
Now suppose we are given a self-adjoint
S\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

with
T_{\alpha}\leqslant S
for all
\alpha
.
With a similar argument we see
T\leqslant S
and so
S
is
the supremum of
(T_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.

†
†
margin:

XIII

(separating normal states)
By
2.2.1
,
the states
\left<x,(\,\cdot\,)x\right>

are separating.
We are done if we can show that
\left<x,(\,\cdot\,)x\right>

is normal, i.e. preserves suprema of bounded directed sets of
self-adjoint elements.
To this end, pick
x\in X
and let
(T_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

be a net with supremum
T
.
As before
(\left<x,T_{\alpha}x\right>)_{\alpha})

is a norm bounded net which converges ultrastrongly to its
supremum.
Now, we just saw

\left<x,Tx\right>\ =\ \mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<x,T_{\alpha}x\right>\ =\ \sup_{\alpha}\left<x,T_{\alpha}x\right>

and so indeed
\left<x,(\,\cdot\,)x\right>
is normal;
the normal states are separating
and consequently
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
is a von Neumann algebra.
\square

†
†
margin:

153

Proposition
Assume
T\colon X\to Y
is an adjointable bounded module map
between Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules.
Define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\mathrm{ad}_{T}}\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

by
\mathrm{ad}_{T}(B)=T^{*}BT
.
The map
\mathrm{ad}_{T}
is completely positive.
If
X
and
Y
are self-dual,
then
\mathrm{ad}_{T}
is normal.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
For any
n\in N
,
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

and
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)

we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}B_{j}^{*}T^{*}A_{j}^{*}A_{i}TB_{i}\ =\ \bigl(\sum_{i}A_{i}TB_{i}\bigr)^{*}\bigl(\sum_{j}A_{j}TB_{j}\bigr)\ \geqslant\ 0

and so
\mathrm{ad}_{T}
is completely positive.

†
†
margin:

III

Now we show
\mathrm{ad}_{S}
is normal.
By
and
2.2.1
,
it suffices to show that
for every
x\in X
, the
map
T\mapsto\langle x,\mathrm{ad}_{S}(T)x\rangle=\langle Sx,T\,Sx\rangle

is normal,
which it indeed is by
2.2.3
.
\square

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Let
\mathscr{A}
be a C
∗
-algebra.
Assume
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
.
Use
2.2.3

to show
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to M_{n}\mathscr{A}

given by
\varphi(d)=(a_{i}^{*}da_{j})_{ij}

is an ncp-map.

2.3

Paschke dilations

†
†
margin:

154

We are ready to show that every ncp-map has a Paschke dilation.
That is: for every ncp-map
\varphi

there is a triple
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)

with
\varphi=h\mathop{\circ}\varrho
via
\mathscr{P}

for some ncp-map
h
, nmiu-map
\varrho
and von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{P}
,
which is minimal in the sense
that for any such triple
(\mathscr{P}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},h^{\prime})

there is a unique ncp-map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}

with
h^{\prime}=h\mathop{\circ}\sigma
and
\varrho=\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}
.
See
2.1
.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be any ncp-map between
von Neumann algebras.
A complex bilinear map
B\colon\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\to X
,
where
X
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module is
called
\varphi
-compatible

if there is an
r>0

such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}

and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in\mathscr{B}
we have

\bigl\|\sum_{i}B(a_{i},b_{i})\bigr\|^{2}\ \leqslant\ r\cdot\bigl\|\sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})b_{j}\bigr\|

(2.9)

and
B(a,b_{1})b_{2}=B(a,b_{1}b_{2})

for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathscr{B}
.

†
†
margin:

III

Theorem
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be any ncp-map between
von Neumann algebras.

1.

There is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}
and
\varphi
-compatible bilinear

\otimes\colon\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

such that for every
\varphi
-compatible bilinear
map
B\colon\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\to Y

there is a unique bounded
module map
T\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to Y

such that
T(a\otimes b)=B(a,b)
for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
b\in\mathscr{B}
.

2.

For
a_{0}\in\mathscr{A}
,
there is a unique
\varrho(a_{0})\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})

fixed by

\varrho(a_{0})(a\otimes b)=(a_{0}a)\otimes b.\text{\qquad($a\in\mathscr{A}$, $b\in\mathscr{B}$)}

Furthermore
a\mapsto\varrho(a)

yields a
nmiu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})
.

3.

The map

h\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{B}

given by
h(T)=\left<1\otimes 1,T(1\otimes 1)\right>

is ncp.

4.

The map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})

together with
1\otimes 1\in\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

has the following universal property.

Let
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

be an nmiu-map
for a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

together with an element
e\in X

such that
\varphi=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}

for
h^{\prime}(T)\equiv\left<e,Te\right>
.
Then: there is a unique
inner product preserving
\mathscr{B}
-linear
map
S\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to X

such that
\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

and
S(1\otimes 1)=e
.

5.

(\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}),\varrho,h)

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi
, see
2.1
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
(This is a simplified version of the proof
we published earlier in
[
127
]
.
The construction of
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

is essentially due to Paschke
[
93
, thm. 5.2]
:
the self-dual completion of
X
in
[
93
, thm. 5.2]

is isomorphic to
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}
.)

†
†
margin:

V

(1:
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}
)
The algebraic tensor product
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}

is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with the action
(\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i})\beta=\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes(b_{i}\beta)
.
On
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
, define

\bigl[\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i},\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\otimes\beta_{j}\bigr]\ \equiv\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi(a_{i}^{*}\alpha_{j})\beta_{j}.

By complete positivity of
\varphi

this is a
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product on
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
.
By
2.2.3
,
there is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

and
\mathscr{B}
-linear inner product-preserving

\eta\colon\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

with ultranorm-dense range.
Define a bilinear map
\otimes\colon\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

by
a\otimes b=\eta(a\otimes b)
.
By definition we have

\bigl\|\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\bigr\|^{2}\ =\ \bigl\|\bigl[\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i},\sum_{j}a_{j}\otimes b_{j}\bigr]\bigr\|\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})b_{j}\bigr\|

and so
\otimes
is a
\varphi
-compatible bilinear map.

Let
B\colon\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\to Y

be a
\varphi
-compatible
bilinear map to some self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
Y
.
We must show that there is a unique bounded module
map
T\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to Y

such that
T(a\otimes b)=B(a,b)
for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
b\in\mathscr{B}
.
By the defining property of the algebraic
tensor product, there is a unique linear
map
T_{0}\colon\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}\to Y

such that
T_{0}(a\otimes b)=B(a,b)
for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
b\in\mathscr{B}
.
By definition of
\varphi
-compatibility
and the inner product on
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
,
the map
T_{0}
is in fact bounded and
\mathscr{B}
-linear.
By
2.2.3

this map extends uniquely
to a bounded module map
T\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to Y

with
T(a\otimes b)=B(a,b)
, as desired.

†
†
margin:

VI

(2:
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})
)
Let
a_{0}\in\mathscr{A}
be given.
To show the module map
\varrho(a_{0})
exists,
it suffices to show that the bilinear
map
B\colon\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

given by
B(a,b)=(a_{0}a)\otimes b
is
\varphi
-compatible.
Clearly
B(a,b\beta)=B(a,b)\beta
.
To prove (
2.9
),
let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}

and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in\mathscr{B}

be given.
The row vector

(a_{1}\ \cdots\ a_{n})

is an
\mathscr{A}
-linear map
a\colon\mathscr{A}^{\oplus n}\to\mathscr{A}

in the usual way:
a(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})=a_{1}\alpha_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}\alpha_{n}
.
Similarly the column vector
(b_{1}\ \ldots\ b_{n})^{\mathrm{t}}

is a
\mathscr{B}
-linear map
b\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{B}^{\oplus n}

with
b(\beta)=(b_{1}\beta,\ldots,b_{n}\beta)
.
We compute

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sum_{i}B(a_{i},b_{i})\bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{i}(a_{0}a_{i})\otimes b_{i}\bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi(a_{i}^{*}a_{0}^{*}a_{0}a_{j})b_{j}\bigr\|

\displaystyle\ =\ \|b^{*}(M_{n}\varphi)(a^{*}a_{0}^{*}a_{0}a)b\|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|a_{0}^{*}a_{0}\|\|b^{*}(M_{n}\varphi)(a^{*}a)b\|

\displaystyle\ =\ \|a_{0}\|^{2}\bigl\|\sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})b_{j}\bigr\|.

Thus
B
is
\varphi
-compatible
and so there is a unique
\mathscr{B}
-linear bounded module
map
\varrho(a_{0})\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

with
\varrho(a_{0})(a\otimes b)=(a_{0}a)\otimes b
.
Clearly
\varrho
is a unital, multiplicative and involution preserving map.
It remains to be shown
\varrho
is normal.
By
and
2.2.3
,
it suffices to show
that
d\ \mapsto\ \langle\hat{x},\varrho(d)\hat{x}\rangle

is normal for every
x\in\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
.
Find
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
row vector
a\equiv(a_{1}\ \cdots\ a_{n})

and column vector
b\equiv(b_{1}\ \cdots\ b_{n})

such that
x=\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}
.
We compute

\langle\hat{x},\varrho(d)\hat{x}\rangle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi(a_{i}^{*}(d)a_{j})b_{j}\\
\ =\ b^{*}(M_{n}\varphi)(a^{*}\,d\,a)b.

So
d\ \mapsto\ \langle\hat{x},\varrho(d)\hat{x}\rangle

is normal by
2.2.3
and
,
whence
\varrho
is normal.

†
†
margin:

VII

(3:
h\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{B}
)
Define
h(T)\equiv\left<1\otimes 1,T\,1\otimes 1\right>
.
It is completely positive by
2.2.1

and normal by
2.2.3
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

(Uniqueness
\sigma
)
Before we continue with 4, we will already prove the uniqueness property
for point 5.
Note that
(h\mathop{\circ}\varrho)(a)=\left<1\otimes 1,a\otimes 1\right>=\varphi(a)
for all
a\in\mathscr{A}

and so
\varphi=h\mathop{\circ}\varrho
.
Assume
\varphi=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}

for some nmiu-map
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
,
ncp-map
h\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}

and von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
.
For point 5 we must show there is a
unique ncp-map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})

such that
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime}
and
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
.
Let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})

be ncp-maps with
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{k}=h^{\prime}
and
\sigma_{k}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
,
k=1,2
.
We must show
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
.
Let
c\in\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
and
x\in\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
be given.
By
2.2.3
it suffices to prove
that
\left<\hat{x},\sigma_{1}(c)\hat{x}\right>=\left<\hat{x},\sigma_{2}(c)\hat{x}\right>
.
Find
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}

and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in\mathscr{B}

such that
x=\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}
.
Note
a_{i}\otimes b_{i}=\varrho(a_{i})(1\otimes 1)b_{i}
.
So for any
k=1,2
, we find

\displaystyle\left<\hat{x},\sigma_{k}(c)\hat{x}\right>

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}h(\,\varrho(a_{i}^{*})\sigma_{k}(c)\varrho(a_{j})\,)b_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}h(\sigma_{k}(\,\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i}^{*})c\varrho^{\prime}(a_{j})\,))b_{j}

by
2.1

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}h^{\prime}(\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i}^{*})c\varrho^{\prime}(a_{j}))b_{j}.

(2.10)

Thus
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
as desired.
One can show such
\sigma
exists by using (
2.3
)
as defining formula.
We will use, however, an indirect but shorter approach
by first considering the ‘spatial case’,
which was suggested by Michael Skeide.

†
†
margin:

IX

(4: spatial case)
Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
together with
e\in X
and nmiu-map
\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

such that
\varphi=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}

with
h^{\prime}(T)=\left<e,Te\right>
.
To prove uniqueness,
assume that for
k=1,2
,
we have
\mathscr{B}
-linear
inner product preserving
S_{k}\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to X

with
\mathrm{ad}_{S_{k}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

and
S_{k}\,1\otimes 1=e
.
Then
h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{S_{k}}=h^{\prime}

and so (as
\mathrm{ad}_{S_{k}}
is an ncp-map by
2.2.3
),
we know
\mathrm{ad}_{S_{1}}=\mathrm{ad}_{S_{2}}
by
2.3
.
Hence there is a
\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\lambda\neq 0

with
S_{1}=\lambda S_{2}
, cf.
[
126
, lemma 9]
.
If
e\neq 0
,
then from
e=S_{1}\,1\otimes 1=\lambda S_{2}\,1\otimes 1=\lambda e

it follows
\lambda=1
and so
S_{1}=S_{2}
.
In the other case, if
e=0
,
then
h^{\prime}=0
and so
\varphi=0
,
whence
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}=\{0\}

and indeed
S_{1}=S_{2}=0
trivially.
We continue with existence.
There is a unique linear
S_{0}\colon\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}\to X

fixed by
S_{0}(a\otimes b)=\varrho^{\prime}(a)eb
.
Clearly
S_{0}
is also
\mathscr{B}
-linear.
For any
x,y\in\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
,
say
x=\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}
and
y=\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\otimes\beta_{j}
,
we have

\displaystyle\left<S_{0}x,S_{0}y\right>

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\left<\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i})eb_{i},\varrho^{\prime}(\alpha_{j})e\beta_{j}\right>

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\left<e,\varrho^{\prime}(a_{i}^{*}\alpha_{j})e\right>\beta_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi(a_{i}^{*}\alpha_{j})\beta_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ [x,y].

Thus
S_{0}
preserves the inner product. In particular
S_{0}
is bounded
and so there is a unique bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear

S\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to X

with
S\mathop{\circ}\eta=S_{0}
.
For all
x\in\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}

we have
\langle\hat{x},S^{*}S\hat{x}\rangle=\langle\hat{x},\hat{x}\rangle

and so
S^{*}S=1

by
2.2.3
.
Thus
S
preserves the inner product.
By definition
S(1\otimes 1)=\varrho^{\prime}(1)e1=e
.
Pick any
a\in\mathscr{A}
.
A straightforward computation shows
S\varrho(a)=\varrho^{\prime}(a)S

hence
S^{*}\varrho^{\prime}(a)=\varrho(a)S^{*}

and
S^{*}\varrho^{\prime}(a)S=\varrho(a)S^{*}S=\varrho(a)
.
We have shown
\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\mathrm{ad}_{S}
.

†
†
margin:

X

(5,
\sigma
existence)
Assume
\varphi=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}

for some nmiu-map
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
,
ncp-map
h\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}

and von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
.
It remains to be shown there is a
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})

with
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
and
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime}
.
To apply the previous point,
we perform the whole construction for
h^{\prime}
instead of
\varphi

yielding

h^{\prime}=h_{h^{\prime}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{h^{\prime}}

with
\varrho_{h^{\prime}}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\otimes_{h^{\prime}}\mathscr{B})

and
h_{h^{\prime}}\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\otimes_{h^{\prime}}\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{B}
.
By
2.3

there is a unique

\mathscr{B}
-linear map
S\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\otimes_{h^{\prime}}\mathscr{B}

with
S^{*}S=1
,
S1\otimes 1=1\otimes 1
and

\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{h^{\prime}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
.
Define
\sigma=\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{h^{\prime}}
.
This
\sigma
fits the bill:

\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{h^{\prime}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

and
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{h^{\prime}}=h_{h^{\prime}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{h^{\prime}}=h^{\prime}
.
\square

†
†
margin:

155

We have shown that any ncp-map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

admits a dilation using Paschke’s generalization of GNS
to Hilbert C
∗
-modules.
There is also a generalization of Stinespring’s theorem
to Hilbert C
∗
-modules
due to Kasparov
[
75
]
:

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem (KSGNS)
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

be a cp-map
for some C
∗
-algebras
\mathscr{A}
,
\mathscr{B}

and Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X
.
There exists a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
Y
,
miu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)

and adjointable
\mathscr{B}
-linear
T\colon Y\to X

with
\varphi=\mathrm{ad}_{T}\mathop{\circ}\varrho
.

†
†
margin:

III

For our purposes (the dilation of an arbitrary ncp-map
\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
),
Paschke’s GNS construction sufficed.
We leave open whether
KSGNS admits a universal property like
2.1

and whether it is a Paschke dilation.

†
†
margin:

156

We have all the tools to characterize
the ncp-maps
\varphi

for which the Paschke representation is injective.
This is a generalization of our answer
[
125
]

to the same question for the Stinespring embedding.

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be any ncp-map
with Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
.
Then
\left\lceil\varrho\right\rceil=\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil
.
(
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil
is the central carrier of
\varphi
, see
.)
Thus
\varrho
is injective (
\left\lceil\varrho\right\rceil=1
) if and only if
\varphi

maps no non-zero central projection to zero (
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil=1
).

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
(This is a simplified version of the proof we published earlier
in
[
93
, thm. 30]
.)
By
2.1
,
it is sufficient to prove the equivalence
for the dilation constructed in
2.3
.
Let
p\in\mathscr{A}
be any projection.
We will show
p\leqslant\left\lceil\varrho\right\rceil^{\perp}
iff
p\leqslant\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil^{\perp}
.
Assume
p\leqslant\left\lceil\varrho\right\rceil
,
viz.
\varrho(p)=0
.
Pick any
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
b\in\mathscr{B}
.
Then
\varrho(p)\,a\otimes b=0
.
By definition of
\varrho
and the inner product,
this is the case iff
b^{*}\varphi(a^{*}p^{*}pa)b=0
.
In particular
\varphi(a^{*}pa)=0
.
In other words
a^{*}pa\leqslant\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil^{\perp}
.
This happens if and only if
a\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil a^{*}\leqslant p^{\perp}

by
.
Equivalently
\left\lceil a\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil a^{*}\right\rceil\leqslant p^{\perp}
.
As
a
was arbitrary,
we see (using
),
that
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil=\bigcup_{a\in\mathscr{A}}\left\lceil a\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil a^{*}\right\rceil\leqslant p^{\perp}
, as desired.
For the converse, note that
the two implications we just used are, in fact, equivalences:
if
\varphi(a^{*}pa)=0
,
then
b^{*}\varphi(a^{*}pa)b=0
for any
b\in\mathscr{B}

and
if
\varrho(p)\,a\otimes b=0

for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
b\in\mathscr{B}
,
then
\varrho(p)=0
, by
2.2.3
.

\square

†
†
margin:

157

We will now prove a useful connection between
the ncp-maps
ncp-below

\varphi
(as defined in a moment)
and the commutant of the image
of a Paschke representation (“
\varrho
”) of
\varphi
.
We will use it to give an alternative proof
of the fact that pure maps and nmiu-maps
are extreme among all ncpu-maps.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
For linear maps
\varphi,\psi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
,
we say
\varphi
is ncp-below
\psi

(in symbols:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\varphi\mathrel{\leqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}}\psi}
)
whenever
\psi-\varphi
is an ncp-map.
Furthermore, write

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}[0,\varphi]_{\mathrm{ncp}}}{\@index{\@indexphrase{$[0,\varphi]_{\mathrm{ncp}}$}}}=\ \{\psi;\ \psi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B};\ 0\leqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}\psi\leqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}\varphi\}.

†
†
margin:

III

Definition
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be any ncp-map with Paschke
dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
.
For
t\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
, the commutant of
\varrho(\mathscr{A})
,
define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\varphi_{t}}{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\varphi_{t}$}}}=h(t\varrho(a))
.

†
†
margin:

IIIa

The following is a generalization
[
93
, prop. 5.4]

to arbitrary Paschke dilations.

†
†
margin:

IV

Theorem
Assume
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
is an ncp-map
with Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
.
The map
t\mapsto\varphi_{t}
is a linear
order isomorphism
[0,1]_{\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}\to[0,\varphi]_{\mathrm{ncp}}
.

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
First we show the correspondence holds
for the Paschke dilation constructed in
2.3
.
This proof is a slight variation on
[
93
, prop. 5.4]
.
Then we show the correspondence carries over to arbitrary Paschke dilations.

†
†
margin:

VI

(Set-up)
Let
(\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}),\varrho,h)

denote the Paschke dilation constructed in
2.3
.
Clearly
T\mapsto\varphi_{T}
is linear
for
T\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
.
Pick
T\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
.
As
\sqrt{T}\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}

we have
\varphi_{T}(a)=h(\sqrt{T}\varrho(a)\sqrt{T})
.
Thus
\varphi_{T}
is ncp.
In particular, if
T\leqslant S

for some
S\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
,
then
\varphi_{S-T}=\varphi_{S}-\varphi_{T}
is ncp
and so
\varphi_{T}\mathrel{\leqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}}\varphi_{S}
.
Consequently, if
T\leqslant 1
, then
\varphi_{T}\leqslant\varphi_{1}=\varphi
.

†
†
margin:

VII

(Order embedding)
Let
T\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}

be given such that
\varphi_{T}
is ncp.
We must show
T\geqslant 0
.
Pick any
x\in\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
.
By
2.2.3
and
the construction of
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}
,
it is sufficient to show
\langle\hat{x},T\hat{x}\rangle\geqslant 0
.
Say
\hat{x}\equiv\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}
.
