Then

\langle\hat{x},T\hat{x}\rangle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\left<1\otimes 1,T\varrho(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})1\otimes 1\right>b_{j}\ =\ \sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi_{T}(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})b_{j}\ \geqslant\ 0.

Thus
T\mapsto\varphi_{T}
is an order embedding
and in particular an injection.

†
†
margin:

VIII

(Surjectivity)
Pick any
\psi\in[0,\varphi]_{\mathrm{ncp}}
.
We will show there is a
T\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}

with
\varphi_{T}=\psi
and
0\leqslant T\leqslant 1
.
Let
(\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\psi}\mathscr{B}),\varrho_{\psi},h_{\psi})
denote the Paschke dilation of
\psi

constructed in
2.3
.
Define
B\colon\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\psi}\mathscr{B}

by
B(a,b)=a\otimes b
.
This
B
is
\varphi
-compatible
— indeed, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}

and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in\mathscr{B}
,
it follows from
\psi\mathrel{\leqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}}\varphi
that

\bigl\|\sum_{i}B(a_{i},b_{i})\bigr\|^{2}\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\psi(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})b_{j}\bigr\|\ \leqslant\ \bigl\|\sum_{i,j}b_{i}^{*}\varphi(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})b_{j}\bigr\|.

So by
2.3
,
there is a unique bounded module
map
W\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\psi}\mathscr{B}

fixed by
Wa\otimes b=a\otimes b
.
As
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\psi}\mathscr{B}
is self-dual,
the map
W
has an adjoint
W^{*}
by
2.2.3
.
For any
a\in\mathscr{A}
, we have
W\varrho(a)=\varrho_{\psi}(a)W

and so
\varrho(a)W^{*}=W^{*}\varrho_{\psi}(a)
.
Thus
W^{*}W\varrho(a)=W^{*}\varrho_{\psi}(a)W=\varrho(a)W^{*}W
.
Apparently
W^{*}W\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
.
Define
T\equiv W^{*}W
. Clearly
0\leqslant T
.
As
\psi\mathrel{\leqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}}\varphi
,
we have for each
x\equiv\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}
,
the inequality
\langle x,x\rangle_{\psi}\leqslant\langle x,x\rangle_{\varphi}

and so

\left<x,Tx\right>_{\varphi}\ =\ \left<Wx,Wx\right>_{\psi}\ =\ \left<x,x\right>_{\psi}\ \leqslant\ \langle x,x\rangle_{\varphi}.

Thus
T\leqslant 1
by
2.2.3
.
For any
a\in\mathscr{A}
we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{T}(a)

\displaystyle\ =\ \left<1\otimes 1,W^{*}W\,a\otimes 1\right>_{\varphi}

\displaystyle\ =\ \left<W\,1\otimes 1,W\,a\otimes 1\right>_{\psi}

\displaystyle\ =\ \left<1\otimes 1,a\otimes 1\right>_{\psi}

\displaystyle\ =\ \psi(a),

as desired. We have shown the correspondence holds for
the Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}),\varrho,h)

constructed in
2.3
.

†
†
margin:

IX

(Arbitrary dilation)
Let
(\mathscr{P}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},h^{\prime})
be any Paschke dilation
of
\varphi
.
For brevity write
\mathscr{P}\equiv\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

and for clarity
write
\varphi_{T}^{\mathscr{P}}

and
\varphi_{t}^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}

to distinguish between
\varphi_{T}
and
\varphi_{t}

for
T\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
and
t\in\varrho^{\prime}(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
.
By
2.1

there is a unique nmiu-isomorphism

\vartheta\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}

such that
\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

and
h\mathop{\circ}\vartheta=h^{\prime}
.
It is easy to see
\vartheta
restricts to a linear order isomorphism

[0,1]_{\varrho^{\prime}(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}\to[0,1]_{\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}
.
For any
t\in\varrho^{\prime}(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
and
a\in\mathscr{A}
we have

\varphi_{t}^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}(a)\ =\ h^{\prime}(t\varrho^{\prime}(a))\ =\ h(\vartheta(t\varrho^{\prime}(a)))\ =\ h(\vartheta(t)\varrho(a))\ =\ \varphi_{\vartheta(t)}^{\mathscr{P}}(a)

and so
t\mapsto\varphi_{t}^{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}}

is a linear order isomorphism as it
is the composition of the linear order isomorphisms

\vartheta^{-1}
and
t\mapsto\varphi_{t}^{\mathscr{P}}
.
\square

†
†
margin:

X

We return to
t\mapsto\varphi_{t}

in
2.6
,
where we connect it to extremeness.

2.4

Kaplansky density theorem for Hilbert C
∗
-modules

†
†
margin:

158

Before we continue our study of Paschke dilations,
we need to develop some more theory on (self-dual) Hilbert C
∗
-modules.
First we will prove a generalization of the Kaplansky density
theorem for Hilbert C
∗
-modules,
which we will need to compute the tensor product of Paschke dilations.

The original density theorem (
)
is an important foundational result
in the theory of operator algebras;
quoting Pedersen:
“The density theorem is Kaplansky’s great gift to mankind.
It can be used every day, and twice on
Sundays.”
[
95
, §2.3.4]
.
We used the original theorem already several times;
notably to show that
a von Neumann algebra is
ultrastrongly complete (

via
).
This ultrastrong completeness was used
to define operator division (
)
(and in turn filters
),
to give the spatial tensor product a universal
property (
)
and in this thesis to show ultranorm completeness
of self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-modules (
2.2.2
).

So what does is this density theorem again?
In essence, it is a remedy for the fact
that strongly converging nets might not be bounded.
The theorem is usually stated as follows:
the unit ball of the SOT-closure of a self-adjoint algebra
of operators
\mathscr{A}

is contained in the SOT-closure of the unit ball of
\mathscr{A}
.
For our generalization, it is more convenient to consider the following
variation.

†
†
margin:

Ia

Kaplansky density theorem
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra with ultrastrongly-dense
C
∗
-subalgebra
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}
.
Then: for every element
a\in\mathscr{A}
,
there is a net
b_{\alpha}
in
\mathscr{B}

with
b_{\alpha}\to a
ultranorm
and
\|b_{\alpha}\|\leqslant\|b\|
for all
\alpha
.

†
†
margin:

Ib

Now we are ready to state and prove our generalization,
which is inspired by the proof of the regular Kaplansky density theorem
given in
[
7
, thm. 1.2.2]
.
Cf.
.

†
†
margin:

II

Kaplansky density theorem for Hilbert C
∗
-modules
Let
X
be a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}

with an ultranorm-dense
\mathscr{A}
-submodule
D\subseteq X
,
where
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{B}
is some C
∗
-subalgebra
with
\langle y,y\rangle\in\mathscr{A}
for all
y\in D
.

Then: for every element
x\in X
,
there is a net
x_{\alpha}
in
D

with
x_{\alpha}\to x
ultranorm
and
\|x_{\alpha}\|\leqslant\|x\|
for all
\alpha
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
Let
x\in X
be given.
The case
x=0
is trivial.
Assume
x\neq 0
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume
\|x\|=1
.
For this
x
and any
y\in X
, define

\displaystyle h(y)

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ y\cdot\frac{2}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ x\cdot\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{1-\langle x,x\rangle}}.

We claim
h
is ultranorm continuous,

h(g(x))=x
and
\|h(y)\|\leqslant 1
for all
y\in X
.
From this, the promised result follows.
Indeed, let
x_{\alpha}
be a net in
D

that converges ultranorm to
g(x)
.
Then
h(x_{\alpha})
is
still in
D

and furthermore
h(x_{\alpha})\to h(g(x))=x
ultranorm
and
\|h(x_{\alpha})\|\leqslant 1=\|x\|
, as desired.

†
†
margin:

IV

We start with
\|h(y)\|\leqslant 1
.
The real map
h_{r}\colon\lambda\mapsto 4\lambda(1+\lambda)^{-2}

is bounded by
1
and so by the functional calculus
we see
\|h(y)\|^{2}=\|4\langle y,y\rangle(1+\langle y,y\rangle)^{-2}\|=\|h_{r}(\langle y,y\rangle)\|\leqslant 1
.
We continue with the proof of
h(g(x))=x
.
Note
\langle g(x),g(x)\rangle=(1+\sqrt{1-\langle x,x\rangle})^{-2}\langle x,x\rangle

and so with basic algebra

\displaystyle h(g(x))

\displaystyle\ =\ x\cdot\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{1-\langle x,x\rangle}}\frac{2}{1+\bigl(1+\sqrt{1-\langle x,x\rangle}\bigr)^{-2}\langle x,x\rangle}

\displaystyle\ =\ x\cdot 2\left(\frac{\bigl(1+\sqrt{1-\langle x,x\rangle}\bigr)^{2}+\langle x,x\rangle}{1+\sqrt{1-\langle x,x\rangle}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\ =\ x.

†
†
margin:

V

(Ultranorm continuity
h
)
The real work is in the proof of the ultranorm continuity of
h
.
Suppose
y_{\alpha}\to y
ultranorm in
X
. Define

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \frac{\langle y,y\rangle}{(1+\langle y,y\rangle)^{2}}-\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}\frac{\langle y,y\rangle}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \frac{\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}{(1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle)^{2}}-\frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}\frac{\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}\langle y_{\alpha}-y,y_{\alpha}\rangle\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}

\displaystyle A_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}\langle y-y_{\alpha},y\rangle\frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}.

A straightforward computations shows

\langle h(y)-h(y_{\alpha}),h(y)-h(y_{\alpha})\rangle\ =\ A_{1}+A_{1}^{\prime}+A_{2}+A_{2}^{\prime}.

(2.11)

We will show that each of these terms converges ultraweakly to
0

as
\alpha\to\infty
.
We start with
A_{1}
.
We need two facts.
First, for any positive
b\in\mathscr{B}
, we have
0\leqslant\frac{1}{1+b}\leqslant 1

and
0\leqslant\frac{b}{1+b}\leqslant 1
.
Secondly

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}-\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}

\displaystyle\ =\ \frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}\bigl((1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle)-(1+\langle y,y\rangle)\bigr)\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}

\displaystyle\ =\ \frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}\bigl(\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle-\langle y,y\rangle\bigr)\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}.

Putting these facts and the definition of
A_{1}
together, we get

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ \left(\frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}-\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}\right)\frac{\langle y,y\rangle}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}

\displaystyle\ =\ \frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}\bigl(\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle-\langle y,y\rangle\bigr)\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}\frac{\langle y,y\rangle}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}

\displaystyle\ =\ \frac{1}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}\bigl(\langle y_{\alpha}-y,y_{\alpha}\rangle+\langle y,y_{\alpha}-y\rangle\bigr)\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}\frac{\langle y,y\rangle}{1+\langle y,y\rangle}.

As multiplying with
constants is ultraweakly continuous (see
),
it is sufficient to show that ultraweakly, as
\alpha\to\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\langle y_{\alpha}-y,y_{\alpha}\rangle\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}

\displaystyle\ \to\ 0

\displaystyle\langle y,y_{\alpha}-y\rangle\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}

\displaystyle\ \to\ 0.

(2.12)

For any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigl|f\Bigl(\left\langle y_{\alpha}-y,y_{\alpha}\right\rangle\frac{1}{1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle}\Bigr)\Bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ |\langle y_{\alpha}-y,y_{\alpha}(1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle)^{-1}\rangle_{f}|^{2}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|y_{\alpha}-y\|^{2}_{f}\cdot\|y_{\alpha}(1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle)^{-1}\|^{2}_{f}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|y_{\alpha}-y\|^{2}_{f}\cdot\|y_{\alpha}(1+\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle)^{-1}\|^{2}\cdot\|f\|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|y_{\alpha}-y\|^{2}_{f}\cdot\|f\|\ \rightarrow\ 0.

We have shown the LHS of (
2.12
).
The proof for the RHS is different, but simpler.
So
A_{1}
vanishes ultraweakly.
In a similar way one sees
A_{1}^{\prime}\to 0
ultraweakly.
The proofs for
A_{2},A_{2}^{\prime}\to 0
are very similar.
\square

2.5

More on self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-modules

†
†
margin:

159

We have a second look at
self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-modules over von Neumann algebras
and their orthonormal bases.
We start with some good news.
In
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
the linear span of
\left|e_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{j}\right|

is ultraweakly dense for any orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}

of
\mathscr{H}
.
For self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules, we have a similar result.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
X
be a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
For any
x,y\in X
,
define the bounded operator
\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|}{\@index{\@indexphrase[*ketbra]{$\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|$ for Hilbert $\mathscr{B}$-modules}}}z=x\left<y,z\right>
.

†
†
margin:

IIa

Remark
The operator
\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|
is usually denoted by
\theta_{x,y}

in the literature,
see for instance
[
88
, §2.2]
.

†
†
margin:

III

The following rules are easy to check.
For
x,y,v,w\in X
and
b\in\mathscr{B}

we have

\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|^{*}=\left|y\right>\!\left<x\right|\qquad\left|xb\right>\!\left<y\right|=\left|x\right>\!\left<yb^{*}\right|\qquad\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|\left|v\right>\!\left<w\right|=\left|x\left<y,v\right>\right>\!\left<w\right|.

If
\left<e,e\right>
is a projection,
then
\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|
is a projection
(using
2.2.2
).
For any
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)

we have
T\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|=\left|Tx\right>\!\left<y\right|

and
\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|T^{*}=\left|x\right>\!\left<Ty\right|
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proposition
Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
If
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is an orthonormal basis of
X
,
then the linear span of
the operators
\{\left|e_{i}b\right>\!\left<e_{j}\right|;i,j\in I,\ b\in\mathscr{B}\}

is ultraweakly dense in
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
.

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
We start with some preparation.

†
†
margin:

VI

For a finite subset
S\subseteq I
,
write
p_{S}=\sum_{i\in S}\left|e_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i}\right|
.
The
(\left|e_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i}\right|)_{i\in I}
are pairwise orthogonal projections.
Thus
p_{S}
is a projection.
Also
p_{S}\leqslant p_{S^{\prime}}
when
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
.
Pick any
x\in X
.
As
p_{S}x=\sum_{i\in S}e_{i}\left<e_{i},x\right>
, we have

\left<x,p_{S}x\right>\ =\ \left<p_{S}x,p_{S}x\right>\ =\ \sum_{i,j\in S}\left<x,e_{i}\right>\left<e_{i},e_{j}\right>\left<e_{j},x\right>\ =\ \sum_{i\in S}\left<x,e_{i}\right>\left<e_{i},x\right>,

and so by Parseval
\sup_{S}\left<x,p_{S}x\right>=\left<x,x\right>
.
Hence
\sup_{S}p_{S}=1
by

and so
p_{S}
converges ultraweakly to
1

by
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Pick any
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
.
For
i,j\in I
we have

\left|e_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i}\right|T\left|e_{j}\right>\!\left<e_{j}\right|\ =\ \left|e_{i}\right>\!\left<e_{i}\right|\left|Te_{j}\right>\!\left<e_{j}\right|\ =\ \left|e_{i}\left<e_{i},Te_{j}\right>\right>\!\left<e_{j}\right|.

and so
p_{S}Tp_{S}
is in the linear
span of
\{\left|e_{i}b\right>\!\left<e_{j}\right|\}
.
Thus it is sufficient to prove
p_{S}Tp_{S}
converges ultraweakly to
T
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Pick any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
.
We want to show
|f(T-p_{S}Tp_{S})|\to 0
. Clearly

|f(T-p_{S}Tp_{S})|\ \leqslant\ |f(\,(1-p_{S})T\,)|+|f(\,p_{S}T(1-p_{S})\,)|.

Using Cauchy–Schwarz and
2.5
we see the second term
vanishes

|f(p_{S}T(1-p_{S}))|^{2}\ \leqslant\ f(p_{S}TT^{*}p_{S})f(1-p_{S})\ \leqslant\ \|T\|^{2}f(1)f(1-p_{S})\ \to\ 0.

Similarly the first term vanishes and
so indeed
p_{S}Tp_{S}\to T
ultraweakly.
\square

†
†
margin:

IX

Proposition
Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
If
x_{\alpha}\to x
ultranorm for a norm-bounded net
x_{\alpha}
in
X
,
then
\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|\to\left|x\right>\!\left<y\right|
ultraweakly
for any
y\in Y
.

†
†
margin:

X

Proof
We need some preparation.
Define

\Omega\ \equiv\ \{f(\langle x,(\,\cdot\,)x\rangle);\ f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}\text{ np-map},\ x\in X\}.

From
2.2.1

it follows
\Omega
is order separating.
We will use this fact twice.
First we will prove
\|\left|z\right>\!\left<y\right|\|\leqslant\|z\|\|y\|
.
For any
\omega\in\Omega
,
say
\omega\equiv f(\langle x,(\,\cdot\,)x\rangle)
, we have

\omega(\left|y\right>\!\left<y\right|)\ =\ f(\langle x,y\rangle\langle y,x\rangle)\ \leqslant\ f(\langle x,x\rangle)\|y\|^{2}\ =\ \|\omega\|\|y\|^{2}

and so from
it follows
\|\left|y\right>\!\left<y\right|\|\leqslant\|y\|^{2}
. Whence

\displaystyle\|\left|z\right>\!\left<y\right|\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \|\left|y\right>\!\left<z\right|\left|z\right>\!\left<y\right|\|

\displaystyle\ =\ \|\left|\smash{y\langle z,z\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right>\!\left<\smash{y\langle z,z\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right|\|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|y\langle z,z\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\|^{2}.

Hence
\|\left|z\right>\!\left<y\right|\|\leqslant\|y\langle z,z\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\leqslant\|y\|\|z\|
, as promised.

†
†
margin:

XI

Now we start the proof proper.
As
\left|\,\cdot\,\right>\!\left<y\right|
is linear
and
x_{\alpha}-x
is norm-bounded,
we may assume without loss of generality
that
x_{\alpha}\to 0
ultranorm.
We have to prove
\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|\to 0
ultraweakly.
Let
g\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)\to\mathbb{C}
be an arbitrary np-map.
Using the order separation of
\Omega
a second time
and the fact that
\omega(T^{*}(\,\cdot\,)T)\in\Omega

for all
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
and
\omega\in\Omega
,
we see that the linear span of
\Omega

is operator norm dense among all np-maps
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)\to\mathbb{C}

by
.
So there are
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n}\in\Omega

with
\|g-g^{\prime}\|\leqslant\varepsilon
,
where
g^{\prime}\equiv\sum^{n}_{i=1}\omega_{i}
.
By unfolding definitions and
2.2.2
,
it follows easily that
\omega_{i}(\left|\,\cdot\,\right>\!\left<y\right|)

is ultranorm continuous.
So
g^{\prime}(\left|\,\cdot\,\right>\!\left<y\right|)
is ultranorm continuous as well.
Pick any
\varepsilon>0
.
By the previous,
there is an
\alpha_{0}
such that for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}

we have
|g^{\prime}(\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|)|\leqslant\varepsilon
,
hence

\displaystyle|g(\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|)|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ |(g-g^{\prime})(\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|)|+|g^{\prime}(\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|)|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|g-g^{\prime}\|\cdot\|\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|\|+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \varepsilon\,(\|x_{\alpha}\|\cdot\|y\|+1).

As
x_{\alpha}
is norm-bounded
and
\varepsilon
was arbitrary,
we conclude
g(\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|)\to 0
.
Thus
\left|x_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<y\right|\to 0
ultraweakly, as desired.

\square

†
†
margin:

160

For a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}

with linear subspace
V\subseteq\mathscr{H}

we have
V\subseteq V^{\perp\perp}=\overline{V}

and
\mathscr{H}\cong V^{\perp\perp}\oplus V^{\perp}
.
We generalize this to self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-modules
over von Neumann algebras.
Let’s introduce the cast:

†
†
margin:

II

Exercise
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra.
Assume
X
and
Y
are self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules
with orthonormal bases
E\subseteq X
and
F\subseteq Y
.
Write
\kappa_{1}\colon X\to X\oplus Y

and
\kappa_{2}\colon Y\to X\oplus Y

for the maps
\kappa_{1}(x)=(x,0)
and
\kappa_{2}(y)=(0,y)
.
Show that
\kappa_{1}(E)\cup\kappa_{2}(F)

is an orthonormal basis of
X\oplus Y
.
Conclude that
X\oplus Y
is self-dual.

†
†
margin:

III

Definition
Let
X
be a Hilbert C
∗
-module
with subset
V\subseteq X
.
Write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}V^{\perp}}
for the
orthocomplement

of
V
, defined by

V^{\perp}=\{x;\ x\in X;\ \left<x,v\right>=0\text{ for all }v\in V\}.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proposition
Assume
X
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Let
V\subseteq X
be any subset. Then

1.

V^{\perp}
is a ultranorm-closed Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-submodule of
X

(and so is
V^{\perp\perp})
;

2.

V^{\perp\perp}
is the ultranorm closure of the
\mathscr{B}
-linear
span of
V

and

3.

V^{\perp\perp}\oplus V^{\perp}\cong X
as Hilbert C
∗
-modules
via
(x,y)\mapsto x+y
.

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
It is easy to see
V^{\perp}
is a submodule of
X

and
V\subseteq V^{\perp\perp}
.
To show
V^{\perp}
is ultranorm closed in
X
,
assume
x_{\alpha}
is a net in
V^{\perp}

converging ultranorm to
x\in X
.
For each
v\in V
we have

\left<v,\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}\right>\overset{\textsf{\ref{ultranormcontstruct}}}{=}\mathop{uslim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\left<v,x_{\alpha}\right>=0

and so
x\in V^{\perp}
as well.
Thus
V^{\perp}
is ultranorm complete
(and so self dual by
2.2.2
).

†
†
margin:

VI

Write
W
for the ultranorm closure of
the
\mathscr{B}
-linear span of
V
in
X
.
It follows from
2.2.2
, that
W
is a submodule.
Clearly the induced uniformity of
X

on
W
is the same as its own ultranorm uniformity:
thus
W
is ultranorm complete
and
W\subseteq V^{\perp\perp}
.
By
2.2.2
there
is an orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of
W
.
Going back to the construction of
(e_{i})_{i\in I}

(in
2.2.2
),
we see that we can extend it to a maximal orthonormal subset of
X
,
which must be a basis for the whole of
X
.
Pick any
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
in
X

such that
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup\{d_{j}\}_{j\in J}

is an orthonormal basis of
X
.

†
†
margin:

VII

By construction
d_{j}\in W^{\perp}\subseteq V^{\perp}

for every
j\in J
.
For any
x\in X

we have
x=\sum_{i}e_{i}\left<e_{i},x\right>+\sum_{j}d_{j}\left<d_{j},x\right>
.
If
x\in V^{\perp\perp}

then
x=\sum_{i}e_{i}\left<e_{i},x\right>

as
\left<x,d_{j}\right>=0
for each
j\in J
.
Thus
V^{\perp\perp}\subseteq W
, so
V^{\perp\perp}=W
.
In particular, as
V^{\perp}
is already an ultranorm closed submodule,
we find
W^{\perp}=(V^{\perp})^{\perp\perp}=V^{\perp}
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

So
(e_{i})_{i}
is a basis for
W=V^{\perp\perp}

and
(d_{j})_{j}
is a basis for
W^{\perp}=V^{\perp}
.
For brevity, let
\vartheta\colon V^{\perp\perp}\oplus V^{\perp}\to X

denote the map
(y,y^{\prime})\mapsto y+y^{\prime}
.
Clearly
\vartheta
is bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear.
As
\left<y,y^{\prime}\right>=0
for
(y,y^{\prime})\in V^{\perp\perp}\oplus V^{\perp}

we easily see
\vartheta
is inner product preserving and thus injective.
As
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup\{d_{j}\}_{j\in J}

is an orthonormal basis of
X
,
\vartheta
is surjective.
As
\vartheta
is inner product preserving, its inverse is bounded
— hence
\vartheta
is an isomorphism of Hilbert C
∗
-modules,
as promised.

\square

†
†
margin:

IX

Exercise
Assume
X
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for a von Neumann
algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Suppose
E\subseteq X
is an orthonormal set. Show

1.

E
is a basis of
E^{\perp\perp}

and

2.

for any
x\in X
,
we have

x\in E^{\perp\perp}

if and only if
\left<x,x\right>=\sum_{e\in E}\left<x,e\right>\left<e,x\right>
.

†
†
margin:

X

Exercise
Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for some von Neumann
algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Show that for any
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X
,
there is a finite orthonormal basis
of
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}^{\perp\perp}

consisting of at most
n
elements.
(Use the orthonormalization in the last part of
2.2.2
.)

†
†
margin:

161

Every Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
is isomorphic to some
\ell^{2}(I)
.
In fact, the cardinality of
I
is the only thing that matters,
in the following sense:
if
\ell^{2}(I)\cong\ell^{2}(J)
, then
I
and
J

have the same cardinality.
What about Hilbert C
∗
-modules?

†
†
margin:

II

Exercise
In this exercise we prove
[
93
, thm. 3.12]
.
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra
and
(p_{i})_{i\in I}

a family of projections from
\mathscr{B}
.
Write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})}

for the set of
I
-tuples
(b_{i})_{i\in I}

from
\mathscr{B}
that are
\ell^{2}
-summable (
2.2.2
)
and furthermore
\left\lceil b_{i}b_{i}^{*}\right\rceil\leqslant p_{i}
for every
i\in I
.
Show that
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})

is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with coordinatewise operations.
Prove that for any
(b_{i})_{i}
and
(c_{i})_{i}

in
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})

the sum
\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}^{*}c_{i}=:\left<(b_{i})_{i},(c_{i})_{i}\right>

converges ultraweakly
and turns
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})
into a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
Conclude
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})
is self-dual
and, in fact, that for every self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

with any orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
,
we have
X\cong\ell^{2}(\,(\left<e_{i},e_{i}\right>)_{i\in I}\,)
.

†
†
margin:

III

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra
and
(p_{i})_{i\in I}
,
(q_{j})_{j\in J}

families of projections from
\mathscr{B}
.
What can we say if
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})\cong\ell^{2}((q_{i})_{j\in J})
?
(
\ell^{2}
as defined in
2.5
.)
Let’s start with some counterexamples.

1.

Do all orthonormal bases have the same cardinality?

\mathscr{B}
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module over itself.
Clearly, the element
1
by itself is an orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{B}
.
However, for every projection
p\in\mathscr{B}

the pair
\{p,1-p\}
is also an orthonormal basis.
Worse still, for
\mathscr{B}\equiv\mathscr{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}))
,
the set

\{\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|,\ \left|1\right>\!\left<1\right|,\ \left|2\right>\!\left<2\right|,\ \ldots\}.

is an orthonormal basis.

2.

Perhaps any two orthonormal bases have a common coarsening in some sense?
Take
\mathscr{B}=M_{3}
.
It might not be obvious at this moment, but both

\{\,\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|+\left|2\right>\!\left<2\right|,\left|1\right>\!\left<+\right|\,\}\quad\text{and}\quad\{\,\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|,\left|2\right>\!\left<2\right|+\left|1\right>\!\left<+\right|\,\}

are orthonormal bases of
M_{3}
over itself.
(Combine
2.5
with
2.5
.)
There does not seem to be an obvious way in which these
two bases have a common coarsening of sorts.

3.

Maybe
\sum_{i\in I}\left<e_{i},e_{i}\right>=\sum_{j\in J}\left<d_{j},d_{j}\right>

for finite orthonormal bases
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
?
For commutative
\mathscr{B}
this indeed holds using Parseval’s identity.
However, in general it fails:
both
\{\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|,\left|1\right>\!\left<1\right|\}
and

\{\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|,\left|1\right>\!\left<0\right|\}

are orthonormal bases of
M_{2}
over itself,
but
\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|+\left|1\right>\!\left<1\right|\neq 2\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Let
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
be some orthonormal basis of a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X
.
Show that if
(u_{i})_{i\in I}

is a family of partial isometries
from
\mathscr{B}

(see
)
such that
u_{i}u_{i}^{*}=\left<e_{i},e_{i}\right>

for all
i\in I
,
then
(e_{i}u_{i})_{i\in I}

is an orthonormal basis of
X
.

Use this and
2.5
to conclude
that
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})\cong\ell^{2}((q_{i})_{i\in I})

for any projections
(p_{i})_{i\in I},(q_{i})_{i\in I}

from
\mathscr{B}

with
p_{i}\sim q_{i}
for
i\in I
.
(Here,
\sim
denotes the
Murray–von Neumann equivalence
.
That is:
p\sim q
iff there is a partial isometry
u

with
u^{*}u=p
and
uu^{*}=q
.
Eg.
\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|\sim\left|+\right>\!\left<+\right|

via
\left|0\right>\!\left<+\right|
.)

†
†
margin:

V

Exercise
Let
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
be some orthonormal basis of a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

with distinguished
1,2\in I
.
Show that if
\left<e_{1},e_{1}\right>+\left<e_{2},e_{2}\right>\leqslant 1
,
then we can make a new orthonormal basis of
X
by removing
e_{1}
and
e_{2}

and inserting
e_{1}+e_{2}
.

Conclude
p\mathscr{B}\oplus q\mathscr{B}\cong(p+q)\mathscr{B}

for projections
p,q\in\mathscr{B}
with
p+q\leqslant 1
.

†
†
margin:

162

Before we can prove our normal form (in
2.5
),
we need some more
comparison theory of projections
,
which was touched upon in
.
Recall that for projections
p,q
in a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{A}
,
we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}p\lesssim q}
if there is a partial isometry
u

with
u^{*}u=p
and
uu^{*}\leqslant q
.
In
we saw
\lesssim
preorders the projections.

†
†
margin:

II

Proposition
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra that is a factor (i.e. with center
\mathbb{C}1
).
For any two projections
p,q\in\mathscr{B}
,
either
p\lesssim q
or
q\lesssim p
(see
).

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
(For a more traditional proof, see e.g.
[
73
, prop. 6.2.4]
.)
Let
p,q\in\mathscr{B}
be any projections.
By Zorn’s lemma, there is a maximal
set
U\subseteq\mathscr{B}
of partial isometries
with
\sum_{u\in U}u^{*}u\leqslant p

and
\sum_{u\in U}uu^{*}\leqslant q
.
Define
p_{0}\equiv p-\sum_{u\in U}u^{*}u

and
q_{0}\equiv q-\sum_{u\in U}uu^{*}
.
Note that if
p_{0}=0
, then
p\lesssim q

via
\sum_{u\in U}u
, c.f.
.
Similarly, if
q_{0}=0
, then
q\lesssim p
.
So, reasoning towards contradiction,
suppose
p_{0}\neq 0
and
q_{0}\neq 0
.
As
\mathscr{B}
is a factor
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil q_{0}\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil=1

and so
0<p_{0}=p_{0}\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil q_{0}\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil p_{0}=p_{0}(\bigcup_{a\in\mathscr{B}}\left\lceil a^{*}q_{0}a\right\rceil)p_{0}=\bigcup_{a\in\mathscr{B}}\left\lceil p_{0}a^{*}q_{0}ap_{0}\right\rceil

by
and
.
Pick an
a\in\mathscr{B}
with
\left\lceil p_{0}a^{*}q_{0}ap_{0}\right\rceil\neq 0
.
Then
u_{0}\equiv[q_{0}ap_{0}]
, see
,
is a non-zero partial isometry
satisfying
u_{0}^{*}u_{0}=\left\lceil p_{0}a^{*}q_{0}ap_{0}\right\rceil\leqslant p_{0}

and
u_{0}u_{0}^{*}=\left\lceil q_{0}ap_{0}a^{*}q_{0}\right\rceil\leqslant q_{0}
,
contradicting maximality of
U
.
\square

†
†
margin:

IV

Theorem
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra that is a factor (i.e. with center
\mathbb{C}1
).
Suppose
X
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
Either there is an infinite cardinal
\kappa

such that
X\cong\ell^{2}((1)_{\alpha\in\kappa})
,
with
\ell^{2}
as in
2.5
,
or there is a natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}

and a projection
p\in\mathscr{B}

such that
X\cong\ell^{2}((1,\ldots,1,p))
,
where
1
occurs
n
times.

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
The case
X=\{0\}
is covered by
n=0
and
p=0
.
Assume
X\neq\{0\}
.

†
†
margin:

VI

As a first step we will prove that
either

1.

X
has an orthonormal basis
E

such that
\left<e,e\right>=1
for some
e\in E

or

2.

there is a single vector
e\in X
such that
\{e\}
is an orthonormal basis.

Pick any orthonormal basis
E_{0}
of
X
.
Let
P
denote the poset of subsets
U\subseteq E_{0}\times\mathscr{B}

satisfying

1.

u
is a partial isometry with
uu^{*}=\left<e,e\right>

for every
(e,u)\in U

and

2.

the domains of these
u
are orthogonal:
\sum_{(e,u)\in U}u^{*}u\leqslant 1
.

For any non-empty chain
C\subseteq P

it is easy to see
\bigcup C\in P

and so by Zorn’s lemma,
there is a maximal element
U_{0}\in P
.
Define

e_{0}\ =\ \sum_{(e,u)\in U_{0}}eu\qquad\qquad E_{1}\ =\ E_{0}\backslash\{e;\ (e,u)\in U_{0}\}.

The set
\{e_{0}\}\cup E_{1}
is an orthonormal basis of
X

— indeed, it is clearly orthonormal and
if
x=\sum_{e\in E_{0}}e\left<e,x\right>
,
then

x\ =\ e_{0}\Bigl(\sum_{(e,u)\in U_{0}}u^{*}\left<e,x\right>\Bigr)+\sum_{e\in E_{1}}e\left<e,x\right>.

If
E_{1}=\emptyset
, then
\{e_{0}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
X
and
we have shown point 2.

For the other case, assume
E_{1}\neq\emptyset
.
Pick
e_{1}\in E_{1}
.
Write
p_{0}=\left<e_{0},e_{0}\right>

and
p_{1}=\left<e_{1},e_{1}\right>
.
Suppose
p_{1}\lesssim 1-p_{0}
.
Then
vv^{*}=p_{1}
and
v^{*}v\leqslant 1-p_{0}
for some
v\in\mathscr{B}
.
Hence
v^{*}v+p_{0}\leqslant 1
.
So
U_{0}\cup\{(e_{1},v)\}\in P
contradicting maximality of
U_{0}
.
Apparently
p_{1}\not\lesssim 1-p_{0}
.
So by
2.5
, we must have
1-p_{0}\lesssim p_{1}
.
Let
v\in\mathscr{B}
be such that
vv^{*}=1-p_{0}

and
v^{*}v\leqslant p_{1}
.
Write
p_{0}^{\prime}=1-v^{*}v
.
As
1-p_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant p_{1}=1-(1-p_{1})

we have
(1-p_{0}^{\prime})+(1-p_{1})\leqslant 1
.
Define
q=1-((1-p_{0}^{\prime})+(1-p_{1}))
.
We now have the following splittings of
1
.
1-p_{1}
q
v^{*}v
p_{1}
p_{0}^{\prime}
p_{0}
vv^{*}

Note
q=p_{0}^{\prime}+p_{1}-1
and
p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}\geqslant q
.
Define

D\ =\ \{d_{0},d_{1}\}\qquad\text{where}\quad d_{0}\ =\ e_{1}q\qquad d_{1}\ =\ e_{0}+e_{1}v^{*}.

From
p_{1}q=q
it follows
D
is orthogonal.
Clearly
\left<d_{0},d_{0}\right>=q
and

\left<d_{1},d_{1}\right>\ =\ \left<e_{0},e_{0}\right>+vp_{1}v^{*}\ =\ p_{0}+vv^{*}\ =\ 1.

So
D
is an orthonormal set.
Using
0=(1-p_{0})p_{0}=vv^{*}p_{0}
and
q+v^{*}v=p_{1}
, we see
that for any
x\equiv e_{0}b_{0}+e_{1}b_{1}

with
b_{0}\in p_{0}\mathscr{B}
and
b_{1}\in p_{1}\mathscr{B}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{0}\langle d_{0},x\rangle+d_{1}\langle d_{1},x\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ d_{0}b_{1}+d_{1}(vb_{1}+b_{0})

\displaystyle\ =\ e_{1}qb_{1}+e_{0}vb_{1}+e_{1}v^{*}vb_{1}+e_{0}b_{0}+e_{1}v^{*}b_{0}

\displaystyle\ =\ e_{1}qb_{1}+e_{0}p_{0}vv^{*}vb_{1}+e_{1}v^{*}vb_{1}+e_{0}b_{0}+e_{1}v^{*}vv^{*}p_{0}b_{0}

\displaystyle\ =\ e_{1}qb_{1}+e_{1}v^{*}vb_{1}+e_{0}b_{0}

\displaystyle\ =\ e_{1}(q+v^{*}v)b_{1}+e_{0}b_{0}

\displaystyle\ =\ e_{1}b_{1}+e_{0}b_{0}\ =\ x.

Thus
D
is an orthonormal basis of
\{e_{0},e_{1}\}^{\perp\perp}
.
Hence
E\equiv D\cup(E_{1}-\{e_{1}\})
is an orthonormal basis of
X

with
d_{1}\in E
such that
\left<d_{1},d_{1}\right>=1
.

†
†
margin:

VII

For brevity, call
X

1-dim
if there is a one-element orthonormal basis of
X
.
In
2.5
we saw
how to create an orthonormal basis
D
of a non-1-dim
X

with an
e_{0}\in D
such that
\left<e_{0},e_{0}\right>=1
.
If
\{e_{0}\}^{\perp}
is not 1-dim,
we can apply
2.5

on
\{e_{0}\}^{\perp}
(instead of
X
)
to find an orthonormal basis
D^{\prime}
of
\{e_{0}\}^{\perp}

with
e_{1}\in D^{\prime}
such that
\left<e_{1},e_{1}\right>=1
.
This procedure can be continued using Zorn’s lemma as follows.
Let
P
denote the poset of orthogonal subsets
E\subseteq X

satisfying
\langle e,e\rangle=1
for all
e\in E
.
Clearly
\emptyset\in P

and for any non-empty chain
C\subseteq P

we have
\bigcup C\in P
.
By Zorn’s lemma there is a maximal
E\in P
.
Suppose
E^{\perp}\neq\{0\}
and
E^{\perp}
is not 1-dim.
Then by
2.5

we can find an orthonormal basis
E^{\prime}
of
E^{\perp}

together with
e\in E^{\prime}
such that
\left<e,e\right>=1
.
Now
E^{\prime}\cup\{e\}\in P
, contradicting maximality of
E
.
Apparently
E^{\perp}=\{0\}
or
E^{\perp}
is 1-dim.
If
E^{\perp}=\{0\}

then we are done
taking for
\kappa
the cardinality of
E
.
For the other case, assume
E^{\perp}=\{e\}^{\perp\perp}

for some
e\in X
with
\left<e,e\right>
a non-zero projection.
If
E
is finite, then we are done as well.
So, assume
E
is infinite.
Pick a sequence
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots\in E
of distinct elements.
Write
p=\left<e,e\right>

and
E_{0}=\{e_{n};\ n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Define

E_{1}\ =\ \{e+e_{1}(1-p),\ e_{1}p+e_{2}(1-p),\ e_{2}p+e_{3}(1-p),\ \ldots\}.

It is easy to see
E_{1}
is an orthogonal set
with
\left<d,d\right>=1
for all
d\in E_{1}
.
Furthermore
E_{1}
is an orthonormal basis for
E_{0}\cup\{e\}
,
hence
(E-E_{0})\cup E_{1}
is the desired orthonormal basis of
X
.
\square

†
†
margin:

VIII

Does this settle the issue raised
in
2.5
?
One might hope
that
\ell^{2}((1_{\mathscr{B}})_{\alpha\in\kappa})\cong\ell^{2}((1_{\mathscr{B}})_{\beta\in\lambda})

implies
\kappa=\lambda
for all cardinals
\kappa
,
\lambda
.
This is not the case.
Indeed, if
\mathscr{B}
is a type III factor
of operators on a separable Hilbert space,
then for any non-zero projection
p
,
we have
p\sim 1-p
(by combining
[
73
, dfn. 6.5.1]

and
[
73
, cor. 6.3.5]
)
and so
\mathscr{B}\cong p\mathscr{B}\oplus(1-p)\mathscr{B}\cong\mathscr{B}\oplus\mathscr{B}
as Hilbert C
∗
-modules.

†
†
margin:

163

Before we continue to the next topic,
the exterior tensor product of Hilbert C
∗
-modules,
we will show that the ultranorm completion
2.2.3

is determined by its universal property.
We will need this fact in our treatment
of the exterior tensor product.

†
†
margin:

II

Proposition
Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra and
V
a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
.

There is an up-to-isomorphism unique
self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

together with inner product preserving
\mathscr{B}
-linear
\eta\colon V\to X

with the following universal property.

For every bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear map
T\colon V\to Y

to some self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
Y
,
there is a unique bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear map
\hat{T}\colon X\to Y

with
\hat{T}\mathop{\circ}\eta=T
.

Moreover, for such
\eta\colon V\to X
,
the image of
V
under
\eta
is ultranorm dense.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
We already know such a
X
and
\eta\colon V\to X

exist by
2.2.3

and
2.2.3
.
For this one
\eta(V)
is ultranorm dense in
X
.
Assume there is another self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X_{2}

with inner product preserving
\mathscr{B}
-linear
\eta_{2}\colon V\to X_{2}

satisfying the universal property.
By the universal property of
\eta
applied to
\eta_{2}
,
there must exist a unique
U\colon X\to X_{2}

with
U\mathop{\circ}\eta=\eta_{2}
.
Similarly, there is a
V\colon X_{2}\to X

with
V\mathop{\circ}\eta_{2}=\eta
.
Clearly
\mathrm{id}_{X}\mathop{\circ}\eta=V\mathop{\circ}\eta_{2}=V\mathop{\circ}U\mathop{\circ}\eta

and so by the uniqueness
\mathrm{id}=V\mathop{\circ}U
.
Similarly
U\mathop{\circ}V=\mathrm{id}
.
It remains to be shown
U
preserves the inner product.
As

\left<\eta(x),\eta(y)\right>\ =\ [x,y]\ =\ \left<\eta_{2}(x),\eta_{2}(y)\right>\ =\ \left<U\eta(x),U\eta(y)\right>

for all
x,y\in X
, we know

\eta(X)
is ultranorm dense.
So by ultranorm continuity and bijectivity of
U
,
we know
U(\eta(X))=\eta_{2}(X)
is ultranorm dense in
X_{2}
.
Hence
U
preserves the inner product by
2.2.2

\square

2.5.1

Exterior tensor product

†
†
margin:

164

Suppose
X
is a Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module
and
Y
is a Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for some C
∗
-algebras
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
.
The algebraic tensor product
X\odot Y

is a right
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-module
via
(x\otimes y)\cdot(a\otimes b)=(xa)\otimes(yb)

and has an
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-valued inner
product fixed by
[x\otimes y,x^{\prime}\otimes y^{\prime}]=\left<x,x^{\prime}\right>\otimes\left<y,y^{\prime}\right>
.
Write
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\sigma}\mathscr{B}
for the spatial
C
∗
-tensor product
[
73
, §11.3]
of
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
.
The inner product on
X\odot Y
can be extended
to an
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\sigma}\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
on the norm completion of
X\odot Y

on which it is definite
[
81
]
.
This is a Hilbert C
∗
-module,
which is called the
exterior tensor product
of
X
and
Y
.
It is quite difficult to perform this construction:
Lance discusses the difficulties in
[
81
, ch. 4]
.
But if we assume
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
are von Neumann algebras

and
that
X
and
Y
are self-dual,
it is rather easier.
In fact, we will directly construct the ultranorm completion
of the exterior tensor product,
which is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-module,
which we call the
self-dual exterior tensor product
.
This self-dual exterior tensor product
behaves much better than the plain exterior tensor product.
As a first example, we will see (in
2.5.1
)
that
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)\otimes\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)\cong\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)

for the self-dual exterior tensor product,
which is false in general for the plain exterior tensor product.
The self-dual exterior tensor product also appears naturally
when computing the Paschke dilation
of a tensor product
\varphi\otimes\psi
, see
2.5.1
.

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem
Suppose
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
are von Neumann algebras.
For any self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module
X

and self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
Y
,
there is an up-to-isomorphism unique
self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-module

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}X\otimes Y}
, called the
(self-dual exterior) tensor product
,
together with a
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-linear injective
inner product preserving map
\eta\colon X\odot Y\to X\otimes Y

with the following universal property.

For every self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-module

Z
with bounded
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-linear

T\colon X\odot Y\to Z
,
there is a unique bounded
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-linear
map
\hat{T}\colon X\otimes Y\to Z

with
\hat{T}\mathop{\circ}\eta=T
,

where we norm
X\odot Y
with
\|t\|=\|[t,t]\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}}
.
Write
x\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}y=\eta(x\otimes y)
.
For any such
X\otimes Y
we have the following.

1.

The image of
X\odot Y
under
\eta
is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
.

2.

If
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is an orthonormal basis of
X

and
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
of
Y
,
then

(a)

(e_{i}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d_{j})_{i,j\in I\times J}

is an orthonormal basis of
X\otimes Y

and

(b)

the linear span of

\{\,\left|\,(e_{i}a)\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}(d_{j}b)\,\right>\!\left<\,e_{k}\otimes d_{l}\,\right|;\ a\in\mathscr{A},\ b\in\mathscr{B},\ i,k\in I,\ j,l\in J\,\}

is ultraweakly dense in
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
Pick orthonormal bases
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(d_{j})_{j\in J}
of
X

and
Y
respectively.
Write
p_{ij}\equiv\left<e_{i},e_{i}\right>\otimes\left<d_{j},d_{j}\right>\in\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
.
Define
X\otimes Y\equiv\ell^{2}((p_{ij})_{i,j\in I\times J})
,
with
\ell^{2}
as in
2.5
.
This definition of
X\otimes Y
seems to depend
on the choice of bases
(e_{i})_{i}
and
(d_{j})_{h}
.
We show it does not (up-to-isomorphism), in
2.5.1
.

†
†
margin:

IV

(Definition
\eta
)
Pick
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.
Write
x_{i}\equiv\left<e_{i},x\right>

and
y_{j}\equiv\left<d_{j},y\right>
.
Then

\displaystyle\left<x,x\right>\otimes\left<y,y\right>

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl(\sum_{i}x_{i}^{*}x_{i}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\sum_{j}y_{j}^{*}y_{j}\bigr)

by Parseval

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}x_{i}^{*}x_{i}\otimes y_{j}^{*}y_{j}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}(x_{i}\otimes y_{j})^{*}(x_{i}\otimes y_{j}).

Hence
(x_{i}\otimes y_{j})_{i,j}
is
\ell^{2}
-summable,
and so there is a unique
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-linear
map
\eta\colon X\odot Y\to X\otimes Y
fixed
by
\eta(x\otimes y)=(x_{i}\otimes y_{j})_{i,j}
.

†
†
margin:

V

(
\eta
preserves inner product)
Pick additional
x^{\prime}\in X
and
y^{\prime}\in Y
.
Writing
x^{\prime}_{i}=\left<e_{i},x^{\prime}\right>

and
y^{\prime}_{j}=\left<d_{j},y^{\prime}\right>
,
we have

\left<\eta(x\otimes y),\eta(x^{\prime}\otimes y^{\prime})\right>\ =\ \sum_{i,j}(x_{i}\otimes y_{j})^{*}\otimes(x_{i}^{\prime}\otimes y_{j}^{\prime})\ =\ \sum_{i,j}x_{i}^{*}x_{i}^{\prime}\otimes y_{j}^{*}y_{j}^{\prime}

and

\bigl(\sum_{i}x_{i}^{*}x_{i}^{\prime}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\sum_{j}y_{j}^{*}y_{j}^{\prime}\bigr)\ =\ \left<x,x^{\prime}\right>\otimes\left<y,y^{\prime}\right>\ =\ \left<x\otimes y,x^{\prime}\otimes y^{\prime}\right>.

Thus it is sufficient to show

\sum_{i,j}x_{i}^{*}x_{i}^{\prime}\otimes y_{j}^{*}y_{j}^{\prime}=\bigl(\sum_{i}x_{i}^{*}x_{i}^{\prime}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\sum_{j}y_{j}^{*}y_{j}^{\prime}\bigr)
,
which holds as the product np-functionals
are separating by definition, see
.
Thus indeed
\eta
preserves the inner product.

†
†
margin:

VI

(
\eta
is injective)
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X

and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in Y

be given such that
\eta(\sum_{l}x_{l}\otimes y_{l})=0

By
2.5

there are orthonormal
e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{m}\in X

such that
x_{l}=\sum_{i}e^{\prime}_{i}\langle e^{\prime}_{i},x_{l}\rangle
.
Similarly
y_{l}=\sum_{j}d^{\prime}_{j}\langle d^{\prime}_{j},y_{l}\rangle

for some orthonormal
d^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,d^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\in Y
.
As
\eta
preserves the inner product,
the elements
e_{i}^{\prime}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d_{j}^{\prime}
are again orthonormal
and so from

0\ =\ \sum_{l}x_{l}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}y_{l}\ =\ \sum_{i,j}(e^{\prime}_{i}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d^{\prime}_{j})\bigl(\sum_{l}\langle e^{\prime}_{i},x_{l}\rangle\otimes\langle d^{\prime}_{j},y_{l}\rangle\bigr)

it follows

\sum_{l}\langle e_{i}^{\prime},x_{l}\rangle\otimes\langle d_{j}^{\prime},y_{l}\rangle=0

for all
i,j
.
Consequently

\sum_{l}x_{l}\otimes y_{l}\ =\ \sum_{i,j}(e^{\prime}_{i}\otimes d^{\prime}_{j})\bigl(\sum_{l}\langle e^{\prime}_{i},x_{l}\rangle\otimes\langle d^{\prime}_{j},y_{l}\rangle\bigr)\ =\ 0,

which shows
\eta
is injective
(and that the inner product on
X\odot Y
is definite).

†
†
margin:

VII

(Image
\eta
ultranorm dense)
For brevity, write
E\equiv\{e_{i}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d_{j};\ i,j\in I\times J\}
.
By
(and
)

\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
is ultrastrongly dense in
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
.
So
E(\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B})

is ultranorm dense in
E(\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B})
,
see
2.2.2
.
In turn the linear span of
E(\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B})

is (by construction) ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
.
Thus the linear span of
E(\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B})

is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
.
Hence the image of
\eta
,
which contains the linear span of
E(\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B})
,
is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

(Universal property)
Let
Z
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-module
with some bounded
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-linear
T\colon X\odot Y\to Z
.
If
X\odot Y
were an
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-module

and
both
\eta
and
T
were
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-linear,
we could simply apply
2.2.3

to get the desired
\hat{T}
.
Instead, we will retrace the steps of its proof
and modify it for the present situation.
Uniqueness of
\hat{T}
is the same:

\hat{T}
is fixed by the ultranorm-dense image of
\eta

as it is bounded
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-linear
and so ultranorm continuous by
2.2.2
.
The argument for existence of
\hat{T}
is more subtle.
We will show

\left<Tt,Tt\right>\leqslant\|T\|^{2}[t,t]\qquad\text{for all~$t\in X\odot Y$}

(2.13)

as a replacement for the ultranorm continuity of
T
.
We cannot apply
2.2.1
directly:
as
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
is not a C
∗
-algebra,
we cannot construct
(\left<t,t\right>+\varepsilon)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

required for its proof.
To prove (
2.13
), we will work in the norm completions.
Write
\overline{\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}}

for the norm-closure of
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
in
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
.
Clearly
\overline{\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}}

is a C
∗
-algebra
(in fact it is the spatial C
∗
-tensor product of
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
).
Write
\overline{X\odot Y}

for the norm-closure of the image of
\eta
in
X\otimes Y
.
By norm continuity
(see
2.2.1
)
the operations of
X\otimes Y

restrict to turn
\overline{X\odot Y}
into a
Hilbert
\overline{\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}}
-module.
As
T
is uniformly continuous,
there is a unique bounded
\overline{\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}}
-linear

\overline{T}\colon\overline{X\odot Y}\to Z

with
\overline{T}\mathop{\circ}\eta=T
and
\|T\|=\|\overline{T}\|
.
Now we can apply the proof of
2.2.1

to
\overline{T}

to find
\langle\overline{T}s,\overline{T}s\rangle\leqslant\|\overline{T}\|^{2}\left<s,s\right>

for
s\in\overline{X\odot Y}
.
Substituting
s=\eta(t)
,
we get (
2.13
).

To define
\hat{T}
, pick any
t\in X\otimes Y
.
As the image of
\eta
is ultranorm dense,
there is a net
(\eta(t_{\alpha}))_{\alpha}
with
\eta(t_{\alpha})\to t
ultranorm.
For any np-map
f\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\|T(t_{\alpha})-T(t_{\beta})\|_{f}^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ f(\langle T(t_{\alpha}-t_{\beta}),T(t_{\alpha}-t_{\beta})\rangle)

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|T\|^{2}f([t_{\alpha}-t_{\beta},t_{\alpha}-t_{\beta}])

\displaystyle\ =\ \|T\|^{2}f(\left<\eta(t_{\alpha})-\eta(t_{\beta}),\eta(t_{\alpha})-\eta(t_{\beta})\right>)\ \to\ 0.

So
(Tt_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is ultranorm Cauchy in
Z
.
With a similar argument we see
(Tt_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

is equivalent to
(Tt^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}

whenever
(\eta t_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is equivalent
to
(\eta t^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
So we may define

\hat{T}t\ =\ \mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}Tt_{\alpha}.

Clearly
\hat{T}\mathop{\circ}\eta=T
.
With the same argument as for
2.2.3
,
we see
\hat{T}
is bounded and additive.
To show
\hat{T}
is
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-linear,
pick any
b\in\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
.
There is a net
b_{\beta}
in
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}

with
b_{\beta}\to b
ultrastrongly.
If
\hat{T}
is ultranorm continuous, we are done:

(\hat{T}t)b=\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{\beta}(\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}Tt_{\alpha})b_{\beta}\\
=\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{\beta}\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}T(t_{\alpha}b_{\beta})\\
=\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{\beta}\hat{T}(tb_{\beta})\\
=\hat{T}(tb).

To show
\hat{T}
is ultranorm continuous,
assume
t^{\alpha}\to 0
ultranorm in
X\otimes Y
.
It is sufficient to show
\hat{T}t^{\alpha}\to 0
ultranorm.
Let
f\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
be any np-map.
Write
\Phi
for the set of entourages for ultranorm uniformity
on
X\otimes Y
.
As the image of
\eta
is ultranorm dense,
there is a
\Phi
-indexed Cauchy net
(t^{\alpha}_{E})_{E\in\Phi}
in

\eta(X\odot Y)

with
t^{\alpha}_{E}\mathrel{E}t^{\alpha}

for each
E\in\Phi
.
Thus
\hat{T}t^{\alpha}=\mathop{unlim}\displaylimits_{E}Tt^{\alpha}_{E}
.
As
t^{\alpha}\to 0

there is some
\alpha_{0}

such that for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}

we have
\|t^{\alpha}\|_{f}\leqslant\varepsilon
.
We can find
E_{0}\geqslant E_{f,\varepsilon}

such that for all
E\geqslant E_{0}

we have
\|\hat{T}t^{\alpha_{0}}-Tt^{\alpha_{0}}_{E}\|_{f}\leqslant\varepsilon
.
For
E\geqslant E_{0}
we have
E\geqslant E_{f,\varepsilon}
and so

\|Tt^{\alpha_{0}}_{E_{0}}\|_{f}\ \overset{\eqref{eq:exttensorcontT}}{\leqslant}\ \|T\|\|t^{\alpha_{0}}_{E_{0}}\|_{f}\ \leqslant\ \|T\|(\|t^{\alpha_{0}}_{E_{0}}-t^{\alpha_{0}}\|_{f}+\|t^{\alpha_{0}}\|_{f})\ \leqslant\ 2\|T\|\varepsilon.

Consequently

\|\hat{T}t^{\alpha_{0}}\|_{f}\ \leqslant\ \|\hat{T}t^{\alpha_{0}}-Tt^{\alpha_{0}}_{E_{0}}\|_{f}+\|Tt^{\alpha_{0}}_{E_{0}}\|_{f}\ \leqslant\ \varepsilon+2\|T\|\varepsilon.

So
\hat{T}t^{\alpha}\to 0
ultranorm, as desired.

†
†
margin:

IX

(Uniqueness)
Assume there is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-module
X\otimes_{2}Y

together with a injective
inner product-preserving
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-linear
map
\eta_{2}\colon X\odot Y\to X\otimes_{2}Y

also satisfying the universal property.
With the same reasoning as in
2.5

there is an invertible bounded
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-linear

U\colon X\otimes Y\to X\otimes_{2}Y

with
U\mathop{\circ}\eta=\eta_{2}
.
Clearly
\left<Ux,Uy\right>=\left<x,y\right>

for
x,y\in E(\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B})
,
where we defined
E=\{e_{i}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d_{j};\ i,j\in I\times J\}
.
As we saw the linear span of
E(\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B})

is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
,
we see
U
must preserve the inner product by
2.2.2
.

†
†
margin:

X

Property 1 from the statement of the Theorem follows immediately
from the fact that the exterior tensor product is unique
up to an isomorphism which respects the embeddings.
Assume
(e^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
is an orthonormal basis of
X

and
(d^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in J^{\prime}}
is an orthonormal basis of
Y
.
We will show
E_{2}=\{e^{\prime}_{i}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d^{\prime}_{j};\ i,j\in I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}\}

is an orthonormal basis of
X\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}Y
.
Clearly
E_{2}
is orthonormal.
By
2.5
and
2.5

it is sufficient to show
e_{i_{0}}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d_{j_{0}}\in E_{2}^{\perp\perp}

for all
i_{0}\in I
and
j_{0}\in J
.
Write
a_{i}=\left<e_{i}^{\prime},e_{i_{0}}\right>

and
b_{j}=\left<d_{j}^{\prime},d_{j_{0}}\right>
.
By
2.5
and
2.5.1

it suffices to show the latter equality in

\bigl(\sum_{i}a_{i}^{*}a_{i}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\sum_{j}b_{j}^{*}b_{j}\bigr)\ =\ \langle e_{i_{0}}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d_{j_{0}},e_{i_{0}}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d_{j_{0}}\rangle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}a_{i}^{*}a_{i}\otimes b_{j}^{*}b_{j},

which holds as product np-functionals are separating
by definition, see
.

†
†
margin:

XI

Finally we prove that the linear span of

D\ \equiv\ \{\left|(e^{\prime}_{i}a)\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}(d^{\prime}_{j}b)\right>\!\left<e_{k}\otimes d_{l}\right|;\ a\in\mathscr{A},\ b\in\mathscr{B},\ i,k\in I^{\prime},\ j,l\in J^{\prime}\}

is ultraweakly dense in
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)
.
By
2.5
,
the linear span
of (the set of operators of
the form)
\left|(e^{\prime}_{i}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d^{\prime}_{j})t\right>\!\left<e^{\prime}_{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d^{\prime}_{l}\right|

with
t\in\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}

is ultraweakly dense in
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)
.
We will show that
t\in\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
suffices.
By
,
and
,
each
t\in\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}

is the ultrastrong limit
of a norm bounded net
t_{\alpha}
in
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
.
So by
2.5
(and
2.2.2
),
we see
\left|(e^{\prime}_{i}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d^{\prime}_{j})t_{\alpha}\right>\!\left<e^{\prime}_{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d^{\prime}_{l}\right|

converges ultraweakly to
\left|(e^{\prime}_{i}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d^{\prime}_{j})t\right>\!\left<e^{\prime}_{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}d^{\prime}_{l}\right|
.
So indeed, the linear span of
D

is ultraweakly dense in
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)
.
\square

†
†
margin:

XII

Examples
We give a few examples of the self-dual exterior tensor product
and relate it to other notions of tensor product.
To avoid confusion, we will denote the self-dual exterior
tensor product by
\otimes_{\text{ext}}

(instead instead of the plain
\otimes
.)

1.

Any Hilbert space is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathbb{C}
module.
For Hilbert spaces
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{K}

we have
\mathscr{H}\otimes_{\text{Hilb}}\mathscr{K}\cong\mathscr{H}\otimes_{\text{ext}}\mathscr{K}
,
where
\otimes_{\text{Hilb}}
denotes the regular tensor product of
Hilbert spaces.

2.

Every von Neumann algebra is a self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-module
over itself.
For any von Neumann algebras
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}

we have
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\text{vN}}\mathscr{B}\cong\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\text{ext}}\mathscr{B}

as Hilbert
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\text{vN}}\mathscr{B}
-modules,
where
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\text{vN}}\mathscr{B}
denotes the (spatial)
tensor product of von Neumann algebras.

3.

Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module
and
Y
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for von Neumann algebras
\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B}
.
The norm closure of
X\odot Y

in
X\otimes_{\text{ext}}Y

is the plain exterior tensor product of
X
and
Y
.
In turn, the ultranorm completion of the exterior tensor product
of
X
and
Y
is isomorphic to
X\otimes_{\text{ext}}Y
,
the self-dual exterior tensor product.

4.

In
2.5.1
it will be shown that

(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\otimes_{\text{ext}}(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2})\ \cong\ (\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2})\otimes_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}(\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2})

for all ncp-maps
\varphi_{i}\colon\mathscr{A}_{i}\to\mathscr{B}_{i}
(
i=1,2
)
between von Neumann algebras.

†
†
margin:

165

For Hilbert spaces
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{K}
,
we have
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K})\cong\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
.
However, for Hilbert modules
X
and
Y

and the regular (not necessarily self-dual)
exterior tensor product
it is not true in general
that
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)\otimes\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)\cong\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)
.
However, if
X
and
Y
are self-dual Hilbert modules over von Neumann
algebras, then we do have the familiar formula
for the self-dual exterior tensor product.
We need some preparation.

†
†
margin:

II

Setting
Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module
and
Y
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for von Neumann algebras
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proposition
For any operators
S\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
and
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)
,
there is a unique operator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}S\otimes T}\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)

fixed by
(S\otimes T)(x\otimes y)=(Sx)\otimes(Ty)

for any
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
We will define
S\otimes T
using the universal
property proven in
2.5.1
.
Write
\Theta\colon X\odot Y\to X\otimes Y

for the map
\Theta(x\otimes y)=(Sx)\otimes(Ty)
.
It is easy to see
\Theta
is
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-linear.
The hard part is to show that
\Theta
is bounded.
Pick any
t\in X\odot Y
— say
t\equiv\sum^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}\otimes y_{i}
.
To start, note
(\left<Sx_{i},Sx_{j}\right>)_{ij}
is positive in
M_{n}\mathscr{A}
—
indeed, for any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}

we have
\sum_{i,j}a_{i}^{*}\left<Sx_{i},Sx_{j}\right>a_{j}=\left<Sx,Sx\right>\geqslant 0

where
x\equiv\sum_{i}x_{i}a_{i}

and so it is positive
by
.
By reasoning in the same way for
\sqrt{\|S\|^{2}-S^{*}S}

instead of
S
,
it follows
(\left<Sx_{i},Sy_{i}\right>)_{ij}\leqslant(\|S\|^{2}\left<x_{i},x_{j}\right>)_{ij}
.
Similarly
0\leqslant(\left<Ty_{i},Ty_{j}\right>)_{ij}\leqslant(\|T\|^{2}\left<y_{i},y_{j}\right>)_{ij}
in
M_{n}\mathscr{B}
.
Write
s\colon(\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B})^{\oplus n}\to\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}

for the
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
-linear map
given by
s(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=t_{1}+\cdots+t_{n}
.
(
s
is the
row vector
filled with
1
s).
It is easy to see
sAs^{*}1=\sum_{i,j}A_{ij}

for any matrix
A
over
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
.
As a final ingredient,
recall that the
bilinear map
M_{n}(\otimes)\colon M_{n}\mathscr{A}\times M_{n}\mathscr{B}\to M_{n}(\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B})

defined in the obvious way
is positive by
and
.
Putting everything together:

\displaystyle\langle\Theta t,\Theta t\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\langle(Sx_{i})\otimes(Ty_{i}),(Sx_{j})\otimes(Ty_{j})\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\langle Sx_{i},Sx_{j}\rangle\otimes\langle Ty_{i},Ty_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ s\,\left(\langle Sx_{i},Sx_{j}\rangle\otimes\langle Ty_{i},Ty_{j}\rangle\right)_{ij}\,s^{*}1

\displaystyle\ =\ s\,M_{n}(\otimes)\bigl(\,\left(\langle Sx_{i},Sx_{j}\rangle\right)_{ij},\left(\langle Ty_{i},Ty_{j}\rangle\right)_{ij}\,\bigr)\,s^{*}1

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ s\,M_{n}(\otimes)\bigl(\,(\|S\|^{2}\langle x_{i},x_{j}\rangle)_{ij},(\|T\|^{2}\langle y_{i},y_{j}\rangle)_{ij}\,\bigr)\,s^{*}1

\displaystyle\ =\ \|S\|^{2}\|T\|^{2}\left<t,t\right>.

We have proven that
\Theta
is bounded (by
\|S\|\|T\|
).
Thus by
2.5.1
,
there exists a unique map
S\otimes T\colon X\otimes Y\to X\otimes Y

with
(S\otimes T)(x\otimes y)=\Theta(x\otimes y)\equiv(Sx)\otimes(Ty)
,
which is what we desired to prove.
\square

†
†
margin:

V

Exercise
Prove that for the
operation
\otimes
defined in
2.5.1
, we have

1.

\left|x_{1}\right>\!\left<x_{2}\right|\otimes\left|y_{1}\right>\!\left<y_{2}\right|=\left|x_{1}\otimes y_{1}\right>\!\left<x_{2}\otimes y_{2}\right|
,

2.

1\otimes 1=1
;

3.

(S\otimes T)(S^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime})=(SS^{\prime})\otimes(TT^{\prime})

and

4.

(S\otimes T)^{*}=S^{*}\otimes T^{*}
.

(Hint:
use that by the reasoning of
2.5.1

the linear
span of

\{(ea)\otimes(db);\ (a,b,e,d)\in\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\times E\times D\}

is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y

for any bases
E
of
X
and
D
of
Y
.)

†
†
margin:

VI

Theorem
There is an
nmiu-isomorphism
\vartheta\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)\otimes\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)\cong\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)

fixed by
\vartheta(S\otimes T)=S\otimes T
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Proof
We will show that
the bilinear map
\Theta\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)\times\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)

given by
\Theta(S,T)=S\otimes T

is a tensor product in the sense of

using
.
From this it follows
by
,
that there is a
nmiu-isomorphism

\vartheta\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)\otimes\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)\ \cong\ \mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)

with
\vartheta(S\otimes T)=\Theta(S,T)=S\otimes T
, as promised.

†
†
margin:

VIII

From
2.5.1
, it follows
\Theta
is an miu-bilinear map.
By
2.5.1

the linear span of operators of the form
\left|e_{i}a\right>\!\left<e_{k}\right|\otimes\left|d_{j}b\right>\!\left<d_{l}\right|=\left|(e_{i}a)\otimes(d_{j}b)\right>\!\left<e_{k}\otimes d_{l}\right|

is ultraweakly dense in
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)

for an orthonormal basis
e_{i}\otimes d_{j}
of
X\otimes Y
.
So the image of
\Theta
generates
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)
.

†
†
margin:

IX

It remains to be shown that there are sufficiently many product functionals
(see
for the definition of product functional
and their sufficiency).
Write

\displaystyle\Omega_{X}

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \{f(\langle x,(\,\cdot\,)x\rangle);\ f\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}\text{ np-map},x\in X\}

\displaystyle\Omega_{Y}

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \{g(\langle y,(\,\cdot\,)y\rangle);\ g\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}\text{ np-map},y\in Y\}.

From
2.2.1

it follows
\Omega_{X}
and
\Omega_{Y}
are order separating.
For
\sigma\in\Omega_{X}
and
\tau\in\Omega_{Y}

— say
\sigma\equiv f(\langle x,(\,\cdot\,)x\rangle)
,

\tau\equiv g(\langle y,(\,\cdot\,)y\rangle)
—
define
\sigma\otimes\tau\equiv(f\otimes g)(x\otimes y,(\,\cdot\,)x\otimes y)

and
\Omega\equiv\{\sigma\otimes\tau;\sigma\in\Omega_{X},\ \tau\in\Omega_{Y}\}
.
We will show
that
\Omega
is faithful
and that
(\sigma\otimes\tau)(\Theta(S,T))=\sigma(S)\tau(T)

for all
S\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
and
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)
,
which is sufficient to show
\Theta
is a tensor product.
Indeed for any
S\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
and
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)
, we have

\displaystyle(\sigma\otimes\tau)(\Theta(S,T))

\displaystyle\ =\ (f\otimes g)(\,\langle x\otimes y,(S\otimes T)(x\otimes y)\rangle\,)

\displaystyle\ =\ (f\otimes g)(\,\langle x\otimes y,(Sx)\otimes(Ty)\rangle\,)

\displaystyle\ =\ (f\otimes g)(\,\langle x,Sx\rangle\otimes\langle y,Ty\rangle\rangle\,)

\displaystyle\ =\ f(\langle x,Sx\rangle)\cdot g(\langle y,Ty\rangle\rangle)

\displaystyle\ =\ \sigma(S)\tau(T).

†
†
margin:

X

Finally, to show that
\Omega
is faithful,
assume
A\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X\otimes Y)
,
A\geqslant 0

and
(\sigma\otimes\tau)(A)=0

for all
\sigma\otimes\tau\in\Omega
.
Then for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,
we have
\langle x\otimes y,A(x\otimes y)\rangle=0
,
thus
\|\sqrt{A}(x\otimes y)\|^{2}=0
,
hence
\sqrt{A}(x\otimes y)=0
.
So
\sqrt{A}
vanishes on
X\odot Y
,
which is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
,
whence
\sqrt{A}=0
. So
A=0
.

\square

To compute the tensor product of Paschke dilations,
we will need to know a bit more about
the ultranorm continuity of the exterior tensor product.

†
†
margin:

II

Lemma
Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module
and
Y
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for von Neumann algebras
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
.
If
x_{\alpha}\to x
and
y_{\alpha}\to y
ultranorm
for norm-bounded nets
x_{\alpha}
in
X
and
y_{\alpha}
in
Y
,
then
x_{\alpha}\otimes y_{\alpha}\to x\otimes y
ultranorm.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
As
x_{\alpha}\otimes y_{\alpha}-x\otimes y=(x_{\alpha}-x)\otimes y_{\alpha}+x\otimes(y_{\alpha}-y)
,
it is sufficient
to show that both
(x_{\alpha}-x)\otimes y_{\alpha}\to 0

and
x\otimes(y_{\alpha}-y)\to 0
ultranorm.
Clearly

\displaystyle\langle(x_{\alpha}-x)\otimes y_{\alpha},(x_{\alpha}-x)\otimes y_{\alpha}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle x_{\alpha}-x,x_{\alpha}-x\rangle\otimes\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ (\langle x_{\alpha}-x,x_{\alpha}-x\rangle\otimes 1)\cdot(1\otimes\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle).

The maps
b\mapsto 1\otimes b
and
a\mapsto a\otimes 1

are ncp so both
1\otimes\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle

and
\langle x_{\alpha}-x,x_{\alpha}-x\rangle\otimes 1

are bounded nets of positive elements.
Also
\langle x_{\alpha}-x,x_{\alpha}-x\rangle\otimes 1\to 0

as
x_{\alpha}\to x
ultranorm.
Thus
applies and we see

(\langle x_{\alpha}-x,x_{\alpha}-x\rangle\otimes 1)\cdot(1\otimes\langle y_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}\rangle)\to 0
ultraweakly,
hence
(x_{\alpha}-x)\otimes y_{\alpha}\to 0
ultranorm.
With a similar argument
one shows
x\otimes(y_{\alpha}-y)\to 0
ultranorm.
\square

†
†
margin:

IV

Lemma
Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module
and
Y
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for von Neumann algebras
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
.
Suppose
U\subseteq X
and
V\subseteq Y

are ultranorm-dense submodules.
Then the linear span of

U\otimes V\ \equiv\ \{u\otimes v;\ u\in U,\ v\in V\}

is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
.

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
It is sufficient to show that every
x\otimes y\in X\otimes Y

is the ultranorm limit of elements from
U\otimes V

as the linear span of elements of the form
x\otimes y

is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
.
So pick any element of the form
x\otimes y\in X\otimes Y
.
By the Kaplansky density theorem for Hilbert C
∗
-modules,
see
2.4
,
there are norm-bounded nets
x_{\alpha}
in
U

and
y_{\alpha}
in
V

with
x_{\alpha}\to x
and
y_{\alpha}\to y
ultranorm.
So by
2.5.1
,
we see
x_{\alpha}\otimes y_{\alpha}\to x\otimes y
.
\square

†
†
margin:

VI

Lemma
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be an ncp-map between von Neumann algebras
together with ultrastrongly
dense
*
-subalgebras
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{A}

and
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{B}
.
Then: the linear span
of
\mathscr{T}\equiv\{a\otimes b;\ a\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime},\ b\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}\}

is ultranorm dense in
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}
,
which was defined in
2.3
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Proof
It is sufficient to show that any
a\otimes b\in\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}

is the ultranorm limit of elements from
\mathscr{T}
.
Pick any
a\otimes b\in\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B}
.
By

there are norm-bounded nets
a_{\alpha}
in
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
and

b_{\alpha}
in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}

with
a_{\alpha}\to a
and
b_{\alpha}\to b
ultrastrongly.
Say
\|b_{\alpha}\|,\|a_{\alpha}\|\leqslant B
for some
B>0
.
We will show
a_{\alpha}\otimes b_{\alpha}-a\otimes b\to 0
ultranorm.
As usual, we split it into two:

a_{\alpha}\otimes b_{\alpha}-a\otimes b\ =\ a_{\alpha}\otimes(b_{\alpha}-b)\ +\ (a_{\alpha}-a)\otimes b.

For the first term, note

\displaystyle\langle a_{\alpha}\otimes(b_{\alpha}-b),a_{\alpha}\otimes(b_{\alpha}-b)\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ (b_{\alpha}-b)^{*}\,\varphi(a_{\alpha}^{*}a_{\alpha})\,(b_{\alpha}-b)

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ B^{2}\|\varphi\|\,(b_{\alpha}-b)^{*}(b_{\alpha}-b),

which converges to
0
ultraweakly as
b_{\alpha}\to b
ultrastrongly.
The second

\displaystyle\langle(a_{\alpha}-a)\otimes b(a_{\alpha}-a)\otimes b\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ b^{*}\,\varphi((a_{\alpha}-a)^{*}(a_{\alpha}-a))\,b,

converges ultraweakly to
0

as and
b^{*}\varphi(\,\cdot\,)b
is normal
and
(a_{\alpha}-a)^{*}(a_{\alpha}-a)
converges ultraweakly to
0

as
a_{\alpha}\to a
ultrastrongly.
\square

†
†
margin:

167

Theorem
Suppose
\varphi_{i}\colon\mathscr{A}_{i}\to\mathscr{B}_{i}

is an ncp-map between von Neumann algebras
with Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P}_{i},\varrho_{i},h_{i})

for
i=1,2
.
Then
(\mathscr{P}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{2},\varrho_{1}\otimes\varrho_{2},h_{1}\otimes h_{2})

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}
.
Furthermore

(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\otimes(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2})\ \cong\ (\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2})\otimes_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}(\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2}).

(2.14)

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
We will prove (
2.14
) first.
We proceed as follows.
To start, we prove that
(\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{1})\odot(\mathscr{A}_{2}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2})

(with the obvious inclusion)
is ultranorm dense in
(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\otimes(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2})
.
Then we show that
(\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{A}_{2})\odot(\mathscr{B}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2})

is ultranorm dense in
(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2})\otimes_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}(\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2})
.
We continue and show that the natural bijection

U_{0}\colon(\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{1})\odot(\mathscr{A}_{2}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2})\cong(\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{A}_{2})\odot(\mathscr{B}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2})

preserves the induced inner products and
so extends uniquely to an
isomorphism (
2.14
).
Using this isomorphism,
we prove that the tensor product of the standard Paschke dilations
is a dilation of the tensor product.
From this, we finally derive the stated result.

†
†
margin:

III

(Density)
By construction,
\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{1}
is an ultranorm-dense

\mathscr{B}_{1}
-submodule
of
\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1}
.
Similarly
\mathscr{A}_{2}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2}
is ultranorm dense
in
\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2}
.
Thus by
2.5.1

(\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{1})\odot(\mathscr{A}_{2}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2})
is ultranorm dense in
(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\otimes(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2})
.
By

(with
and
),
we know
\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{A}_{2}

is an ultrastrongly-dense subalgebra
of
\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2}
.
Similarly
\mathscr{B}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2}

is ultrastrongly dense in
\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2}
.
Hence
(\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{A}_{2})\odot(\mathscr{B}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2})
is ultranorm dense
in

(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2})\otimes_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}(\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2})

by
2.5.1
,

†
†
margin:

IV

(Inner product preservation)
Clearly

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\,(a_{1}\otimes b_{1})\otimes(a_{2}\otimes b_{2})\,,\,(\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1})\otimes(\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2})\,\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ \langle a_{1}\otimes b_{1},\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}\rangle\,\otimes\,\langle a_{2}\otimes b_{2},\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ (b_{1}^{*}\varphi_{1}(a_{1}^{*}\alpha_{1})\beta_{1})\,\otimes\,(b_{2}^{*}\varphi_{2}(a_{2}^{*}\alpha_{2})\beta_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ (b_{1}\otimes b_{2})^{*}\,(\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2})\bigl((a_{1}\otimes a_{2})^{*}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})\bigr)\,(\beta_{1}\otimes\beta_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ \bigl\langle\,(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\otimes(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})\,,\,(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})\otimes(\beta_{1}\otimes\beta_{2})\,\bigr\rangle;

so
U_{0}\colon(\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{1})\odot(\mathscr{A}_{2}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2})\cong(\mathscr{A}_{1}\odot\mathscr{A}_{2})\odot(\mathscr{B}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2})

preserves the inner product by linearity.

†
†
margin:

V

As
U_{0}
preserves the inner product,
it is ultranorm continuous.
So with the usual reasoning, there is a unique bounded

\mathscr{B}_{1}\odot\mathscr{B}_{2}
-linear extension

U_{1}\colon(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\odot(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2})\ \to\ (\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2})\otimes_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}(\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2}).

In turn, by the universal property of the self-dual exterior tensor product,
there exists a unique
bounded
\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2}
-linear extension

U\colon(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\otimes(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2})\ \to\ (\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2})\otimes_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}(\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2}).

By the ultranorm density
2.5.1

and
2.2.2

we know
U
also preserves the inner product.
Also from
2.5.1
,
it follows that
U
is surjective.
Thus
U
is an isomorphism with
U^{-1}=U^{*}
.
We have shown (
2.14
).
By
2.5.1

there is a unique nmiu-isomorphism

\vartheta\colon\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\otimes\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2})\ \to\ \mathscr{B}^{a}((\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\otimes(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2}))

fixed by
\vartheta(S\otimes T)=S\otimes T
.
Let
(\mathscr{B}^{a}((\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2})\otimes_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}(\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2})),\varrho,h)

and
(\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}_{i}\otimes_{\varphi_{i}}\mathscr{B}_{i}),\varrho_{i},h_{i})

(for
i=1,2
)
denote the Paschke dilations
of
\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}
and
\varphi_{i}
, respectively,
constructed in
2.3
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Our next goal is to show

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta\mathop{\circ}(\varrho_{1}\otimes\varrho_{2})

\displaystyle\ =\ \varrho

\displaystyle h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{1}\otimes h_{2},

(2.15)

which is sufficient to show that
(\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes_{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{B}_{1})\otimes\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}_{2}\otimes_{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{B}_{2}),\varrho_{1}\otimes\varrho_{2},h_{1}\otimes h_{2})

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}
.
We start with the equation on the left.

\displaystyle\bigl((\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta\mathop{\circ}(\varrho_{1}\otimes\varrho_{2}))(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})\bigr)\,(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\otimes(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ \bigl((\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta)(\varrho_{1}(\alpha_{1})\otimes\varrho_{2}(\alpha_{2}))\bigr)\,(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\otimes(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ U\,(\varrho_{1}(\alpha_{1})\otimes\varrho_{2}(\alpha_{2}))\,U^{*}\,(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\otimes(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ U\,(\varrho_{1}(\alpha_{1})\otimes\varrho_{2}(\alpha_{2}))\,(a_{1}\otimes b_{1})\otimes(a_{2}\otimes b_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ U\,((\alpha_{1}a_{1})\otimes b_{1})\otimes((\alpha_{2}a_{2})\otimes b_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ ((\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})\cdot(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}))\otimes(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ \varrho(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})\,(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\otimes(b_{1}\otimes b_{2}).

As the linear span of
elements of the form
(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\otimes(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})

is ultranorm dense in
(\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2})\otimes_{\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}}(\mathscr{B}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{B}_{2})
,
we see
(\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta\mathop{\circ}(\varrho_{1}\otimes\varrho_{2}))(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})=\varrho(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})

for all
\alpha_{1}\in\mathscr{A}_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}\in\mathscr{A}_{2}
.
In turn, as the linear span of elements of the form
\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}

is ultrastrongly dense in
\mathscr{A}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{A}_{2}

and ncp-maps are ultrastrongly continuous,
we see
\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta\mathop{\circ}(\varrho_{1}\otimes\varrho_{2})=\varrho
.
We continue with the equation on the right
of (
2.15
).

\displaystyle(h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta)(T\otimes S)

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle(1\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1),\ U\,(T\otimes S)\,U^{*}(1\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1)\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle U^{*}\,(1\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1),\ (T\otimes S)\,U^{*}(1\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1)\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle(1\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1),\ (T\otimes S)\,(1\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1)\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle 1\otimes 1,\,T\,1\otimes 1\rangle\,\otimes\,\langle 1\otimes 1,\,S\,1\otimes 1\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{1}(T)\otimes h_{2}(S)

\displaystyle\ =\ (h_{1}\otimes h_{2})(T\otimes S)

From ultrastrong density, again, it
follows
h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta=h_{1}\otimes h_{2}
.
\square

2.6

Pure maps

†
†
margin:

168

Schrödinger’s equation is invariant under the reversal of time
and so the isolated quantum mechanical processes (ncp-maps) described by
it are invertible.
In stark contrast, the ncp-maps corresponding to measurement and discarding
are rarely invertible.
There are, however, many processes in between
which might not be invertible, but are still pure enough to be ‘reversed’
in some consistent and useful fashion.
For instance, the ncp-map
\mathrm{ad}_{V}

has the obvious reversal
(\mathrm{ad}_{V})^{\dagger}=\mathrm{ad}_{V^{*}}

(which is in general not the inverse.)
Stinespring’s theorem (
2
)
implies that every ncp-map into
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

splits as a reversible
\mathrm{ad}_{V}
after a (possibly) non-reversible
nmiu-map
\varrho
.
Using Paschke’s dilation,
we see that every ncp-map factors
as an nmiu-map
\varrho
before some particular ncp-map
h
.
Is this ncp-map
h
reversible in some sense?
In
2.6
we will see that these
h

are pure (in the sense of
)
and in
3.7

we will see that these pure maps are (in a sense) reversible.

†
†
margin:

II

The reversibility of pure maps (in a much more general setting)
is the main result of the next chapter.
In the remainder of this chapter, we study purity of ncp-maps a bit more.
We consider a seemingly unrelated alternative notion of purity,
which is based on Paschke’s dilation,
and show (in
2.6
)
that this alternative notion is in fact equivalent to the original.
We end this chapter by giving a different proof
of the ncp-extremeness of pure and nmiu-maps.

†
†
margin:

III

Before we continue, let’s rule out generalizations
of more familiar notions of purity
to arbitrary ncp-maps.

1.

A state
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}
is called pure if it is an extreme
point among all states.
It is unreasonable to define an arbitrary ncp-map to be pure
if it is extreme, as every nmiu-map (including
von Neumann measurements and discarding)
is extreme among the unital ncp-maps.
This follows from
and
,
but will be proven again in
2.6
.
In fact, in
2.6

we show that every ncp-map is the composition
of two extreme maps.

2.

Inspired by the GNS-correspondence between pure states and
irreducible representations,
Størmer defines a map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
to be pure
if the only maps below
\varphi
in the completely positive ordering
are scalar multiples of
\varphi
.
For every central element
z
of
\mathscr{A}
,
the map
a\mapsto za
is below
\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{A}}

and so the identity on non-factors is not pure in the sense
of Størmer,
even though the identity map is clearly reversible.

†
†
margin:

IV

Let’s recall how we came to the notion of purity in
,
by generalizing
\mathrm{ad}_{V}
.
Pick
V\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{K}
.
By polar decomposition, see
, we know that
V=UA

for
A\equiv\sqrt{V^{*}V}\colon\mathscr{H}\to\left\lceil A\right\rceil\mathscr{H}

and some isometry
U\colon\left\lceil A\right\rceil\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{K}
.
Thus we have
\mathrm{ad}_{V}=\mathrm{ad}_{A}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{U}
.
These two maps admit dual universal properties:

\mathrm{ad}_{U}
is a
corner

and
\mathrm{ad}_{A}
is a
filter
.
An ncp-map
\varphi
is pure
if it is the composition of a filter after a corner.
It turns out that
\varphi
is pure in this sense
if and only if the map on the left-hand side of
its Paschke dilation is surjective.

†
†
margin:

169

We recall the definition of corner
from
[
126
, dfn. 2]
and
.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
An ncp-map
h\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

is a
corner
for
a\in[0,1]_{\mathscr{A}}
if
h(a)=h(1)

and

for every ncp-map
f\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}

with
f(1)=f(a)
,
there is a unique ncp-map
f^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}

with
f=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h
.

†
†
margin:

III

Remark
In the next chapter we will study maps with a similar universal property
as corners in a more general setting,
but with all arrows in the reverse direction.
To help alleviate some confusion caused by the change of direction,
we call those maps
comprehensions
,
see
3.4
. See also
[
23
]
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Example
The map
h_{a}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor\mathscr{A}\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor
given
by
b\mapsto\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor b\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor

is a corner of
a\in[0,1]_{\mathscr{A}}
.
We call
h_{a}
the
standard corner
of
a
.
See
,

or
[
126
, prop. 5]
.

†
†
margin:

V

Lemma
If
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
is a Paschke dilation of a unital ncp-map,
then
h
is a corner.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
(This is a different proof of our similar result
[
127
, cor. 27]
.)
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be any unital ncp-map.
It is sufficient to prove
h

is a corner for the
standard dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)

constructed in
2.3
,
so
\mathscr{P}\equiv\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{\varphi}\mathscr{B})

and
h(T)=\langle e,Te\rangle
, where
e\equiv 1\otimes 1
.
Write
p\equiv\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|
.
From
\langle e,e\rangle=\varphi(1)=1

it follows
p
is a projection.
Define
\vartheta\colon\mathscr{B}\to p\mathscr{P}p

by
\vartheta(b)=\left|e\cdot b\right>\!\left<e\right|
.
Some straightforward computations (using
2.5
)
show
\vartheta
is an miu-map
with inverse
pTp\mapsto h(pTp)
.
So
\vartheta
is an miu-isomorphism and thus also normal.
Furthermore
h=\vartheta^{-1}(p(\,\cdot\,)p)

and so
h
is indeed a corner (of
p
).
\square

†
†
margin:

VII
