We recall the definition of filter, see
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Definition
An ncp-map
c\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

is a
filter
for
b\in\mathscr{B}
,
b\geqslant 0
if
c(1)\leqslant b

and

for every ncp-map
f\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{B}

with
f(1)\leqslant b
,
there is a unique ncp-map
f^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{A}

with
f=c\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}
.

†
†
margin:

IX

Remark
The direction-reversed counterpart
of filters are called
quotients
,
see
3.3
and
[
23
]
.
Filters were introduced by us in
[
126
, dfn. 2]

under the name
compressions
.
To avoid confusion with
[
4
]

and be closer to e.g.
[
132
]
,
we changed the name from compression to filter.
Filters are named after polarization filters:
the action of a polarization filter on the polarization
of a photon is the same as a filter for
the projection of the space of polarizations
the polarization filter allows to pass without disturbance.
Combining two polarization filters corresponds
to composing the corresponding filters.
This composed filter is in general a filter for an effect instead
of a projection.
This corresponds with the fact that there might not be a polarization
of the photon anymore,
with which it can pass both filters undisturbed.

†
†
margin:

X

Example
The map
c_{b}\colon\left\lceil b\right\rceil\mathscr{B}\left\lceil b\right\rceil\to\mathscr{B}

given by
a\mapsto\sqrt{b}a\sqrt{b}
is a filter
of
b\in\mathscr{B}
,
b\geqslant 0
.
We call
c_{b}
the
standard filter
of
b
.
See
,

or
[
126
, prop. 6]
.

†
†
margin:

XI

Exercise
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be any ncp-map
between von Neumann algebras.

1.

Let
c\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}
be any filter.
Show that if
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
is a Paschke dilation
of
\varphi
,
that then
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,c\mathop{\circ}h)
is a Paschke dilation
of
c\mathop{\circ}\varphi
.

2.

Assume
c^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}
is a filter of
\varphi(1)
.
Prove that there is a unique unital ncp-map
\varphi^{\prime}

with
\varphi=c\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{\prime}
.
Conclude that if
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
is a Paschke dilation
of
\varphi^{\prime}
,
that then
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,c^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h)

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi
.

(See
3.7.1
and
3.3
for the proofs
in the more general case.)

†
†
margin:

XII

Exercise
Derive (perhaps from
2.6
and
),
that filters are injective.

†
†
margin:

170

Definition
Filters, corners and their compositions are called
pure
.

†
†
margin:

Ia

Remark
This definition is equivalent to our previous definition
of purity given in
[
127
, dfn. 21]
;
see
.

†
†
margin:

II

Examples
Isomorphisms are pure. Less trivial examples follow.

1.

The pure maps
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

are precisely of the form
\mathrm{ad}_{T}

for some bounded operator
T\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}
.

2.

Let
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
be a Paschke dilation of some ncp-map
\varphi
.
In
2.6
we saw that if
\varphi
is unital,
that then
h
is a corner (and thus pure).
In general,
h=c\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}
for some filter
c
and corner
h^{\prime}

by
2.6
,
and so
h
is pure.

†
†
margin:

III

Remark
By definition, the set of pure maps is closed under composition.
In the next chapter we will see (
3.7
) that
there is a unique dagger
\dagger

which turns the subcategory of von Neumann
algebras with pure maps into a
\dagger
-category
such that
f^{\dagger}
is contraposed to
f
;

\diamond
-positive maps are
\dagger
-positive
and
\dagger
-positive maps have unique roots.
For this unique
\dagger
, we have
(\mathrm{ad}_{V})^{\dagger}=\mathrm{ad}_{V^{*}}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Show that any surjective nmiu-map between von Neumann algebras
is a corner of a central projection, hence pure
— and conversely,
that any corner of a central projection is a surjective nmiu-map.

†
†
margin:

171

We will prove that a map is pure if and only if
the left-hand side map of its Paschke dilation
is surjective. We need the following result.

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem
Let
\mathscr{A}
be a von Neumann algebra together with a projection
p\in\mathscr{A}
.
Then a Paschke dilation of the standard corner
h_{p}\colon\mathscr{A}\to p\mathscr{A}p
,

a\mapsto pap

is given by
(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A},h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil},h^{\prime}_{p})
,
where
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil
is the central carrier of
p
,
see
;

h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}
is the standard corner for
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil

and
h^{\prime}_{p}\colon\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}\to p\mathscr{A}p

is the restriction of
h_{p}
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
(This is a simplified proof of the result we published earlier.

[
127
, thm. 28]
)
Let
(\mathscr{A}\otimes_{h_{p}}p\mathscr{A}p,\varrho,h)

denote the Paschke dilation constructed in
2.3
.
We proceed in three steps:
first we show
\mathscr{A}p
is a self-dual Hilbert
p\mathscr{A}p
-module,
then we prove
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{h_{p}}p\mathscr{A}p\cong\mathscr{A}p

and finally we show
\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}p)\cong\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Note
\mathscr{A}p
is a pre-Hilbert
p\mathscr{A}p
-module
with scalar multiplication
(\alpha p)\cdot(pap)=\alpha pap

and inner product
\left<\alpha p,ap\right>=p\alpha^{*}ap
.
Since by the C
∗
-identity the norm on
\mathscr{A}p

as a Hilbert module coincides with that as a subset of
p\mathscr{A}p
;

\mathscr{A}p
is norm closed in
\mathscr{A}

and

\mathscr{A}
is norm complete,
we see
\mathscr{A}p
is a Hilbert
p\mathscr{A}p
-module.

Recall
\mathscr{A}
itself is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module.
The uniformity induced on
\mathscr{A}p
by the ultranorm uniformity
of
\mathscr{A}
coincides with the ultranorm uniformity of
\mathscr{A}p
itself.
The module
\mathscr{A}p
is ultranorm closed in
\mathscr{A}

as
x_{\alpha}\to x
ultranorm
implies
x_{\alpha}p\to xp
ultranorm.
As self-duality is equivalent to ultranorm completeness,
we see
\mathscr{A}p
is self dual as well.

†
†
margin:

V

Concerning
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{h_{p}}p\mathscr{A}p\cong\mathscr{A}p
,
define
B\colon\mathscr{A}\times p\mathscr{A}p\to\mathscr{A}p

by
B(\alpha,pap)=\alpha pap
.
A straightforward computation
shows
B
is a
h_{p}
-compatible complex bilinear map
(see
2.3
)
and so by
2.3
, there is a unique bounded module
map
U\colon\mathscr{A}\otimes_{h_{p}}p\mathscr{A}p\to\mathscr{A}p

fixed by
U(\alpha\otimes pap)=B(\alpha,pap)\equiv\alpha pap
.
We will show
U
is an isomorphism.
Clearly
U
is surjective.

An easy computation shows
a\otimes p\alpha p-ap\alpha p\otimes p=0

for all
a,\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
.
So the set
\{a\otimes p;\ a\in\mathscr{A}\}

is ultranorm dense in
\mathscr{A}\otimes_{h_{p}}p\mathscr{A}p
.
From this and

\left<\alpha\otimes p,a\otimes p\right>=p\alpha^{*}ap=\left<U\alpha\otimes p,Ua\otimes p\right>

we see
U
preserves the inner product.
Hence
U
is an isomorphism with
U^{*}=U^{-1}

given by
U^{*}a=a\otimes p
.

†
†
margin:

VI

To show
\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}p)\cong\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}
,
we will first find a convenient orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{A}p
.
By
,
there are partial isometries
(u_{i})_{i}

with
\sum_{i}u_{i}u_{i}^{*}=\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil

and
u_{i}^{*}u_{i}\leqslant p
.
Write
E=\{u_{i};\ i\in I\}
.
As
u_{i}=u_{i}p
and
u_{i}=u_{i}u_{i}^{*}u_{i}

we see
E
is an orthonormal subset of
\mathscr{A}p
.
For any
ap\in\mathscr{A}p
, we have

ap\ =\ a\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil p\ =\ \left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil ap\ =\ \bigl(\sum_{i}u_{i}u_{i}^{*}\bigr)ap\ =\ \sum_{i}u_{i}u_{i}^{*}ap\ =\ \sum_{i}u_{i}\left<u_{i},ap\right>,

where the sums converge ultrastrongly in
\mathscr{A}
.
Thus the last sum converges ultranorm in
\mathscr{A}p
as well.
Hence
E
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{A}p
.

Define
\varrho_{0}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}p)

by
\varrho_{0}=\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho
.
Note
\varrho_{0}
is an nmiu-map and
\varrho_{0}(\alpha)ap=\alpha ap
.
The map
\varrho_{0}
is surjective:
indeed for any
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}p)

T(ap)\ =\ \sum_{i}T(u_{i})u_{i}^{*}ap\ =\ \bigl(\sum_{i}T(u_{i})u_{i}^{*}\bigr)ap\ =\ \varrho_{0}\bigl(\sum_{i}T(u_{i})u_{i}^{*}\bigr)ap.

Next, we show
\left\lceil\varrho_{0}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil
.
It suffices to prove for all
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
,
\alpha\geqslant 0
, that

\alpha ap=0\text{ \ for all\ }a\in\mathscr{A}\quad\iff\quad\alpha\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil=0.

Clearly,

\alpha\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil=0
implies
\alpha ap=\alpha\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil ap=0
.
For the converse, assume that we have
\alpha ap=0
for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
.
Then in particular
\alpha u_{i}p=0
,
hence
\alpha\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil=\alpha\sum_{i}u_{i}u_{i}^{*}=\sum_{i}\alpha u_{i}pu_{i}^{*}=0
.
Indeed
\left\lceil\varrho_{0}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil
.

By
2.6

there is an nmiu-isomorphism
\varphi\colon\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}p)

with
\varrho_{0}=\varphi\mathop{\circ}h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}
.
Hence
h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}=(\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}})\mathop{\circ}\varrho
.
We compute

h^{\prime}_{p}\mathop{\circ}h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}\ =\ h_{p}\ =\ h\mathop{\circ}\varrho\ =\ h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{U}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{0}\ =\ h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{U}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}.

Using surjectivity of
h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}

and rearranging, we find
h^{\prime}_{p}\mathop{\circ}(\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{U^{*}})=h
.
Thus indeed
(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A},h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil},h^{\prime}_{p})

is a Paschke dilation of
h_{p}
.
\square

†
†
margin:

VII

Theorem
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be an ncp-map
between von Neumann algebras with
Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
.
The map
\varphi
is pure if and only if
\varrho
is surjective.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Proof
Assume
\varrho
is surjective.
The kernel of
\varrho
is an ultraweakly closed
two-sided ideal and so
\ker\varrho=z\mathscr{A}
for some central projection
z
,
see
.
The standard corner
h_{z^{\perp}}\colon\mathscr{A}\to z^{\perp}\mathscr{A}

is the quotient-map of
z\mathscr{A}

and so by the isomorphism theorem
\varrho
is a corner as well.
In
2.6
we saw
h
is pure.
So
\varphi
is the composition of pure maps, hence pure itself.

For the converse, assume
\varphi
is pure.
For brevity, write
p\equiv\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil
.
There is a unique ncp-map
c
with
\varphi=c\mathop{\circ}h_{p}
,
where
h_{\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil}
is the standard corner of
p
.
The map
c
is a filter,
see
.
By
2.6
,
the triple
(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A},h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil},h^{\prime}_{p})

is a Paschke dilation of
h_{p}
,
where
h^{\prime}_{p}
denotes the restriction of
h_{p}
to
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}
.
So by
2.6

we see
(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A},h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil},c\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}_{p})

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi
.
Clearly
h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}
is surjective.
If
(\mathscr{P}^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},h^{\prime})
is any other Paschke dilation of
\varphi
,
then
\varrho^{\prime}=\vartheta\mathop{\circ}h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}

for some isomorphism
\vartheta

and so
\varrho^{\prime}
is surjective as well.
\square

†
†
margin:

172

The order correspondence
2.3

has several corollaries.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Assume
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
is an ncp-map between von Neumann
algebras.
We say
\varphi
is
ncp-extreme

if
\varphi
is an extreme point among
the ncp-maps with the same value on
1

[
108
]

—
that is:
for any
0<\lambda<1
, ncp-maps
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

with
\varphi_{1}(1)=\varphi_{2}(1)=\varphi(1)

we have

\lambda\varphi_{1}+(1-\lambda)\varphi_{2}\ =\ \varphi\quad\implies\quad\varphi_{1}\ =\ \varphi_{2}\ =\ \varphi.

†
†
margin:

III

Theorem
Assume
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
is an ncp-map
with Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
.
Then the following are equivalent.

1.

The map
h
is injective on
\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
,
the commutant of
\varrho(\mathscr{A})
.

2.

The map
h
is injective on
[0,1]_{\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}
.

3.

The map
\varphi
is ncp-extreme.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
(Based on
[
8
, prop. 1.4.6]

and
[
93
, thm. 5.4]
.)
We prove
1\Rightarrow 2\Rightarrow 3\Rightarrow 1
.

†
†
margin:

V

(
1\Rightarrow 2
)
Trivial.

†
†
margin:

VI

(
2\Rightarrow 3
)
Assume
h
is injective on
[0,1]_{\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}
.
Suppose
\varphi=\lambda\psi+(1-\lambda)\psi^{\prime}

for some
0<\lambda<1

and ncp
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

with
\psi(1)=\psi^{\prime}(1)=\varphi(1)
.
Note
0\leqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}\lambda\psi\leqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}\varphi

hence
\lambda\psi=\varphi_{t}
for some
t\in[0,1]_{\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}

by
2.3
.
So

h(\lambda 1)\ =\ \varphi_{\lambda 1}(1)\ =\ \lambda\varphi(1)\ =\ \lambda\psi(1)\ =\ \varphi_{t}(1)\ =\ h(t).

By the injectivity of
h
,
we find
t=\lambda 1
,
thus
\lambda\psi=\lambda\varphi

and so
\psi=\varphi
.
The proof of
\psi^{\prime}=\varphi
is the same.
Hence
\varphi
is ncp-extreme.

†
†
margin:

VII

(
3\Rightarrow 1
)
Suppose
\varphi
is ncp-extreme.
Pick any
a\in\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}
with
h(a)=0
.
We want to show
a=0
.
Clearly
h(a)_{\mathbb{R}}=h(a_{\mathbb{R}})=0

and
h(a)_{\mathbb{I}}=h(a_{\mathbb{I}})=0
,
so it is sufficient to prove
a_{\mathbb{R}}=a_{\mathbb{I}}=0
.
Thus we may assume without loss of generality that
a
is self adjoint.

By
2.6

h
is pure
and thus splits as
h=c\mathop{\circ}h_{p}

for some filter
c\colon p\mathscr{P}p\to\mathscr{B}

and the standard corner
h_{p}\colon\mathscr{P}\to p\mathscr{P}p

for
p=\left\lceil h\right\rceil
.
By
2.6
filters are injective
and so from the assumption
0=h(a)=c(pap)

it follows
pap=0
.
Using
and some easy algebra,
we can find
0<\mu,\lambda

such that
\frac{1}{4}\leqslant\mu a+\lambda\leqslant\frac{3}{4}
.
Define
t=\mu a+\lambda
.
For the moment, note
\varphi_{t}
is ncp.
As
pap=0
, we see
ptp=\lambda p
and
so
\frac{1}{4}p\leqslant ptp=\lambda p\leqslant\frac{3}{4}p
.
Thus either
0<\lambda<1
or
p=0
.
If
p=0
then
\varphi=0
hence
\mathscr{P}=\{0\}

and
a=0
as desired.
For the non-trivial case, assume
0<\lambda<1
.
Note
\varphi_{t}(1)=h(t)=c(ptp)=\lambda c(p1p)=\lambda\varphi(1)

and similarly
\varphi_{1-t}(1)=(1-\lambda)\varphi(1)
.
Define
\psi_{1}=\lambda^{-1}\varphi_{t}

and
\psi_{2}=(1-\lambda)^{-1}\varphi_{1-t}
.
By the previous,
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are ncp-maps
with
\psi_{1}(1)=\psi_{2}(1)=\varphi(1)
.
Also
\lambda\psi_{1}+(1-\lambda)\psi_{2}=\varphi
.
As
\varphi
is ncp-extreme,
we must have
\psi_{1}=\psi_{2}=\varphi
.
So
\varphi_{t}=\lambda\varphi=\varphi_{\lambda 1}
,
thus
\lambda=t\equiv\mu a+\lambda
.
Rearranging:
\mu a=0
.
As
\mu>0
, we find
a=0
, as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

VIII

Corollary
Any nmiu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
is ncp-extreme.
(For a different proof, see e.g.
[
108
, thm. 3.5]
.)

†
†
margin:

IX

Proof
Easy:
(\mathscr{B},\varrho,\mathrm{id})
is a Paschke dilation of
\varrho

and
\mathrm{id}
is injective.
\square

†
†
margin:

X

Theorem
Any pure ncp-map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
is ncp-extreme.

†
†
margin:

XI

Proof
By

\varphi=c\mathop{\circ}h_{p}

for a filter
c\colon p\mathscr{A}p\to\mathscr{B}

and projection
p\equiv\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil
.
Combining
2.6
and
2.6

we see
(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A},h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil},c\mathop{\circ}h_{p})

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi
.
Appealing to
2.6
,
we are done if we can show
c\mathop{\circ}h_{p}

is injective on
[0,1]_{h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}
.
As
h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}
is surjective,

h_{\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil}(\mathscr{A})^{\square}=Z(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A})
.
Assume
t\in[0,1]_{Z(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A})}
with
c(ptp)=0

We want to show
t=0
.
As
c
is injective by
2.6
,
we must have
ptp=0
.
So
p\leqslant 1-\left\lceil t\right\rceil

and
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\leqslant 1-\left\lceil t\right\rceil
.
Hence
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\leqslant\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil-\left\lceil t\right\rceil\leqslant\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil
.
So
t\leqslant\left\lceil t\right\rceil=0
, as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

XII

Corollary
Any ncp-map is the composition of two ncp-extreme maps.

Chapter 3

Diamond, andthen, dagger

†
†
margin:

173

In the previous chapter and in
[
124
]

we have studied categorical properties of von Neumann algebras.
In this chapter we change pace:
we study categories that resemble the
category of von Neumann algebras.
The goal of this line of study is to identify
axioms which uniquely pick out the category of von Neumann algebras.
We do not reach this ambitious goal —
instead we will build up to a characterization of
the existence of a unique
\dagger
-structure
on the pure maps of a von Neumann algebras-like category.

†
†
margin:

II

As a basic axiom we will require the categories we consider to be
effectuses (which will be defined in
3.2
).
For us the definition of an effectus will play a similar role
to that of a topological space for a geometer.
In many applications,
general topological spaces on their own are of little interest:
the axioms are so weak that there are
many (for the application) pathological examples.
These weak axioms, however, are very expressive in the sense
that they allow for the definition of many useful notions.
Herein lies the strength of topological spaces —
as a stepping stone:
many important classes of mathematical spaces
are just plain topological spaces with a few additional axioms.
Similarly, there will be many pathological effectuses.
Their use for us lies in their expressiveness.

†
†
margin:

III

Before we dive into effectuses,
we need to learn about effect algebras (and related structures),
which were introduced by mathematical physicists as
a generalization of Boolean algebra to study fuzzy predicates
in quantum mechanics.
For us, effectuses are the
categorical counterpart of effect algebras.
*
*

*

Effect
algebroids
[
98
,
97
]
are a different
categorification with applications in cohomology.

After this, we continue with a brief, but thorough development
of the basic theory of effectuses.
There is a lot more to say about effectuses
(see
[
23
]
), which has been omitted
to avoid straining the reader.
We will indulge, however,
in one tangent,
which can be skipped:
the study of abstract convex sets in
§
3.2.4
.
For the moment, think of an effectus as a generalization
of the opposite category of von Neumann algebras:
the objects represent the physical systems
(or data types, if you like)
and the maps represent the physical processes
(or properly typed programs).

†
†
margin:

IV

The first two axioms we add on top of those of an effectus
are the existence of quotients and comprehension.
It’s helpful to discuss the origin of these axioms.
It started with the desire to axiomatize categorically
the sequential product on a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{A}
,
that is the operation
a\mathbin{\&}b\equiv\sqrt{a}b\sqrt{a}
,
which represents sequential measurement
of first
a

andthen

b
.
The sequential product is a quantum mechanical generalization
of classical conjunction (logical ‘and’).
In contrast to the tame
\wedge
in a Boolean algebras,
the sequential product does not obey a lot of insightful
formulas.
The sequential product as a binary operation
has received quite some attention
[
46
,
37
,
47
,
83
,
44
,
104
,
45
,
72
,
122
,
111
,
71
,
6
]
.
We approach the sequential product in a rather different way:
we take a step back and
consider the map
\mathrm{asrt}_{a}(b)\equiv a\mathbin{\&}b

for fixed
a
. (
\mathrm{asrt}_{a}
is named after the
assert
statement
used in many programming languages,
see
3.6
.)
This map
\mathrm{asrt}_{a}
factors in two:

\textstyle{\mathscr{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\left\lceil a\right\rceil\mathscr{A}\left\lceil a\right\rceil\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{A}}
\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left\lceil a\right\rceil b\left\lceil a\right\rceil\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sqrt{a}b\sqrt{a}.}

It turned out that both
\pi
and
\xi
have
nice and dual (in a sense) defining universal properties
which can be expressed in an effectus:

\pi
is a comprehension and
\xi
is a quotient.
†
†

†

Beware: an effectus quotient is not the same thing
as a von Neumann-algebra quotient.

†
†
margin:

V

The existence of quotients and comprehension in some effectus
C

is interesting on its own, but with
two additional axioms (the existence of images and
preservation of images under orthocomplementation),
we get a surprisingly firm handle
on the
possibilistic
side of
C
,
by means of the existence
of a certain functor
(\ )_{\diamond}\colon C\to\mathsf{OMLat}

to the category of orthomodular lattices.
In the guiding example of von Neumann algebras,
we have
f_{\diamond}=g_{\diamond}

if and only
there are no post-measurement
a
and initial state
\omega

that can determine with certainty
whether
f
or
g
has been performed.
‡
‡

‡

In symbols:
f_{\diamond}=g_{\diamond}\iff\bigl(\forall a,\omega.\ \omega(f(a))=0\ \Leftrightarrow\ \omega(g(a))=0\bigr).

With this functor we can introduce several
possibilistic notions of which
\diamond
-adjoint
and
\diamond
-positive are the most important.
Because of the central role of
\diamond
,
we call an effectus with these axioms
a
\diamond
-effectus.

†
†
margin:

VI

The axioms of a
\diamond
-effectus do not force
any coherence between the quotients and comprehension.
This has been a roadblock to the axiomatization
of the sequential product for quite a while.
The key insight was the following:
the map
\mathrm{asrt}_{a}
(i.e.
b\mapsto a\mathbin{\&}b
)
is the unique
\diamond
-positive map on
\mathscr{A}

with
\mathrm{asrt}_{a}(1)=a
.
We turn this theorem into an axiom:
an
\&
-effectus is
a
\diamond
-effectus
where there are such unique
\diamond
-positive maps
\mathrm{asrt}_{a}

and where an additional polar decomposition axiom holds.
In an
\&
-effectus, the predicates on an object carry a canonical
binary operation
\&

which is the intended sequential product in
the case of von Neumann algebras.

†
†
margin:

VII

In an effectus, we call a map pure if it can be written
as the composition of quotients and comprehensions.
In
2.6
we saw that in the case of von Neumann algebras,
a map is pure (in this sense) if and only if the corresponding Paschke
embedding is surjective.
In particular, the pure maps
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

are exactly of the form
\mathrm{ad}_{v}

(for bounded operators
v\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}
)
which carry a
\dagger
-structure,
namely
(\mathrm{ad}_{v})^{\dagger}=\mathrm{ad}_{v^{*}}
.
This begs the question: can we define a
\dagger
on all pure maps
in an
\&
-effectus?
The main result of this chapter
is Theorem
3.7
,
which gives necessary and sufficient conditions
for an
\&
-effectus
to have a well-behaved
\dagger
-structure on its pure maps.
After this apogee,
we tie up some lose ends
and discuss how these
structures compare to those already known in the literature.

3.1

Effect algebras and related structures

†
†
margin:

174

Before we turn to effectuses,
it is convenient to introduce some lesser-known algebraic structures
of which the effect algebra is the most important.
It will turn out that the set of predicates associated to an object in
an effectus can be arranged into an effect algebra.
In this way, effect algebras play the same role
for effectuses as Heyting algebras for toposes.
First, we will recall the definition of partial commutative monoid (PCM)
— as later in
3.2

we will see that the partial maps between
two objects in an effectus can be arranged into a PCM.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
A
partial commutative monoid
(
PCM
)
M

is a set
M
together with distinguished element
0\in M

and a partial binary operation
\ovee
such that
for all
a,b,c\in M

— writing
a\perp b
whenever
a\ovee b
is defined
— we have

1.

(partial commutativity)

if
a\perp b
, then
b\perp a
and
a\ovee b=b\ovee a
;

2.

(partial associativity)

if
a\perp b
and
a\ovee b\perp c
,
then
b\perp c
,
a\perp b\ovee c

and
(a\ovee b)\ovee c=a\ovee(b\ovee c)

and

3.

(zero)

0\perp a
and
0\ovee a=a
.

A map
f\colon M\to N

between PCMs
M
and
N

is called an
PCM homomorphism

if for all
a,b\in E

with
a\perp b
,
we have
f(a)\perp f(b)

and
f(a)\ovee f(b)=f(a\ovee b)
.
Write
PCM
for the category

of PCMs with these homomorphisms. (Cf.
[
65
,
121
]
)

For
a,b
in a PCM
M
,
we say
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}a\leqslant b}

iff
a\ovee c=b
for some
c\in M
.

†
†
margin:

III

Exercise
Show a PCM is preordered by
\leqslant
.

†
†
margin:

IV

The partial commutativity and associativity of a PCM
ensure that a sum only depends on which elements
occur (and how often), instead of their order.
Formally:
if
(\cdots(x_{1}\ovee x_{2})\ovee\cdots)\ovee x_{n}

exists some
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
in some PCM, then so
does
(\cdots(x_{\pi(1)}\ovee x_{\pi(2)})\ovee\cdots)\ovee x_{\pi(n)}

for any permutation
\pi
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
and

(\cdots(x_{1}\ovee x_{2})\ovee\cdots)\ovee x_{n}\ =\ (\cdots(x_{\pi(1)}\ovee x_{\pi(2)})\ovee\cdots)\ovee x_{\pi(n)}.

Thus parantheses are superfluous and we will often leave them out:

\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}\ \equiv\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}x_{1}\ovee\cdots\ovee x_{n}}\ \equiv\ (\cdots(x_{1}\ovee x_{2})\ovee\cdots)\ovee x_{n}.

†
†
margin:

175

Definition
A PCM
E
together with distinguished element
1\in E

is called an
effect algebra
(
EA
)
[
31
]

provided that

1.

(orthocomplement)

for every
a

there is a unique
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}a^{\perp}}

with
a\ovee a^{\perp}=1

and

2.

(zero–one)

if
a\perp 1
, then
a=0
.

A map
f\colon E\to F

between effect algebras
E
and
F

is called an
effect algebra homomorphism

if it is a PCM homomorphism that preserves
1
;
concretely:

1.

(additive)

a\perp b
implies
f(a)\perp f(b)
and
f(a)\ovee f(b)=f(a\ovee b)

and

2.

(unital)

f(1)=1
.

(It follows that
f(0)=0
and
f(a^{\perp})=f(a)^{\perp}
. See
3.1
.)
Write
EA

for the category
of effect algebras with these homomorphisms.
A subset
D\subseteq E
is a
sub-effect algebra

of
E

if
0,1\in D
and for any
a,b\in D

with
a\perp b
, we have
a\ovee b\in D

and
a^{\perp}\in D
.

†
†
margin:

II

Examples
There are many examples of effect algebras
— we only give a few.

1.

The unit interval

[0,1]
with partial addition is an effect algebra —
i.e.:
x\perp y

whenever
x+y\leqslant 1
and then
x\ovee y=x+y
,

x^{\perp}=1-x
.

2.

Generalizing the previous:
if
G
is an ordered group
with distinguished element
1
,
then the order interval
[0,1]_{G}\equiv\{x;\ x\in G;\ 0\leqslant x\leqslant 1\}

is an effect algebra
with
x\perp y
whenever
x+y\leqslant 1
;

x\ovee y=x+y
and
x^{\perp}=1-x
.

3.

In particular,
if
\mathscr{A}
is any von Neumann algebra,
then the set of
effects

[0,1]_{\mathscr{A}}

forms an effect algebra
with
a\perp b
whenever
a+b\leqslant 1
;

a\ovee b=a+b
and
a^{\perp}=1-a
.
The ‘effect’ in effect algebra originates from this example.

4.

Any orthomodular lattice
L
(defined in
3.1
)
is an effect algebra
with the same orthocomplement,

x\perp y
whenever
x\leqslant y^{\perp}

and
x\ovee y=x\vee y
. (See e.g.
[
129
, prop. 27]
.)

5.

In particular,
any Boolean algebra
L

is an effect algebra
with complement as orthocomplement,

x\perp y
whenever
x\wedge y=0
and

x\ovee y=x\vee y
.

6.

The one-element Boolean algebra
1\equiv\{0=1\}

is the final object in
EA

and the two-element Boolean algebra
2\equiv\{0,1\}

is the initial object in
EA
.

†
†
margin:

III

Exercise
Let
E
and
F
be effect algebras.
First show that the cartesian product
E\times F

is an effect algebra with componentwise operations.
Show that this is in fact the categorical product of
E
and
F

in
EA
.
(The category
EA
is in fact complete and cocomplete;
for this and more categorical properties,
see
[
65
]
.)

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
There is a small redundancy in our definition of effect algebra:
show that the zero axiom (
x\ovee 0=x
)
follows from the remaining axioms (partial commutativity,
partial associativity, orthocomplement and zero–one.)

†
†
margin:

V

Proposition
In any effect algebra
E
with
a,b,c\in E
, we have

1.

(involution)

a^{\perp\perp}=a
;

2.

1^{\perp}=0
and
0^{\perp}=1
;

3.

(positivity)

if
a\ovee b=0
, then
a=b=0
;

4.

(cancellation)

if
a\ovee c=b\ovee c
, then
a=b
;

5.

the relation
\leqslant
(from
3.1
) partially orders
E
;

6.

a\leqslant b
if and only if
b^{\perp}\leqslant a^{\perp}
;

7.

if
a\leqslant b
and
b\perp c
, then
a\perp c

and
a\ovee c\leqslant b\ovee c

and

8.

a\perp b
if and only if
a\leqslant b^{\perp}
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
(These proofs are well-known,
see for instance
[
29
]
.)
By partial commutativity and definition
of orthocomplement both
a^{\perp}\ovee a=1

and
a^{\perp}\ovee a^{\perp\perp}=1
.
So by uniqueness of orthocomplement,
we must have
a=a^{\perp\perp}
, which is point 1.
Clearly
0\ovee 1=1
,
so
1^{\perp}=0
and
0^{\perp}=1
,
which is point 2.
For point 3, assume
a\ovee b=0
.
Then
a\ovee b\perp 1

and so by partial associativity
b\perp 1
.
By zero–one, we get
b=0
.
Similarly
a=0
, which shows point 3.
For point 4, assume
a\ovee c=b\ovee c
.
From partial associativity and commutativity, we get

((a\ovee b)^{\perp}\ovee a)\ovee c=((a\ovee b)^{\perp}\ovee a)\ovee b=1

and so by uniqueness of
orthocomplement
c=((a\ovee b)^{\perp}\ovee a)^{\perp}=b
,
which is point 4.
By
3.1
,
we only need to show that
\leqslant
is antisymmetric
for point 5.
So assume
a\leqslant b
and
b\leqslant a

for some
a,b\in E
.
Pick
c,d\in E
with
a\ovee c=b
and
b\ovee d=a
.
Then
a=(a\ovee c)\ovee d=a\ovee(c\ovee d)
.
By cancellation
c\ovee d=0
.
So by positivity
c=d=0
.
Hence
a=b
.
For point 6, assume
a\leqslant b
.
Pick
c\in E
with
a\ovee c=b
.
Clearly
a\ovee a^{\perp}=1=b\ovee b^{\perp}=a\ovee c\ovee b^{\perp}
,
so by cancellation
a^{\perp}=c\ovee b^{\perp}
,
which is to say
b^{\perp}\leqslant a^{\perp}
.
For point 7, assume
a\leqslant b
and
b\perp c
.
Pick
d
with
a\ovee d=b
.
By partial associativity and commutativity
we have
b\ovee c=(a\ovee d)\ovee c=(a\ovee c)\ovee d
,
so
a\perp c
and
a\ovee c\leqslant b\ovee c
.
For point 8, first assume
a\perp b
.
Then
a\ovee b\ovee(a\ovee b)^{\perp}=1=b\ovee b^{\perp}
.
So by cancellation
b^{\perp}=a\ovee(a\ovee b)^{\perp}
,
hence
a\leqslant b^{\perp}
.
For the converse, assume
a\leqslant b^{\perp}
.
Then
a\ovee c=b^{\perp}
for some
c
.
Hence
a\ovee c\perp b

and so by partial associativity and commutativity,
we get
a\perp b
, as desired.

\square

†
†
margin:

176

Definition
Suppose
E
is an effect algebra.
Write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}b\ominus a}

for the (by cancellation) unique element (if it exists)
with
a\ovee(b\ominus a)=b
.

†
†
margin:

II

Exercise*
Show that for any effect algebra
E
, we have

(D1)

a\ominus b
is defined if and only if
b\leqslant a
;

(D2)

a\ominus b\leqslant a
(if defined);

(D3)

a\ominus(a\ominus b)=b
(if defined)
and

(D4)

if
a\leqslant b\leqslant c
,
then
c\ominus b\leqslant c\ominus a

and
(c\ominus a)\ominus(c\ominus b)=b\ominus a
.

†
†
margin:

III

Let
E
be a poset with maximum element
1

and partial binary operation
\ominus

satisfying (D1)–(D4).
Define
a\ovee b=c\Leftrightarrow c\ominus b=a

and
a^{\perp}=1\ominus a
.
Show that this turns
E
into an effect algebra
with compatible order and
\ominus
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Remark
Such a structure
(E,\ominus,1)
is called a
difference-poset

(
D-poset
)
[
77
]
and is, as we have just seen,
an alternative way to axiomatize effect algebras.

†
†
margin:

V

Exercise
Show that for an effect algebra homomorphism
f\colon E\to F
,
we have

1.

(preserves zero)

f(0)=0
;

2.

(order preserving)
if
a\leqslant b
, then
f(a)\leqslant f(b)
;

3.

if
a\ominus b
is defined,
then
f(a\ominus b)=f(a)\ominus f(b)

and

4.

consequently
f(a^{\perp})=f(a)^{\perp}
.

†
†
margin:

177

For real numbers we have
a+b=\min\{a,b\}+\max\{a,b\}
.
The following is a generalization to effect algebras.

†
†
margin:

Ia

Proposition
Suppose
E
is an effect algebra.
If the infimum
a\wedge b

exists for some
a,b\in E
with
a\perp b
,
then their supremum
a\vee b
exists as well and

a\ovee b\ =\ (a\wedge b)\,\ovee\,(a\vee b).

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
(This result appeared in my master’s thesis
[
129
, prop. 15]
.
It turns out that it was already proven before
(in D-poset form)
[
29
, prop. 1.8.2]
.)
The result follows from this lemma:
if
(x\ominus c)\wedge(x\ominus d)
exists,
then
c\vee d
exists as well
and
(x\ominus c)\wedge(x\ominus d)=x\ominus(c\vee d)
.
Indeed:

a\wedge b\ =\ ((a\ovee b)\ominus a)\wedge((a\ovee b)\ominus b)\ =\ (a\ovee b)\ominus(a\vee b)

and so
(a\wedge b)\ovee(a\vee b)=a\ovee b
, as desired.

†
†
margin:

III

Now, we prove the lemma.
Assume
(x\ominus c)\wedge(x\ominus d)
exists.
Note
x\ominus c=(x^{\perp}\ovee c)^{\perp}
.
As
z\mapsto z^{\perp}
is an order anti-isomorphism,
we see
(x^{\perp}\ovee c)\vee(x^{\perp}\ovee d)

exists and
(x^{\perp}\ovee c)\vee(x^{\perp}\ovee d)=((x\ominus c)\wedge(x\ominus d))^{\perp}
.
Clearly
(x^{\perp}\ovee c)\vee(x^{\perp}\ovee d)\geqslant x^{\perp}
.
Write
r:=((x^{\perp}\ovee c)\vee(x^{\perp}\ovee d))\ominus x^{\perp}
.
We will show
r=c\vee d
.
It is easy to see
r\geqslant c
and
r\geqslant d
.
Assume
s
is any element with
s\geqslant c
and
s\geqslant d
.
Note
x^{\perp}\ovee s\geqslant x^{\perp}\ovee c

and
x^{\perp}\ovee s\geqslant x^{\perp}\ovee d
,
hence
x^{\perp}\ovee s\geqslant(x^{\perp}\ovee c)\vee(x^{\perp}\ovee d)

and so
s\geqslant r
.
We have shown
r=c\vee d
.
Consequently
(x\ominus c)\wedge(x\ominus d)=((x^{\perp}\ovee c)\vee(x^{\perp}\ovee d))^{\perp}=(r\ovee x^{\perp})^{\perp}=x\ominus(c\vee d)
, as promised.
\square

†
†
margin:

IV

Definition
An
ortholattice

L

is a bounded lattice with orthocomplement
— that is: it has a minimum
0
,
a maximum
1

and a unary operation
(\ )^{\perp}

satisfying

1.

a\wedge a^{\perp}=0
;

2.

a\vee a^{\perp}=1
;

3.

a\leqslant b\ \Rightarrow\ b^{\perp}\leqslant a^{\perp}
and

4.

a^{\perp\perp}=a
.

An ortholattice is
orthomodular

provided

a\ \leqslant\ b\quad\implies\quad a\vee(a^{\perp}\wedge b)\ =\ b.

See, for instance
[
74
,
29
,
11
]
.

†
†
margin:

V

Example
The lattice of projections in a von Neumann algebra
is an orthomodular lattice with
orthocomplement
p^{\perp}\equiv 1-p
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proposition
An effect algebra
E

that is an ortholattice
is also orthomodular.

†
†
margin:

VII

Proof
(For a different proof, see
[
29
, prop. 1.5.8]
.)
Assume
a\leqslant b
.
We have to show
a\vee(a^{\perp}\wedge b)=b
.
Note
a\wedge(a^{\perp}\wedge b)\leqslant a\wedge a^{\perp}=0

and so by
3.1

we have
a\vee(a^{\perp}\wedge b)=a\ovee(a^{\perp}\wedge b)
.
Thus it is sufficient to prove
a^{\perp}\wedge b=b\ominus a
.
Note
a^{\perp}=(b\ominus a)\ovee b^{\perp}

and
b=(b\ominus a)\ovee a
.
Similar to
3.1

one can show that

(b\ominus a)\ovee(b^{\perp}\wedge a)\ =\ ((b\ominus a)\ovee b^{\perp})\wedge((b\ominus a)\ovee a)\ \equiv\ a^{\perp}\ovee b,

but
b^{\perp}\wedge a\leqslant b^{\perp}\wedge b=0

and so
a^{\perp}\ovee b=b\ominus a
, as desired.

\square

3.1.1

Effect monoids

†
†
margin:

178

In
3.2.3
we will see that the scalars of an effectus
form an effect monoid:

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
An
effect monoid

M

[
56
]

is an effect algebra
together with a binary operation
\odot

such that for all
a,b,c,d\in M
, we have

1.

(unit)

1\odot a=a\odot 1=a
;

2.

(associativity)

(a\odot b)\odot c=a\odot(b\odot c)

and

3.

(distributivity)

if
a\perp b
and
c\perp d
,
then the following sum exists and
furthermore
(a\odot c)\ovee(b\odot c)\ovee(a\odot d)\ovee(b\odot d)=(a\ovee b)\odot(c\ovee d)
.

(Phrased categorically:
an effect monoid is a monoid in
EA

with the obvious tensor product
that relates bimorphisms to morphisms,
see
[
65
,
56
]
.)
An effect monoid
M
is said to be
commutative

if we have
a\odot b=b\odot a
for all
a,b\in M
.
A map
f\colon M\to N
between effect monoids
is called an
effect monoid homomorphism

if it is an effect algebra homomorphism
and furthermore
f(a\odot b)=f(a)\odot f(b)

for all
a,b\in M
.

†
†
margin:

III

Examples
Effect monoids are less abundant than effect algebras.

1.

The effect algebra
[0,1]

is a commutative effect monoid with the usual product.
(This is the only way to turn
[0,1]
into an effect monoid

[
129
, prop. 41]
.)

2.

We saw earlier that every Boolean algebra is an effect algebra
with
x\ovee y=x\vee y
defined iff
x\wedge y=0
.
The Boolean algebra is turned into an effect monoid
with
x\odot y\equiv x\wedge y
.
In fact, every finite (not necessarily commutative) effect monoid
is of this form
[
129
, prop. 40]
and thus commutative.

3.

In particular: the two-element Boolean algebra
2={0,1}

from
3.1

is an effect monoid
with
x\odot y=x\wedge y
.

4.

There is a non-commutative effect monoid
based on the lexicographically ordered vector space
\mathbb{R}^{5}
,
see
[
129
, cor. 51]
.

5.

Let
M
be an effect monoid.
Write
M^{\mathsf{op}}

for the effect monoid
M
with the opposite multiplication
— that is:
a\odot_{M}b=b\odot_{M^{\mathsf{op}}}a
.

†
†
margin:

IIIa

Exercise
Show that
a\odot 0=a=0\odot a

for any
a
in an effect monoid
M
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Later, in a tangent,
we will need the following specific fact about effect monoids.

†
†
margin:

V

Exercise
Assume
M
is an effect monoid
with
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in M

such that
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}a_{i}=1

and
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}a_{i}\odot b_{i}=1
.
Prove
a_{i}\odot b_{i}=a_{i}
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

3.1.2

Effect modules

†
†
margin:

179

We will see that in an effectus,
the effect monoid of scalars will act on
every effect algebra of predicates,
turning them into effect modules (see
3.2.3
):

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Suppose
M
is an effect monoid.
An
effect module

E
over
M

[
65
]

is an effect algebra together with an operation

M\times E\to E

denoted by
(\lambda,a)\mapsto\lambda\cdot a

such that for all
a,b\in E
and
\lambda,\mu\in M
, we have

1.

(\lambda\odot\mu)\cdot a=\lambda\cdot(\mu\cdot a)
;

2.

if
a\perp b
,
then
\lambda\odot a\perp\lambda\odot b

and
(\lambda\odot a)\ovee(\lambda\odot b)=\lambda\odot(a\ovee b)
;

3.

if
\lambda\perp\mu
,
then
\lambda\odot a\perp\mu\odot a

and
(\lambda\odot a)\ovee(\mu\odot a)=(\lambda\ovee\mu)\odot a

and

4.

1\odot a=a
.

(Categorically: an effect module over
M

is an
M
-action.)
An effect algebra homomorphism
f\colon E\to F

between effect modules over
M

is an
M
-
effect module homomorphism

provided
\lambda\cdot f(a)=f(\lambda\cdot a)

for all
\lambda\in M
and
a\in E
.
Write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\mathsf{EMod}_{M}}

for the category of effect modules over
M

with effect module homomorphisms between them.

†
†
margin:

III

Examples
There are many effect modules.

1.

Every effect algebra is an effect module over
the two-element effect monoid
2
.
(In fact
\mathsf{EA}\cong\mathsf{EMod}_{2}
.)
The only effect module up-to-isomorphism over the one-element effect monoid
1

is the one-element effect algebra
1
itself.

2.

Effect modules over
[0,1]

are the same thing as

convex effect algebras
,
see
[
48
,
43
]
.

If
V
is an ordered real vector space with order unit
u
,
then
[0,u]
is an effect module over
[0,1]
.
In fact, every effect module over
[0,1]

is of this form
[
48
]
.
See also
[
66
, thm. 3]

for the stronger categorical equivalence.

3.2

Effectuses

An effectus comes in two guises:
axiomatizing either a category of total maps
or a category of partial maps.
We will start off with the total form as it has the simplest axioms.
Later we will prefer to work with the partial form.

†
†
margin:

180

Definition
A category
C
is said to be an
effectus in total form

[
23
,
57
,
69
]

if

1.

C
has finite coproducts (hence an initial object
0
)
and a final object
1
;

2.

all diagrams of the following

form
§
§

§

We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\kappa_{i}}
for coproduct coprojections;
square brackets
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}[f,g]}
for coproduct cotupling;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}h+k}=[\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}h,\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}k]

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}!}
for the unique maps associated to
either the final object
1
or initial object
0
.
are pullbacks

\textstyle{X+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+{!}}
\scriptstyle{!+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{X+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{1+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+!}
\textstyle{1+1}

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{X+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{!}+{!}}
\textstyle{1+1}

(3.1)

3.

and the following two arrows are jointly monic.

\textstyle{1+1+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{2}]}
\scriptstyle{[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}]}
\textstyle{1+1}

An arrow
f\colon X\to Y+1
is called a
partial map
and written

f\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}X\rightharpoonup Y}
.

†
†
margin:

II

One with an interest in physics might think of the objects
of an effectus as physical systems and its arrows as
the physical operations between them.
The final object
1
is the physical system with a single state.
The coproduct
X+Y
is the system that can be prepared as either
X
or as
Y
.
An arrow
1\to X
corresponds to a physical preparation of the system
X

and an arrow
X\to 1+1
is a yes–no measurement.

†
†
margin:

III

In this sense an effectus can be seen as a generalized
probabilistic theory
— not unlike the operational-probabilistic theories (OPT)
of Chiribella et al
[
17
]
.
There are differences:
for instance, OPTs are equipped with a parallel composition of systems,
effectuses are not and OPTs always have the unit interval as scalars,
effectuses might not.
In
[
113
]
Tull compares effectuses and OPTs.
There are other approaches to categorical probabilistic theories;
see, for instance
[
131
,
40
]
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Studying programming languages, one would do better thinking of
the objects of an effectus as data types and
its arrows as the allowed operations between them (semantics of programs).
The final object
1
is the unit data type.
The coproduct
X+Y
is the union data type of
X
and
Y
.
An arrow
1\to X
is a value of
X

and an arrow
X\to 1+1
is a predicate on
X
.

†
†
margin:

V

Our main example of an effectus in total form
is the category
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}

of von Neumann algebras with completely positive normal unital
maps in the opposite direction.
(To see
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
is an effectus in total form,
adapt the proof of
3.2.3
.)
The partial maps correspond to
ncp-maps
f
with
f(1)\leqslant 1
.
(Equivalently: contractive ncp-maps.)
Some other examples appear later on in

3.2.3
,
3.2.3
,

3.2.4
and
3.2.2
.
For a comprehensive list of examples, see
[
23
]
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Let
C
be an effectus in total form.
Given two arrows
f\colon X\to Y+1

and
g\colon Y\to Z+1
(i.e. partial maps
X\rightharpoonup Y
and
Y\rightharpoonup Z
)
their composition as partial maps
is defined as
g\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f\equiv[g,\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}f
.
Write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\Par C}
for the
category of partial maps
,

which has the same objects
as
C
, but as arrows
X\to Y
in
\Par C

we take arrows of the form
X\to Y+1
in
C
,
which we compose using
\mathop{\hat{\circ}}

and with identity on
X
in
\Par C

given by
\kappa_{1}\colon X\to X+1
.
¶
¶

¶

In categorical
parlance:
\Par C
is the Kleisli category of
the monad
(\ )+1\colon C\to C
.

The category
\Par C
is not an effectus in total form — instead it
is an effectus
in partial form
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Definition
A category
C
is called an
effectus in partial form

[
23
,
19
]
if

1.

C
is a finPAC
∥
∥

∥

Finitarily partially additive category.

[
19
]
(cf.
[
5
]
) — that is:

(a)

C
has finite coproducts
(+,0)
;

(b)

C
is
\mathsf{PCM}
-enriched — that is:

i.

every homset
C(X,Y)
has a partial binary operation
\ovee

and distinguished element
0\in C(X,Y)

that turns
C(X,Y)
into a partial commutative monoid,
see
3.1
;

ii.

if
f\perp g
then both

(h\mathop{\circ}f)\perp(h\mathop{\circ}g)
and

(f\mathop{\circ}k)\perp(g\mathop{\circ}k)

and

h\mathop{\circ}(f\ovee g)=(h\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(h\ovee g)\qquad(f\ovee g)\mathop{\circ}k=(f\mathop{\circ}k)\ovee(g\mathop{\circ}k)

for any
f,g\colon X\to Y
,

h\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}

and
k\colon X^{\prime}\to X

and

iii.

(zero)

0\mathop{\circ}f=0
and
f\mathop{\circ}0=0
for
any
f\colon X\to Y

and

(c)

(compatible sum)

for any
b\colon X\to Y+Y
we have

\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}b\perp\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}b
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{i}}\colon Y+Y\to Y

are
partial projectors
**
**

**

Later, in
3.2.1
,
we will use the more slightly more general

{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}}\colon X_{1}+X_{2}\to X_{1}
and

{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}}\colon X_{1}+X_{2}\to X_{2}

defined by
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}=[\mathrm{id},0]

and
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}=[0,\mathrm{id}]
.
defined

by
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\equiv[\mathrm{id},0]

and
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\equiv[0,\mathrm{id}]

and

(d)

(untying)
if
f\perp g
,
then
\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f\perp\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g
,
where
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
are coprojections
on the same coproduct

and

2.

it has effects — that is: there is a distinguished object
I

such that

(a)

for each object
X
, the PCM
C(X,I)\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\Pred X}

is an effect algebra, see
3.1

— in particular
C(X,I)

has a maximum element
1
;

(b)

if
1\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}g
,
then
f\perp g

and

(c)

if
1\mathop{\circ}f=0
, then
f=0
.

In this setting,
a map
f\colon X\to Y
is called
total
if
1\mathop{\circ}f=1
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Remark
The untying and zero axioms are redundant: they follows from the others.
We include them, as they are part of the definition of finPAC.

†
†
margin:

IX

At first glance an effectus in partial form seems
to have a much richer structure than an effectus in total form.
This is not the case —
effectuses in total and partial form are two views
on the same thing
[
19
,
23
]
:

†
†
margin:

X

Theorem (Cho)
Let
C
be an effectus in total form
and
D
be an effectus in partial form.

1.

The category
\Par C
is an effectus in partial form
with
I=1
.

2.

The total maps of
D
form an effectus in total form
\Tot D
.

3.

Nothing is lost:

\Par(\Tot D)\cong D
and
\Tot(\Par C)\cong C
.

†
†
margin:

XI

(The result can be rephrased categorically as
a 2-equivalence of the 2-category
of effectuses in partial form and
the 2-category of effectuses in total form.
For the details, see
[
19
, §5]
.)
To prove the Theorem
(in
3.2.2
),
we need some preparation.

3.2.1

From partial to total

†
†
margin:

181

We will first show that the subcategory of
total maps of an effectus in partial form
is an effectus in total form.
This proof and especially the demonstration
that the squares in (
3.1
) are pullbacks,
will elucidate the axioms of an effectus in total form
and will make the proof in the opposite direction more palatable.

†
†
margin:

II

Lemma
In an effectus in partial form,
coprojections are total.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
(For the original and different proof see
[
19
, lem. 4.7(5)]
.)
Let
\kappa_{1}\colon X\to X+Y
be any coprojection.
We have to show
1\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}=1
.
Note that the first
1
denotes the maximum of
\Pred X+Y

and the second the maximum of
\Pred X
.
Hence
1_{X}=1_{X}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}_{X}=(1_{X}\mathop{\circ}[\mathrm{id}_{X},0])\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\leqslant 1_{X+Y}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\leqslant 1_{X}
.
Indeed
1\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}=1
.
\square

†
†
margin:

IV

Proposition
In an effectus in partial form,
the cotupling bijection
(f,g)\mapsto[f,g]

is a PCM-isomorphism
[
23
]
—
that is:

1.

[f,g]\perp[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}]
if and only if

f\perp f^{\prime}
and
g\perp g^{\prime}
;

2.

if
[f,g]\perp[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}]
,
then
[f,g]\ovee[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}]=[f\ovee f^{\prime},g\ovee g^{\prime}]

and

3.

[0,0]=0
.

Furthermore
[1,1]=1
for maps into
I
,
so the cotupling map is an effect algebra
isomorphism
\Pred(X)\times\Pred(Y)\cong\Pred(X+Y)
.

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
First we show
[h,l]=[h,0]\ovee[0,l]
.
By the compatible sum axiom

[h,0]\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}(h+l)\ \perp\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}(h+l)\ =\ [0,l].

By
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichment

([h,0]\ovee[0,l])\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}=([h,0]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1})\ovee([0,l]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1})=h
.
Similarly
([h,0]\ovee[0,l])\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}=l
.
Thus indeed
[h,l]=[h,0]\ovee[0,l]
.

Assume
[f,g]\perp[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}]
.
By
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichment
we have
f=[f,g]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\perp[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}=f^{\prime}
.
Similarly
g\perp g^{\prime}
.
Conversely, assume
f\perp f^{\prime}
and
g\perp g^{\prime}
.
Again, by
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichment
[f,0]=f\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\perp f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}=[f^{\prime},0]

and
[f\ovee f^{\prime},0]=[f,0]\ovee[f^{\prime},0]
.
Similarly
[0,g\ovee g^{\prime}]=[0,g]\ovee[0,g^{\prime}]
.
Putting it all together:

\displaystyle[f,g]\ovee[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\ =\ [f,0]\ovee[0,g]\ovee[f^{\prime},0]\ovee[0,g^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\ =\ [f\ovee f^{\prime},0]\ovee[0,g\ovee g^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\ =\ [f\ovee f^{\prime},g\ovee g^{\prime}].

To show cotupling is a PCM-isomorphism,
it only remains to be shown that
[0,0]=0
.
As
0\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}=0

and
0\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}=0
,
we indeed have
0=[0,0]
.
Similarly by
3.2.1
we have
1\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}=1

and
1\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}=1
,
so
1=[1,1]
.
\square

†
†
margin:

VI

The coproduct in an effectus in partial form
is almost a (bi)product:

†
†
margin:

VII

Proposition
In an effectus in partial form, we have a bijective correspondence

h\colon Z\to X+Y

f\colon Z\to X\quad g\colon Z\to Y\quad 1\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}g

as follows
[
19
, lem. 4.8]
:

for every
f\colon Z\to X

and
g\colon Z\to Y

with
1\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}g
,
there is a unique map
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\langle f,g\rangle}\colon Z\to X+Y

such that
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=f

and
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=g
,
where
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}=[\mathrm{id},0]
and
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}=[0,\mathrm{id}]
.
In fact
\langle f,g\rangle=(\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g)
.

Conversely
h=\langle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h,\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h\rangle

with
1\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h\perp 1\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h

for
h\colon Z\to X+Y
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Proof
Let
f\colon Z\to X
and
g\colon Z\to Y
be given
such that
1\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}g
.
By
3.2.1
,
we have
1\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}g=1\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g
.
Thus
\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f\perp\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g
.
Define
\langle f,g\rangle=(\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g)
.
By the
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichedness, we have

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ [\mathrm{id},0]\mathop{\circ}((\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g))

\displaystyle\ =\ ([\mathrm{id},0]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee([\mathrm{id},0]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g)

\displaystyle\ =\ f\ovee(0\mathop{\circ}g)\ =\ f.

Similarly
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=g
.
To show uniqueness, assume
f=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h

and
g=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h
for some

h\colon Z\to X+Y
.
Note
\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}=[\kappa_{1},0]

and
\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}=[0,\kappa_{2}]
,
so by
3.2.1
we have
(\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1})\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2})=[\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}]=\mathrm{id}
,
hence

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h,\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ (\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h)

\displaystyle\ =\ ((\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1})\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}))\mathop{\circ}h

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}h\ =\ h,

which demonstrates uniqueness.

Finally, let
h\colon Z\to X+Y
be any map.
We must show that
h=\langle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h,\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h\rangle
.
Note
1\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}=[1,0]

and
1\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}=[0,1]
.
So by
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichment
1\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h\perp 1\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h
and so
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h\perp\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h
.
By the previous

\langle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h,\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h\rangle=(\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}h)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}h)=h
as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

IX

Exercise
Show that in an effectus in partial form, we have

1.

[a,b]\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=(a\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(b\mathop{\circ}g)
;

2.

1\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=(1\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(1\mathop{\circ}g)
;

3.

(k+l)\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=\langle k\mathop{\circ}f,l\mathop{\circ}g\rangle

and

4.

\langle f,g\rangle\mathop{\circ}k=\langle f\mathop{\circ}k,g\mathop{\circ}k\rangle

assuming
1\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}g
.
[
23
]

†
†
margin:

X

Remark
In an effectus in partial form,
there is a straightforward generalization
of the bijective correspondence
3.2.1
to

h\colon Z\to X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n}

f_{1}\colon Z\to X_{1}\ \cdots\ f_{n}\colon Z\to X_{n}\quad\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2.1}{$\displaystyle\perp$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\perp$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}}}\displaylimits_{n}1\mathop{\circ}f_{n}

with
f_{i}=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{i}\mathop{\circ}h

and
h=\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\rangle\equiv(\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f_{1})\ovee\cdots\ovee(\kappa_{n}\mathop{\circ}f_{n})
,
where
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{i}
is defined in the obvious way.
The expected generalizations of the rules in
3.2.1
also hold.

†
†
margin:

XI

Theorem
Let
C
be an effectus in partial form.
The category of total maps of
C
is an effectus in total form.

[
19
, thm. 4.10]

†
†
margin:

XII

Proof
The total maps indeed form a subcategory:
1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=1

and
1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}g=1\mathop{\circ}g=1
for composable total
f,g
.
In
3.2.1
we saw coprojections are total.
By
3.2.1

we have
1\mathop{\circ}[f,g]=[1\mathop{\circ}f,1\mathop{\circ}g]=[1,1]=1

for total
f\colon X\to Z
and
g\colon Y\to Z
,
so
\Tot C
has binary coproducts.
The unique map
!\colon 0\to X
must be total
as
1\mathop{\circ}!=1
is the unique map
0\to I
,
so
\Tot C
has initial object
0
, hence all finite coproducts.

†
†
margin:

XIII

To show
I
is the final object of
\Tot C
,
we need
\mathrm{id}_{I}=1
.
As
C(I,I)
is an effect algebra
1=\mathrm{id}\ovee\mathrm{id}^{\perp}

for some
\mathrm{id}^{\perp}
.
So by
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichment
1=1\mathop{\circ}1=(1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id})\ovee(1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}^{\perp})=1\ovee(1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}^{\perp})
.
By the zero–one axiom
1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}^{\perp}=0
.
So
\mathrm{id}^{\perp}=0
and indeed
\mathrm{id}=1
.
To show
I
is final in
\Tot C
, pick any object
X
in
\Tot C
.
We claim
1\colon X\to I
is the unique total map.
Indeed, by the previous
1=\mathrm{id}_{I}\mathop{\circ}1=1\mathop{\circ}1
, so
1
is total
and if
h\colon X\to I
is total, then
1=1\mathop{\circ}h=\mathrm{id}_{I}\mathop{\circ}h=h
.

†
†
margin:

XIV

To show the square on the left of (
3.1
) is a pullback
in
\Tot C
,
let
f\colon Z\to X+I

and
g\colon Z\to I+Y
be total maps
with
(\mathrm{id}+1)\mathop{\circ}g=(1+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\circ}f
.
By
3.2.1
,
f=\langle\alpha,a\rangle

and
g=\langle b,\beta\rangle

for some maps
\alpha\colon Z\to X
,
\beta\colon Z\to Y

and
a,b\colon Z\to I
.

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\alpha,a\rangle\equiv f}
\scriptstyle{g\equiv\langle b,\beta\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}
\textstyle{X+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+1}
\scriptstyle{1+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{X+I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{I+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+1}
\textstyle{I+I}

By
3.2.1
,
we have
1=1\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}\langle\alpha,a\rangle=(1\mathop{\circ}\alpha)\ovee a

and so
a^{\perp}=1\mathop{\circ}\alpha
.
Similarly
b^{\perp}=1\mathop{\circ}\beta
.
Again, using
3.2.1
,
we see that

\langle b,1\mathop{\circ}\beta\rangle\ =\ (\mathrm{id}+1)\mathop{\circ}\langle b,\beta\rangle\ =\ (\mathrm{id}+1)\mathop{\circ}g\ =\ (1+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\circ}f\ =\ \langle 1\mathop{\circ}\alpha,a\rangle,

so
1\mathop{\circ}\beta=a=(1\mathop{\circ}\alpha)^{\perp}
,
hence
1\mathop{\circ}\beta\perp 1\mathop{\circ}\alpha
,
so
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\colon Z\to X+Y
exists
and is total as
1\mathop{\circ}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=(1\mathop{\circ}\alpha)\ovee(1\mathop{\circ}\beta)=1
.
We compute
(\mathrm{id}+1)\mathop{\circ}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\langle\alpha,1\mathop{\circ}\beta\rangle=\langle\alpha,a\rangle=f
.
Similarly
(1+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\circ}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=g
.
Assume
h\colon Z\to X+Y
is any map with
(1+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\circ}h=g
and
(\mathrm{id}+1)\mathop{\circ}h=f
.
Say
h=\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle
.
Then
\langle\alpha,a\rangle=f=(\mathrm{id}+1)\mathop{\circ}h=\langle h_{1},1\mathop{\circ}h_{2}\rangle
, so
\alpha=h_{1}
. Similarly
\beta=h_{2}
.
Thus
h=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
,
which shows our square is indeed a pullback.

†
†
margin:

XV

To show the square on the right of (
3.1
) is a pullback
in
\Tot C
,
assume (using
3.2.1
)

\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\colon Z\to X+Y

is some total map
with
(1+1)\mathop{\circ}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}1
.

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{X+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1+1}
\textstyle{I+I}

With
3.2.1
,
we see
\langle 1\mathop{\circ}\alpha,1\mathop{\circ}\beta\rangle=(1+1)\mathop{\circ}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}1=\langle 1,0\rangle
.
So
\alpha
is total and
\beta=0
.
Hence
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=\langle\alpha,0\rangle=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha
as desired.

†
†
margin:

XVI

Finally, to show
m_{1}\equiv[\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{2}],m_{2}\equiv[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}]\colon I+I+I\to I+I
are jointly monic,
let
f_{1}\equiv\langle a_{1},b_{1},c_{1}\rangle,f_{2}\equiv\langle a_{2},b_{2},c_{2}\rangle\colon X\to I+I+I
be any total maps
with
m_{1}\mathop{\circ}f_{1}=m_{1}\mathop{\circ}f_{2}

and
m_{2}\mathop{\circ}f_{1}=m_{2}\mathop{\circ}f_{2}
.
Then

a_{1}\ =\ [\mathrm{id},0,0]\mathop{\circ}f_{1}\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}m_{1}\mathop{\circ}f_{1}\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}m_{1}\mathop{\circ}f_{2}\ =\ a_{2}.

and similarly from the equality involving
m_{2}
, we get
b_{1}=b_{2}
.
As
f_{1}
is total, we have
1=1\mathop{\circ}f_{1}=a_{1}\ovee b_{1}\ovee c_{1}
,
so
c_{1}=(a_{1}\ovee b_{1})^{\perp}
.
With the same reasoning
c_{2}=(a_{2}\ovee b_{2})^{\perp}
.
Thus
c_{1}=c_{2}
and so
f_{1}=f_{2}
, as desired.
\square

3.2.2

From total to partial

†
†
margin:

182

Let
C
be an effectus in total form.
In this section we will show that
\Par C
is an effectus in partial form.
Before we get to work, it is helpful to discuss the axioms
of an effectus in total form, now we have some experience
with an effectus in partial form.

1.

In
3.2.1
we saw that the coproduct in an effectus
(in partial form) is almost a biproduct.
This structure is hidden (for the most part)
in the left pullback square of
(
3.1
), which
allows the formation of
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
given
partial maps
\alpha,\beta
.

2.

The right pullback square of (
3.1
)
is used to extract a total map in
C

from a partial map
f
in
\Par C
that is total (i.e.
1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f=1
).
See
3.2.2
.

3.

The joint-monicity
of
[\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{2}]

and
[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}]

will imply the joint monicity of
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}
and
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}
,
which is required for uniqueness of the partial sum of partial maps.
See
3.2.2
.

To prove the theorem, we need to study pullbacks:
first in any category,
then in an effectus
C
in total form
and finally in
\Par C
.
Our proof is different from the original proof of Cho
by using several more general facts about pullbacks,
which we will prove next.

†
†
margin:

183

We start with two classic facts about pullbacks.

†
†
margin:

II

Exercise
Show that if we have any pullback square — say

\textstyle{{P\ }\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{m_{2}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{A\ }\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X}

,

then
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are jointly monic.

†
†
margin:

III

Exercise
Prove the
pullback lemma
--- that is:

if we have a commuting diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{l}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z}

,

then we have the following two implications.

1.

If the left and right inner squares are pullbacks,
then so is the outer square.

2.

If the outer square is a pullback
and
l
and
g
are jointly monic,
then the left inner square is a pullback.

†
†
margin:

IV

Remark
In the literature (e.g.
[
87
, III.5 exc. 8]
),
one often finds a weaker second implication,
which assumes that the right square is a pullback.

†
†
margin:

184

where we used
to denote a monic map.
We will need an analogous result for jointly monic maps.

†
†
margin:

II

Lemma
If the pairs
(m_{1},m_{2})
,
(n_{1},g_{1})
,
(n_{2},g_{2})
and
(h_{1},h_{2})

in the following commuting diagram
are jointly monic (for instance: if they span pullback squares),
then
(n_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1},n_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2})
is jointly monic as well.

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{h_{2}}
\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{n_{2}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{m_{2}}
\textstyle{B_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{B_{2}}

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
To show joint monicity of
n_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}
and
n_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}
,
assume
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\colon Z\to P

are maps with
n_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}=n_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}

and
n_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}=n_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}
.
To start

m_{1}\mathop{\circ}g_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}\ =\ f_{1}\mathop{\circ}n_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}\ =\ f_{1}\mathop{\circ}n_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}\ =\ m_{1}\mathop{\circ}g_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}.

Reasoning on the other side of the diagram, we find

\displaystyle m_{2}\mathop{\circ}g_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ m_{2}\mathop{\circ}g_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ f_{2}\mathop{\circ}n_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ f_{2}\mathop{\circ}n_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ m_{2}\mathop{\circ}g_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ m_{2}\mathop{\circ}g_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}.

So by joint monicity of
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
,
we conclude
g_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}=g_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}
.
So by the joint monicity of
n_{1}
and
g_{1}

(and by assumption
n_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}=n_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}
),
we conclude
h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}=h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}
.
Reasoning in the same way mirrored over the diagonal,
we find
h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{1}=h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha_{2}
.
Thus, by joint monicity of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, we conclude that
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
, as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

185

Proposition
In an effectus in total form,
any square of one of the two following forms is a pullback.

\textstyle{X+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+f}
\scriptstyle{g+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{X+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{Y+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+f}
\textstyle{Y+B}

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{X+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f+g}
\textstyle{Y+B}

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
To start with the left square, consider the following commuting diagram.

\textstyle{X+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+f}
\scriptstyle{g+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{X+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(2)}
\scriptstyle{g+\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+!}
\textstyle{X+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{Y+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(3)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+f}
\scriptstyle{!+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{Y+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(4)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+!}
\scriptstyle{!+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{Y+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{1+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+f}
\textstyle{1+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+!}
\textstyle{1+1}

We want to show (1) is a pullback.
The inner square (4) and right rectangle (2,4) are pullbacks by axiom.
Thus the inner square (2) is also a pullback
by the pullback lemma, see
3.2.2
.
With the same reasoning, we see square (3) is a pullback.
Thus by the pullback lemma, it is sufficient to show that
left rectangle (1,3) is a pullback.
The left rectangle is indeed a pullback
as both the outer square (1,2,3,4)
and the right rectangle (2,4) are pullbacks.

For the right square, we consider the following diagram.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{X+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f+g}
\textstyle{Y+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!+!}
\textstyle{1+1}

The inner right and outer square are pullbacks by axiom.
So the inner left square is also a pullback by the pullback lemma, as desired.

\square

†
†

186

Definition
Assume
C
is an effectus in total form.
For a map
f\colon X\to Y
,
write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\hat{f}}=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f\colon X\to Y+1
.

(This is the Kleisli embedding
C\to\Par C
.)
[
19
]

†
†
margin:

Ia

(Recall that we use
f\colon X\rightharpoonup Y

to denote a map
f\colon X\to Y+1
,
see
3.2
.)

†
†
margin:

II

Exercise
Assume
C
is an effectus in total form.
Show that if

\kappa_{1}\colon X\to X+Y\leftarrow Y\colon\kappa_{2}

is a coproduct in
C
, then

\hat{\kappa}_{1}\colon X\rightharpoonup X+Y\leftharpoonup Y\colon\hat{\kappa}_{2}

is a coproduct in
\Par C
.
Show
0
is also the initial object of
\Par C
.

†
†
margin:

III

Beware
While cotupling in
C
and
\Par C
coincide:

[f,g]_{C}=[f,g]_{\Par C}

for any (properly typed) partial maps
f,g

and
\widehat{f+_{C}g}=\hat{f}+_{\Par C}\hat{g}
,
however, in general

[\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f,\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g]\ =\ f+_{C}g\ \neq\ f+_{\Par C}g\ =\ [[\kappa_{1},\kappa_{3}]\mathop{\circ}f,[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}]\mathop{\circ}g].

†
†
margin:

IV

Proposition
Let
C
be an effectus in total form.
Squares of the following form are pullbacks in
\Par C
.

\textstyle{X+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+\hat{f}}
\scriptstyle{\hat{g}+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{X+B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{g}+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{Y+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{Y+B}

\textstyle{A+X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{B}

, lem. 4.1(5)]

\textstyle{X+(A+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{g+\mathrm{id}}

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+!}
\textstyle{A+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

These are indeed pullbacks by
3.2.2
.

VIII

Proposition
Assume

and

\Par C
.

,

for a unique

in
C
.

If
1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f=0
, then
f=0
.

†

margin:

IX

Proof
(This proof is essentially
the same as
[
19
, lem. 4.7(1) & prop. 4.6]
.)
Both points follow from the right pullback square of (
3.1
)
as follows.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{Y+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!+!}
\textstyle{1+1}

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{2}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{2}}
\textstyle{Y+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!+!}
\textstyle{1+1}

If
1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f=1
, then
(!+!)\mathop{\circ}f=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}!

and so there is a unique
g
with
f=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}g

as shown above on the left.
For the other point, if

,

and so

as shown above on the right.

Proposition
Assume
C

\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\equiv[\mathrm{id},0]

\Par C
.

XI

Proof
(This is a new proof.)
Consider the following diagram in
\Par C
.

\textstyle{X+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+\hat{!}}
\scriptstyle{\hat{!}+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{X+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+\hat{!}}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}}

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{!}}

This diagram commutes and

.
The maps
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1},\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\colon 1+1\rightharpoonup 1

are jointly monic,
which is a reformulation

\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}
