187

Theorem
If
C
is an effectus in total form,
then
\Par C
is an effectus in partial form.
[
19
, thm. 4.2]

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
In
3.2.2
, we saw that
\Par C
has finite coproducts.

†
†
margin:

III

(
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichment, I)
Assume
f,g\colon X\rightharpoonup Y
.
We define that
f\perp g

iff there is a
bound

b\colon X\rightharpoonup Y+Y

with
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=f

and
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=g
.
(By
3.2.2

this
b
is unique if it exists.)
In this case, define
f\ovee g=\nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\nabla}\equiv[\mathrm{id},\mathrm{id}]
.

We will show
\ovee
with
0

as defined in
3.2.2

forms a PCM.
Partial commutativity is obvious.
The map
\kappa_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f\colon X\rightharpoonup Y+Y

is a bound for
f\perp 0
and
so
f\ovee 0=\nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f=f
,
which shows
0

is indeed a zero for
\ovee
.
Only partial associativity remains.
Assume
f\perp g
via bound
b

and
f\ovee g\perp h
via bound
c
.
Note
\nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=f\ovee g=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}c

and thus by the right pullback square of
3.2.2
(see diagram below),
there is a unique
d\colon X\rightharpoonup(Y+Y)+Y

with
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d=b
and
(\nabla+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d=c
.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{(Y+Y)+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla+\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}}
\textstyle{Y+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}}
\textstyle{Y+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{Y}

The map
(\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

is a bound for
g\perp h
,
indeed:

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(\nabla+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\ =\ g

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}c

\displaystyle\ =\ h.

We need one more:

[\mathrm{id},\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d
is a bound for
f\perp g\ovee h
, indeed

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}[\mathrm{id},\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}[\mathrm{id},\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\ =\ \nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b

\displaystyle\ =\ g\ovee h

\displaystyle\ =\ f.

Finally,
we compute

f\ovee(g\ovee h)\ =\ \nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}[\mathrm{id},\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d\ =\ \nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(\nabla+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d\ =\ \nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}c\ =\ (f\ovee g)\ovee h,

which shows the partial associativity.
Homsets of
\Par C
are indeed PCMs.

†
†
margin:

IV

(
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichment, II)
Assume
f\perp g
with bound
b
.
It is easy to see
b\mathop{\hat{\circ}}k

is a bound for
f\mathop{\hat{\circ}}k\perp g\mathop{\hat{\circ}}k

and consequently
(f\mathop{\hat{\circ}}k)\ovee(g\mathop{\hat{\circ}}k)=\nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b\mathop{\hat{\circ}}k=(f\ovee g)\mathop{\hat{\circ}}k
.
For the other side:

(h+h)\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b
is a bound for
h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f\perp h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}g
,
indeed

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(h+h)\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b

\displaystyle\ =\ h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b\ =\ h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(h+h)\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b

\displaystyle\ =\ h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b\ =\ h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}g.

And so
(h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f)\ovee(h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}g)=\nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(h+h)\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(f\ovee g)
.

Finally, the zero axiom holds by
3.2.2
.

†
†
margin:

V

(FinPAC)
We just saw
\Par C
is
\mathsf{PCM}
-enriched.
We already know
\Par C
has coproducts.
The compatible sum axiom holds by definition of
\perp
.
To show
\Par C
is a finPAC,
only the untying axiom remains to be proven.
If
f\perp g
with bound
b
,
then
(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b
is a bound for
\kappa_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f\perp\kappa_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}g
,
which proves the untying axiom.

†
†
margin:

VI

(Effect algebra of predicates)
Pick any predicate
p\colon X\to 1+1
.
We define
p^{\perp}=[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1}]\mathop{\circ}p
.
(
p^{\perp}
is
p
with swapped outcomes.)
We compute

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{p}

\displaystyle\ =\ [\mathrm{id},\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}p\ =\ p

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{p}

\displaystyle\ =\ [[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1}],\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}p\ =\ [\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1}]\mathop{\circ}p

\displaystyle\nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{p}

\displaystyle\ =\ [[\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}],\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}p\ =\ \kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}!\ =\ 1.

So
\hat{p}
is a bound for
p\perp p^{\perp}

and
p\ovee p^{\perp}=1
.
To show
p^{\perp}
is the unique orthocomplement,
assume
p\ovee q=1
for some
q
via a bound
b\colon X\rightharpoonup 1+1
.
Note
1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=\nabla\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=1

and so by
3.2.2

we know
b=\hat{c}
for some
c\colon X\to 1+1
.
And so
p=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=[\mathrm{id},\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}c=c
. Hence

q\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b\ =\ [[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1}],\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}c\ =\ [\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1}]\mathop{\circ}p\ =\ p^{\perp}.

To show
\Pred X
is an effect algebra,
it only remains to be proven that the zero–one axiom holds.
So assume
1\perp p
via some bound
b
. We must show
p=0
.
By the following instance of the right pullback square of (
3.1
),
we see
b=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}!
.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{(1+1)+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\mathrm{id},\kappa_{2}]}
\textstyle{1+1}

Hence
p=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=[[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1}],\kappa_{1}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}!=\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}!=0
.

†
†
margin:

VII

(Final axioms)
In
3.2.2
we proved that
f=0
whenever
1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f=0
.
It only remains to be shown that
f\perp g

provided
1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f\perp 1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}g
.
Let
b
be a bound for
1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f\perp 1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}g
.
We apply the right pullback square of
3.2.2

twice in succession as follows
(using
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}c=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(\hat{!}+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}c=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}b=1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}g
for the right one.)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{!}+\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}}
\textstyle{1+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{!}}
\textstyle{1}

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+\hat{!}}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}}
\textstyle{Y+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{!}}
\textstyle{1}

Clearly
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d=g

and
\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}(\mathrm{id}+\hat{!})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}d=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}c=f
, so
d
is a bound for
f\perp g
,
as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

188

We are ready to show the equivalence of effectuses in partial and total form.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof of
3.2
Let
C
and
D
be effectuses in total and respectively partial form.
The first point, that
\Par C
is an effectus in partial form,
is shown in
3.2.2
.
The second point, that
\Tot D
is an effectus in total form,
is shown in
3.2.1
.
It remains to be shown that nothing is lost.

†
†
margin:

III

(
\Par\Tot D\cong D
)
Let
D
be an effectus in partial form.
A map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Par\Tot D

is by definition a map
f\colon X\to Y+1

in
D
with
1\mathop{\circ}f=1
.
Let
P\colon\Par\Tot D\to D

denote the identity-on-objects map
f\mapsto\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f
.
This is a functor: clearly
P\hat{\mathrm{id}}=P\kappa_{1}=\mathrm{id}
and
if
g\colon Y\to Z
in
\Par\Tot D
, then

\displaystyle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}(g\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}[g,\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}f

\displaystyle\ =\ [\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}g,\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}f

\displaystyle\ =\ [\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}g,0]\mathop{\circ}\langle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f,\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}f\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}g\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f.

The functor
P
is an isomorphism with inverse
P^{\prime}f\equiv\langle f,(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\rangle
.
Indeed, we have

PP^{\prime}f=\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\langle f,(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\rangle=f

and

P^{\prime}Pf\ =\ \langle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f,(1\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\rangle\ =\ (\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\ =\ f.

†
†
margin:

IV

(
\Tot\Par C\cong C
)
Assume
C
is an effectus in total form.
Recall
1\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{g}=1

and
\smash{\widehat{f\mathop{\circ}g}}=\hat{f}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{g}
,
so
Q\colon C\to\Tot\Par C

given by
Qg=\hat{g}
is an identity-on-objects functor.
It is an isomorphism by the first part of
3.2.2
:
for every
f
in
\Tot\Par C
,
there is a unique
g
in
C

with
f=\hat{g}
.
\square

†
†
margin:

189

Exercise
In this exercise we will
distinguish effectuses in total and partial form.

1.

To start, show that the initial object of an effectus in total form is strict
— that is: show that any map into
0
is an isomorphism.
(Hint: use the right pullback square of
3.2.2

with
X=Y=0
.)

2.

Conclude that if
C
is both an effectus in total and partial form,
then every object in
C
is isomorphic to
0
.

†
†
margin:

II

Convention
For the remainder of this text, we will work with effectuses in partial form
and write
1
instead of
I
.
To spare ink, a phrase like “let
C
be an
effectus
’’

should be read as “let
C
be an effectus in partial form”
and simply “
C
is an effectus” means either “
C
is an effectus in total form”
or “
C
is an effectus in partial form”.
(In non-trivial cases, this
is unambiguous, as seen in
3.2.2
.)
As before, when we took the effectus in partial form as base category,
we will denote partial maps by
f\colon X\to Y

(instead of
f\colon X\rightharpoonup Y
)
and their composition as
f\mathop{\circ}g

(instead of
f\mathop{\hat{\circ}}g
).

†
†
margin:

189a

Examples
Our main example of an effectus (in total form)
is the category
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
of von Neumann algebras with
ncpu-maps between them in the opposite direction,
see
3.2
.
Its full subcategory
{\mathsf{CvN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
of commutative von Neumann algebras
is an effectus as well.

†
†
margin:

II

In
[
23
]
several other examples of effectuses are
discussed in detail:

1.

The category
{\mathsf{OUS}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
of order unit spaces
(i.e. ordered real vector spaces with distinguished order unit)
with positive unit-preserving linear maps in the opposite
direction is an effectus (in total form).

2.

A related example is the category
{\mathsf{OUG}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
of order unit groups
(i.e. ordered abelian groups with distinguished order unit)
with positive
unit-preserving homomorphisms in the opposite direction,
which is also an effectus.

3.

Every extensive category
[
15
]

with final object is an effectus in total form.
Examples of extensive categories with final object
include

(a)

\mathsf{Set}
, the category
of sets with maps between them;

(b)

{\mathsf{CRng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
, the category
of commutative unit rings with unit-preserving
homomorphisms between them
in the opposite direction;

(c)

\mathsf{CH}
, the category of
compact Hausdorff spaces with continuous maps
between them;
and

†
†
margin:

III

Together with van de Wetering, we recently investigated
[
128
]

the category
{\mathsf{EJA}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
of Euclidean Jordan algebras
with positive unit-preserving linear maps
in the opposite direction, which is also an effectus (in total form).

3.2.3

Predicates, states and scalars

†
†
margin:

190

We turn to the internal structures of an effectus.
[
23
]

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
C
be an effectus (in partial form).

1.

As alluded to in the definition of an effectus in partial form,
a
predicate
on
X
is a map
X\to 1
.

The set of predicates
\Pred X
on
X
is (by definition of an effectus
in partial form) an effect algebra.

2.

A
scalar
is a predicate on
1
; that is, a map
1\to 1
.

We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\Scal C}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}M}\equiv\Pred 1
for the set of scalars.

We multiply two scalars
\lambda,\mu\colon 1\to 1

simply by composing
\lambda\odot\mu\equiv\lambda\mathop{\circ}\mu
.
As
1_{M}=\mathrm{id}_{M}
(see
3.2.1
) and
C
is
\mathsf{PCM}
-enriched,
the set of scalars
M
with
\odot

is an effect monoid,
see
3.1.1
.

3.

A
real effectus
is an effectus

where the set of scalars
M
is isomorphic (as effect monoid) to
[0,1]
.

4.

For a scalar
\lambda\colon 1\to 1
and a predicate
p\colon X\to 1
,
we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\lambda\cdot p}\equiv\lambda\mathop{\circ}p
.

Again due to
\mathsf{PCM}
-enrichment and
1_{M}=\mathrm{id}_{M}
,
this scalar multiplication
turns
\Pred X
into an
M
-effect module, see
3.1.2
.

5.

For
f\colon X\to Y
in
C
,
define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\Pred(f)}\colon\Pred Y\to\Pred X

by
\Pred(f)(p)=p\mathop{\circ}f
.
It is easy to see

\Pred(f)
is an
M
-effect module homomorphism
if
f
is total
and that, in fact,
\Pred\colon\Tot C\to\mathsf{EMod}^{\mathsf{op}}_{M}

is a functor.

6.

A
substate
on
X
is a map
\omega\colon 1\to X
.
A
state
is a total substate.

We denote the set of states on
X
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\Stat X}
.

7.

An effectus
C
has
separating predicates

if
\Pred X
(as a set of maps
X\to 1
)
is jointly monic for every
X
in
C
.
Similarly, an effectus has
separating states

if
\Stat X
(as a set of maps
1\to X
)
is jointly epic for every
X
in
C
.

†
†
margin:

III

Examples
The category
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
(see
3.2
)
is a real effectus (
M\cong[0,1]
)
with separating states and predicates.
[
23
]

The predicates on a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{A}

correspond to the set of effects
[0,1]_{\mathscr{A}}

and
\Stat\mathscr{A}
can be identified with the convex set of normal states.

†
†
margin:

IV

We briefly cover the examples from
[
23
]

mentioned in
3.2.2
.

1.

The category
{\mathsf{OUS}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
(see
3.2.2
)
is a real effectus with separating predicates,
but without seperating states.
(The states on a single order unit space are separating
if and only if the order unit space is archimedean.)
A predicate on an order unit space
X

corresponds to a point
x\in X
with
0\leqslant x\leqslant 1
,
where
1
is the distinguished order unit.
The states are exactly what are called states for order unit spaces
in the literature.

2.

The category
{\mathsf{OUG}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
(see
3.2.2
)
has the two-element effect monoid
2
as scalars
and seperating predicates.
The predicates of an order unit group
G
correspond
to the elements
x\in G
with
0\leqslant x\leqslant 1
,
where
1
is the distinguished order unit.
States on
G
correspond to unit-preserving positive
homomorphisms
G\to\mathbb{Z}
, which are not separating.

3.

Any extensive category with final object
(such as
\mathsf{Set}
,
{\mathsf{CRng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
and
\mathsf{CH}
)
has as scalars the two-element effect monoid
2
.

(a)

In
\mathsf{Set}
the predicates on a set
X
correspond to subsets
U\subseteq X

and are thus separating. States on
X
correspond t
elements
x\in X
, which are also separating.

(b)

In
{\mathsf{CRng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
the predicates on
R
correspond to idempotents of
R
,
which are not separating.
States correspond to unit-preserving
homomorphisms
R\to\mathbb{Z}
, which are not separating either.

(c)

In
\mathsf{CH}
the predicates on
X
correspond to clopen subsets
U\subseteq X
,
which are not separating.
States correspond on
X
correspond to its points
x\in X
,
which are separating.

†
†
margin:

V

The category
{\mathsf{EJA}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
is a real effectus with separating states
and predicates. The predicates on an Euclidean Jordan algebra (EJA)
E

correspond to its effects; i.e. the elements
0\leqslant a\leqslant 1
.
The states are exactly what are usually considered
states for EJAs.

†
†
margin:

191

Does every effect monoid
M
occur as the effect monoid of scalars of some
effectus? Does every
M
-effect module occur as
effect module of predicates?
We will show they do, in the following strong sense.

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem
Let
M
be any effect monoid.
The category
\mathsf{EMod}^{\mathsf{op}}_{M}
is an effectus in total form
with scalars
M
and separating predicates.
[
23
]

In fact: every effectus
C
in total form
with scalars
M
and separating predicates
is equivalent to a subcategory of
\mathsf{EMod}^{\mathsf{op}}_{M}
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
To show
\mathsf{EMod}^{\mathsf{op}}_{M}
is an effectus,
we have to show the dual axioms for
\mathsf{EMod}_{M}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

(Finite products)
For every
M
-effect module
E
,
there is a unique
M
-effect module map
!\colon M\to E
,
which is given by
\lambda\mapsto\lambda\cdot 1
.
So
M
is the initial object of
\mathsf{EMod}_{M}
.
The one-element
M
-effect module
\{0=1\}
is the final object.

If
E
and
F
are
M
-effect modules,
then the set of pairs
E\times F

with componentwise operations
is again an
M
-effect module.
The effect module
E\times F

with obvious projections
E\xleftarrow{\pi_{1}}E\times F\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}F

is the categorical product of
E
and
F
in
\mathsf{EMod}_{M}

(as is the case with effect algebras, see
3.1
).

†
†
margin:

V

(Pushout diagrams)
To show the pushout diagrams corresponding to (
3.1
) hold,
assume we are given
M
-effect modules
E,F,G

with module maps
\alpha,\beta,\delta

that make the outer squares of the following diagrams commute.

\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{E\times F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times!}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{M\times F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{M\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times!}
\scriptstyle{!\times\mathrm{id}}

\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{E\times F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{M\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!\times!}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}

We have to show that there are unique dashed arrows (as shown)
that make these diagrams commute.
We start with the left diagram.
By assumption
\alpha\mathop{\circ}(!\times\mathrm{id})=\beta\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{id}\times!)
,
so in particular
\alpha(1,0)=\beta(1,0)
.
For any
(x,y)\in E\times F
,
we have
\alpha(x,0)\leqslant\alpha(1,0)=\beta(1,0)\perp\beta(0,1)\geqslant\beta(0,y)
,
so
\alpha(x,0)\perp\beta(0,y)
.
Define
f\colon E\times F\to G

by
f(x,y)=\alpha(x,0)\ovee\beta(0,y)
.
It is easy to see
f
is (partially) additive
and
f(1,1)=\alpha(1,0)\ovee\beta(0,1)=\beta(1,1)=1
,
so
f
is a module map.
We compute

f(x,\lambda\cdot 1)\ =\ \alpha(x,0)\ovee\beta(0,\lambda\cdot 1)\ =\ \alpha(x,0)\ovee\lambda\cdot\alpha(0,1)\ =\ \alpha(x,\lambda)

and so
f\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{id}\times!)=\alpha
.
Similarly
\beta=f\mathop{\circ}(!\times\mathrm{id})
.
It is easy to see
f

is the unique map with
f\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{id}\times!)=\alpha

and
\beta=f\mathop{\circ}(!\times\mathrm{id})

and so the left square of (
3.1
)
is a pullback in
\mathsf{EMod}_{M}^{\mathsf{op}}
.

Concerning the right diagram:
as
\delta\mathop{\circ}(!\times!)=!\mathop{\circ}\pi_{1}
,
we have
\delta(\lambda\cdot 1,\mu\cdot 1)=\lambda\cdot 1

and so
\delta(0,y)\leqslant\delta(0,1)=0
.
Hence
\delta(x,y)=\delta(x,0)
.
Define
g\colon E\to G
by
g(x)=\delta(x,0)
.
Clearly
g
is additive,
g(1)=\delta(1,0)=\delta(1,1)=1

and
\delta=g\mathop{\circ}\pi_{1}
by definition.
Obviously
g
is the unique such map.
Thus the right square of (
3.1
)
is a pullback in
\mathsf{EMod}_{M}^{\mathsf{op}}
.

†
†
margin:

VI

(Joint epicity)
To show
\mathsf{EMod}^{\mathsf{op}}_{M}
is an effectus,
it only remains to be shown
that
\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2}\rangle,\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle\colon M\times M\to M\times M\times M
are jointly epic.
So assume
f,g\colon M\times M\times M\to E

are two
M
-effect module maps
with
f\mathop{\circ}\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2}\rangle=g\mathop{\circ}\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2}\rangle

and
f\mathop{\circ}\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle=g\mathop{\circ}\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle
.
From the first equality, it follows that
f(1,0,0)=g(1,0,0)
.
The other one implies
f(0,1,0)=g(0,1,0)
.
Thus
f(0,0,1)=f(1,1,0)^{\perp}=g(1,1,0)^{\perp}=g(0,0,1)
.
As for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in M^{3}
,
we have
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\lambda_{1}\cdot(1,0,0)\ovee\lambda_{2}\cdot(0,1,0)\ovee\lambda_{3}\cdot(0,0,1)

we get
f=g
.

†
†
margin:

VII

(Representation)
Let
C
be an effectus in total form.
Clearly
\Pred f=\Pred g
(for
f,g\colon X\to Y
in
C
)
if and only if
p\mathop{\circ}f=(\Pred f)(p)=(\Pred g)(p)=p\mathop{\circ}g

for every
p\in\Pred X
.
Thus the functor
\Pred\colon C\to\mathsf{EMod}^{\mathsf{op}}_{M}

is faithful if and only if
C
has separating predicates.
So if
C
has separating predicates,

C
is equivalent to the subcategory
\Pred C

of
\mathsf{EMod}_{M}^{\mathsf{op}}
.

\square

†
†
margin:

VIII

Exercise
Show that the category
{\mathsf{Rng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
of unit rings
with unit-preserving homomorphisms in the opposite direction
is an effectus in total form.

1.

Show that the predicates on a ring
R

correspond to its idempotents;
that
p\perp q
iff
pq=qp=0
,

p^{\perp}=1-p

and
p\ovee q=p+q
.
(So
2
is its effect monoid of scalars.)
Conclude
{\mathsf{Rng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
does not have separating predicates.

2.

Show that there is no unit-preserving
ring-homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
Conclude
{\mathsf{Rng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
does not have separating states.

†
†
margin:

192

We studied the structure of predicates in an effectus. What about the states?
They will turn out to form an abstract
M
-convex set.
Before we define these, we introduce a generalized distribution monad.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
M
be an effect monoid
and
X
be any set.
A
formal
M
-convex combination
over
X

is a function
p\colon X\to M

with finite support
such that
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x\in X}p(x)=1
.
We use
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\lambda_{1}\ket{x_{1}}\ovee\cdots\ovee\lambda_{n}\ket{x_{n}}}

as a shorthand for the formal
M
-convex combination
p\colon X\to M

given by
p(x)=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x_{i}=x}\lambda_{i}
.
(So
p(x_{i})=\lambda_{i}
if the
x_{i}
are distinct.)
We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\mathcal{D}_{M}}X

for the set of all formal
M
-convex combinations over
X
.
[
56
]

†
†
margin:

III

Exercise*
In this exercise you study
\mathcal{D}_{M}
as a monad.
See
[
56
,
129
]
.

1.

For
f\colon X\to Y
,
define
\mathcal{D}_{M}f\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}X\to\mathcal{D}_{M}Y

by

(\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(p)(y)\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;f(x)=y}p(x).

(That is:
(\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(\lambda_{1}\ket{x_{1}}\ovee\cdots\ovee\lambda_{n}\ket{x_{n}})=\lambda_{1}\ket{f(x_{1})}\ovee\cdots\ovee\lambda_{n}\ket{f(x_{n})}
.)
Show that this turns
\mathcal{D}_{M}
into a functor
\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
.

2.

Define
\eta\colon X\to\mathcal{D}_{M}X

by
\eta(x)=1\ket{x}

and
\mu\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}X\to\mathcal{D}_{M}X
by

\mu(\Phi)(x)\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{\varphi}\Phi(\varphi)\odot\varphi(x).

(That is:

\mu(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\sigma_{i}\ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\lambda_{ij}\ket{x_{ij}}})=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i,j}(\sigma_{i}\odot\lambda_{ij})\ket{x_{ij}}
.)

Show that
(\mathcal{D}_{M},\eta,\mu)

is a monad.

3.

Show that
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}
,
the Kleisli category of
\mathcal{D}_{M}

(see e.g.
[
129
, §2.6]
),
is an effectus
with
M
as scalars.

†
†
margin:

IV

Definition
Let
X
be a set together
with a map
h\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}X\to X
.
We say
(X,h)
is an
abstract
M
-convex set

provided
h(\ket{x})=x

and

h\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i,j}\sigma_{i}\odot\lambda_{ij}\ket{x_{ij}}\Bigr)\ =\ h\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\sigma_{i}\,\ket{h\bigl(\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\lambda_{ij}\ket{x_{ij}}\,\bigr)}\,\Bigr)

(3.2)

for every
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\sigma_{i}\ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\lambda_{ij}\ket{x_{ij}}}

in
\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.
(Equivalently:
h\mathop{\circ}\mu=h\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}h

and
h\mathop{\circ}\eta=\mathrm{id}
.)
We call
h
the convex structure on
X
.
A map
f\colon X\to Y

between abstract
M
-convex sets
is
M
-affine
if

f\bigl(h_{X}\bigl(\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{x_{i}}\,\bigr)\bigr)\ =\ h_{Y}\bigl(\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{f(x_{i})}\,\bigr)

for every
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{x_{i}}
in
\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.
(Equivalently:
h_{X}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}f=f\mathop{\circ}h_{Y}
.)
Write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\mathsf{AConv}_{M}}
for the category

of abstract
M
-convex sets with

M
-affine maps between them.
(Equivalently:
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}

is the Eilenberg–Moore category
of the monad
\mathcal{D}_{M}
.)
We call an abstract
M
-convex set
(X,h)

cancellative

provided

h(\lambda\ket{y}\ovee\lambda^{\perp}\ket{x_{1}})\ =\ h(\lambda\ket{y}\ovee\lambda^{\perp}\ket{x_{2}})\quad\text{implies}\quad~x_{1}=x_{2}

for any
x_{1},x_{2},y\in X
and
\lambda\in M,\lambda\neq 1
.

†
†
margin:

IVa

Remark
For an abstract
[0,1]
-convex set
(X,h)
,
binary convex combinations are sufficient:
for instance, for any
\lambda\ovee\mu\neq 1

and
x,y,z\in X
we have

h\bigl(\lambda\ket{x}\ovee\mu\ket{y}\ovee(\lambda\ovee\mu)^{\perp}\ket{z}\bigr)\ =\ h\Bigl(\lambda\ket{x}\ovee\lambda^{\perp}\ket{h\bigl(\nicefrac{{\mu}}{{\lambda^{\perp}}}\ket{y}\ovee\nicefrac{{(\lambda\ovee\mu)^{\perp}}}{{\lambda^{\perp}}}\ket{z}}\bigr)\Bigr).

This reduction to binary convex combinations uses
the division on
[0,1]
.
Some effect monoids have a suitable division as well,
but many do not
as we will see later on
in
3.2.4
and
3.2.4
.
It seems plausible that
it is impossible to write an arbitrary
three-element
M
-convex combination
over
\mathcal{D}_{M}\{x,y,z\}

using only binary convex combinations
in the case that
M
is the effect monoid
on the unit interval of
C[0,1]
.
I do not have a proof to back this claim.

†
†
margin:

V

Examples
We give some examples of abstract convex sets.

1.

Every convex subset
X\subseteq V

of some real vector space
V

is a cancellative abstract
[0,1]
-convex set
with
h(\lambda_{1}\ket{x_{1}}\ovee\cdots\ovee\lambda_{n}\ket{x_{n}})=\lambda_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}x_{n}
.

2.

Not every abstract
[0,1]
-convex set is
a convex subset of a real vector space.
Consider
T\subseteq[0,1]^{2}

defined by
T\equiv\{(x,y);\ x+y\leqslant 1\}
.
Say
(x,y)\sim(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

whenever
x=x^{\prime}
and either
x\neq 0
or
y=y^{\prime}
.
This equivalence relation
\sim

is a congruence (we will cover these in
3.2.4
)
for the convex structure on
T

(inherited by
\mathbb{R}^{2}
)
and so
T/_{\sim}
is an abstract
[0,1]
-convex set.
That is: we start off with a filled triangle

3.

Semilattices are exactly abstract
2
-convex sets.
(
2
is the two-element Effect Monoid,
see
3.1.1
.)
Furthermore, every semilattice
(L,\vee)

is an abstract
[0,1]
-convex set
with

h\bigl(\lambda_{1}\ket{x_{1}}\ovee\cdots\ovee\lambda_{n}\ket{x_{n}}\bigr)\ =\ \bigvee_{i;\lambda_{i}\neq 0}x_{i}.

A semilattice
L
is cancellative as abstract
[0,1]
-convex set
if and only if
x=y
for all
x,y\in L
.
See
[
90
]
.

4.

Every cancellative
[0,1]
-convex set
is isomorphic to a convex subset of a real vector space.
See e.g.
[
69
, thm. 8]
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Remarks
The study of
convex subsets (of real vector spaces)
as algebraic structures goes back a long time,
see e.g.
[
107
,
90
,
30
,
33
,
42
]
.
The more pathological abstract
[0,1]
-convex sets have been studied before
under different names:
they are semiconvex sets in
[
30
,
109
]
and
convex spaces in
[
33
]
.
The description of convex sets using Eilenberg–Moore algebras is probably
due to Świrszcz
[
109
]
.
For the even more exotic abstract
M
-convex
sets, see
[
23
]
or

[
56
]
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Proposition
Let
C
be an effectus with scalars
M
.
For any object
X
, the set of states

\Stat X
is an abstract
M^{\mathsf{op}}
-convex set
with
h\colon\mathcal{D}_{M^{\mathsf{op}}}\Stat X\to\Stat X

defined by

h\bigl(\lambda_{1}\ket{\varphi_{1}}\ovee\cdots\ovee\lambda_{n}\ket{\varphi_{n}}\bigr)=[\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}]\mathop{\circ}\langle\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\rangle
.
Furthermore,
for any total map
f\colon X\to Y
,
the map
(\Stat f)(\varphi)\ =\ f\mathop{\circ}\varphi

is an
M^{\mathsf{op}}
-affine map
\Stat X\to\Stat Y
yielding a
functor
\Stat\colon\Tot C\to\mathsf{AConv}_{M^{\mathsf{op}}}
.
[
23
]

†
†
margin:

VIII

Proof
Clearly
h(\ket{\varphi})=\varphi
.
To show the other axiom (
3.2
),
assume we have any
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m_{i}}_{j=1}\lambda_{ij}\ket{\varphi_{ij}}}

in
\mathcal{D}_{M^{\mathsf{op}}}\mathcal{D}_{M^{\mathsf{op}}}\Stat X
.
Write
\lambda_{i}\equiv\langle\lambda_{i1},\ldots,\lambda_{im_{i}}\rangle

and
\varphi_{i}\equiv[\varphi_{i1},\ldots,\varphi_{i{m_{i}}}]
.
Then

\displaystyle h\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\sigma_{i}\,\ket{h\bigl(\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\lambda_{ij}\ket{\varphi_{ij}}\,\bigr)}\,\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ [\varphi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\lambda_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\mathop{\circ}\lambda_{n}]\mathop{\circ}\langle\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\varphi_{i}\mathop{\circ}\lambda_{i}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\varphi_{ij}\mathop{\circ}\lambda_{ij}\bigr)\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i,j}\varphi_{ij}\mathop{\circ}(\lambda_{ij}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{i})

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i,j}\varphi_{ij}\mathop{\circ}(\sigma_{i}\odot_{M^{\mathsf{op}}}\lambda_{ij})

\displaystyle\ =\ h\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i,j}\sigma_{i}\odot_{M^{\mathsf{op}}}\lambda_{ij}\ket{\varphi_{ij}}\Bigr).

So
\Stat X
is indeed an abstract
M^{\mathsf{op}}
-convex set.
Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any total map.
To show
\Stat f
is affine,
it is sufficient to show that
for every
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{\varphi_{i}}

in
\mathcal{D}_{M^{\mathsf{op}}}
,
we have

f\mathop{\circ}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\varphi_{i}\mathop{\circ}\lambda_{i}=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}f\mathop{\circ}\varphi_{i}\mathop{\circ}\lambda_{i}
,
which is clearly true.
Functoriality of
\Stat
is obvious.
\square

3.2.4

Effectus of abstract
M
-convex sets

†
†
margin:

193

For our next project,
we indulge ourselves in a tangent:
we investigate whether the category
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
is an effectus.
We can show it is, if
M
has a certain partial division.
Unfortunately it will remain unclear if
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
is an effectus in general.
The case
M=[0,1]
is much easier and has already been dealt with
in
[
69
]
.
The first step is to study the coproduct in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
.
To construct the coproduct, we will need to know
about quotients of abstract
M
-convex sets.

†
†
margin:

II

Exercise
In this exercise you construct the quotient of an
abstract
M
-convex set by a congruence.
Assume
(X,h)
is an abstract
M
-convex set
and
{\sim}\subseteq X^{2}
is an equivalence relation.
Write
X/_{\sim}
for the set of equivalence classes
of
\sim

and
q\colon X\to X/_{\sim}

for
q(x)=[x]_{\sim}
, where
[x]_{\sim}
is the equivalence class of
x
.
For
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
,
we write
\varphi\sim\psi

provided
(\mathcal{D}_{M}q)(\varphi)=(\mathcal{D}_{M}q)(\psi)
.
We say
\sim
is a
congruence
for
(X,h)

if
\varphi\sim\psi
implies
h(\varphi)\sim h(\psi)
.

1.

Prove that
q
,
\mathcal{D}_{M}q
and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}q

are all surjective.

2.

Show that the following are equivalent.

(a)

\sim
is a congruence.

(b)

There is a map
h_{\sim}\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}X/_{\sim}\to X/_{\sim}

such that
h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}q=q\mathop{\circ}h
.

By the previous point
h_{\sim}
is unique, if it exists.

3.

Assume
\sim
is a congruence.
Use the previous two points to show

h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}q\ =\ h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mu\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}q.

Conclude that
(X/_{\sim},h_{\sim})
is an abstract
M
-convex set
and that the qoutient map
q\colon X\to X/_{\sim}
is
M
-affine.

†
†
margin:

III

Exercise
Assume
f\colon X\to Y
is an affine map between abstract

M
-convex sets.
Show that the kernel
\{(x,y);\ f(x)=f(y)\}
of
f

is a congruence.

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Assume
(X,h)
is an abstract
M
-convex set and
R\subseteq X^{2}

is some relation.
It is easy to see there is a least congruence containing
R
.
We need to know a bit more than mere existence.
Write
R^{*}
for the reflexive symmetric transitive closure of
R

(i.e. the least equivalence relation containing
R
).

For
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
,
we write
\psi_{1}\approx\psi_{2}

if there is a
derivation
: a tuple
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X

(say
\varphi_{i}\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n_{i}}_{j=1}\lambda_{ij}\ket{x_{ij}}
)
such that
\varphi_{1}=\psi_{1}
,
\varphi_{n}=\psi_{2}

and for each
1\leqslant i<n

one
of the following two conditions holds.

1.

n_{i}=n_{i+1}

and for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant n_{i}
we have

x_{ij}\mathrel{R^{*}}x_{(i+1)j}

and
\lambda_{ij}=\lambda_{(i+1)j}
.

2.

h(\varphi_{i})=h(\varphi_{i+1})
.

We will show that
\sim

given by
x\sim y\iff\eta(x)\approx\eta(y)

is the least congruence containing
R
:

1.

To start, show that
\eta(h(\psi))\approx\psi

and so
\varphi\approx\psi
implies
h(\varphi)\sim h(\psi)
.

2.

Show that if
\varphi\approx\psi
,
then

\mu\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\psi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\Bigr)\ \approx\ \mu\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\varphi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\Bigr).

for any
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\lambda_{j}=1
in
M
and
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.

3.

Use the previous point to show that
\varphi\sim\psi

implies
\varphi\approx\psi
.
Conclude
\sim
is the smallest congruence containing
R
.

†
†
margin:

V

Proposition
Assume
(X,h_{X})
and
(Y,h_{Y})

are abstract
M
-convex sets.
We will construct their coproduct.
Note that
\mu
turns
\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)

into an abstract
M
-convex set.
Let
\sim
denote the least congruence on
\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)

with

(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\varphi)\ \sim\ \eta(\kappa_{1}(h_{X}(\varphi)))\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\psi)\ \sim\ \eta(\kappa_{2}(h_{Y}(\psi)))

(3.3)

for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X

and
\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}Y
.
(Alternatively:
\sim
is the least congruence
that makes
\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}
and
\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}
affine.)
Write
q\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)\to C

for the quotient of
\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)
by
\sim
, see
3.2.4
.
Denote the convex structure on
C
by
h
.
Define
c_{1}\colon X\to C
and
c_{2}\colon Y\to C

by
c_{1}\equiv q\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}

and
c_{2}\equiv q\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}
.

Then:
c_{1}\colon X\to C\leftarrow Y\colon c_{2}

is a coproduct of
X
and
Y
in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
We start with the affinity of the coprojections:

\displaystyle c_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{X}

\displaystyle\ =\ q\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{X}

\displaystyle\ =\ q\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1}

by dfn. of
\sim

\displaystyle\ =\ q\mathop{\circ}\mu\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\eta\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1}.

\displaystyle\ =\ \mu\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}q\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\eta\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1}.

\displaystyle\ =\ \mu\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}c_{1},

so
c_{1}
is affine. With similar reasoning, we see
c_{2}
is affine.

†
†
margin:

VII

Assume
f\colon X\to Z
and
g\colon Y\to Z

are two affine maps to some abstract
M
-convex set
(Z,h_{Z})
.
We want to show
h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]

lifts to
C
.
With an easy calculation,
one sees
h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D_{M}}[f,g]
is affine.
We compute

\displaystyle h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}f

\displaystyle\ =\ f\mathop{\circ}h_{X}

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}[f,g]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{X}

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{X}.

So
(\,(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\varphi),\,\ \eta(\kappa_{1}(h_{X}(\varphi)))\,)

is in the kernel (see
3.2.4
)
of
h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]

for
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
.
Similarly
(\,(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\psi),\,\ \eta(\kappa_{2}(h_{Y}(\psi)))\,)

is also in the kernel of
h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]

for all
\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}
.

As kernels of affine maps are congruences
and
\sim
is the least congruence that contains these pairs,
we conclude
\sim
is a subset of the kernel
of
h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]
.
Thus there is a unique affine
k\colon C\to Z

fixed by
k\mathop{\circ}q=h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]
.
Now

\displaystyle k\mathop{\circ}c_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ k\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}[f,g]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ f

and similarly
k\mathop{\circ}c_{2}=g
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Only uniqueness of
k
remains.
So assume
k^{\prime}\colon C\to Z
is an affine map
such that
k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}c_{1}=f

and
k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}c_{2}=g
.
We turn the wheel:

\displaystyle k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}q

\displaystyle\ =\ k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}\mu\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\eta

\displaystyle\ =\ k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}(q\mathop{\circ}\eta)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}(k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}\eta)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1},k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}]

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}c_{1},k^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}c_{2}]

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{Z}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}[f,g]

\displaystyle\ =\ k\mathop{\circ}q.

So
k=k^{\prime}
by surjectivity of
q
.
\square

†
†
margin:

IX

Remark
The existence of coproducts of abstract
M
-convex sets already
follows from the more general theorem of Linton
[
86
]
.
(Cf.
[
10
]
.)
However, for the results in the remainder of this section we need to know
more about the coproduct than Linton’s construction provides.

By our construction, we know that
every
z\in X+Y

is of the
form

z\ =\ h\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{c_{1}(x_{i})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{c_{2}(y_{i})}\Bigr)

for some
\lambda_{i},\sigma_{i}\in M
,

x_{i}\in X
and
y_{i}\in Y
.
In general these are not unique.
Indeed
using
3.2.4
, we see that

h\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{c_{1}(x_{i})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{c_{2}(y_{i})}\Bigr)\ =\ h\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n^{\prime}}_{i=1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\ket{c_{1}(x^{\prime}_{i})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m^{\prime}}_{i=1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\ket{c_{2}(y^{\prime}_{i})}\Bigr)

iff there is a
derivation

\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{l}\in\mathcal{D}^{2}_{M}(X+Y)
,
say
\Phi_{i}\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}\ket{\varphi_{ij}}
,
with

\Phi_{1}\ =\ \ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y_{i})}},\quad\Phi_{l}\ =\ \ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n^{\prime}}_{i=1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m^{\prime}}_{i=1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y_{i})}}

and for each
1\leqslant i<l
either

1.

\mu(\Phi_{i})=\mu(\Phi_{i+1})

or

2.

for each
j
we have
\zeta_{ij}=\zeta_{(i+1)j}

and one of the following.

(a)

\varphi_{ij}=\varphi_{(i+1)j}
.

(b)

\varphi_{ij}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\chi)

and
\varphi_{(i+1)j}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\chi^{\prime})

for some
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X

with
h_{X}(\chi)=h_{X}(\chi^{\prime})
.

(c)

\varphi_{ij}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\chi)

and
\varphi_{(i+1)j}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\chi^{\prime})

for some
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}Y

with
h_{Y}(\chi)=h_{Y}(\chi^{\prime})
.

†
†
margin:

X

Exercise
Show that the one-element set is an abstract
M
-convex set
and, in fact, the final object of
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
.
Moreover, show that in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}

n\cdot 1\ \equiv\ \underbrace{1+\cdots+1}_{\text{$n$ times}}\ \cong\ \mathcal{D}_{M}\{1,\ldots,n\}.

(You might want to use that
\mathcal{D}_{M}
, as a left-adjoint,
preserves coproducts.)

†
†
margin:

194

Proposition
The category
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
obeys all the axioms of an effectus in total
form except perhaps for the left pullback square of (
3.1
).

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
In
3.2.4
we proved
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
has binary coproducts
and in
3.2.4

that the one-element set
1
is its final object.
As
\mathcal{D}_{M}\emptyset=\emptyset
,
the empty set is trivially also an abstract
M
-convex set
and in fact the initial object of
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
.

†
†
margin:

III

We continue with joint monicity
of
[\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{2}],[\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}]\colon 1+1+1\to 1+1
.
We can represent the convex set
1+1+1

as the set of triples
(a,b,c)\in M^{3}

with
a\ovee b\ovee c=1

with the obvious convex structure,
cf.
3.2.4
.
Similarly, we identify
1+1

with pairs
(a,a^{\perp})\in M^{2}
.
The maps at hand are given by

(a,b,c)\ \mapsto\ (a,b\ovee c)\quad\text{and}\quad(a,b,c)\ \mapsto\ (b,a\ovee c).

(3.4)

Assume
(a,b\ovee c)=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\ovee c^{\prime})

and

(b,a\ovee c)=(b^{\prime},a^{\prime}\ovee c^{\prime})

for
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime},c,c^{\prime}\in M
with
a\ovee b\ovee c=1

and
a^{\prime}\ovee b^{\prime}\ovee c^{\prime}=1
.
Then clearly
a=a^{\prime}
,
b=b^{\prime}

and so
c=(a\ovee b)^{\perp}=(a^{\prime}\ovee b^{\prime})^{\perp}=c^{\prime}
.
Thus the maps (
3.4
) are jointly injective,
hence jointly monic.

†
†
margin:

IV

At last, we are ready to prove that the square on the right of (
3.1
)
is a pullback diagram in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
.
So assume
\alpha\colon Z\to X+Y

is a map in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}

with
(!+!)\mathop{\circ}\alpha=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}!
.

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{1}}
\textstyle{X+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!+!}
\textstyle{1+1}

We have to show
\alpha=\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\gamma
,
for a unique
\gamma\colon Z\to X+Y
in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
.
For the moment, assume
\kappa_{1}
is injective.
Then
\gamma
, if it exists, is unique.
Pick
z\in Z
.
We know

\alpha(z)\ =\ h\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y_{i})}\Bigr)

for some
\lambda_{i},\sigma_{i}\in M
,

x_{i}\in X
and
y_{i}\in Y
,
where
h
is the convex structure on
X+Y
.
As
(!+!)\mathop{\circ}\alpha=!\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}
,
we must have
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\sigma_{i}=0

and so
\alpha(z)=\kappa_{1}(x_{z})

for some
x_{z}\in X
.
This
x_{z}
is unique by supposed injectivity of
\kappa_{1}
.
Define
\gamma(z)=x_{z}
.
By definition
\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\alpha
.
To see
\gamma
is affine, pick any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}(Z)
.
We have

\kappa_{1}(\gamma(h_{Z}(\varphi)))=\alpha(h_{Z}(\varphi))=h(\mathcal{D}_{M}\alpha(\varphi))=h(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{M}\gamma(\varphi)))=\kappa_{1}(h_{X}(\mathcal{D}_{M}\gamma(\varphi)))
and
so indeed
\gamma(h_{Z}(\varphi))=h_{X}(\mathcal{D}_{M}\gamma(\varphi))

— i.e.
\gamma
is affine.

†
†
margin:

V

Finally, we will show that
\kappa_{1}
is indeed injective.
Assume
\kappa_{1}(x_{0})=\kappa_{1}(x^{\prime}_{0})

for some
x_{0},x^{\prime}_{0}\in X
.
Let
\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{l}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}^{2}{(X+Y)}

be a derivation
of
\kappa_{1}(x_{0})=\kappa_{1}(x^{\prime}_{0})

as in
3.2.4
.
The remainder of the proof is, in essence, a simple induction
over
1\leqslant i<l
, which has been complicated
by a technicality.
Define

\mathrm{IH}(i)\ \equiv\ \text{``}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y}\mu(\Phi_{i})(\kappa_{2}(y))=0\text{ and }h_{X}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\mu(\Phi_{i})(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr)=x_{0}\text{''}.

Clearly
\mathrm{IH}(1)
holds
and if
\mathrm{IH}(l)
should hold,
then
x_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime}
as desired.
So, to prove the inductive step, assume
\mathrm{IH}(i)
holds.
There are two possible derivation steps, see
3.2.4
.
In the first case (i.e.
\mu(\Phi_{i})=\mu(\Phi_{i+1})
)
it is obvious
\mathrm{IH}(i+1)
holds.
So we continue with the other case,
where (among others) we have
\zeta_{ij}=\zeta_{(i+1)j}
,
where
\Phi_{i}\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}\ket{\varphi_{ij}}
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume
\zeta_{ij}\neq 0
.
For each
j
there are three possibilities, see
3.2.4
.
The third does not occur:
if
\varphi_{ij_{0}}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\chi)

for some
\chi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}Y
and
j_{0}
,
then we reach a contradiction:

0\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y}\mu(\Phi_{i})(\kappa_{2}(y))\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y,j}\zeta_{ij}\odot\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{2}(y))\ \geqslant\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y}\zeta_{ij_{0}}\odot\chi(y)\ \neq\ 0.

We are left with two possibilities —
to distinguish those,
write
j\in J
if
\varphi_{ij}=\varphi_{(i+1)j}

and
j\notin J

if
\varphi_{ij}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\chi)

and
\varphi_{(i+1)j}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\chi^{\prime})

for some
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X

with
h_{X}(\chi)=h_{X}(\chi^{\prime})
.

We will show the first part of
\mathrm{IH}(i+1)
.
Pick any
y\in Y
.
If
j\in J
, then
\zeta_{(i+1)j}\odot\varphi_{(i+1)j}(\kappa_{2}(y))=\zeta_{ij}\odot\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{2}(y))=0
.
In the other case, if
j\notin J
,
then clearly
\zeta_{(i+1)j}\odot\varphi_{(i+1)j}(\kappa_{2}(y))=0

as
\varphi_{(i+1)j}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\chi^{\prime})
.
So, we have
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y}\mu(\Phi_{i+1})(\kappa_{2}(y))=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y,j}\zeta_{(i+1)j}\odot\varphi_{(i+1)j}(\kappa_{2}(y))=0
.

Write
r_{ij}=(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x)))^{\perp}
.
The remainder of the proof is complicated
by the fact that in general
r_{ij}\neq 0
.
We do have
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}\odot r^{\perp}_{ij}=1

and
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}=1

so by
3.1.1

we see
\zeta_{ij}\odot r^{\perp}_{ij}=\zeta_{ij}
,
which forces
\zeta_{ij}\odot r_{ij}=0
.
We compute

\displaystyle h_{X}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\mu(\Phi_{i})(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{X}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x,j}\zeta_{ij}\odot\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{X}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}\odot r_{ij}\ket{x_{0}}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\zeta_{ij}\odot\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{X}\Bigl(\mu\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}\ket{r_{ij}\ket{x_{0}}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}}\Bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{X}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}\ket{h_{X}\Bigl(r_{ij}\ket{x_{0}}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr)}\Bigr).

So, if we show that for each
j
,
we have

h_{X}\Bigl(r_{ij}\ket{x_{0}}\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr)\\
\ =\ h_{X}\Bigl(r_{(i+1)j}\ket{x_{0}}\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\varphi_{(i+1)j}(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr),

(3.5)

then

x_{0}\ =\ h_{X}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\mu(\Phi_{i})(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr)\ =\ h_{X}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\mu(\Phi_{i+1})(\kappa_{1}(x))\ket{x}\Bigr),

as desired.
If
j\in J
,
then
\varphi_{ij}=\varphi_{(i+1)j}

and so
r_{ij}=r_{(i+1)j}

and obviously (
3.5
).
So assume
j\notin J
.
Then clearly
r_{ij}=0=r_{(i+1)j}

as
\varphi_{ij}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\chi))

and
\varphi_{(i+1)j}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{1})(\chi^{\prime})
.
Now (
3.5
) follows from
h_{X}(\chi)=h_{X}(\chi^{\prime})
.
\square

†
†
margin:

195

It is unclear whether the left square of (
3.1
)
is a pullback in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
.
We will see that it is, if we assume that
M
has
a partial division in the following sense.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
An
effect divisoid

is an effect monoid
M

with partial binary operation
(a,b)\mapsto\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}

that is defined iff
a\leqslant b
and
satisfies the following axioms.

1.

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}

is the unique element of
M

with
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}

and
b\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}\ =\ a

2.

a\ \leqslant\ \reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}

3.

\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$})$}}}{{\reflectbox{$(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$})$}}}$}\ =\ \reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}

†
†
margin:

IIa

Beware
The first axiom might be read as “

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}

is defined to be the
unique element such that (…)” in which case the definition
is cyclical and thus problematic.
What is meant instead is that

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}

is
part of the structure and satisfies

•

\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}

and
b\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}\ =\ a
and

•

if
c\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}

and
b\odot c\ =\ a
,
then
c=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}

for all
a,b,c\in M
with
a\leqslant b
.
In particular, it is not ruled out
that there is an effect monoid
which is an effect divisoid in two different ways.

†
†
margin:

III

Remark
The element

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}

behaves like a support-projection for
a
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Show that for an effect divisoid
M
, the following holds.

1.

\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}$}=0
,
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$1$}}}{{\reflectbox{$1$}}}$}=1
,
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$1$}}}$}=a
,
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}
and

\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a\odot b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\odot b
.

2.

For any
a\leqslant b\leqslant c
,
we have
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}
.

†
†
margin:

V

Examples
Almost all effect monoids we encountered
are effect divisoids.

1.

The effect monoid
[0,1]

is an effect divisoid with
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}=\frac{a}{b}

if
b\neq 0
and
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}$}=0
.
With the same partial division, we see the two-element effect monoid
2

is also an effect divisoid.

2.

More interestingly,
if
M_{1}
and
M_{2}

are effect divisoids,
then
M_{1}\times M_{2}

is an effect divisoid
with
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$(a_{1},a_{2})$}}}{{\reflectbox{$(b_{1},b_{2})$}}}$}=(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a_{1}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b_{1}$}}}$},\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a_{2}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b_{2}$}}}$})
.
In particular
[0,1]^{n}
is an effect divisoid
with component-wise partial division.

3.

Later, in
3.6
,
we will see that if
C
is an
\&
-effectus,
then
(\Scal C)^{\mathsf{op}}
(the scalars with multiplication in the
opposite direction)
is an effect divisoid.

4.

If
M
is a division effect monoid as in
[
19
, dfn. 6.3]
,
then
M^{\textrm{op}}
is an effect divisoid in the obvious way.

5.

The effect monoid on the unit interval of
C[0,1]

is
not
an effect divisoid,
but the effect monoid on the unit interval of
L^{\infty}[0,1]

is, see
3.2.4
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Exercise*
Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space.
In this exercise you will show
that the unit interval of
C(X)

is an effect divisoid
if and only if
X

is
basically disconnected

[
39
, 1H]

— that is: if
\overline{\supp f}
is open
for every
f\in C(X)
.
Equivalently:

C(X)
is
\sigma
-Dedekind complete

[
39
, 3N.5]
,
which is in turn equivalent to bounded
\omega
-completeness
(i.e.
C(X)
has suprema, resp. infima, of
bounded ascending, resp. descending, sequences).

1.

Suppose the unit interval of
C(X)
is an effect divisoid.
Let
f\in C(X)
with
0\leqslant f\leqslant 1
.
If
\overline{\supp f}=X
, then you’re done.
Otherwise pick any
y\notin\overline{\supp f}
.
Show, using Urysohn’s lemma,
that there is a
g\in C(X),0\leqslant g\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}

with
g(y)=0
and
g(x)=1
for all
x\in\overline{\supp f}
.
Show that this implies
(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$})(y)=0

and so

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}

is the characteristic function
of
\overline{\supp f}
. Conclude
X
is basically disconnected.

2.

Assume
X
is basically disconnected.
Let
f,g\in C(X)
with
0\leqslant f\leqslant g\leqslant 1
.
For
n>0
,
show that
U_{n}\equiv\{x;\ g(x)>\frac{1}{n}\}

has open closure and so

h_{n}\ \equiv\ \begin{cases}\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}&x\in\overline{U_{n}}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

is continuous.
Note
h_{1}\leqslant h_{2}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant 1

and define
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}=\sup_{n}h_{n}
.
Show

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}

is the characteristic function
of
\overline{\supp f}
and

\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}
.
Prove that this partial division turns the unit interval of
C(X)

into an effect divisoid.

†
†
margin:

VII

Proposition
If
a\perp b
and
a\ovee b\leqslant c
in an effect divisoid,
then
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a\ovee b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\ovee\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Proof
We will first show that if
c\odot a\perp c\odot b
,
then
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot a\perp\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot b
.
To start,
note
c\odot b^{\perp}\leqslant c\odot b^{\perp}\ovee c^{\perp}=(c\odot b)^{\perp}\leqslant c\odot a

and so

\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c\odot a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\ =\ \reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c\odot b^{\perp}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c\odot a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c\odot b^{\perp}$}}}$}\ =\ \reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot b^{\perp}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c\odot a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c\odot b^{\perp}$}}}$}\ \leqslant\ \reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot b^{\perp}.

Hence
(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot b)^{\perp}\geqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot b^{\perp}\geqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c\odot a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot a
.
Indeed:
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot a\perp\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot b
.

†
†
margin:

IX

Assume
a\perp b
and
a\ovee b\leqslant c
.
Clearly
c\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}=a\perp b=c\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}
.
By our initial lemma, we get

\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\perp\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}
.
Clearly
c\odot(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\ovee\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$})=a\ovee b
, so

\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a\ovee b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\ =\ \reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c\odot(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\ovee\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$})$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\ =\ \reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\ovee\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$})\ =\ \reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\ovee\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$},

as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

196

The following is a generalization
of
[
19
, prop. C.3]
.
See also
[
69
, prop. 15]
.

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem
If
M
is an effect divisoid,
then
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
is an effectus.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
We only have to show that the square on the left
of (
3.1
) is a pullback in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}

— the other axioms were proven in
3.2.4
.

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{X+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+!}
\scriptstyle{!+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{X+1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!+\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{1+Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}+!}
\textstyle{1+1}

To this end, assume
\alpha\colon Z\to X+1

and
\beta\colon Z\to Y+1

are maps in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}

with
(!+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\circ}\beta=(\mathrm{id}+!)\mathop{\circ}\alpha
.
We have to show there is a unique
\gamma\colon Z\to X+Y
in
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}

with
(\mathrm{id}+!)\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\beta

and
(!+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\alpha
.
Pick any
z\in Z
.
Write
\cdot
for the unique element of
1
.
By construction of the coproduct, see
3.2.4
,
we have

\displaystyle\alpha(z)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{1}\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\kappa_{1}(\cdot)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y_{i})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\beta(z)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{2}\Bigl(\sigma_{0}\ket{\kappa_{2}(\cdot)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}\Bigr)

for some
\lambda_{i},\sigma_{i}\in M
,

x_{i}\in X
and
y_{i}\in Y
,
where
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are the convex structures
on
1+Y
and
X+1
, respectively.
Unfolding definitions
(and identifying
1+1\cong M
via
\kappa_{1}(\cdot)=1
),
it is easy to
see that by assumption

\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ =\ (!+\mathrm{id})(\beta(z))\ =\ (\mathrm{id}+!)(\alpha(z))\ =\ \lambda_{0}\ =\ \Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\Bigr)^{\perp}.

(3.6)

This suggests the following definition

\gamma(z)\ =\ h_{3}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y_{i})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}\Bigr),

(3.7)

where
h_{3}
is the convex structure on
X+Y
.
To show this a proper definition,
let
n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda^{\prime}_{i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\in M
,

x^{\prime}_{i}\in X
and
y^{\prime}_{i}\in Y
be any elements such that

\displaystyle\alpha(z)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{1}\Bigl(\lambda^{\prime}_{0}\ket{\kappa_{1}(\cdot)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n^{\prime}}_{i=1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y^{\prime}_{i})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\beta(z)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{2}\Bigl(\sigma^{\prime}_{0}\ket{\kappa_{2}(\cdot)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m^{\prime}}_{i=1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x^{\prime}_{i})}\Bigr).

†
†
margin:

IV

We have to show

h_{3}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n^{\prime}}_{i=1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y^{\prime}_{i})}\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m^{\prime}}_{i=1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x^{\prime}_{i})}\Bigr)\\
\ =\ h_{3}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y_{i})}\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}\Bigr).

(3.8)

Without loss of generality,
we may assume
n,n^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}\geqslant 1

(as we allow, for instance,
\lambda_{1}=0
).
Due to (
3.6
),
it is clear
\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
and
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
.
By
3.2.4

we know there
are derivations
\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{l}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}^{2}(1+Y)

and
\Psi_{1},\ldots,\Psi_{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}^{2}(X+1)
,
say
\Phi_{i}\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}\ket{\varphi_{ij}}
,

\Psi_{i}\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\xi_{ij}\ket{\psi_{ij}}

with

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ \ket{\,\lambda_{0}\ket{\kappa_{1}(\cdot)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y_{i})}\,}

\displaystyle\Phi_{l}

\displaystyle\ =\ \ket{\,\lambda_{0}\ket{\kappa_{1}(\cdot)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n^{\prime}}_{i=1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y^{\prime}_{i})}\,}

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ \ket{\,\sigma_{0}\ket{\kappa_{2}(\cdot)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}\,}

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}

\displaystyle\ =\ \ket{\,\sigma_{0}\ket{\kappa_{2}(\cdot)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m^{\prime}}_{i=1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x^{\prime}_{i})}\,}.

We are going to combine the
\Phi_{i}
and
\Psi_{i}

into a single derivation of (
3.8
)
by first ‘applying’ the
\Phi_{i}
and then
the
\Psi_{i}
.
Define
\Omega_{1},\ldots,\Omega_{l+k}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}^{2}(X+Y)
by

\displaystyle\Omega_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\zeta_{ij}\ket{\omega_{ij}}

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant l

\displaystyle\Omega_{i+l}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j}\xi_{ij}\ket{\omega_{(i+l)j}}

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
,

where
\omega_{ij}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)

are given by

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}(\kappa_{2}(y))

\displaystyle\ =\ \varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{2}(y))

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))

\displaystyle\ =\ \varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))\odot(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\psi_{11}(\kappa_{1}(x))$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}$}\ovee r(\kappa_{1}(x)))

\displaystyle\omega_{(i+l)j}(\kappa_{1}(x))

\displaystyle\ =\ \psi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))

\displaystyle\omega_{(i+l)j}(\kappa_{2}(y))

\displaystyle\ =\ \psi_{ij}(\kappa_{2}(\cdot))\odot(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\varphi_{l1}(\kappa_{2}(y))$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\sigma_{0}$}}}$}\ovee r(\kappa_{2}(y)))

\displaystyle r(z)

\displaystyle\ =\ \begin{cases}(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}$})^{\perp}&z=\kappa_{1}(x_{1})\\
(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\sigma_{0}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\sigma_{0}$}}}$})^{\perp}&z=\kappa_{2}(y_{1})\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where on the first two lines
1\leqslant i\leqslant l

and on the second two
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.
Before we continue, we check whether the
\omega_{ij}

are distributions, as claimed.
For
1\leqslant i\leqslant l

this amounts to
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\omega_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))=\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))
.
Indeed

\displaystyle\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\omega_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(x))

\displaystyle\ =\ \varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))\odot r(\kappa_{1}(x_{1}))\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\psi_{11}(\kappa_{1}(x))$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}$}

\displaystyle\ =\ \varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))\odot(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}$})^{\perp}\,\ovee\,\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\psi_{11}(\kappa_{1}(x))$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}$}

\displaystyle\ =\ \varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))\odot(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}$})^{\perp}\,\ovee\,\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}$}

\displaystyle\ =\ \varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot)).

With an analogous argument, one checks
\omega_{(i+l)j}

is a distribution for
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.

To show
\Omega_{i}
is
a derivation (in the sense of
3.2.4
),
first pick any
1\leqslant i<l
.
We distinguish between the two allowable derivation steps.
If
\mu(\Phi_{i})=\mu(\Phi_{i+1})
,
then
\mu(\Omega_{i})=\mu(\Omega_{i+1})

by a straightforward computation
and so
\Omega_{i}
has a valid step.
In the other case, we have
\zeta_{ij}=\zeta_{(i+1)j}
and
for each
j
we have three possibilities.
In the first case, if
\varphi_{ij}=\varphi_{(i+1)j}
,
then clearly
\omega_{ij}=\omega_{(i+1)j}
as well.
For the second case, assume
\varphi_{ij}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\chi)

and
