\varphi_{(i+1)j}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\chi^{\prime})

for some
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}Y

with
h_{Y}(\chi)=h_{Y}(\chi^{\prime})
.
Clearly
\varphi_{ij}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))=0

and so
\omega_{ij}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\chi)
.
Similarly
\omega_{(i+1)j}=(\mathcal{D}_{M}\kappa_{2})(\chi^{\prime})
.
The third case is trivial.
So
\Omega_{i}
also makes a valid step of the second kind.

With the same kind of argument, we cover

\Omega_{i+l}
for
1\leqslant i<k
.
The only step left to check is the one
from
\Omega_{l}
to
\Omega_{l+1}
.
Note
\zeta_{l1}=1
,
\varphi_{l1}(\kappa_{1}(\cdot))=\lambda_{0}

and
\lambda_{0}\odot(\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}{{\reflectbox{$\lambda_{0}$}}}$})^{\perp}=0
,
so with some easy manipulation, we find

\Omega_{l}\ =\ \ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n^{\prime}}_{i=1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y^{\prime}_{i})}\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}}.

and in a similar fashion

\displaystyle\Omega_{l+1}

\displaystyle\ =\ \ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n^{\prime}}_{i=1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y^{\prime}_{i})}\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}}

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}

\displaystyle\ =\ \ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y_{i})}\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m}_{i=1}\sigma_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x_{i})}}

\displaystyle\Omega_{l+k}

\displaystyle\ =\ \ket{\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n^{\prime}}_{i=1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{2}(y^{\prime}_{i})}\,\ovee\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{m^{\prime}}_{i=1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\ket{\kappa_{1}(x^{\prime}_{i})}}.

So
\Omega_{i}
is a valid derivation of (
3.8
).

†
†
margin:

V

We are in the home stretch now.
Define
\gamma(z)
as in (
3.7
).
It is easy to see
\gamma

is the unique map with
(\mathrm{id}+!)\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\beta

and
(!+\mathrm{id})\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\alpha
.
It remains to be shown
\gamma
is affine.
Write

q_{1}\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)\to X+Y\quad q_{2}\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}(1+Y)\to 1+Y\quad q_{3}\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+1)\to X+1

for the quotient maps used in the construction of the coproducts.
We worked hard to show
that
for all
z\in Z
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}Y
,

\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
,
and
\chi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)

with
\chi(\kappa_{1}(y))=\varphi(\kappa_{1}(y))

and
\chi(\kappa_{2}(x))=\psi(\kappa_{2}(x))
,
we have

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha(z)\ =\ q_{1}(\varphi)\\
\beta(z)\ =\ q_{2}(\psi)\end{array}\right]\quad\implies\quad\gamma(z)=q_{3}(\chi).

Assume
z=h_{Z}(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{z_{i}})
.
We want to show
\gamma(z)=h_{3}(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{\gamma(z_{i})})
.
To this end, pick
\varphi_{i}
,
\psi_{i}
,
\chi_{i}

with
\alpha(z_{i})=q_{1}(\varphi_{i})
,

\beta(z_{i})=q_{2}(\psi_{i})
,

\chi_{i}(\kappa_{1}(y))=\varphi_{i}(\kappa_{1}(y))

and
\chi_{i}(\kappa_{2}(x))=\psi_{i}(\kappa_{2}(x))
.
So
\gamma(z_{i})=q_{3}(\chi_{i})
.
Define
\varphi,\psi,\chi
as follows.

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \mu\bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{\varphi_{i}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \mu\bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{\psi_{i}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \mu\bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{\chi_{i}}\bigr)

Note

q_{1}(\varphi)=h_{1}(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{q_{1}(\varphi_{i})})=h_{1}(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{\alpha(z_{i})})=\alpha(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{z_{i}})=\alpha(z)
.
Similarly
q_{2}(\psi)=\beta(z)
.
For any
y\in Y
,
we have

\chi(\kappa_{1}(y))=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\odot\chi_{i}(\kappa_{1}(y))=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\odot\varphi_{i}(\kappa_{1}(y))=\varphi(\kappa_{1}(y))
.
Similarly
\chi(\kappa_{2}(x))=\psi(\kappa_{2}(x))
for any
x\in X
.
So
\gamma(z)=q_{3}(\chi)
and consequently

\gamma(z)\ =\ q_{3}(\chi)\ =\ h_{3}\bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{q_{3}(\chi_{i})}\bigr)\ =\ h_{3}\bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\ket{\gamma(z_{i})}\bigr),

as desired.
\square

3.3

Effectuses with quotients

†
†
margin:

197

We return to the main program
of this chapter: the attempted axiomatisation
of the effectus
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
C
be an effectus.
We say
C
is an
effectus with quotients

if for each predicate
p\colon X\to 1
,
there exists a map
\xi_{p}\colon X\to X/_{p}

with
1\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p}\leqslant p^{\perp}

satisfying the following universal property.

For any map
f\colon X\to Y

with
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant p^{\perp}
,
there is a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon X/_{p}\to Y

such that
f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p}=f
.

Any map with this universal property
is called a
quotient
for
p
.

†
†
margin:

IIa

Remarks
The universal property of quotients
is essentially the same as
the universal property of (contractive)
compressions
,
which we proposed in
[
126
]
.
Effectuses with quotients also appear in
[
23
]
.

†
†
margin:

III

Notation
Unless otherwise specified
\xi_{p}

will denote some quotient for
p
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Examples
In
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
quotients are exactly the same thing
as contractive filters,
see
2.6
,

and
.
An example of a quotient map for
1-a\in\mathscr{A}
is given
by
\xi\colon\left\lceil a\right\rceil\mathscr{A}\left\lceil a\right\rceil\to\mathscr{A}

with
\xi(b)=\sqrt{a}b\sqrt{a}
.

†
†
margin:

IVa

The effectus
{\mathsf{EJA}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
has quotients,
which are in essence very similar to those of
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
,
when ignoring the technical complications Jordan algebras
impose.
[
128
, prop. 25]

†
†
margin:

IVb

The effectus
{\mathsf{OUS}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
has quotients,
which are rather different from the previous two examples.
An example of a quotient for a predicate
v
on an order unit space
V

is given by the inclusion of the order ideal generated by
1-v

\langle 1-v\rangle\ \equiv\ \{a;\ a\in V;\ \exists n.\,-n(1-v)\leqslant a\leqslant n(1-v)\}\ \subseteq\ V,

where the order ideal is considered as an order unit space
with order-unit
1-v
.
The effectus
{\mathsf{OUG}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
has very similar quotients. See
[
23
]
.

†
†
margin:

V

Exercise
Verify the following basic properties of quotients.
(See
[
23
]
.)

1.

If
\xi\colon X\to Y
is a quotient for
p

and
\vartheta\colon Y\to Z
is an isomorphism,
then
\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi
is a quotient for
p

as well.

2.

Conversely, if
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}

are both quotients for
p
,
then there is a unique isomorphism
\vartheta

with
\xi_{1}=\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi_{2}
.

3.

Isomorphisms are quotients (for 0).

4.

Maps into
0
are quotients (for 1).

5.

If
\xi
is a quotient for
p
, then
1\mathop{\circ}\xi=p^{\perp}

(Hint: apply the universal property to
p^{\perp}
).

6.

Quotients are epic.

†
†
margin:

VI

The following proposition is easy to prove,
but shows an important property of quotients:
any map
f
factors as a total map after a quotient
for
(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Proposition
Assume
\xi_{p^{\perp}}\colon X\to X/_{p^{\perp}}
is a quotient for
p^{\perp}
.
For any
f\colon X\to Z

with
1\mathop{\circ}f=p
,
there is a unique
total

g\colon X/_{p^{\perp}}\to Z

with
f=g\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p^{\perp}}
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Proof
(This is a simplified version of our previous
proof in
[
23
]
.)
By definition of quotient, there is a unique
g\colon X/_{p^{\perp}}\to Z

with
g\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p^{\perp}}=f
.
Note
1\mathop{\circ}g\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p^{\perp}}=1\mathop{\circ}f=p=1\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p^{\perp}}
.
Thus, as
\xi_{p^{\perp}}
is epi (
3.3
),
we conclude
1\mathop{\circ}g=1
.
That is:
g
is total.
\square

†
†
margin:

IX

Proposition
In an effectus with quotients,
quotients are closed under composition.

†
†
margin:

X

Proof
(This is essentially the same proof as we gave in
[
23
]
.)
Assume
\xi_{1}\colon X\to Y
is a quotient for
p^{\perp}

and
\xi_{2}\colon Y\to Z
is a quotient for
q^{\perp}
.
We will prove
\xi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}

is a quotient for
(q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1})^{\perp}
.
As our effectus has quotients,
we can pick a quotient
\xi\colon X\to X/_{(q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1})^{\perp}}

of
(q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1})^{\perp}
.
First, some preparation.
Note
1\mathop{\circ}\xi=q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}=p
.
Thus by the universal property of
\xi_{1}
,
there is a unique map
h_{1}\colon Y\to X/_{(q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1})^{\perp}}

with
h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}=\xi
.
As
1\mathop{\circ}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}=1\mathop{\circ}\xi=q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}

and
\xi_{1}
is epi, we see
1\mathop{\circ}h_{1}=q
.
Thus by
3.3
,
there is a unique total map
h_{2}\colon Z\to X/_{(q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1})^{\perp}}

with
h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{2}=h_{1}
.
Let
g\colon X/_{(q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1})^{\perp}}\to Z

be the unique map such that
g\mathop{\circ}\xi=\xi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}
.
We are in the following situation.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{2}}
\textstyle{X/_{(q\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1})^{\perp}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}

We claim
g
and
h_{2}
are each other’s inverse.
Indeed: from
g\mathop{\circ}h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}=g\mathop{\circ}\xi=\xi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}

we get
g\mathop{\circ}h_{2}=\mathrm{id}

and from
h_{2}\mathop{\circ}g\mathop{\circ}\xi=h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}=\xi

we find
h_{2}\mathop{\circ}g=\mathrm{id}
.
Thus
\xi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}=g\mathop{\circ}\xi
for an isomorphism
g
,
which shows
\xi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}
is a quotient, see
3.3
.
\square

†
†
margin:

XI

Exercise
By
3.3
we know that each
map
f
in an effectus with quotients factors as
t\mathop{\circ}\xi

for some total map
t
and quotient
\xi
.
Show that this forms an orthogonal factorization system (cf.
[
79
]
) —
that is: prove that if
t^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi^{\prime}=t\mathop{\circ}\xi

for some quotient
\xi^{\prime}
and total map
t^{\prime}
,
then there is a unique isomorphism
\vartheta

with
\xi^{\prime}=\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi

and
t=t^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta
.

†
†
margin:

198

In
3.3
we saw
that every map
f

factors uniquely via a quotient for
(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}
.
In this sense quotients are the universal way to restrict to the
support on the domain — so why do we call them quotients
instead of, say, initial-support-maps?
We answer this question in
3.3
,
but need some preparation first.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
For an effectus
C
,
write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\int\Pred_{\square}}

for the category with

1.

as objects pairs
(X,p)
,
where
X
is an object of
C

and
p\in\Pred X
and

2.

an morphism between
(X,p)
and
(Y,q)

corresponds to a partial map
f\colon X\to Y
in
C

for which we have
p\leqslant(q^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}
.

Write
U\colon\int\Pred_{\square}\to C

for the forgetful functor — that is:
U(X,p)=X

and
Uf=f
for arrows
f
.
The functor
U
has left- and right-adjoint

0\dashv U\dashv 1

with

0,1\colon C\to\int\Pred_{\square}

given by
0X=(X,0)
,
1X=(X,1)

and
0f=1f=f
.

†
†
margin:

III

Exercise*
Show that for an effectus
C
, the following are equivalent.

1.

C
has quotients.

2.

The functor
0\colon C\to\int\Pred_{\square}
,
has a left-adjoint
Q\colon\int\Pred_{\square}\to C
.

†
†
margin:

IV

If the existence of a left adjoint
Q
to
0

gives quotients,
what does the existence of a right adjoint
K
to
1
amount to?
It will turn out (
3.4
)
this is equivalent to the existence of
so-called
comprehensions
,
the topic of the next section.
With both quotients and comprehension, we have a chain of four adjunctions:

\textstyle{\int\Pred_{\square}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dashv\;}
\scriptstyle{\;\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\;\dashv}
\scriptstyle{Q}
\scriptstyle{\dashv\;}
\scriptstyle{K}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!1}

There are numerous other examples
of categories which also
have such a chain of four adjunctions
between it and a natural category of object-with-predicates.
See
[
22
]
for several examples.
In most of these examples, the predicates correspond to subspaces
and the left-most functor to quotienting by this subspace.
This is the reason for the name ‘effectus with quotients’.

†
†
margin:

V

Originally, we considered the universal properties of
quotients and comprehensions separately and under different names.
It was Jacobs who recognized that both universal properties
appear together in a chain of adjunctions.

3.4

Effectuses with comprehension and images

†
†
margin:

199

We continue with the formal introduction
of our second axiom:
the existence of
comprehension
.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
C
be an effectus.
We say
C

has comprehension

if for each predicate
p\colon X\to 1
,
there exists a map
\pi_{p}\colon\{X|{\kern 0.86108pt}p\}\to X

with
p\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}=1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}

satisfying the following universal property.

For any map
g\colon Z\to X

with
p\mathop{\circ}g=1\mathop{\circ}g
,
there is a unique map
g^{\prime}\colon Z\to\{X|{\kern 0.86108pt}p\}

such that
\pi_{p}\mathop{\circ}g^{\prime}=g
.

Any map with this universal property
is called a
comprehension
for
p
.

†
†
margin:

III

Beware
We do not assume comprehensions are total
(in contrast to
[
23
]
.)
In
3.4
we will see that
in an effectus with quotients, comprehensions must be total.

†
†
margin:

IIIa

Remarks
The universal property of comprehensions is essentially the same
as the universal property of (contractive)
corners
,
which we proposed in
[
126
]
.
Effectuses with compression also appear in
[
23
]
.
In partial form, comprehensions are
essentially the same
as categorical kernels, see
3.4
later on.

†
†
margin:

IV

Notation
Unless otherwise specified
\pi_{p}

will denote some comprehension of
p
.

†
†
margin:

V

Examples
In
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
comprehensions are exactly the same thing
as corners, see
2.6
or
.
An example of a comprehension for
a\in\mathscr{A}

is given by
\pi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor\mathscr{A}\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor

with
\pi(b)=\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor b\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor

as proven in
.

†
†
margin:

Va

The effectus
{\mathsf{EJA}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
also has comprehension,
which is very similar to those of
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
.
[
128
, prop. 24]

The comprehension maps of
{\mathsf{OUS}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
are again quite different
from those of
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
and
{\mathsf{EJA}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
.
Confusingly, a comprehension for a predicate
v

of an order unit space
V

is given by the vector-space-quotient
map
q\colon V\to V/_{\langle 1-v\rangle}
,
where
\langle 1-v\rangle
is the order ideal generated
by
1-v
defined in
3.3
.
The effectus
{\mathsf{OUG}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
has similar comprehension.
See
[
23
]
.

Any extensive category with final object has comprehension,
which includes
\mathsf{Set}
,
\mathsf{CH}
and
\mathsf{CRng}
,
see
[
23
]
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Exercise*
Show that an effectus
C
has comprehension if and only
if the functor
1\colon C\to\int\Pred_{\square}

from
3.3

has a right adjoint
K
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Exercise
Show the following basic properties of comprehensions.
(See
[
23
]
.)

1.

If
\pi\colon X\to Y
is a comprehension for
p

and
\vartheta\colon Z\to X
is an isomorphism,
then
\pi\mathop{\circ}\vartheta
is a comprehension for
p

as well.

2.

Conversely, if
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}

are both comprehensions for
p
,
then there is a unique isomorphism
\vartheta

with
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta
.

3.

Isomorphisms are comprehensions (for 1).

4.

Zero maps are comprehensions (for 0).

5.

Comprehensions are monic.

6.

p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi=0
if
\pi
is a comprehension for
p
.

†
†
margin:

200

Comprehensions and categorical kernels are very similar.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
C
be a category with a zero object.
A
(categorical) kernel
of an arrow
f

(in symbols:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\ker f}
)

is an equalizer of
f
with the parallel zero map.
(That is:
f\mathop{\circ}(\ker f)=0

and for every
g
with
f\mathop{\circ}g=0
,
there exists a unique
g^{\prime}
with
(\ker f)\mathop{\circ}g^{\prime}=g
.)
We will use
\cok f

to denote a cokernel of
f

— that is: a kernel of
f
in
{C}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proposition
An effectus with comprehension has all kernels.
The kernel of a map
f
is given by
a comprehension
\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}

for
(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
Clearly
1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=0

and so
f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=0
.
Assume
g
is any map with
f\mathop{\circ}g=0
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}g=0

and so
(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}g=1\mathop{\circ}g
.
Thus there exists a unique
g^{\prime}

with
\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}\mathop{\circ}g^{\prime}=g
.
\square

†
†
margin:

V

Exercise
Show that in an effectus,
a map
f
is a comprehension for
p

if and only if it is a kernel of
p^{\perp}
.

†
†
margin:

201

We are ready to define purity in effectuses.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
In an effectus a map
f
is called
pure

if
f=\pi\mathop{\circ}\xi

for some comprehension
\pi

and quotient
\xi
.

†
†
margin:

III

Example
Due to

pure maps in
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}

are precisely the pure maps
as defined in
2.6

and
.

The pure maps
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

are exactly the maps of the form
\mathrm{ad}_{T}

where
T
is a contractive map
\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Remark
In the previous chapter
we discussed (in
2.6
)
why some more familiar notions of purity for states,
like extremality,
do not generalize properly to arbitrary maps.
Recently, three other definitions of purity
have been proposed in different contexts.

[
26
,
16
,
101
]

All three are inspired by the essential uniqueness
of purification (see
2.1
)
and require a monoidal structure to state.
The identity map might not be pure
in the sense of
[
16
]
,
which is too restrictive for our taste.
Hazarding a guess,
it seems likely
that
[
26
]
considers maps pure which we don’t
and that
[
101
]
does not contain all our pure maps.
The exact relation between
our definition of pure
and
[
26
,
101
]

is left open.

†
†
margin:

V

We do not have a good handle on the structure of pure maps in
an arbitrary effectus. We will need a few additional assumptions.

†
†
margin:

202

Definition
Let
C
be an effectus.

1.

We say
C
has
images

if for each map
f\colon X\to Y
,
there is a least predicate
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\IM f}
on
Y

with the property
(\IM f)\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}f
—
that is,
for every predicate
p
on
Y

with
p\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}f
,
we must have
\IM f\leqslant p
.
For brevity, write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\IMperp f}\equiv(\IM f)^{\perp}
.
Note
\IMperp f
is the greatest predicate
with the property
(\IMperp f)\mathop{\circ}f=0
.

2.

We say a map
f\colon X\to Y
is
faithful

if
\IM f=1
.
That is:
f
is faithful if and only if

p\mathop{\circ}f=0
implies
p=0

for every predicate
p
.

†
†
margin:

II

Notation
The expression
\IM f\mathop{\circ}g

is read as
\IM(f\mathop{\circ}g)
.

†
†
margin:

III

Remarks
The predicate
\IM f
will play for comprehension the same role
as
1\mathop{\circ}f
plays for quotients.
Similarly faithful is the analogue of total.
Images in effectuses are also studied in
[
23
]
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Examples
In
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
the image of a map
f\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

is given by the least projection
e\in\mathscr{A}

with the property
f(1-e)=0
.
The map
f
is faithful if and only
if
f(a^{*}a)=0
implies
a^{*}a=0

for all
a\in\mathscr{A}
.

†
†
margin:

IVa

The effectus
{\mathsf{EJA}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
also has all images,
which are defined similarly, see
[
128
, prop. 29]
.
In contrast, the effectuses
{\mathsf{OUG}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
and
{\mathsf{OUS}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}

do not have all images, see
[
23
]
.

†
†
margin:

V

Exercise
Show
\IM f\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant\IM f
.
Conclude
\IM f\mathop{\circ}\alpha=\IM f

for any iso
\alpha
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Exercise
Show that in an effectus, any quotient is faithful.

†
†
margin:

VII

In contrast to quotients,
it is not clear at all whether (without additional assumptions)
comprehensions are total;
they are part of a factorization system
or are closed under composition.

†
†
margin:

VIII

Lemma
In an effectus with quotients, comprehensions are total.

†
†
margin:

IX

Proof
Assume
\pi
is some comprehension for
p
.
Let
\xi
be a quotient for
(1\mathop{\circ}\pi)^{\perp}
.
There is a total
\pi_{t}
with
\pi=\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}\xi
.
As
1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}\xi=1\mathop{\circ}\pi=p\mathop{\circ}\pi=p\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}\xi

and
\xi
is epi,
we see
1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}=p\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}
.
Thus, as
\pi
is a comprehension for
p
,
there exists an
f
with
\pi_{t}=\pi\mathop{\circ}f
.
Now
\pi=\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}\xi=\pi\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\xi

and so
\mathrm{id}=f\mathop{\circ}\xi
, since
\pi
is mono.
Hence
1=1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\xi\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}\xi=1\mathop{\circ}\pi

and so
\pi
is total.
\square

3.4.1

Sharp predicates

†
†
margin:

203

Definition
Let
C
be an effectus with comprehension and images.

1.

We say a predicate
p
is (image)
sharp

if
p=\IM f
for some map
f
.
Write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\SPred X}
for the set of all sharp predicates on
X
.

2.

We define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor}=\IM\pi_{p}
,
where
\pi_{p}
is some comprehension for
p
.
(Comprehensions for the same predicate have the same image
by
3.4
.)
Also write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\left\lceil p\right\rceil}=\left\lfloor p^{\perp}\right\rfloor^{\perp}
.

†
†
margin:

II

Beware
In
[
23
]

p
is called sharp whenever
p\wedge p^{\perp}=0
.
In general this is weaker than image-sharpness, which we use in this text.
In
3.4.1

we will see
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor
is sharp.
It is unclear whether
\left\lceil p\right\rceil
is sharp (without additional
assumptions).

†
†
margin:

III

Example
In
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
the sharp predicates are the projections.
For a predicate
a\in\mathscr{A}
,
the sharp predicate
\left\lceil a\right\rceil

is the least projection above
a
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Lemma
In an effectus with comprehension and images, we have

1.

\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor\leqslant p

2.

\pi_{p}=\pi_{\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor}\mathop{\circ}\alpha

for some iso
\alpha
;

3.

\left\lfloor\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor
;

4.

p\leqslant q

\implies

\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor\leqslant\left\lfloor q\right\rfloor
;

5.

\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil

and

6.

\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f=0
iff
p\mathop{\circ}f=0
,

for any map
f\colon X\to Y
,
predicates
p,q
on
X
and

\pi_{p}
and
\pi_{\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor}

comprehensions for
p
and
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor
respectively.

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
We will prove the statements in listed order.

†
†
margin:

VI

(Ad 1)
Let
\pi
be a comprehension for
p
.
By definition
p\mathop{\circ}\pi=1\mathop{\circ}\pi
.
Thus
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor=\IM\pi\leqslant p
, as desired.

†
†
margin:

VII

(Ad 2)
It is sufficient to show
\pi_{p}
is a comprehension for
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor
.
First, note
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}=(\IM\pi_{p})\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}=1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}
.
To show the universal property,
assume
g\colon Z\to X

is some map with
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor\mathop{\circ}g=1\mathop{\circ}g
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}g=\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant p\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}g

and so
1\mathop{\circ}g=p\mathop{\circ}g
.
As
\pi_{p}
is a comprehension for
p
,
there is a unique
g^{\prime}
with
\pi_{p}\mathop{\circ}g^{\prime}=g

and so
\pi_{p}
is indeed a comprehension for
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor
as well.

†
†
margin:

VIII

(Ad 3)
Follows from the previous point and
3.4
.

†
†
margin:

IX

(Ad 4)
Pick a comprehension
\pi_{p}
for
p
and
\pi_{q}
for
q
.
Note
1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}=p\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}\leqslant q\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}

so
q\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}=1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p}

and thus
\pi_{p}=\pi_{q}\mathop{\circ}f
for some
f
.
By
3.4

we see
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor=\IM\pi_{p}=\IM\pi_{q}\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\IM\pi_{q}=\left\lfloor q\right\rfloor
.

†
†
margin:

X

(Ad 5)
Clearly
p\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{(p\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=0
.
(Recall our convention
3.4
for
\pi
’s.)
Thus there is some
h

with
f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{(p\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=\pi_{p^{\perp}}\mathop{\circ}h
.
By point 2 there is some (isomorphism)
\alpha

with
\pi_{p^{\perp}}=\pi_{\left\lfloor p^{\perp}\right\rfloor}\mathop{\circ}\alpha
.
We compute

\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{(p\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p^{\perp}}\mathop{\circ}h=\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lfloor p^{\perp}\right\rfloor}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}h=\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil^{\perp}}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}h=0.

Thus
\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\IMperp\pi_{(p\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=\lfloor(p\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\rfloor^{\perp}=\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil
, as promised.

†
†
margin:

XI

(Ad 6)
Assume
\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
From
p\leqslant\left\lceil p\right\rceil

it follows
p\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
Thus
p\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
For the converse, assume
p\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
Then
\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil=\left\lceil 0\right\rceil=(\IM\mathrm{id})^{\perp}=0

as
\mathrm{id}
is a comprehension for
1
.
Thus
\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil=0
.
\square

†
†
margin:

XII

Exercise
Let
C
be an effectus with comprehension and images.
Show that
p
is sharp if and only if
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor=p
.
Conclude
\IM\pi_{s}=s
for sharp
s
.

†
†
margin:

XIII

Exercise
Show that in an effectus with comprehension and images
we have the equality

\left\lceil\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil=\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil

for any map
f\colon X\to Y
and predicate
p
on
X
.

†
†
margin:

XIV

Exercise
Show that in an effectus with
images
\IM\left<f,g\right>=[\IM f,\IM g]
.
Conclude that a predicate
[p,q]
is sharp
if and only if
p
and
q
are sharp.

†
†
margin:

204

Lemma
In an effectus with comprehension and images
we have

s\leqslant t\quad\iff\quad\pi_{s}=\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}h\quad\text{for some $h$},

for all sharp predicates
s,t
on the same object.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
(This simpler proof than ours in
[
23
]
.)
Assume
\pi_{s}=\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}h
.
Then

s\ \overset{\textsf{\ref{img-of-compr}}}{=}\ \left\lfloor s\right\rfloor\ =\ \IM\pi_{s}\ =\ \IM\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}h\ \overset{\textsf{\ref{im-ineq}}}{\leqslant}\ \IM\pi_{t}=\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor\ \overset{\textsf{\ref{img-of-compr}}}{=}\ t.

as desired.
Conversely assume
s\leqslant t
.
Then
t^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\leqslant s^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=0

and so
\pi_{s}=\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}h

for some
h
by the universal property of
\pi_{t}
.
\square

†
†
margin:

III

Lemma
In an effectus with images,
we have
\IM[f,g]=(\IM f)\vee(\IM g)
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
We get
\IM[f,g]\geqslant\IM f
and
\IM[f,g]\geqslant\IM g

from

\displaystyle[1\mathop{\circ}f,1\mathop{\circ}g]

\displaystyle\ =\ 1\mathop{\circ}[f,g]

\displaystyle\ =\ (\IM[f,g])\mathop{\circ}[f,g]

\displaystyle\ =\ [(\IM[f,g])\mathop{\circ}f,(\IM[f,g])\mathop{\circ}g].

To show
\IM[f,g]
is the least upper-bound,
assume
p\geqslant\IM f
and
p\geqslant\IM g
.
Then

1\mathop{\circ}[f,g]\ \geqslant\ p\mathop{\circ}[f,g]\ =\ [p\mathop{\circ}f,p\mathop{\circ}g]\ \geqslant\ [(\IM f)\mathop{\circ}f,(\IM g)\mathop{\circ}g]\ =\ 1\mathop{\circ}[f,g],

hence
1\mathop{\circ}[f,g]=p\mathop{\circ}[f,g]

and so
p\geqslant\IM[f,g]
, as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

V

Corollary
In an effectus with comprehension and images,
we have for any sharp
s,t

a supremum (among all predicates)
given by
s\vee t=\IM[\pi_{s},\pi_{t}]
.
In particular:
s\vee t
is sharp.

†
†
margin:

205

In
3.4
we saw that comprehensions
are precisely kernels of predicates.
What about cokernels and quotients?

†
†
margin:

II

Proposition
An effectus with quotients and images has all cokernels.
A cokernel of a map
f

is given by a quotient
\xi_{\IM f}
of
\IM f
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
As
0=(\IMperp f)\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}\xi_{\IM f}\mathop{\circ}f
,
we have
\xi_{\IM f}\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
Assume
g
is a map with
g\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant\IMperp f

and so
g=g^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{\IM f}

for a unique
g^{\prime}
.
Thus indeed,
\xi_{\IM f}
is a cokernel of
f
.
\square

†
†
margin:

IV

Exercise
Show that in an effectus with comprehension and images,
a map
f
is a quotient of sharp
s

if and only if
f
is a cokernel
of a comprehension
\pi_{s}
of
s
.

3.5

\diamond
-effectuses

†
†
margin:

206

In quantum physics it is not uncommon to restrict one’s attention
to sharp predicates (i.e. projections).
Does this restriction hurt the expressivity?
It does not: every (normal) state on a von Neumann algebra
is determined by its values on projections.
In fact, on
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
with
\dim\mathscr{H}\geqslant 3
,
Gleason’s famous theorem states that every
measure on the projections extends uniquely to a state.
We take the idea of restricting oneself to projections
one step further: we also want to restrict to projections
for our outcomes.
It is rare for quantum processes to send projections to projections
(see
3.5
),
so instead we consider the least projection above the outcome.
The simple idea of taking the restriction to sharp predicates
seriously leads to a host of interesting new notions.
We will give these right off the bat and study their relevance later on.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
A
\diamond
-effectus
(‘‘diamond effectus’’)

is an effectus with quotients, comprehension and images
such that
s^{\perp}
is sharp for every sharp predicate
s
.
In a
\diamond
-effectus,
define for
f\colon X\to Y

the following restrictions to sharp predicates.

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.11864pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.11864pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\SPred X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 46.12526pt\raise 5.21242pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.4259pt\raise-12.42209pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.061pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f^{\diamond}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 22.12035pt\raise-5.27888pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\quad\text{by}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}f^{\diamond}(s)}=\left\lceil s\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil{\@index{\@indexphrase[*par1]{$(\ )_{\diamond}$, $(\ )^{\diamond}$}}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}f_{\diamond}(s)}=\IM f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}

1.

We say maps
f\colon X\leftrightarrows Y\colon g

are
\diamond
-adjoint

if
f^{\diamond}=g_{\diamond}
.

2.

An endomap
f\colon X\to X
is
\diamond
-self-adjoint

if
f
is
\diamond
-adjoint to itself.

3.

Two maps
f,g\colon X\to Y

are
\diamond
-equivalent

if
f^{\diamond}=g^{\diamond}

(or equivalently whenever
f_{\diamond}=g_{\diamond}
,
see
3.5
.)

4.

A pure endomap
f
is
\diamond
-positive

if
f=g\mathop{\circ}g
for some
\diamond
-self-adjoint
g
.

For brevity, write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(s)}=f^{\diamond}(s^{\perp})^{\perp}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}f_{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(s)}=f_{\diamond}(s^{\perp})^{\perp}

,
{\mathsf{CvN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
,
{\mathsf{EJA}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}

and
\mathsf{Set}
are all
\diamond
-effectuses.

margin:

III

Proposition
For
f\colon X\to Y
in a
\diamond
-effectus we have

f^{\diamond}(s)\ \leqslant\ t^{\perp}\quad\iff\quad f_{\diamond}(t)\ \leqslant\ s^{\perp}

(3.9)

for all sharp
s,t
.
In other words:
f_{\diamond}
is the left order-adjoint of
f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
To start, let’s prove the order-adjunction reformulation

f_{\diamond}(s)\leqslant t\ \overset{\eqref{diamond-main-lemma}}{\iff}\ f^{\diamond}(t^{\perp})\equiv f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t)^{\perp}\leqslant s^{\perp}\ \iff\ s\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t).

To prove (
3.9
),
first assume
f^{\diamond}(s)\leqslant t^{\perp}
.
Then
s\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\left\lceil s\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil=f^{\diamond}(s)\leqslant t^{\perp}=\IMperp\pi_{t}
, where the last equality is
due to
3.4.1
.
Thus
s\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}=0

which is to say
s\leqslant\IMperp f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}
,
so
f_{\diamond}(t)=\IM f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}\leqslant s^{\perp}
.

For the converse, assume

f_{\diamond}(t)\leqslant s^{\perp}
.
Then as before (but in the other direction)
we find
s\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}=0

and so
s\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant t^{\perp}
.
Hence
f^{\diamond}(s)=\left\lceil s\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil\leqslant\lceil t^{\perp}\rceil=\left\lfloor t\right\rfloor^{\perp}=t^{\perp}
, as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

VI

Lemma
In a
\diamond
-effectus

(\ )^{\diamond}
,

(\ )^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
and

(\ )_{\diamond}

are functorial — that is

1.

(\mathrm{id})^{\diamond}=\mathrm{id}
,

2.

(f\mathop{\circ}g)^{\diamond}=g^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}f^{\diamond}
,

3.

(\mathrm{id})^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}=\mathrm{id}
,

4.

(f\mathop{\circ}g)^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}=g^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}\mathop{\circ}f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
,

5.

(\mathrm{id})_{\diamond}=\mathrm{id}
and

6.

(f\mathop{\circ}g)_{\diamond}=f_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}g_{\diamond}
.

†
†
margin:

VII

Proof
We get
(\mathrm{id})^{\diamond}=\mathrm{id}

directly from
3.4.1
.
For 2 we only need a single line:

(f\mathop{\circ}g)^{\diamond}(s)\ =\ \left\lceil s\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}g\right\rceil\ \overset{\textsf{\ref{ceiling-within-ceiling}}}{=}\ \left\lceil\left\lceil s\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}g\right\rceil\ =\ g^{\diamond}(f^{\diamond}(s)).

Note that we used that ceilings are sharp.
Point 3 and 4 follow easily from 1 and 2 respectively.
The identity
(\mathrm{id})_{\diamond}=\mathrm{id}
is again
3.4.1
.
We claim
f_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}g_{\diamond}

is left order-adjoint to
(f\mathop{\circ}g)^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
,
indeed

f_{\diamond}(g_{\diamond}(s))\leqslant t\ \iff\ g_{\diamond}(s)\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t)\ \iff\ s\leqslant g^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t))=(f\mathop{\circ}g)^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t).

Thus by uniqueness of order adjoints, we find

(f\mathop{\circ}g)_{\diamond}=f_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}g_{\diamond}
.
\square

†
†
margin:

VIIa

Exercise
Derive from
3.5

that
f^{\diamond}=g^{\diamond}

if and only if
f_{\diamond}=g_{\diamond}
.

†
†
margin:

208

Lemma
In a
\diamond
-effectus,
sharp predicates are
order sharp
---

that is:
for any predicate
p

and sharp predicate
s

with
p\leqslant s
and
p\leqslant s^{\perp}
,
we must have
p=0
.

†
†
margin:

II

Proof
Note
\left\lceil p\right\rceil\leqslant\left\lceil s\right\rceil=s

and
\left\lceil p\right\rceil\leqslant\lceil s^{\perp}\rceil=s^{\perp}
.
So by
3.4.1
,
there is an
h
with
\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}h
.
We compute

1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\ =\ \left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\ =\ \left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}h\ \leqslant\ s^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}h\ =\ 0.

Thus
\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}=0

and so
p\leqslant\left\lceil p\right\rceil=\IM\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}=0
, as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

III

Proposition (Cho)
In a
\diamond
-effectus,
the poset
\SPred X
of sharp predicates on any object
X
,
is a sub-effect algebra of
\Pred X

and an orthomodular lattice.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
Let
C
be a
\diamond
-effectus with some object
X
.
We will show
\SPred X
is a sub-effect algebra of
\Pred X
,
which is additionally an ortholattice.
By
3.1
this is sufficient to show

\SPred X
is orthomodular.

†
†
margin:

V

(Ortholattice)
In
3.4.1

we already saw that sharp
s,t
have a sharp supremum

s\vee t=\IM[\pi_{s},\pi_{t}]

in
\Pred X
.
So
s\vee t
is also the supremum of
s
and
t
in
\SPred X
.
As
(\ )^{\perp}
is an order anti-automorphism of both
\Pred X
and
\SPred X
,
we know
(s^{\perp}\vee t^{\perp})^{\perp}

is the infimum of
s
and
t
in
\Pred X
and
\SPred X
.
As any sharp predicate
s
is order sharp by
3.5
,
we find
s\wedge s^{\perp}=0
(and consequently
s^{\perp}\vee s=1
).
We have shown
\SPred X
is an ortholattice.

†
†
margin:

VI

(Sub-EA)
Clearly
0,1
are sharp and
s^{\perp}
is sharp for sharp
s

by definition of
\diamond
-effectus.
To prove
\SPred X
is a sub-effect algebra of
\Pred X
,
it only remains to be shown
s\ovee t
is sharp
for sharp and summable
s,t
.
So, assume
s,t
are sharp and
s\perp t
.
Note
s\wedge t\leqslant s\wedge s^{\perp}=0
as
\SPred X
is an ortholattice
and so by
3.1

we find
s\ovee t=s\vee t
, which is indeed sharp.
\square

†
†
margin:

VII

Corollary
In an
\diamond
-effectus
C

the assignment
X\mapsto\SPred X
,
f\mapsto(f_{\diamond},f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}})

yields a functor from
C

to
\mathsf{OMLatGal}
,
the category of orthomodular lattices
with Galois connection between them,
as defined in
[
55
]
.

†
†
margin:

VIII

There is a rather different
formula for the infima of sharp predicates,
which will be useful later on.

†
†
margin:

IX

Lemma
In a
\diamond
-effectus, we have

s\wedge t=(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}(\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t))

for sharp predicates
s,t
.

†
†
margin:

X

Proof
It is easy to see

(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}(\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t))
is a lower bound:
indeed

(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}(\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t))\leqslant t

and
(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}(\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t))\leqslant(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}(1)=\IM\pi_{s}=s
.
We have to show
(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}(\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t))
is the greatest lower bound.
Let
r
be any sharp predicate with
r\leqslant s
and
r\leqslant t
.
By
3.4.1

we have
\pi_{r}=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}h
for some
h
.
Thus

(\pi_{r})_{\diamond}\ =\ (\pi_{s})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}h_{\diamond}\ =\ (\pi_{s})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}\mathop{\circ}(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}h_{\diamond}\ =\ (\pi_{s})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}\mathop{\circ}(\pi_{r})_{\diamond}.

Hence
r=(\pi_{r})_{\diamond}(1)=((\pi_{s})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}\mathop{\circ}(\pi_{r})_{\diamond})(1)=(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}(\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(r))\leqslant(\pi_{s})_{\diamond}(\pi_{s}^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t))
.
\square

†
margin:

]

XII

Exercise
Show that in a
\diamond
-effectus,
we have

(\xi^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

for sharp
s,t
and quotient
\xi
of
s
.
