\displaystyle\ \overset{\smash{\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{dagger-iso-alpha2}}}}}{=}\ \mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}k}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}k\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\beta^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\chi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}\right\rceil}

\displaystyle\ \overset{\smash{\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{dagger-iso-omega2}}}}}{=}\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}g}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}g\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\omega^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\lceil p\mathop{\circ}k\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}k\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\beta^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\chi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}\right\rceil}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}g}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}g\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\omega^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\beta^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\chi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}\right\rceil}

\displaystyle\ \overset{\smash{\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{dagger-of-fg}}}}}{=}\ (f\mathop{\circ}g)^{\dagger},

whichever the Reader might prefer.
\square

†
†
margin:

220

We are ready to finish the proof of
3.7
.

†
†
margin:

II

Theorem
A
\dagger^{\prime}
-effectus is a
\dagger
-effectus
with
\dagger

as defined in
3.7
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
Let
C
be a
\dagger^{\prime}
-effectus.

†
†
margin:

IV

(Ax. 1)
By
3.7
,
3.7

and
3.7

the
\dagger
defined in
3.7

turns
\Pure C
into a
\dagger
-category.
Also by
3.7
,
we have
\mathrm{asrt}_{p}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{asrt}_{p}

for any predicate
p
.
Pick any pure
f
.
We have to show
f
is
\diamond
-adjoint to
f^{\dagger}
.
By
3.6

we have
f=\pi_{\IM f}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}f}

for some iso
\alpha
.
We compute

\displaystyle f_{\diamond}

\displaystyle\ =\ (\pi_{\IM f})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}\varphi_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\zeta_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}f})^{\diamond}

\displaystyle\ \overset{\mathclap{\smash{\textsf{\ref{quotcompr-diamond-adjoint}}}}}{=}\ (\zeta_{\IM f})^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}\varphi_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\pi_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil})^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}f})^{\diamond}

\displaystyle\ \overset{\mathclap{\smash{\textsf{\ref{iso-diamond-adjoint}}}}}{=}\ (\zeta_{\IM f})^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\varphi^{-1})^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\pi_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil})^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}f})^{\diamond}

\displaystyle\ =\ (\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}f}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}{\varphi^{-1}}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\IM f})^{\diamond}

\displaystyle\ =\ (f^{\dagger})^{\diamond},

so
f
is indeed
\diamond
-adjoint to
f^{\dagger}
.

†
†
margin:

V

(Ax. 2)
Let
f
be a
\dagger
-positive map.
That is:
f=h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}h
for some pure map
h
.
By
3.6

we have
h=\pi_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}h\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}

for some iso
\alpha
.
We compute

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\ =\ h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}h

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}h\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}h\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}h\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}

\displaystyle\ \overset{\smash{\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{asrt-absorp-rule}}}}}{=}\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}.

Form this it follows that
\dagger
-positive maps are
\diamond
-positive.
Let
q
be the predicate such that
q\mathbin{\&}q=1\mathop{\circ}h
.
Then

\mathrm{asrt}_{q}^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\ \overset{\textsf{\ref{andthen-square-rule}}}{=}\ \mathrm{asrt}_{q\mathbin{\&}q}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}.

Thus
\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}
is a
\dagger
-positive
with
\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}=f
.
We have to show
\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}

is the unique map with this property.
Let
g
be any
\dagger
-positive map with
g\mathop{\circ}g=f
.
Recall both
g
and
f
are
\diamond
-positive, hence

\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}f}\ =\ f\ =\ g\mathop{\circ}g\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}g}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}g}\ \overset{\smash{\textsf{\ref{andthen-square-rule}}}}{=}\ \mathrm{asrt}_{(1\mathop{\circ}g)\mathbin{\&}(1\mathop{\circ}g)}.

So
(1\mathop{\circ}g)\mathbin{\&}(1\mathop{\circ}g)=1\mathop{\circ}f=(1\mathop{\circ}h)\mathbin{\&}(1\mathop{\circ}h)
.
Hence
1\mathop{\circ}g=1\mathop{\circ}h
by uniqueness of the square root.
So
g=\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}g}=\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}=h
, as desired.

†
†
margin:

VI

(Ax. 3)
Let
\mathrm{asrt}_{p}
be any
\diamond
-positive map.
Write
q
for the unique predicate with
q\mathbin{\&}q=p
.
Then

\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{q\mathbin{\&}q}\ \overset{\smash{\textsf{\ref{andthen-square-rule}}}}{=}\ \mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{q}^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q},

so
\mathrm{asrt}_{p}
is
\dagger
-positive, as desired.
\square

3.7.1

Dilations

†
†
margin:

221

Being the main topic of the first part of this thesis,
we cannot finish our discussion of
\dagger
-effectuses
without mentioning dilations.
The presented abstract theory of dilations in a
\dagger
-effectus
is preliminary and as of yet rather unimpressive.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any map in an effectus.
A
dilation
of
f

is a triple
(P,\varrho,h)

of a sharp total map
\varrho\colon P\to Y

and a pure map
h\colon X\to P

such that
\varrho\mathop{\circ}h=f

and the following universal property holds.

For every triple
(P^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},h^{\prime})

with
\varrho^{\prime}\colon P^{\prime}\to Y
total sharp,

h^{\prime}\colon X\to P
arbitrary and
f=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}
,
there is a unique
\sigma\colon P\to P^{\prime}

with
\sigma\mathop{\circ}h=h^{\prime}

and
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=\varrho
.

We say an effectus
has dilations
if
there exists a dilation for every map.

†
†
margin:

III

Example
The effectus
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
(
3.2
) has dilations,
as shown in
2.3
,
but the full subcategory
{\mathsf{CvN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}

of commutative von Neumann algebras
does not as shown in the next exercise.

†
†
margin:

IIIa

Exercise
In this exercise you will show that
{\mathsf{CvN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}

does not have dilations.
First, show that a corner between commutative von Neumann algebras
is multiplicative.
Conclude that if
{\mathsf{CvN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
were to have dilations,
then any ncpu-map would be multiplicative, quod non.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proposition
Let
C
be an effectus with dilations.

1.

If
(P_{1},\varrho_{1},h_{1})

and
(P_{2},\varrho_{2},h_{2})

are both dilations of
f
,
then there is a unique isomorphism
\alpha\colon P_{1}\to P_{2}

with
\alpha\mathop{\circ}h_{1}=h_{2}
and
\varrho_{2}\mathop{\circ}\alpha=\varrho_{1}
.

2.

Conversely,
if
(P,\varrho,h)

is a dilation of
f
and
\alpha\colon P\to P^{\prime}
is some isomorphism,
then
(P^{\prime},\varrho\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1},\alpha\mathop{\circ}h)

is also a dilation of
f
.

3.

(X,\varrho,\mathrm{id})
is the dilation
of a sharp total map
\varrho
.

4.

If
(P,\varrho,h)
is some dilation,
then
(P,\mathrm{id},h)
is the dilation of
h
.

5.

For any quotient
\xi\colon X\to Q

and map
f\colon Q\to Y

with dilation
(P,\varrho,h)
,
the triple
(P,\varrho,h\mathop{\circ}\xi)

is a dilation of
f\mathop{\circ}\xi
.

6.

Conversely, if
(P,\varrho,h)

is a dilation of
f\mathop{\circ}\xi

for a map
f\colon Q\to Y

and quotient
\xi\colon X\to Q
,
then
(P,\varrho,h^{\prime\prime})
is a dilation of
f
,
where
h^{\prime\prime}\colon Q\to P
is the unique map with
h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi=h
.

7.

If for
i=1,2
the triple
(P_{i},\varrho_{i},h_{i})

is a dilation of
f_{i}\colon X_{i}\to Y
,
then
(P_{1}+P_{2},[\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}],h_{1}+h_{2})

is a dilation of
[f_{1},f_{2}]\colon X_{1}+X_{2}\to Y
.

†
†
margin:

V

Proof
Point 1 has been proven in
2.1
.
Point 2 is easily verified.
To prove point 3, assume
\varrho
is a sharp total map.
Assume
\varrho=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}
for some
h^{\prime}
and sharp total
\varrho^{\prime}
.
Then
h^{\prime}
is clearly the unique map
satisfying
h^{\prime}=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}
(and
\varrho=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}
),
which demonstrates point 3.

For point 4, assume
(P,\varrho,h)
is some dilation.
Assume
h=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}
for some
h^{\prime}
and sharp total
\varrho^{\prime}
.
There is a unique
\sigma
with
h^{\prime}=\sigma\mathop{\circ}h

and
\varrho\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=\varrho
.
Now
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma\mathop{\circ}h=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}=h
.
So
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma

satisfies the properties of the unique mediating map
\mathrm{id}\colon P\to P
,
hence
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=\mathrm{id}
.
Only uniqueness of
\sigma
remains,
so assume
\sigma^{\prime}
is a ncp-map with
h^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h

and
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}
.
Then
\varrho\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}=\varrho

and so
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
.

To demonstrate point 5, assume
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}=f\mathop{\circ}\xi

for some
h^{\prime}
and total sharp
\varrho^{\prime}
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}=1\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}=1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\xi\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}\xi
,
so there is a unique
h^{\prime\prime}

with
h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi=h^{\prime}
.
By epicity of
\xi
,
we get
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime\prime}=f

from
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}=f\mathop{\circ}\xi
.
Thus there is a unique
\sigma

with
\sigma\mathop{\circ}h=h^{\prime\prime}
and
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=\varrho

and so
\sigma\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\xi=h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi=h^{\prime}
.
Only uniqueness of
\sigma
remains,
so suppose
\sigma^{\prime}
is a map with
\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\xi=h^{\prime}

and
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=\varrho
.
Then
\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h=h^{\prime\prime}
by epicity of
\xi
,
hence
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
as desired.

For point 6, assume
f=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}

for some
h^{\prime}
and sharp total
\varrho^{\prime}
.
Then
f\mathop{\circ}\xi=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi

and so there is a unique
\sigma

with
\varrho\mathop{\circ}\sigma=\varrho^{\prime}

and
\sigma\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi=h\equiv h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi
.
Thus
\sigma\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}=h^{\prime\prime}
by epicity of
\xi
.
To show uniqueness,
assume
\sigma^{\prime}
is such that
\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}=h^{\prime\prime}

and
\varrho\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}=\varrho^{\prime}
.
Then
\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi=h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}xi=h

and indeed
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
.

To show point 7, assume
[f_{1},f_{2}]=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}

for some
h^{\prime}
and total sharp
\varrho^{\prime}
.
For any
i=1,2

we have
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{i}=f_{i}
,
so there is a unique
\sigma_{i}

with
\sigma_{i}\mathop{\circ}h_{i}=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{i}

and
\varrho_{i}=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{i}
.
Define
\sigma=[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]
.
Clearly
\sigma\mathop{\circ}(h_{1}+h_{2})=[\sigma_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1},\sigma_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}]=[h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1},h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}]=h^{\prime}

and
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=[\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{1},\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{2}]=[\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}]
.
Assume
\sigma^{\prime}
is any ncp-map with
\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}(h_{1}+h_{2})=h^{\prime}

and
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}=[\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}]
.
For
i=1,2

we have
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{i}=\varrho_{i}

and
h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{i}=\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}(h_{1}+h_{2})\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{i}=\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h_{i}
,
so
\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{i}=\sigma_{i}
.
Hence
\sigma^{\prime}=[\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1},\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}]=[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]=\sigma
.

\square

†
†
margin:

222

With some additional assumptions,
the existence of dilations
forces the existence of some
familiar quantum gates.
Assume
C
is a
\dagger
-effectus with dilation
with a scalar
\lambda
such that
\lambda^{\perp}=\lambda
.
(For instance:
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
in
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
.)
Let
(P,\langle s,s^{\perp}\rangle,h)
be a dilation
of the map
\left<h,h^{\perp}\right>\colon 1\to 1+1
.
We think of
P
as an abstract qubit
— indeed in
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
any
dilation of
\left<\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right>

is of the form
(M_{2},\langle e,e^{\perp}\rangle,h)

where
h
is the vector state for some
v\in\mathbb{C}^{2}

and
e
is a projector on
w\mathbb{C}
,
with
\left<v,w\right>=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

(for instance
v=\ket{0}
and
w=\ket{+}
).

†
†
margin:

II

By assumption
\langle s,s^{\perp}\rangle\mathop{\circ}h=\langle\lambda,\lambda\rangle
,
so
s\mathop{\circ}h=s^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}h=\lambda
.
and
\langle s^{\perp},s\rangle\mathop{\circ}h=\langle\lambda,\lambda\rangle
.
Hence by the definition of dilation,
there is a unique map
X\colon P\to P

with
X\mathop{\circ}h=h

and
\langle s^{\perp},s\rangle\mathop{\circ}X=\langle s,s^{\perp}\rangle
,
whence
s\mathop{\circ}X=s^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}X

and
s^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}X=s\mathop{\circ}X
.
Clearly
X\mathop{\circ}X\mathop{\circ}h=h

and
\langle s,s^{\perp}\rangle\mathop{\circ}X\mathop{\circ}X=\langle s,s^{\perp}\rangle
,
so by uniqueness of the mediating map
\mathrm{id}\colon P\to P
,
we must have
X\mathop{\circ}X=\mathrm{id}
.
Thus the isomorphism
X
behaves like the
X
-gate
— indeed, in
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
, we have
X=\mathrm{ad}_{X}

if
e=\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|
.

†
†
margin:

III

For the next gate,
assume
s
is pure and
\IM s=1
.
Note
s^{\dagger}
is total.
As
\mathrm{asrt}_{\lambda}=\lambda
,
we have
h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}s^{\dagger}=\lambda^{\dagger}=\lambda

and so
(h^{\dagger})^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}s^{\dagger}=\lambda^{\perp}=\lambda
.
Combined:
\langle h^{\dagger},(h^{\dagger})^{\perp}\rangle\mathop{\circ}s^{\dagger}=\langle\lambda,\lambda\rangle
.
Thus using the universal property of the dilation,
there is a unique map
H\colon P\to P

with
H\mathop{\circ}h=s^{\dagger}

and
\langle h^{\dagger},(h^{\dagger})^{\perp}\rangle\mathop{\circ}H=\langle s,s^{\perp}\rangle
.
So
h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}H=s
.
In general, we do not know a lot more about
H
.
Assume
H
is an comprehension (as it is in
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
in this situation).
Then
s^{\dagger}=H^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}h
and

s=h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}H^{\dagger}
,
so
s^{\perp}=(h^{\dagger})^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}H^{\dagger}
,
whence
\langle s,s^{\perp}\rangle=\langle h^{\dagger},(h^{\dagger})^{\perp}\rangle\mathop{\circ}H^{\dagger}
.
Thus, by uniqueness of
H
, we must have
H^{\dagger}=H
.
Thus both
H^{\dagger}
and
H
are total,
which forces
H\mathop{\circ}H^{\dagger}=H^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}H=\mathrm{asrt}_{1}=\mathrm{id}
,
by uniqueness of
\diamond
-positive maps.
Hence
H\mathop{\circ}H=\mathrm{id}
,

s\mathop{\circ}H=h^{\dagger}

and
h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}H=s
.
So
H
is similar to the Hadamard-gate
— indeed, in
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
, we have
H=\mathrm{ad}_{H}
if
e=\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Do our additional assumptions (purity of
s
,
\IM s=1
, etc.)
follow from more general principles? Can we define the other gates, like
the CNOT? Do these gates interact in the usual way?
Unfortunately, we do not have an answer to any of these questions.

†
†
margin:

223

In
2.3

we saw that for any ncp-map
\varphi

with Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
,
there is a linear order
isomorphism
[0,1]_{\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}\cong[0,\varphi]_{\mathrm{ncp}}
.
To finish the discussion of dilations,
we mention how to state this correspondence
in an
\&
-effectus.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
In an
\&
-effectus,
for any predicate
p
,
define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\mathrm{seff}_{p}}\equiv\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\ovee\mathrm{asrt}_{p^{\perp}}
.

This map models the side-effect of measuring
p
.
For any map
f\colon X\to Y
, define

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\Inv f}\ \equiv\ \{p;\ p\in\Pred X;\ f\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{seff}_{p}=f\}.{\@index{\@indexphrase[Inv]{$\Inv f$}}}

This is the set of predicates whose measurement does not disturb
f
.

†
†
margin:

III

Lemma
In
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
, for any nmiu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
,
we have
\Inv\varrho=[0,1]_{\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}
—
that is:
\Inv\varrho

is the set of effects of the commutator of the image of
\varrho
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
Assume
a\in[0,1]_{f(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}
.
Simply unfolding definitions, we find
\mathrm{seff}_{a}(\varrho(b))=\sqrt{a}\varrho(b)\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{1-a}\varrho(b)\sqrt{1-a}=\varrho(b)
for any
b\in\mathscr{A}
, so
a\in\Inv\varrho
.
For the converse, assume
a\in\Inv\varrho
.
Recall
(\mathscr{A},\varrho,\mathrm{id})
is a Paschke dilation of
\varrho
.
As

\varrho\ =\ \mathrm{seff}_{a}\mathop{\circ}\varrho\ =\ (\mathrm{asrt}_{a}\mathop{\circ}\varrho)\ovee(\mathrm{asrt}_{1-a}\mathop{\circ}\varrho)\ \geqslant_{\mathrm{ncp}}\ \mathrm{asrt}_{a}\mathop{\circ}\varrho,

we know by
2.3

that there is a
t\in[0,1]_{\varrho(\mathscr{A})^{\square}}

with
\mathrm{asrt}_{a}(\varrho(b))\equiv\sqrt{a}\varrho(b)\sqrt{a}=t\varrho(b)
for all
b\in\mathscr{A}
,
but then clearly
a=\sqrt{a}\varrho(1)\sqrt{a}=t\varrho(1)=t
.
\square

†
†
margin:

V

Definition
Let
C
be an
\&
-effectus.
For a map
f\colon X\to Y
,
write

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\downarrow\!f}\ \equiv\ \{g;\ g\colon X\to Y;\ g\leqslant f\}.

We say a dilation
(P,\varrho,h)
of
f

has
the order correspondence
if

there is an order isomorphism
\Theta\colon\downarrow\!f\to\Inv\varrho

such that for every
g\in\downarrow\!f
,
we have

g\ =\ \varrho\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{\Theta(g)}\mathop{\circ}h.

†
†
margin:

VI

Example
By
2.3

every dilation in
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}

has the order correspondence.

3.8

Comparisons

3.8.1

Dagger kernel categories

†
†
margin:

224

To finish this chapter, we relate the structures in a
\dagger
-effectus
with existing structures in the literature.
Just like we try to axiomatize
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
,
Heunen in the third chapter of his Ph.D-thesis attempted
to axiomatize
\mathsf{Hilb}
,
the category of Hilbert spaces with bounded operators.
Heunen came very close:
he proves that a
\dagger
-category obeying
certain additional axioms must embed into
\mathsf{Hilb}
.

[
49
, 3.7.18]

We will discover that some of his axioms for
\mathsf{Hilb}

hold in
\Pure(C)
for a
\dagger
-effectus
C

and others do not in general.
(We do not cover all his axioms.)

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let
C
be a
\dagger
-category.
We say that an arrow
f

is
\dagger
-mono

iff
f^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}f=\mathrm{id}

and dually
f
is
\dagger
-epi

iff
f\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}
.
An arrow
f
is a
\dagger
-partial isometry

if
f=m\mathop{\circ}e

for some
\dagger
-mono
m

and
\dagger
-epi
e
.

A
\dagger
-kernel
of
f

is an equalizer of
f
with
0
, which is
\dagger
-mono.
A
\dagger
-kernel category
is a
\dagger
-category

with zero object and where each arrow has a
\dagger
-kernel

[
49
, 3.2.20]
.

†
†
margin:

III

Proposition
In
\Pure(C)
for some
\dagger
-effectus
C
,
the following holds.

1.

f
is
\dagger
-mono iff
f
is a comprehension.

Dually:

f
is
\dagger
-epi iff
f
is a quotient of a sharp predicate.

2.

The
\dagger
-partial isometries are exactly the pristine maps, see
3.7
.

Furthermore:
\Pure C
is a
\dagger
-kernel category:
the
\dagger
-kernel of
f
is given by
\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Proof
Let
f
be any
\dagger
-mono
pure map, say
f\equiv\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}

for some iso
\alpha
,
s\equiv\IM f
and
p\equiv 1\mathop{\circ}f
.
As we saw before (using
3.6
),
we see
\mathrm{id}=f^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}f=\mathrm{asrt}_{p^{2}}
.
Thus
p\mathbin{\&}p=p^{2}=1=1\mathbin{\&}1

and so by uniqueness of the square root
we get
p=1
.
Hence
f
is a comprehension.

Dualizing, we see
\dagger
-epis are exactly quotients of sharp predicates.
Now it is clear that the
\dagger
-partial isometries
are exactly the pristine maps.

†
†
margin:

V

By the previous
\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}
is
\dagger
-mono.
To see it is the
\dagger
-kernel of
f
,
we have to show it is the equalizer of
f
with
0
.
Clearly
(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=1

and so
1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=0
,
whence
f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}=0
.
Assume
f\mathop{\circ}g=0
for some pure map
g
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}g=0
,
so
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\IMperp g
,
hence
(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\geqslant\IM g
,
so
g=\pi_{(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}}\mathop{\circ}g^{\prime}

for a unique
g^{\prime}
.
\square

†
†
margin:

VI

Exercise*
Let
C
be a
\dagger
-effectus.
In this exercise, you will show that
in general
\Pure C

does not have finite coproducts.
In particular,
\Pure C
does not have
\dagger
-biproducts,
see
[
49
, 3.2.15]
.
Consider
C\equiv{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
.
Reasoning towards contradiction,
assume
(\Pure{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})^{\mathsf{op}}

has a product
\mathbb{C}\xleftarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathbb{C}

of
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}
.
First show that for any non-zero pure map
f\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}
,
there exists a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
, an element
x\in\mathscr{H}
,
a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{C}
and
isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{A}

with
f(varphi(T,c))=\langle x,Tx\rangle
.
Use this on
\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}
and
\langle\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}}\rangle

to show there is an
isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\mathscr{C}\cong\mathscr{A}

with
\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varphi=\left<x,\,(\cdot\,)x\right>

and
\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\varphi=\left<y,(\,\cdot\,)y\right>

for some von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{C}
,
Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
and
x,y\in\mathscr{H}
.
Press on: show
\mathscr{C}
is trivial.
Then prove
\dim\mathscr{H}\leqslant 2
.
Almost there: show
\dim\mathscr{H}\geqslant 2
.
Derive a contradiction.

†
†
margin:

VII

Exercise*
In this exercise you will show that
\Pure({\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})
does not have
all coequalizers. It is helpful to first consider
two concrete coequalizers in
\mathsf{Hilb}
and
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
.
(These coequalizers capture unordered pairs of qubits.)

Write
\sigma\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}

for the unitary
fixed by
\sigma\ket{ij}=\ket{ji}
.
The equalizer of
\sigma
with
\mathrm{id}
exists in
\mathsf{Hilb}

and is given by
e_{\mathscr{S}}
, the inclusion
of the subspace
\mathscr{S}

spanned by
\{\ket{00},\ket{11},\ket{01}+\ket{10}\}

into
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
(
\mathscr{S}
is called the symmetric tensor of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with itself.)
The coequalizer of
\sigma
with
\mathrm{id}
is simply
e_{\mathscr{S}}^{\dagger}
.
There is also an equalizer of
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\colon M_{4}\to M_{4}
with
\mathrm{id}

in
\mathsf{vN}
, but curiously enough, it is given
by
e\colon M_{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}\to M_{4}
,
where
e(a,\lambda)=\mathrm{ad}_{e^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{S}}}(a)+\mathrm{ad}_{e^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{A}}}(\lambda)

and
\mathscr{A}
is the subspace spanned by
\ket{01}-\ket{10}
.
For the proof and similar results, see
[
35
]
.

Assume
\xi\colon\mathscr{C}\to M_{4}

is some filter (i.e. quotient in the opposite category)
with
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\mathop{\circ}\xi=\xi
.
Show that for each
b\in\mathscr{C}
,
we have
\xi(b)\sigma=\sigma\xi(b)
.
From this, derive (perhaps in the same way as
[
35
]
),
that
\xi(b)=p_{\mathscr{A}}\xi(b)p_{\mathscr{A}}+p_{\mathscr{S}}\xi(b)p_{\mathscr{S}}

for all
b\in\mathscr{C}
,
where
p_{\mathscr{A}}=e_{\mathscr{A}}e_{\mathscr{A}}^{\dagger}

and
p_{\mathscr{S}}=e_{\mathscr{S}}e_{\mathscr{S}}^{\dagger}
.
Prove that either
\xi(b)\leqslant p_{\mathscr{A}}
for all
b\in\mathscr{C}

or
\xi(b)\leqslant p_{\mathscr{S}}
for all
b\in\mathscr{C}
.
Conclude
\Pure({\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})
does not have all coequalizers.

†
†
margin:

VIII

To summarize:
the category
\Pure(C)

of a
\dagger
-effectus
C

is a
\dagger
-category,
has
\dagger
-kernels
and each
\dagger
-mono is a
\dagger
-kernel,
but in general it does not have
\dagger
-biproducts or

\dagger
-equalizers,
which shows it is not, in general,
a pre-Hilbert category as in
[
49
, 3.7.1]
.

†
†
margin:

VIIIa

Recently Tull axiomatized
[
115
]

the
\dagger
-category
\mathsf{Hilb}_{\sim}

of Hilbert spaces with bounded operators between
them modulo global phase (i.e.
T=S
in
\mathsf{Hilb}_{\sim}

iff
T=\lambda S
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C},|\lambda|=1
.)
Write
\mathsf{Hilb}^{c}_{\sim}

for the restruction of
\mathsf{Hilb}_{\sim}

to contractive operators.
This category
\mathsf{Hilb}^{c}_{\sim}

is a full
\dagger
-subcategory of
\Pure({\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})

via the functor

\mathscr{H}\ \mapsto\ \mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}),\quad\bigl(T\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{K}\bigr)\ \mapsto\ \bigl(\mathrm{ad}_{T}\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\bigr).

The category
\mathsf{Hilb}^{c}_{\sim}

(and in fact
\mathsf{Hilb}_{\sim}
)
do not have all (co)products.
Tull shows that
\mathsf{Hilb}_{\sim}
does have

phased biproducts
.
[
115
,
114
]

This raises the question: do the phased biproducts
of
\mathsf{Hilb}_{\sim}
extend to
\Pure({\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})
.
If this is the case, then a
phased product of
\mathbb{C}
with itself in
\Pure({\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})

is given by:
\left|0\right>\!\left<0\right|,\left|1\right>\!\left<1\right|\colon\mathbb{C}\to M_{2}
.
Unfortunately this leads to a contradiction.
It hasn’t been determined yet whether
\Pure({\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})

has phased products at all.

3.8.2

Sequential product

†
†
margin:

225

The sequential product — the
operation
a\mathbin{\&}b\equiv\sqrt{a}b\sqrt{a}

on the effects of a von Neumann algebra
— has been studied before.
In
[
47
,
37
]

Gudder and coauthors establish some basic algebraic properties of
\&

on
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
.
Most of them are surprisingly hard to prove —
for instance, the proof that
a\mathbin{\&}b=b\mathbin{\&}a

implies
ab=ba
, requires
the Fuglede–Rosenblum–Putnam Theorem.

†
†
margin:

II

Then, in
[
46
]
,
Gudder and Latrémolière (G&L)
characterize
\&
on
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
as the unique operation satisfying
(for effects
a,b
and density matrix
\varrho
)

1.

\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}[(a\mathbin{\&}\varrho)b]=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}[\varrho(a\mathbin{\&}b)]

2.

a\mathbin{\&}1=1\mathbin{\&}a=a

3.

a\mathbin{\&}(a\mathbin{\&}b)=(a\mathbin{\&}a)\mathbin{\&}b=a^{2}\mathbin{\&}b

4.

a\mapsto a\mathbin{\&}b
is strongly continuous

In
we characterized
\mathrm{asrt}_{a}\colon b\mapsto a\mathbin{\&}b

as the unique
\diamond
-positive map with
a\mathbin{\&}1=a
.
How do these characterizations compare?
G&L’s first axiom plays a very similar role
as the
\diamond
-self adjointness of
\diamond
-positive maps for us.
Their third axiom is somewhat related to
\diamond
-positivity.
Their fourth axiom seems unrelated to our characterization
and conversely the purity of our
\diamond
-positive maps
has no counterpart in their axioms.

†
†
margin:

III

A few years earlier, Gudder and Greechie started

[
47
]

the abstract study of some of the algebraic properties of the
sequential product on arbitrary effect algebras,
which has been picked up by several other authors

[
83
,
44
,
104
,
45
,
72
,
122
,
111
,
71
,
6
,
120
,
119
]
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Definition
A
sequential effect algebra
(SEA)

is an effect algebra
E
together
with a binary operation
\&

satisfying

(S1)

if
a\perp b
,
then
c\mathbin{\&}a\perp c\mathbin{\&}b

and
(c\mathbin{\&}a)\ovee(c\mathbin{\&}b)=c\mathbin{\&}(a\ovee b)
;

(S2)

1\mathbin{\&}a=a
;

(S3)

if
a\mathbin{\&}b=0
,
then
a\mathbin{\&}b=b\mathbin{\&}a
;

(S4)

if
a\mathbin{\&}b=b\mathbin{\&}a
,
then
a\mathbin{\&}b^{\perp}=b^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}a

and
(a\mathbin{\&}b)\mathbin{\&}c=a\mathbin{\&}(b\mathbin{\&}c)

and

(S5)

if
c\mathbin{\&}a=a\mathbin{\&}c
,

c\mathbin{\&}b=b\mathbin{\&}c
and
a\perp b
,

then
c\mathbin{\&}(a\mathbin{\&}b)=(a\mathbin{\&}b)\mathbin{\&}c

and
c\mathbin{\&}(a\ovee b)=(a\ovee b)\mathbin{\&}c
.

†
†
margin:

V

Examples
The effect algebra
[0,1]_{\mathscr{A}}

of effects on a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{A}

is a sequential effect algebra with
a\mathbin{\&}b=\sqrt{a}b\sqrt{a}
.
Any commutative effect monoid is a sequential effect algebra
with
a\mathbin{\&}b=a\odot b
.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proposition
In a
\dagger
-effectus,
the set of predicates
\Pred X
on any object
X

with
p\mathbin{\&}q=q\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}

satisfies axioms (S1), (S2) and (S3) of a SEA.

†
†
margin:

VII

Proof
The proofs of (S1) and (S2) are obvious.
To show (S3), assume
p\mathbin{\&}q=0

for some predicates
p,q
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}=p\mathbin{\&}q=0

and so
\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}=0=\mathrm{asrt}_{0}
.
Applying the dagger, we find

0=\mathrm{asrt}_{0}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{asrt}_{p}^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}
.
Thus
q\mathbin{\&}p=0=p\mathbin{\&}q
, as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

VIII

Remark
It’s unclear whether the set of predicates in a
\dagger
-effectus
forms a sequential effect algebra.
A seemingly related open problem
is whether
p\mathbin{\&}q=q\mathbin{\&}p

implies that
\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}=\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}
.

3.8.3

Homological categories

†
†
margin:

226

Next we compare our effectuses
to Grandis’ homological categories
[
41
]
,
which generalize abelian categories
used in homological algebra.
First, we need a lemma.

†
†
margin:

II

Lemma
Suppose
s,t
are any sharp predicates on the same object in a
\dagger
-effectus.
If
s^{\perp}\leqslant t
,
then
s\mathbin{\&}t
is sharp.

†
†
margin:

III

Proof
By
3.5
,
it is sufficient to show

s\mathbin{\&}t^{\perp}
is sharp
as
(s\mathbin{\&}t)^{\perp}=s\mathbin{\&}t^{\perp}\ovee s^{\perp}
.
Note
s^{\perp}\perp t^{\perp}
,
so by
3.6
,
we find
t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s^{\perp}=0
,
hence
t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s=t^{\perp}
is sharp.
By the same reasoning as in the final
part of the proof of
3.7
,
there exists a (unique) pristine map
l

with
\mathrm{asrt}_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}}=l\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}
,

l\mathop{\circ}l^{\dagger}=\mathrm{asrt}_{\left\lceil t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s\right\rceil}

and
l^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}l=\mathrm{asrt}_{\left\lceil s\mathbin{\&}t^{\perp}\right\rceil}
.
It follows
l\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}\mathop{\circ}l^{\dagger}=\mathrm{asrt}_{s\mathbin{\&}t^{\perp}}
and so

\displaystyle\mathrm{asrt}_{s\mathbin{\&}t^{\perp}}^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ l\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}\mathop{\circ}l^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}l\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}\mathop{\circ}l^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\ =\ l\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{\left\lceil s\mathbin{\&}t^{\perp}\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}\mathop{\circ}l^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\ \overset{\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{asrt-absorp-rule}}}}{=}\ l\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}\mathop{\circ}l^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\ \overset{\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{sharp-prop}}}}{=}\ l\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t^{\perp}\mathbin{\&}s}\mathop{\circ}l^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{s\mathbin{\&}t^{\perp}},

which shows
s\mathbin{\&}t^{\perp}
is sharp.
\square

†
†
margin:

IV

Definition
A category
C
is a
pointed semiexact category

[
41
, §1.1]

if
C
has a zero object and all kernels and cokernels.
In a pointed semiexact category:

1.

We put a partial order on kernels in the usual way:
we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}n\leqslant m}

for kernels
n\colon A\to X
and
m\colon B\to X
,
if there is an
f\colon A\to B

with
n=m\mathop{\circ}f
.
If both
n\leqslant m
and
m\leqslant n
,
then we write
n\approx m
.
We denote the poset of kernels on
A
modulo
\approx

by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}\Nsb A}
. (
N
ormal
s
ubo
b
jects,

[
41
, §1.5]
.)

2.

For every map
f

there is a unique map
g

with
f=(\ker\cok f)\mathop{\circ}g\mathop{\circ}(\cok\ker f)
.
If this
g
is an iso, then
f
is called
exact
.

A pointed semiexact category
C

is a
pointed homological category
if

1.

kernels are closed under composition;

2.

cokernels are closed under composition
and

3.

(homology axiom)

for any kernel
m\colon M\to A

and cokernel
q\colon A\to Q

with
\ker q\leqslant m
,
the composition
q\mathop{\circ}m
is exact.

†
†
margin:

V

Theorem
Any
\dagger
-effectus (in partial form) is a (pointed) homological category:

1.

a map is a kernel iff it is a comprehension;

2.

a map is a cokernel iff it is a quotient of a sharp predicate
and

3.

a map is exact iff it is pristine.

†
†
margin:

VI

Proof
Let
C
be a
\dagger
-effectus.
By
3.4
and
3.4.1
,
we know
C
has all kernels and cokernels:
a kernel of
f
is exactly a comprehension of
(1\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}

and a cokernel of
f
is exactly a quotient of
\IM f
.
By
3.6
,
comprehensions are closed under composition,
and so kernels are closed under composition as well.
As for the cokernels,
assume
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}
are two composable cokernels.
Both
\zeta_{1}
and
\zeta_{2}
are quotients of a sharp predicate.
By
3.3
the composition
\zeta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{2}

is a quotient as well.
The map
\zeta_{2}
is sharp by
3.3

and so the predicate
1\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{2}
is sharp.
Hence
\zeta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{2}
is a cokernel too.
Unfolding definitions, it is easy to see exact maps correspond precisely
to pristine maps.

†
†
margin:

VII

(Homology axiom)
We have to show
q\mathop{\circ}m

is exact for any cokernel
q
and kernel
m
with
\ker q\leqslant m
.
As
q\mathop{\circ}m
is pure,
it is sufficient to show
1\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}m
is sharp.
By
3.4.1
,
the assumption
\ker q\leqslant m

is equivalent to
(1\mathop{\circ}q)^{\perp}\equiv\IM\ker q\leqslant\IM m

and so
\IMperp m\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}q
.
Our lemma
3.8.3

shows
(\IM m)\mathbin{\&}(1\mathop{\circ}q)\equiv 1\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}m\mathop{\circ}m^{\dagger}
is sharp
and so

\displaystyle\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}m\right\rceil

\displaystyle\ =\ \left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}m\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}m^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}m

as comprehensions are
\dagger
-mono

\displaystyle\ =\ \left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}m\mathop{\circ}m^{\dagger}\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}m

as
m^{\dagger}
is sharp

\displaystyle\ =\ 1\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}m\mathop{\circ}m^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}m

\displaystyle\ =\ 1\mathop{\circ}q\mathop{\circ}m,

which show
q\mathop{\circ}m
is sharp, as desired.
\square

†
†
margin:

227

In any homological category, a generalization
of the famous
Snake Lemma
holds.
Before we can discuss it, we need some more homological category theory.

†
†
margin:

II

Definition
Let a (pointed) semiexact category be given.

1.

We say the diagram
A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}C

is
exact at

B
,

if
\ker\cok(f)\approx\ker(g)
.
We say
A_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}A_{2}\to\cdots\to A_{n}

is a
(long) exact sequence

if it is exact at
A_{2},\ldots,A_{n-1}
.

2.

The poset
\Nsb A
of kernels modulo
\approx

is a bounded lattice with minimum
0
and maximum
1\equiv\mathrm{id}
.

[
41
, §1.5]

3.

For any
f\colon A\to B
,
define
f_{*}\colon\Nsb A\leftrightarrows\Nsb B\colon f^{*}

by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}f_{*}}(k)=\ker\cok(f\mathop{\circ}k)

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.75}f^{*}}(k)=\ker((\cok k)\mathop{\circ}f)
.

4.

We say
f
is
left-modular at

k

if
f^{*}(f_{*}(k))=k\vee f^{*}(0)

and
f
is
right-modular at

k

if
f_{*}(f^{*}(k))=k\wedge f_{*}(1)
.
We call
f
simply
left-modular
(resp. right-modular)
if it is left-modular (resp. left-modular) at every
k
.
We say
f
is
modular

if it is both left- and right-modular.

†
†
margin:

III

Example
In a
\dagger
-effectus, the previous concepts are related
to our familiar notions as follows.

1.

A diagram
A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}C

is exact at
B

if and only if
\IMperp f=\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil
.

2.

The lattice
\Nsb A
is isomorphic to the lattice
\SPred A

via
k\mapsto\IM k
.

3.

For any
f
, we
have
\IM f_{*}(k)=f_{\diamond}(\IM k)

and
\IM f^{*}(k)=f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(\IM k)
.

4.

A map
f
is left-modular at
k

iff
f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(f_{\diamond}(\IM k))=(\IM k)\vee\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil

and right-modular at
k

iff
f_{\diamond}(f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(\IM k))=(\IM k)\wedge\IM f
.

†
†
margin:

IV

Notation
As
\Nsb A
and
\SPred A
are isomorphic,
we will abbreviate “
f
is left-modular at
\pi_{s}
”
by “
f
is left-modular at
s
”
and similarly for right-modularity.

margin:

Lemma
In a
\dagger
-effectus,
we have
\pi^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }

and
\zeta_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}\zeta^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}=\mathrm{id}

for any comprehension
\pi

and sharp quotient
\zeta
.

margin:

VI

Proof
We start with
\pi
. The trick is to use
\pi^{\dagger}
is sharp:

\pi^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }

.
As for the other equality:

\zeta_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}\zeta^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

.

228

The following is a translation of (a part of) Grandis’ Snake
Lemma
[
41
, §3.4]

into
\dagger
-effectus jargon.
We include a proof as it highlights how
the reasoning in a homological category is subtly different
from the reasoning we already saw in a
\dagger
-effectus:
maps
l^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
appear, where we would have expected
l^{\diamond}
.

†
†
margin:

II

Snake Lemma
Suppose we have a commuting
diagram in
\dagger
-effectus as follows.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}}

Furthermore, assume

1.

the diagram has exact rows;

2.

b
is left-modular over
\IM f
;

3.

b
is right-modular over
\IM h
;

4.

f
is right-modular over
\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}b\right\rceil^{\perp}

and

5.

k
is left-modular over
\IM b
.

These additional assumptions are equivalent to the following conditions.

1.

b^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}\mathop{\circ}b_{\diamond}(\IM f)=\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}b\right\rceil^{\perp}\vee\IM f
,

2.

III

Proof
Before we start, we give an overview of the proof.
We will first show
there are comprehensions
m,h^{\prime}

and sharp quotients
g^{\prime},v

such that the left and right faces of the following cube commute.

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{a_{\zeta}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{Q}
\textstyle{\ker c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\cok a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}

(These two faces are, what Grandis calls, subquotients.)
Then we will prove using exactness of the rows
that there is a unique lifting of
b

to
b^{\prime}\colon Z\to A^{\prime}
along the comprehensions
m,h
.
In turn,
d
is defined as the unique lifting of
b^{\prime}

along the sharp quotients
h^{\prime},a_{\zeta}
.
(
d
is the map regularly induced by
b
along the two subquotients.)
Before demonstrating the exactness of the snake,
we need some non-trivial identities for
d_{\diamond}
and
d^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
.
(These also follow from Grandis’ calculus of direct and inverse images
along an induced morphism.)

IV

Define
v\equiv\xi_{h_{\diamond}(\IM a)}
.
As
(v\mathop{\circ}h)^{\diamond}(1)=h^{\diamond}(h_{\diamond}(\IM a))=\IM a=1\mathop{\circ}a_{\zeta}
,
there must be a total pure map
h^{\prime}

such that
v\mathop{\circ}h=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}a_{\zeta}
.
Note
\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}v\right\rceil^{\perp}=h_{\diamond}(\IM a)\leqslant h_{\diamond}(1)=\IM h
.
So by the homology axiom, we know
v\mathop{\circ}h
is pristine
and so
h^{\prime}
must be a comprehension.
Moving to the other side,
define
m\equiv\pi_{g^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to1.46pt{\vbox to1.46pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0861pt\lower-0.0861pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 1.79 L 1.79 1.79 L 1.79 0 Z M 1.79 1.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}c\right\rceil^{\perp})}
.
Reasoning in a similar way,
we see there is a unique sharp quotient
g^{\prime}

with
g\mathop{\circ}m=c_{\pi}\mathop{\circ}g^{\prime}
.

in

using the right-modularity of

\square

†
†
margin:

IX

Remark
As
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
is a
\dagger
-effectus,
Grandis’ Snake Lemma also holds for von Neumann algebras.
This is somewhat puzzling for the following reason.
To study spaces,
one associates in algebraic geometry
to each space
an object
(or even a diagram of objects) of
an abelian category representing some algebraic invariant.
The key point is that it is easier to compute and understand
these algebraic invariants than it is to work with the spaces
themselves.
One normally thinks of a category of spaces as being quite different
from a category of algebraic invariants, hence it’s surprising
that
{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
, which is a rather complicated
category of (non-commutative) spaces,
behaves in this way, similar to an abelian category.

Chapter 4

Outlook

†
†
margin:

229

For my closing remarks, I would like to speculate
on possible applications
and suggest possible
directions for future research.

We started this thesis by asking the question
whether the incredibly useful Stinespring dilation theorem
extends to any ncp-map between von Neumann algebras.
Now that we have seen it does, the next question is obvious:
do the applications that make the Stinespring dilation so useful
also extend to the Paschke dilation?
I want to draw attention to one application in particular:
proving security bounds on quantum protocols.
Here one uses a continuous version
of the Stinespring dilation theorem
[
80
]
.
Is there also such a continuous version for Paschke dilation?

†
†
margin:

II

Next, we have seen that self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-modules
over von Neumann algebras
are very well-behaved compared
to arbitrary Hilbert C
∗
-modules.
Frankly, I’m surprised they haven’t been studied
more extensively before:
the way results about Hilbert spaces
generalize elegantly to self-dual Hilbert C
∗
-modules
over von Neumann algebras seems hard to ignore.
Only one immediate open question remains here:
does the normal form of
2.5

extend in some way for to arbitrary von Neumann algebras?

†
†
margin:

III

As announced, in the second part of this thesis, we did not reach
our goal of axiomatizing the category of von Neumann algebras
categorically.
In our attempt, we did find several new concepts
such as
\diamond
-adjointness,
\diamond
-positivity,
purity defined with quotient and comprehension and
the
\dagger
on these pure maps.
Building on this work,
van de Wetering recently announced
[
118
,
117
]

a reconstruction of finite-dimensional quantum theory.
To discuss it, we need a definition: call an effectus
operational

if its scalars are isomorphic to the real interval;
both the states and the predicates are order separating;
all predicate effect modules are embeddable in
finite-dimensional order unit spaces

and
each state space is a closed subset
of the base norm space of all
unital positive functionals on the corresponding
order unit space of predicates.
It follows from
[
118
]

that the state space of any operational
\&
-effectus
is a spectral convex set in the sense of Alfsen and Shultz
and that any operational
\dagger
-effectus
is equivalent to a subcategory of the category of Euclidean Jordan Algebras
with positive maps between them in the opposite direction.
Are there infinite-dimensional generalizations of these results?
Are the state spaces of a real
\&
-effectuses perhaps
spectral convex sets?
Is any real
\dagger
-effectus equivalent to a subcategory of, say,
the category of JBW-algebras with positive maps between them
in the opposite direction?

Chapter 5

Lekensamenvatting

De volgende twee technische vraagstukken
in de wiskundige theorie van quantum computers
liggen ten grondslag aan dit proefschrift.

1.

Breidt de stelling van Stinespring uit naar
alle von Neumann algebra’s?

2.

Is de categorie van von Neumann algebra’s te axiomatiseren?

Het eerste vraagstuk wordt beantwoord: ja, de stelling breidt uit.
Het tweede vraagstuk blijft helaas voor een groot deel onbeantwoord.
De belangrijkste bijdragen van dit proefschrift aan de wetenschap
zijn niet per se deze directe resultaten,
maar de bijvangst die het onderzoek erna heeft opgeleverd:
er worden verscheidene nieuwe stellingen bewezen
en begrippen ingevoerd
die toepassing hebben buiten deze vraagstukken
(zoals bijvoorbeeld een uitbreiding van de stelling
van Kaplansky en het begrip
\diamond
-geadjungeerdheid).

Maar waar gaan deze twee originele vraagstukken eigenlijk over?
Wat is die stelling van Stinespring?
Wat zijn von Neumann algebra’s?
En wat is een quantum computer überhaupt?

Quantum computers

Een quantum computer gebruikt
bepaalde quantum mechanische eigenaardigheden
om sommige berekeningen veel efficiënter uit
te voeren dan een traditionele computer.
Een zorgelijk voorbeeld is
dat quantum computer
erg goed is in het ontbinden van getallen in priemfactoren.
Het grootste deel van de moderne cryptografie
die ons beveiligt is gestoeld op de aanname
dat het vinden van zulke ontbindingen lastig is
en deze wordt dan ook makkelijk door
een voldoende grote quantum computer gebroken.
Gelukkig zijn de huidige quantum computers nog erg klein
en hebben wij de tijd om onze cryptografie aan te passen.
Aan de positieve kant zijn
quantum computer erg geschikt voor het simuleren
van het gedrag van moleculen,
waaronder het lokale effect van potentiële medicijnen.

In functie is een quantum computer te vergelijken met
een grafische kaart van een computer:
voor de meeste berekeningen is een normale CPU het best,
maar voor sommige berekeningen
is een grafische kaart veel efficiënter.
Dit komt doordat een normale CPU gemaakt is om zo snel mogelijk
losse rekenstappen op afzonderlijke getallen
achter elkaar uit te voeren,
terwijl een grafische kaart rekenstappen
uitvoert op miljoenen getallen tegelijkertijd.
Niet elke berekening heeft baat bij dat grote parallelisme.
Voor diegene die dat wel hebben (zoals het
weergeven van een 3D-wereld in een computerspel)
is het lastig om het originele algoritme aan te passen
om optimaal gebruik te maken van de grafische kaart.
Programmeren voor een grafische kaart vraagt om een
heel andere
mindset
.
Ook de theoretische analyse van algoritmes voor grafische kaarten
is van een andere aard dan die voor een traditionele computer.

De situatie bij een quantum computer is vergelijkbaar, maar extremer:
de efficiëntiewinst tussen een quantum computer en PC
is groter dan die tussen een CPU en grafische kaart.
Helaas is het vinden en analyseren van algoritmes voor quantum computers
ook vele malen ingewikkelder.
Er is ook geen methode of zelfs vuistregel bekend
om te bepalen of een berekening efficiënter uit te voeren
is op een quantum computer of niet.

Von Neumann algebra’s

Het gedrag van een algoritme voor een traditionele computer,
zoals diegene die het kwadraat van een telgetal berekent,
wordt vaak beschreven met wiskundige functie,
zoals in dit geval de functie
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

met
f(n)=n^{2}
.
De notatie
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

betekent dat de functie
f
als invoer
een telgetal
\mathbb{N}\equiv\{0,1,2,\ldots\}

neemt en ook een telgetal als uitvoer geeft.
Een echt programma draait op een computer
dat maar een beperkte hoeveelheid geheugen heeft:
er is dus een grootste telgetal dat in de computer past.
Dat we het algoritme beschrijven
met willekeurig grote telgetallen is een idealisering van de situatie.
Het zou echter onhandig zijn om alle generiek toepasbare algoritme
te beschrijven als wiskundige functies tussen
eindige verzamelingen (zoals
\{0,1,2,3\}
)
in plaats van tussen oneindige verzamelingen zoals
\mathbb{N}
.

Dit is helaas precies de situatie voor de meeste beschrijvingen van
algoritmes voor quantum computers.
Het probleem is dat het analoog van
\mathbb{N}

en de wiskundige functies daartussen
vele malen complexer van aard zijn
dan de wiskundige functies die het gedrag
beschrijven voor het eindige geval.
Ook al dit eindige geval op zichzelf is
stukken ingewikkelder dan de simpele functies
tussen eindige verzamelingen voor de normale algoritmes.

Von Neumann algebra’s
zijn de wiskundige structuren
die gebruikt worden om oneindige quantumdatatypes
te beschrijven zoals het analoog van
\mathbb{N}
.
Zogenoemde
ncp-functies
tussen von Neumann algebras
zijn het analoog van de normale wiskundige functies
tussen verzamelingen.
Het zijn deze von Neumann algebra’s met bijbehorende
ncp-functies, die in dit proefschrift onderzocht worden
om ze beter te begrijpen en eenvoudiger toe te kunnen passen.

De stelling van Stinespring

Het is lastig om grip te krijgen
op een willekeurige ncp-functie
tussen von Neumann algebra’s.
De stelling van Stinespring helpt hierbij:
deze zegt dat elke ncp-functie
f\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}_{I}

tussen von Neumann algebra’s
\mathscr{A}
en
\mathscr{B}_{I}

(waar
\mathscr{B}_{I}
van een speciale soort is)
in feite de samenstelling is
van twee veel simpelere ncp-functies:
eerst
f_{1}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}

en dan
f_{2}\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{B}_{I}
.
Deze bijzondere opsplitsing wordt
ook wel een
dilatie
genoemd
en wordt gebruikt als cruciale stap in het
bewijs van vele stellingen, niet alleen over ncp-functies zelf,
maar ook over bijvoorneeld quantum protocollen en quantum informatie.
Het probleem is dat de stelling van Stinespring alleen toepasbaar
is als
\mathscr{B}_{I}
van de speciale soort
type I
is.
Daarmee zijn de stellingen die ermee worden bewezen vaak ook maar toepasbaar
op zulke speciale von Neumann algebra’s.
In het eerste hoofdstuk van het proefschrift
wordt een generalisatie van de stelling van Stinespring
bewezen, die toepasbaar is op alle ncp-functies.

Axiomatisatie

Waarom zijn juist von Neumann algebra’s en ncp-functies
geschikt voor oneindige quantum datatypes?
In feite is het een wiskundige gok:
von Neumann algebra’s en ncp-functies
lijken de eenvoudigste structuren die
de voorbeelden die we kennen goed beschrijven.
Eenvoud hier is relatief: de ingewikkelde definitie
van een von Neumann algebra
staat ver van de intuïtie van een
informaticus en natuurkundige.
Het valt ook te betwijfelen of elke von Neumann
algebra een realistisch quantum mechanisch systeem beschrijft,
laat staan een quantum datatype.

Een mogelijke manier om deze kwestie te beslechten is de volgende:
wij zoeken een lijst van kenmerkende
(en voor onze toepassing relevante) eigenschappen waaraan
von Neumann algebra’s samen met ncp-functies aan voldoen.
Als er bewezen kan worden dat alleen von Neumann algebra’s
met ncp-functies aan deze lijst van eigenschappen kan voldoen
(en geen ander soort wiskundige structuur)
dan hebben wij met deze lijst van eigenschappen
(nu verheven tot axioma’s)
een nieuw inzicht in de aard van von Neumann algebra’s.

Een dergelijke aanpak is eerder al succesvol gebruikt
om de regels van quantum theorie voor eindige systemen beter te begrijpen.
Een voorbeeld hiervan zijn de axioma’s van Chiribella et al,
die quantum theorie karakteriseren door
de wijze waarop informatie verwerkt wordt.

In het tweede hoofdstuk van dit proefschrift
wordt gezocht naar een dergelijke axiomatisatie.
Een van de kernaxioma’s is het bestaan
van een soort
omkering
, een zogenoemde
dagger
,
op de speciale ncp-functies die aan de rechterkant
voorkomen van een dilatie,
wat de twee hoofdstuken met elkaar verbindt.
Helaas wordt een volledige axiomatisatie niet bereikt.
Het verhaal eindigt hier niet:
mijn college van de Wetering
heeft recent bewezen dat met een paar toevoegingen,
de axioma’s uit dit proefschrift
zogeheten Euclidische Jordan algebra’s (EJA’s) karakteriseren.
Hiermee is de kous nog niet af:
EJA’s zijn niet geschikt voor oneindige quantum datatypes.
Dit resultaat suggereert wel de mogelijkheid
dat JBW-algebra’s,
welke een kruising zijn van von Neumann en Jordan algebra’s,
uiteindelijk de juiste algebra’s zijn voor onze toepassing.

Hoewel ons tweede vraagstuk niet beantwoord is, heeft de zoektocht
naar geschikte axioma’s een aantal nieuwe begrippen aan het licht
gebracht (zoals
\diamond
-geadjungeerdheid, hoeken en filters), die een
nieuw inzicht verschaffen in von Neumann algebra’s
en daarmee quantum computers in het algemeen.

Chapter 6

About the author

Bas Westerbaan, born in 1988,
enrolled to the Radboud Universiteit
as a physics student in 2007.
He received his bachelor’s degree in
mathematics with a thesis on computability theory
supervised by dr. Wim Veldman.
He continued his study of logic and foundational computer science
during his master’s eduction and developed a keen interest for,
what he thought to be, an unrelated mathematical field:
functional analysis.
These interests however, are conveniently combined in the present thesis
which continues the research of his master’s thesis,
which was supervised by prof. Bart Jacobs as well.
During his undergraduate studies,
Bas also served as secretary of the board of the 150 member student club
Karpe Noktem and he was elected to
the student board of the faculty of science.
His master’s degree in mathematics was awarded cum laude in 2013.

While perfoming the research as a
promovendus

at the Digital Security group that led to this thesis,
Bas coauthered several security audits
including those of the
DigiD-
and
BerichtenBox
-app;
the Dutch digital identity and mail system.
In the year between the submission and defense
of his thesis, Bas was employed as a post-doctoral researcher
where he assisted with the development of a cryptographic system
for polymorphic pseudonymisation and encryption.
During that same year he also taught a quarter-year course on
computer networking
and was kindly invited by dr. Heunen
to speak about his research at the University of Edinburgh.

Chapter 7

Solutions to exercises

Solution to
2.1
.

To prove the first statement,
let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
be any ncp-map.
If
\varphi=0
then
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}=0
and
V=0
does the job,
so for the other case, assume
\varphi\neq 0
.
By
2

there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}^{\prime}
,
a bounded operator
W\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{H}^{\prime}
and an
nmiu-map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}^{\prime})

such that
\varphi=\mathrm{ad}_{W}\mathop{\circ}\varrho
.
Clearly
\varrho\neq 0
.
By
2.1

there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and a unitary
U\colon\mathscr{H}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
\varrho(A)=U^{*}(A\otimes 1)U

for all
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
.
Define
V\equiv UW
.
Then
\varphi(A)=W^{*}\varrho(A)W=W^{*}U^{*}(A\otimes 1)UW=V^{*}(A\otimes 1)V
, as desired.

Before we can continue with the second statement,
we need to understand the relationship
between quantum channels and ncpu-maps.
This relationship is best understood with
predual characterization of von Neumann algebras
due to Sakai
[
99
]
, which we have been avoiding.
The characterization is as thus:
a C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{A}
is a von Neumann algebra
if and only if it is isomorphic to the dual of a Banach space.
Then this Banach space is unique up-to-isomoprhism
as it must be isomorphic to the space of normal functionals on
\mathscr{A}

(denoted by
\mathscr{A}_{*}
)
and is appropriately called the
predual
of
\mathscr{A}
.
Any normal linear map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

between von Neumann algebras
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}

yields a linear map
\varphi_{*}\colon\mathscr{B}_{*}\to\mathscr{A}_{*}

via
\varphi_{*}(\omega)=\omega\mathop{\circ}\varphi
.
In the other direction, any linear map
\varphi_{*}\colon\mathscr{B}_{*}\to\mathscr{A}_{*}

gives rise to a normal linear map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

by defining
\varphi(a)(\omega)=\varphi_{*}(\omega)(a)

where we identified
\mathscr{A}\equiv(\mathscr{A}_{*})^{*}
.
Clearly
\varphi
is positive precisely
if
\varphi_{*}
maps positive functionals to positive functionals.

A normal state
\omega\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathbb{C}

is precisely of the form
\omega(a)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}[\rho a]
for some density
matrix
\rho
over
\mathscr{H}
.
Thus the predual of
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
can be identified
with the set of trace-class operators over
\mathscr{H}
.
Let
\varphi_{*}
be a linear map from the density operators
on
\mathscr{H}
to those on
\mathscr{K}
.
The map
\varphi_{*}
is completely positive in its usual sense
if the corresponding map
\varphi
is completely positive.
Furthermore
\varphi
is unital if and only if
\varphi_{*}

is trace-preserving.

To prove the second statement,
let
\Phi
be any quantum channel
mapping density matrices over
\mathscr{H}
to those of
\mathscr{H}
again.
(Note: in the printed version of the thesis
the exercise incorrectly
assumes
\Phi
to map density matrices over
\mathscr{H}

to those over some other Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}
.)
It follows from the previous, that there is a unique
ncpu-map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
with

\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}[\Phi(\rho)A]\ =\ \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}[\rho\varphi(A)]\quad\text{for any density matrix~$\rho\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})$}.

See also
[
112
]
for a more direct approach.
By the first part if the exercise,
we know that there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and a bounded operator
V\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
\varphi(A)=V^{*}(A\otimes 1)V
.
Tracing back the definition of
V
, we see that
V

is an isometry because
\varphi
is unital.
Pick any orthonormal bases
E
and
F
of
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

respectively.
We may assume, without loss of generality,
that
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
is not zero-dimensional
by setting
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}
and
V=0

in the case that
\Phi=0
.
Pick any
f_{0}\in F

and any unitary
U\colon\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
U^{*}x\otimes f_{0}=Vx
,
which exists as
V
is an isometry.
Now we compute

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}[\varphi(A)\rho]

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}[\rho V^{*}(A\otimes 1)V]

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}\langle V\rho e,(A\otimes 1)Ve\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}\bigl\langle U^{*}(\rho\otimes 1)\,e\otimes f_{0},\ (A\otimes 1)U^{*}\,e\otimes f_{0}\bigr\rangle.

Inserting
\left|f_{0}\right>\!\left<f_{0}\right|

in the previous,
we may sum over all
f\in F
and get

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}[\varphi(A)\rho]

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in E\\
f\in F\end{subarray}}\bigl\langle U^{*}(\rho\otimes\left|f_{0}\right>\!\left<f_{0}\right|)\,e\otimes f,\ (A\otimes 1)U^{*}\,e\otimes f\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in E\\
f\in F\end{subarray}}\bigl\langle(U^{*}(e\otimes f)),\ U^{*}(\rho\otimes\left|f_{0}\right>\!\left<f_{0}\right|)U\,(A\otimes 1)\,(U(e\otimes f))\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl[U^{*}(\rho\otimes\left|f_{0}\right>\!\left<f_{0}\right|)U\,(A\otimes 1)\bigr]

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl[A\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits_{\mathscr{K}^{\prime}}[U^{*}(\rho\otimes\left|f_{0}\right>\!\left<f_{0}\right|)U]\bigr].

This show that indeed
\Phi(\rho)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{\mathscr{K}^{\prime}}[U^{*}(\rho\otimes\left|v_{0}\right>\!\left<v_{0}\right|)U]

as desired with
v_{0}\equiv f_{0}
.

Solution to
2.1
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})

be any ncp-map.
By
2.1

there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and a bounded operator
V\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
\varphi(A)=V^{*}(A\otimes 1)V
.
Let
E
be any orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
.
Then
1=\sum_{e\in E}\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|

where the sum converges ultraweakly
and so by ultraweak continuity of
\mathrm{ad}_{V}
(
)
and
B\mapsto A\otimes B
(
), we see

\varphi(A)\ =\ V^{*}\Bigl(A\otimes\sum_{e\in E}\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|\Bigr)V)\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}V^{*}(A\otimes\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|)V.

(7.1)

For
e\in E
, define
P_{e}\colon\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{H}

by
P_{e}\equiv 1\otimes\bra{e}
,
i.e.
P_{e}(x\otimes y)=x\langle e,y\rangle
.
Define
V_{e}\equiv P_{e}V
.
Note that
P_{e}^{*}AP_{e}=A\otimes\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|
and so

\displaystyle\varphi(A)

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}V^{*}(A\otimes\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|)V

by (
7.1
)

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}V^{*}P_{e}^{*}AP_{e}V

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{e\in E}V_{e}^{*}AV_{e},

as desired.
From the special case
A=1
, we see
that
\sum_{e\in E}V_{e}^{*}V_{e}=\varphi(1)

and so the partial sums of
\sum_{e\in E}V_{e}^{*}V_{e}
are bounded.

For the final part, assume
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{K}
are finite dimensional.
Recall that the standard Stinespring dilation space (say
\mathscr{K}^{\prime\prime}
)
for
\varphi
is constructed using a completion
and quotient of
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\odot\mathscr{K}
.
As
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\odot\mathscr{K}
is finite dimensional
it is already complete.
Hence
\mathscr{K}^{\prime\prime}
has dimension at most
(\dim\mathscr{H})^{2}(\dim\mathscr{K})
.
By construction
\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\cong\mathscr{K}^{\prime\prime}
,
hence
\dim\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\leqslant(\dim\mathscr{H})(\dim\mathscr{K})
.
Recall
E
is a basis of
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and so there are indeed at most
(\dim\mathscr{H})(\dim\mathscr{K})

Kraus operators.

Solution to
2.1
.

We will show that
U\colon\mathsf{Rep}\to\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathrm{cp}}

has a left adjoint by demonstrating the universal mapping property.
Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
be any object of
\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathrm{cp}}
.
Pick any minimal Stinespring dilation
(\mathscr{K},\varrho,V)
of
\varphi
.
The map
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
is an object
of
\mathsf{Rep}
.
Clearly
\mathrm{ad}_{V}\mathop{\circ}\varrho\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=\varphi

and so
\eta_{\varphi}\equiv(\mathrm{id},V)\colon\varphi\to U\varrho

is a morphism in
\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathrm{cp}}
.
We will show that for each
f\colon\varphi\to U\varrho^{\prime}

in
\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathrm{cp}}
,
there is a unique
f^{\prime}\colon\varrho\to\varrho^{\prime}

in
\mathsf{Rep}
with
Uf^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\eta_{\varphi}=f
.
This is sufficient to show that
U

has a left adjoint.

So let
f\colon\varphi\to U\varrho^{\prime}
be any morphism
in
\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathrm{cp}}
.
Say
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K}^{\prime})
.
Then
f\equiv(m^{\prime},V^{\prime})
consists of
a nmiu-map
m^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{A}^{\prime}

and bounded operator
V^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{H}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
\mathrm{ad}_{V^{\prime}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}m^{\prime}=\varphi
.
By
2.1

there is a unique bounded operator
S\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
SV=V^{\prime}
and
\varrho=\mathrm{ad}_{S}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}m^{\prime}
.
This turns
f^{\prime}\equiv(m^{\prime},S)
into a
morphism
\varrho\to\varrho^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{Rep}
.
Furthermore
Uf^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\eta_{\varphi}=(m^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id},SV)=(m^{\prime},V^{\prime})=f
.
To show uniqueness, assume
there is some
f^{\prime\prime}\colon\varrho\to\varrho^{\prime}

in
\mathsf{Rep}

with
Uf^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}\eta_{\varphi}=f
.
Say
f^{\prime\prime}=(m^{\prime\prime},S^{\prime\prime})
.
Then
(m^{\prime},V^{\prime})=f=Uf^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\eta_{\varphi}=(m^{\prime\prime},S^{\prime\prime}V)
.
So
m^{\prime\prime}=m^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}=S^{\prime\prime}V
.
The fact that
f^{\prime\prime}
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Rep}

is
equivalent to
\mathrm{ad}_{S^{\prime\prime}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}m^{\prime\prime}=\varrho
.
Thus
\mathrm{ad}_{S^{\prime\prime}}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}m^{\prime}=\varrho
.
By uniqueness of
S
, we get
S^{\prime\prime}=S
.
Hence
f^{\prime\prime}=(m^{\prime\prime},S^{\prime\prime})=(m^{\prime},S)=f^{\prime}
, as desired.

Solution to
2.1
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
be any ncp-map.
As in the description of the exercise,
let
\mathscr{K}
be a Hilbert space
and
V,W\colon\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

be bounded operators
with
V^{*}(a\otimes 1)V=\varphi(a)=W^{*}(a\otimes 1)W
.
Write
\mathscr{V}
for the closed linear span
of
\{(a\otimes 1)Vx;\ a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}),\ x\in\mathscr{K}\}

in
\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

and similarly
\mathscr{W}
for that
of
\{(a\otimes 1)Wx;\ a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}),\ x\in\mathscr{K}\}
.
Note that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathscr{K}

and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sum_{i}(a_{i}\otimes 1)Vx_{i}\bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\langle x_{i},\,V^{*}((a_{i}^{*}a_{j})\otimes 1)Vx_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i,j}\langle x_{i},\,W^{*}((a_{i}^{*}a_{j})\otimes 1)Wx_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl\|\sum_{i}(a_{i}\otimes 1)Wx_{i}\bigr\|^{2}.

Thus there is a unique unitary
U_{0}\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V}

fixed by
U_{0}(a\otimes 1)Wx=U_{0}(a\otimes 1)Vx
.
We see
U_{0}W=V
by setting
a=1
.
Furthermore

(\alpha\otimes 1)U_{0}(a\otimes 1)Wx\ =\ ((\alpha a)\otimes 1)Vx\ =\ U_{0}(\alpha\otimes 1)(a\otimes 1)Wx

for any
\alpha,a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
and
x\in\mathscr{K}
,
hence
(\alpha\otimes 1)U_{0}=U_{0}(\alpha\otimes 1)
.

For any
a\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
,
the operator
a\otimes 1\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{K}^{\prime})

restricts to
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{W})
.
Pick an orthonormal basis
E
of
\mathscr{H}

and some
e_{0}\in E
.
Note that
(\left|e_{0}\right>\!\left<e_{0}\right|\otimes 1)\mathscr{W}=e_{0}\otimes\mathscr{W}^{\prime}

for some closed subspace
\mathscr{W}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
.
In fact, for any
e\in E

we have
e\otimes\mathscr{W}^{\prime}=(\left|e\right>\!\left<e_{0}\right|\otimes 1)(e_{0}\otimes\mathscr{W}^{\prime})=(\left|e\right>\!\left<e_{0}\right|\otimes 1)(1\otimes\left|e_{0}\right>\!\left<e_{0}\right|)\mathscr{W}=(\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|T\otimes 1)\mathscr{W}=(\left|e\right>\!\left<e\right|\otimes 1)\mathscr{W}
,
where
T
is the unitary on
\mathscr{H}
that only swaps
e
and
e_{0}
.
Hence
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{W}^{\prime}
.
Similarly
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{V}^{\prime}

for some closed subspace
\mathscr{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
.

For any non-zero
w\in\mathscr{W}^{\prime}
and unit-vector
x\in\mathscr{H}
,
we have
U_{0}(x\otimes w)=U_{0}(\left|x\right>\!\left<x\right|\otimes 1)(x\otimes w)=(\left|x\right>\!\left<x\right|\otimes 1)U_{0}(x\otimes w)

so
U_{0}(x\otimes w)=x\otimes y
for some
y\in\mathscr{V}^{\prime}
.
Clearly
\|w\|=\|x\otimes w\|=\|U_{0}(x\otimes w)\|=\|x\otimes y\|=\|y\|
,
so there is a unique unitary
U_{1}\colon\mathscr{W}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{V}^{\prime}

with
U_{0}(x\otimes w)=x\otimes U_{1}w
.
It follows
U_{0}=1\otimes U_{1}
.

As
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{W}
are isomorphic, they have the same dimension
and so do
\mathscr{V}^{\perp}
and
\mathscr{W}
.
Consequently, there is an unitary
U\colon\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

extending
U_{1}
.
We have
V=(1\otimes U)W=(1\otimes U_{1})W=U_{0}W=V
as desired.

Solution to
2.1
.

We cover the points in order.

1.

Let
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be a mniu-map.
Assume there are nmiu
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}

and ncp
h^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}

with
h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
.
We have to show there is a unique map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{B}

with
\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
.
Clearly
\sigma\equiv h^{\prime}
fits the bill.

2.

Let
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
be any Paschke dilation.
We will show that
(\mathscr{P},\mathrm{id},h)
is a Paschke dilation of
h
.
To this end, let
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}

be any nmiu-map and
h^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}

be any ncp-map with
h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=h
.
Consider
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho
and
h^{\prime}
.
By the universal property of the original dilation,
there is a unique ncp-map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}

with
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho=\varrho

and
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime}
.
Furthermore
\mathrm{id}\colon\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}

is the unique ncp-map
with
\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}\varrho=\varrho

and
h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=h
.
Now
(\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime})\mathop{\circ}\varrho=\varrho

and
h\mathop{\circ}(\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime})=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=h
,
so
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}
.
We are are halfway demonstrating that
\sigma
is also the mediating map
for our dilation of
h
.
It remains to be shown that
\sigma
is the unique ncp-map
with
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}

and
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime}
.
So assume there is a ncp-map
\sigma^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}

with
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}=h^{\prime}
and
