\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}
.
Clearly
\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho=\varrho

and so by uniqueness of
\sigma
as the mediating map
for the orignal dilation,
we see
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
, as desired.

3.

Let
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}_{1}\oplus\mathscr{B}_{2}

be any ncp-map.
Pick Paschke dilations
(\mathscr{P}_{i},\varrho_{i},h_{i})

of
\varphi_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
We will show that
(\mathscr{P}_{1}\oplus\mathscr{P}_{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varrho_{1}\\
\varrho_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),h_{1}\oplus h_{2})

is a Paschke dilation of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Clearly
h_{1}\oplus h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varrho_{1}\\
\varrho_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(\begin{smallmatrix}h_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{1}\\
h_{2}\mathop{\circ}\varrho_{2}\end{smallmatrix})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Let
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
be any nmiu-map
and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}_{1}\oplus\mathscr{B}_{2}

any ncp-map with
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Note
h_{i}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varphi_{i}

(for
i=1,2
)
and so there is a unique
\sigma_{i}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}_{i}

with
\sigma_{i}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho_{i}

and
h_{i}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{i}=h_{i}^{\prime}
.
We will show that
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma_{1}\\
\sigma_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}_{1}\oplus\mathscr{P}_{2}

is the unique mediating map.
Clearly
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma_{1}\\
\sigma_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\\
\sigma_{2}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varrho_{1}\\
\varrho_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

and
(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})\mathop{\circ}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma_{1}\\
\sigma_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}h^{\prime}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{1}\\
h^{\prime}_{2}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.
To show uniqueness of
(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma_{1}\\
\sigma_{2}\end{smallmatrix})
,
assume
there is ncp-map
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\\
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}_{1}\oplus\mathscr{P}_{2}

such that
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\\
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\varrho_{1}\\
\varrho_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

and
(h_{1}\oplus h_{2})\mathop{\circ}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\\
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.
Then
h_{i}\mathop{\circ}\sigma_{i}^{\prime}=h_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho_{i}

for
i=1,2
and so
\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
by the uniqueness
of the seperate
\sigma_{i}
.
Thus indeed
(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma_{1}\\
\sigma_{2}\end{smallmatrix})=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\\
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.

4.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
be any ncp-map
with Paschke dilation
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
.
Assume
\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\lambda>0
.
We will show
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,\lambda h)

is a Paschke dilation of
\lambda\varphi
.
Clearly
\lambda h\mathop{\circ}\varrho=\lambda\varphi
.
To this end, assume
\varrho^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
is a nmiu-map
and
h^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}
is an ncp-map
with
h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\lambda\varphi
.
Then
\lambda^{-1}h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varphi
.
Thus there is a unique
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}

with
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho

and
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=\lambda^{-1}h^{\prime}
.
Clearly
\lambda h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime}
and so
\sigma

also serves as the unique mediating map
for the dilation of
\lambda\varphi
.

Solution to
2.2.1
.

Let
X
be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle

for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Using the C
∗
-identity,
2.2.1
and the definition
of
\|\,\cdot\,\|
on
X
, we
get
\|\langle x,y\rangle\|^{2}=\|\langle x,y\rangle^{*}\langle x,y\rangle\|=\|\langle y,x\rangle\langle x,y\rangle\|\leqslant\bigl\|\|\langle x,x\rangle\|\langle y,y\rangle\bigr\|=\|x\|^{2}\|y\|^{2}
,
so indeed
\|\langle x,y\rangle\|\leqslant\|x\|\|y\|
.

Now we will show
\|x\|\equiv\|\langle x,x\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}

is a seminorm on
X
.
Clearly
\|x\|\geqslant 0
for any
x\in X
.
For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and
x\in X

we have
\langle\lambda x,\lambda x\rangle=\overline{\lambda}\langle x,x\rangle\lambda=|\lambda|^{2}\langle x,x\rangle
,
hence
\|\lambda x\|=\|\lambda^{2}\langle x,x\rangle\|^{\frac{1}{2}}=|\lambda|\|x\|
.
Next, for any
x,y\in X
we have

\displaystyle\|x+y\|^{2}

\displaystyle\ =\ \|\langle x+y,x+y\rangle\|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|\langle x,x\rangle\|+\|\langle y,y\rangle\|+\|\langle x,y\rangle\|+\|\langle x,y\rangle^{*}\|

\displaystyle\ =\ \|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}+2\|\langle x,y\rangle\|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}+2\|x\|\|y\|

\displaystyle\ =\ (\|x\|+\|y\|)^{2}

and thus
\|\,\cdot\,\|
is indeed a seminorm.

Finally, we will show
\|x\cdot b\|\leqslant\|x\|\|b\|

for any
b\in\mathscr{B}
and
x\in X
.
As
\langle x,x\rangle
is positive,
we have
\langle x,x\rangle\leqslant\|\langle x,x\rangle\|=\|x\|^{2}
.
Also recall
a\mapsto b^{*}ab
is clearly positive.
Thus
\|x\cdot b\|^{2}\equiv\|\langle xb,xb\rangle\|=\|b^{*}\langle x,x\rangle b\|\leqslant\bigl\|\|x\|^{2}b^{*}b\bigr\|=\|b\|^{2}\|x\|^{2}
as desired.

Solution to
2.2.1
.

Let
B
be any
\mathscr{B}
-sesquilinear form on a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Distributing
B
in
\sum_{k=0}^{3}i^{k}B(i^{k}x+y,i^{k}x+y)

we get 16 terms
consisting of four of each
B(x,x)
,
B(y,y)
,
B(x,y)
and
B(y,x)

with the following coefficients.

B(x,x)

B(y,y)

B(x,y)

B(y,x)

k\,=\,0

1

1

1

1

k\,=\,1

i\cdot(-i)\cdot i\,=\,i

i

i\cdot(-i)\,=\,1

i^{2}\,=\,-1

k\,=\,2

(-1)^{3}\,=\,-1

-1

(-1)^{2}\,=\,1

(-1)^{2}\,=\,1

k\,=\,3

(-i)\cdot i\cdot(-i)\,=\,-i

-i

(-i)\cdot i\,=\,1

(-i)^{2}\,=\,-1

Note that the coefficients in
every column sum to
0
, except for the coefficients for
B(x,y)
which
sum to
4
.
Hence
\sum_{k=0}^{3}i^{k}B(i^{k}x+y,i^{k}x+y)=4B(x,y)
.

Solution to
2.2.2
.

Let
X
be a set together with a subbase
B
.
Write
\Phi
for the filter generated by
B
.
Note
B\subseteq\Phi
.
We will show
(X,\Phi)
is a uniform space, by proving
its axioms in order.

1.

By construction
\Phi
is a filter.

2.

Pick any
\varepsilon\in\Phi
.
We have to show
\Delta\equiv\{(x,x);\ x\in X\}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
By definition of
\Phi
, there
are
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}\in B

with
\varepsilon_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\varepsilon_{n}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
By definition of a base, we have
\Delta\subseteq\varepsilon_{i}

for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n

and so
\Delta\subseteq\varepsilon_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\varepsilon_{n}\subseteq\varepsilon
as well.

3.

Pick any
\varepsilon\in\Phi
.
By definition of
\Phi
, there
are
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}\in B

with
\varepsilon_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\varepsilon_{n}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
As
B
is a base, there are
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n}

with
\delta_{i}\mathop{\circ}\delta_{i}\subseteq\varepsilon_{i}

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.
Define
\delta=\delta_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\delta_{n}
.
Then
\delta\mathop{\circ}\delta\subseteq\bigcap_{i,j}\delta_{i}\mathop{\circ}\delta_{j}\subseteq\bigcap_{i}\delta_{i}\subseteq\bigcap_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\subseteq\varepsilon
.

4.

Pick any
\varepsilon\in\Phi
.
By definition of
\Phi
, there
are
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}\in B

with
\varepsilon_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\varepsilon_{n}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
As
B
is a base, there are
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n}

with
\delta_{i}^{-1}\subseteq\varepsilon_{i}
.
Define
\delta=\delta_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\delta_{n}
.
Then
\delta^{-1}=\bigcap_{i}\delta_{i}^{-1}\subseteq\bigcap_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\subseteq\varepsilon
.

Thus indeed,
(X,\Phi)
is a uniform space.

Solution to
2.2.2
.

We prove the subexercises in order.

1.

First we will show that equivalence of Cauchy nets is an equivalence
relation.
As for every entourage
\varepsilon
we have
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}x
,
we see that every Cauchy net is equivalent to itself.
Assume
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(y_{\beta})_{\beta}
.
We will show
(y_{\beta})_{\beta}\sim(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
Let
\varepsilon
be some entourage.
There is some entourage
\delta
with
\delta^{-1}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
By assumption there are
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}

such that
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\delta}y_{\beta}

for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
and
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}
.
But then
y_{\beta}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x_{\alpha}

for
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
and
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}
.
Hence
(y_{\beta})_{\beta}\sim(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
To prove transitivity,
assume we are given Cauchy nets
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(y_{\beta})_{\beta}\sim(z_{\gamma})_{\gamma}
.
Let
\varepsilon
be some entourage.
There is an entourage
\delta
with
\delta\mathop{\circ}\delta\subseteq\varepsilon
.
There are
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0}

such that
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\delta}y_{\beta}

and
y_{\beta}\mathrel{\delta}z_{\gamma}

for
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
,
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}

and
\gamma\geqslant\gamma_{0}
.
Hence
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon}z_{\gamma}
for such
\alpha
and
\gamma
, which shows
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(z_{\gamma})_{\gamma}
.

Next, assume that
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is a subnet
of a Cauchy net
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
To show
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
,
assume
\varepsilon
is some entourage.
By the definition of Cauchy net,
there is some
\alpha_{0}

such that
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x_{\beta}

for all
\alpha,\beta\geqslant\alpha_{0}
.
In particular
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon}y_{\beta}

for all
\alpha,\beta\geqslant\alpha_{0}

which shows
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(x_{\beta})_{\beta}
.

2.

Assume
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\sim(y_{\beta})_{\beta}

and
x_{\alpha}\to x
.
To prove
y_{\alpha}\to x
, pick any entourage
\varepsilon
.
Pick
\delta
such that
\delta^{2}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
There are
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}

such that
y_{\beta}\mathrel{\delta}x_{\alpha}

and
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\delta}x

for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
and
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}
.
Then
y_{\beta}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x
for all
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}
,
whence
y_{\beta}\to x
.

3.

Assume
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\to x
and
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\to y

in some Hausdorff uniform space.
Let
\varepsilon
be any entourage.
Pick
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
with
\delta^{2}\subseteq\varepsilon

and
\delta^{\prime}\subseteq\delta^{-1}
.
There is an
\alpha_{0}
such that
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\delta}x

and
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\delta^{\prime}}y

for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
.
Thus
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}y
.
As our space is Hausdorff the previous implies
x=y
.

4.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a continuous map between uniform spaces.
Assume
x_{\alpha}\to x
in
X
.
Let
\varepsilon
be any entourage of
Y
.
By continuity there is a
\delta

such that
x\mathrel{\delta}y
implies
f(x)\mathrel{\varepsilon}f(y)

for any
y\in Y
.
There is an
\alpha_{0}
such that
x\mathrel{\delta}x_{\alpha}

for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
.
For those
\alpha
we also have
f(x)\mathrel{\varepsilon}f(x_{\alpha})
,
which shows
f(x_{\alpha})\to f(x)
.

5.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a uniformly continuous map
between uniform spaces.
Let
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
and
(y_{\beta})_{\beta}

be nets of
X

such that for each entourage
\varepsilon
of
X

there are
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}
with
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\varepsilon}y_{\beta}

for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}
and
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}
.
The map
f
preserves this relation between the nets
in the following way.
Let
\varepsilon
be any entourage of
Y

By uniform continuity there is a
\delta

such that
x\mathrel{\delta}y
implies
f(x)\mathrel{\varepsilon}f(y)
.
There are
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}

with
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\delta}y_{\beta}
for all
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{0}

and
\beta\geqslant\beta_{0}
.
Hence
f(x_{\alpha})\mathrel{\varepsilon}f(y_{\beta})

for such
\alpha,\beta
.

From the previous it follows that
f

preserves Cauchy nets (by setting
x_{\alpha}=y_{\alpha}
)
and that it preserves equivalence between Cauchy nets.

6.

Let
D\subseteq X
be a dense subset of a uniform space
X
.
Let
x\in X
be any point.
Pick for every
\varepsilon\in\Phi

an element
d_{\varepsilon}\in D

with
x\mathrel{\varepsilon}d_{\varepsilon}
.
Clearly
(d_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in\Phi}
is a net with inverse
inclusion. We have
d_{\varepsilon}\to x

as
d_{\delta}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x

whenever
\delta\subseteq\varepsilon
.

7.

Assume
f,g\colon X\to Y
are continuous maps between uniform
spaces that agree on a dense subset
D\subseteq X
.
Let
x\in X
be any point.
Pick a net
x_{\alpha}
from
D
with
x_{\alpha}\to x
.
Then
f(x)=f(\lim_{\alpha}x_{\alpha})=\lim_{\alpha}f(x_{\alpha})=\lim_{\alpha}g(x_{\alpha})=g(\lim_{\alpha}x_{\alpha})=g(x)
.
Hence
f=g
.

Solution to
2.2.2
.

Write
B\equiv\{\hat{\varepsilon};\ \varepsilon\in\Phi_{i};\ i\in I\}
.
First we show
B
is a subbase,
i.e. that is satisfies conditions 2, 3 and 4
of
2.2.2
.
Let
\hat{\varepsilon}
be an arbitrary element of
B

and
i\in I
denote the index element with
\varepsilon\in\Phi_{i}
.
Assume
(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in\Pi_{i}X_{i}
.
Clearly
x_{i}\mathrel{\varepsilon}x_{i}

and so
(x_{i})_{i}\mathrel{\hat{\varepsilon}}(x_{i})_{i}
.
Thus
B
satisfies condition 2 of
2.2.2
.
Pick a
\delta\in\Phi_{i}
with
\delta^{2}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
Then
{\hat{\delta}}^{2}=\widehat{\delta^{2}}\subseteq\hat{\varepsilon}

and so
B
satisfies condition 3 of
2.2.2
.
Now let
\delta
denote an entourage of
X_{i}

with
\delta^{-1}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
Then
\hat{\delta}^{-1}=\widehat{\delta^{-1}}\subseteq\hat{\varepsilon}

and so
B
also satisfies condition 4 of
2.2.2
.

Next we show that the projectors
\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}X_{i}\to X_{i}

are uniformly continuous.
Assume
i_{0}\in I
and
\varepsilon
is an entourage of
X_{i_{0}}
.
Define
\delta\equiv\hat{\varepsilon}
.
Let
(x_{i})_{i}
and
(y_{i})_{i}

from
\prod_{i}X_{i}
be given
with
(x_{i})_{i}\mathrel{\delta}(y_{i})_{i}
.
Then
x_{i_{0}}\mathrel{\varepsilon}y_{i_{0}}
.
Thus
\pi_{i_{0}}
is indeed uniformly continuous.

To show
(\pi_{i})_{i}
is a categorical product,
assume we are given a uniform space
Y
together
with uniformly continuous maps
f_{i}\colon Y\to X_{i}

for each
i\in I
.
We have to show that there is a unique uniformly continuous
map
f\colon Y\to\prod_{i}X_{i}

with
\pi_{i}\mathop{\circ}f=f_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
Define
f
by
(f(y))_{i}\equiv f_{i}(y)
.
Clearly
\pi_{i}\mathop{\circ}f=f_{i}
.

To show
f
is uniformly continuous,
pick any entourage
\varepsilon
of
\prod_{i}X_{i}
.
By definition, there are
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}

and
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}

with
\varepsilon_{j}\in\Phi_{i_{j}}

and
\bigcap_{j}\widehat{\varepsilon_{j}}\subseteq\varepsilon
.
For each
1\leqslant j\leqslant n

pick an entourage
\delta_{j}
of
Y

such that
x\mathrel{\delta}_{j}y

implies
f_{i_{j}}(x)\mathrel{\varepsilon_{j}}f_{i_{j}}(y)
.
Define
\delta\equiv\bigcap_{j}\delta_{j}

Assume
x\mathrel{\delta}y
.
Then for each
1\leqslant j\leqslant n

we have
f_{i_{j}}(x)\mathrel{\varepsilon_{j}}f_{i_{j}}(y)

and so
f(x)\mathrel{\widehat{\varepsilon_{j}}}f(y)
,
hence
f(x)\mathrel{\varepsilon}f(y)
.
Thus
f
is uniformly continuous.

To show uniqueness of
f
, assume there is a uniformly continuous
map
f^{\prime}\colon Y\to\prod_{i}X_{i}
with
\pi_{i}\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}=f_{i}
.
Then
(f^{\prime}(y))_{i}=f_{i}(y)=(f(y))_{i}
for every
y\in Y

and so
f^{\prime}=f
.

Solution to
2.2.2
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a von Neumann algebra and
X

a right
\mathscr{B}
-module with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product.
We will show that
x\mapsto xb
is ultranorm continuous
for any
b\in\mathscr{B}
.
It is sufficient to show it is ultranorm continuous at
0
,
so assume
x_{\alpha}\to 0
ultranorm for some net
x_{\alpha}
in
X
.
Let
f\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
be any np-map.
Then
\|x_{\alpha}b\|_{f}^{2}=f([x_{\alpha}b,x_{\alpha}b])=f(b^{*}[x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}]b)\leqslant\|b^{*}b\|f([x_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}])=\|b\|^{2}\|x_{\alpha}\|_{f}^{2}\to 0

thus
x_{\alpha}b\to 0
ultranorm as well.

Solution to
2.2.2
.

For brevity, write
p\equiv\langle e,e\rangle
.
Note
\|ep-e\|^{2}=\|e(1-p)\|^{2}=\langle e(1-p),e(1-p)\rangle=(1-p)\langle e,e\rangle(1-p)=(1-p)p(1-p)=0
.
Thus
ep-e=0
and so
ep=e
as desired.

Solution to
2.2.2
.

Let
X
be a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for a von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}

with orthonormal basis
E\subseteq X
.
Assume
x\in X
.
By definition of orthonormal basis,
we know
x=\sum_{e\in E}e\langle e,x\rangle

where the sum converges ultranorm.
That is:
\sum_{e\in S}e\langle e,x\rangle\to x

as
S
ranges over the finite subsets of
E
.
By
2.2.2

we
see
\bigl\langle\sum_{e\in S}e\langle e,x\rangle,\sum_{e\in S}e\langle e,x\rangle\bigr\rangle\to\langle x,x\rangle
ultraweakly.
Thus
\sum_{e\in S}\langle x,e\rangle\langle e,x\rangle=\sum_{e,d\in S}\langle x,e\rangle\langle e,d\rangle\langle d,x\rangle=\bigl\langle\sum_{e\in S}e\langle e,x\rangle,\sum_{e\in S}e\langle e,x\rangle\bigr\rangle\to\langle x,x\rangle
ultraweakly, as desired.

Solution to
2.2.3
.

Let
T\colon X\to Y
be a bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear map between Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules.
Assume
X
is self dual.
For any
y\in Y
, the map
x\mapsto\langle y,Tx\rangle

is
\mathscr{B}
-linear and bounded
and so by self-duality of
X
,
there is a
t_{y}\in X

with
\langle t_{y},x\rangle=\langle y,Tx\rangle

for all
x\in X
.
For any
z,y\in Y
and
x\in X
,
we have
\langle t_{z}+t_{y},x\rangle=\langle t_{z},x\rangle+\langle t_{y},x\rangle=\langle z,Tx\rangle+\langle y,Tx\rangle=\langle z+y,Tx\rangle=\langle t_{z+y},x\rangle
.
Thus
t_{z}+t_{y}=t_{z+y}

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,
y\in Y
and
x\in X

we have
\langle\lambda t_{y},x\rangle=\langle\lambda y,Tx\rangle=\langle t_{\lambda y},x\rangle

and so
\lambda t_{y}=t_{\lambda y}
.
Hence
T^{*}y\equiv t_{y}
defines a linear map from
Y
to
X

with
\langle y,Tx\rangle=\langle t_{y},x\rangle\equiv\langle T^{*}y,x\rangle
for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.
So
T^{*}
is the adjoint of
T
.

Solution to
2.2.3
.

Let
V
be a right
\mathscr{B}
-module with
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
for some von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Write
\eta\colon V\to X
for the ultranorm completion of
V

from
2.2.3
.
Let
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
be given
with
\langle\hat{x},T\hat{x}\rangle\geqslant 0

for all
x\in V
.
We have to show
T\geqslant 0
.
Let
x\in X
be an arbitrary vector.
As all vector states on
\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
are order separating
by
2.2.2
,
it is sufficient to show
\langle x,Tx\rangle\geqslant 0
.
As the image of
V
under
\eta
is ultranorm dense in
X
,
we can find find a net
x_{\alpha}
with
\widehat{x_{\alpha}}\to x
.
Then by
2.2.2

we get
\langle x,Tx\rangle=\mathop{uwlim}\displaylimits_{\alpha}\langle\widehat{x_{\alpha}},T\widehat{x_{\alpha}}\rangle\geqslant 0
.
So indeed
T\geqslant 0
, as desired.

Solution to
2.2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a C
∗
-algebra
with
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathscr{A}
.
Define
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to M_{n}\mathscr{A}

by
\varphi(d)\equiv(a_{i}^{*}da_{j})_{ij}
.
We have to show
\varphi
is an ncp-map.
Recall
\mathscr{A}
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module
with
\langle a,b\rangle\equiv a^{*}b

and so its
n
-fold direct product
\mathscr{A}^{n}

is also self dual (see also
2.5
.)
Define
T\colon\mathscr{A}^{n}\to\mathscr{A}

by
T((b_{i})_{i})\equiv\sum_{i}b_{i}a_{i}

(i.e.
T
is the row-vector
(a_{i})_{i}
.)
Clearly
T
is
\mathscr{A}
-linear.
It is also bounded:
\|T(b_{i})_{i}\|^{2}=\sum_{i}\|b_{i}a_{i}\|^{2}\leqslant\sum_{i}A\|b_{i}\|^{2}=A\|(b_{i})_{i}\|^{2}
,
where
A\equiv\max_{i}\|a_{i}\|^{2}
.
It’s easy that
T^{*}(b)\equiv(a_{i}^{*}b)_{i}
is the adjoint of
T
.
We may identify
\mathscr{B}^{a}(\mathscr{A}^{n})=M_{n}

and then
\mathrm{ad}_{T}(d)\,((b_{i})_{i})=T^{*}dT(b_{i})_{i}=T^{*}d\sum_{i}a_{i}b_{i}=(\sum_{i}(a_{j}^{*}da_{i})b_{i})_{j}=\varphi(d)\,((b_{i})_{i})
.
Thus
\mathrm{ad}_{T}=\varphi
.
By
2.2.3
the map
\mathrm{ad}_{T}
and thus
\varphi
is as well.

Solution to
2.5
.

Assume
X
and
Y
are self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-modules over a
von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
with orthonormal bases
E\subseteq X

and
F\subseteq Y
.
Write
G\equiv\{(e,0);\ e\in E\}\cup\{(0,d);\ d\in D\}
.
Clearly
G
is orthonormal.
To show
G
is an orthonormal basis,
two conditions remain.
For the first, let
(x,y)\in X\oplus Y
be given.
As
E
and
F
are orthonormal bases
we know
x=\sum_{e\in E}e\langle e,x\rangle

and
y=\sum_{f\in F}f\langle f,x\rangle
,
where the sums converge ultranorm.
The inclusions
x\mapsto(x,0)
and
y\mapsto(0,y)

are bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear and thus ultranorm continuous,
hence

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in G}g\langle g,(x,y)\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \Bigl(\sum_{e\in E}(e,0)\langle(e,0),(x,y)\rangle\Bigr)\ +\ \Bigl(\sum_{f\in F}(0,f)\langle(0,f),(x,y)\rangle\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ \Bigl(\sum_{e\in E}(e,0)\langle e,x\rangle\Bigr)\ +\ \Bigl(\sum_{f\in F}(0,f)\langle f,y\rangle\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ \Bigl(\sum_{e\in E}e\langle e,x\rangle,\sum_{f\in F}f\langle f,y\rangle\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ (x,y),

which proves the first condition.
For the second condition, let
(b_{g})_{g\in G}

be some
\ell^{2}
-summable family from
\mathscr{B}
.
The subfamilies
(b_{e_{0}})_{e\in E}

and
(b_{0,f})_{f\in F}

are
\ell^{2}
-summable as well.
Hence the
sums
\sum_{e\in E}eb_{(e,0)}

and
\sum_{f\in F}fb_{(0,f)}
converge ultranorm.
Thus
\sum_{e\in E}(e,0)b_{(e,0)}+\sum_{f\in F}(0,f)b_{(0,f)}=\sum_{g\in G}gb_{g}
converges ultranorm as well.
We have shown
G
is an orthonormal basis of
X\oplus Y
.
Consequently
X\oplus Y
is self dual
by
2.2.2
.

Solution to
2.5
.

Assume
X
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module for a von Neumann
algebra
\mathscr{B}
. Suppose
E\subseteq X
is an orthonormal set.
We will show
E
is an orthonormal basis of
E^{\perp\perp}
.
Clearly
E
is orthonormal.
Because of this, and the fact that
E^{\perp\perp}
is ultranorm
closed (by
2.5
)
we know
\sum_{e}bb_{e}
converges ultranorm in
E^{\perp\perp}

for any
\ell^{2}
-family
(b_{e})_{e}
.
Assume
x\in E^{\perp\perp}
.
To show
E
is an orthonromal base,
it only remains to be shown that
x=\sum_{e}e\langle e,x\rangle
.
Define
x^{\prime}\equiv x-\sum_{e}e\langle e,x\rangle
.
By
2.5
we know
E^{\perp\perp}
is ultranorm closed
and so
x^{\prime}\in E^{\perp\perp}
.
For any
e_{0}\in E

we also have
\langle e_{0},x^{\prime}\rangle=\langle e_{0},x\rangle-\sum_{e\in E}\langle e_{0},e\rangle\langle e,x\rangle=0
and so
x^{\prime}\in E^{\perp}
.
Hence
\langle x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\rangle=0
, so indeed
x=\sum_{e}e\langle e,x\rangle

and
E
is an orthonormal basis of
E^{\perp\perp}
.

For the second part, assume
x\in X
.
By Parseval’s identity (see
2.2.2
)
we have
\langle x,x\rangle=\sum_{e}\langle x,e\rangle\langle e,x\rangle

for any
x\in E^{\perp\perp}
.
To prove the converse,
assume
\langle x,x\rangle=\sum_{e}\langle x,e\rangle\langle e,x\rangle
.
By
2.5
we know
x=x^{\prime}+x^{\prime\prime}

for
x^{\prime}\in E^{\perp\perp}
and
x^{\prime\prime}\in E^{\perp}
.
Note
\langle e,x^{\prime}\rangle=\langle e,x\rangle
for any
e\in E

and so by Parseval’s identity for
E^{\perp\perp}

we see
\langle x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\rangle=\sum_{e}\langle x,e\rangle\langle e,x\rangle=\langle x,x\rangle
.
Now, using
\langle x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}\rangle=0

we see
\langle x,x\rangle=\langle x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\rangle+\langle x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\rangle=\langle x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\rangle+\langle x,x\rangle

and so
\langle x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\rangle=0
, whence
x\in E^{\perp\perp}
.

Solution to
2.5
.

Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for some von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}
.
Assume
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X
.
We will show that there is a finite orthonormal set
E

of
n
or fewer elements such that
E
is a
basis of
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}^{\perp\perp}
.
We do this by induction over
n
.
For
n=0
, the set
E=\emptyset
suffices.
For the induction step,
assume
n>0
and
E^{\prime}
is an
orthonormal basis of
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}\}^{\perp\perp}
.
Write
x^{\prime}\equiv x_{n}-\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}e\langle e,x_{n}\rangle
.
If
x^{\prime}=0
, then
E\equiv E^{\prime}
suffices.
For the other case, assume
x^{\prime}\neq 0
.
By polar decomposition (see the end of
2.2.2
),
there is an
u\in X

with
x^{\prime}=u\langle x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}

and
\langle u,u\rangle=\left\lceil\langle x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\rangle\right\rceil
.
Define
E\equiv E^{\prime}\cup\{u\}
.
Clearly
E
is an othonormal set of
n
or fewer elements.
By the induction assumption
and
2.5
,
we know
x_{i}\in E^{\prime\perp\perp}\subseteq E^{\perp\perp}
for
i\leqslant 1\neq n-1
.
For any
d\in E^{\perp}

we have
\langle d,x^{\prime}\rangle=\langle d,u\rangle\langle x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}=0

and so
x^{\prime}\in E^{\perp\perp}
.
Clearly
\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}e\langle e,x_{n}\rangle\in E^{\prime\perp\perp}\subseteq E^{\perp\perp}

and so
x_{n}=x^{\prime}+\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}e\langle e,x_{n}\rangle\in E^{\perp\perp}
.
Together with the previous,
we see
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}^{\perp\perp}\subseteq E^{\perp\perp\perp\perp}=E^{\perp\perp}\subseteq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}^{\perp\perp}
,
thus
E^{\perp\perp}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}^{\perp\perp}
.
By
2.5

we know
E
is an orthonormal basis of
E^{\perp\perp}
,
which completes the proof by induction.

Solution to
2.5
.

For brevity write
\ell^{2}\equiv\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})
.
We will first proof that
\ell^{2}
is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module.
Assume
(a_{i})_{i},(b_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}
.
We want to show
(a_{i}+b_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}
.
First, we to show
\sum_{i}(a_{i}+b_{i})^{*}(a_{i}+b_{i})
is bounded.
Pick
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
\sum_{i}a_{i}^{*}a_{i}\leqslant A

and
\sum_{i}b_{i}^{*}b_{i}\leqslant B
which exist
as
(a_{i})_{i}
and
(b_{i})_{i}
are
\ell^{2}
.
Let
f
be any normal state on
\mathscr{B}

and
S\subseteq I
some finite subset.
Then

\displaystyle f\Bigl(\sum_{i\in S}(a_{i}+b_{i})^{*}(a_{i}+b_{i})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{i\in S}\|a_{i}+b_{i}\|_{f}^{2}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \sum_{i\in S}\|a_{i}\|_{f}^{2}+\|b_{i}\|_{f}^{2}+2\|a_{i}\|_{f}\|b_{i}\|_{f}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ A+B+2\sum_{i\in S}\|a_{i}\|_{f}\|b_{i}\|_{f}.

By Cauchy–Schwarz
\sum_{i\in S}\|a_{i}\|_{f}\|b_{i}\|_{f}\leqslant\bigl(\sum_{i\in S}\|a_{i}\|^{2}_{f}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(\sum_{i\in S}\|b_{i}\|^{2}_{f}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant(AB)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
As normal states are order separating,
we see that we have a bounded and thus
converging sum of positive elements
\sum_{i}(a_{i}+b_{i})^{*}(a_{i}+b_{i})\leqslant A+B+(AB)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Suppose
i\in I
.
It remains to be shown
\left\lceil(a_{i}+b_{i})(a_{i}+b_{i})^{*}\right\rceil\leqslant p_{i}
.
Recall from

that
\left\lceil xx^{*}\right\rceil\leqslant p_{i}
if and only if
p_{i}x=x
.
Clearly
p_{i}(a_{i}+b_{i})=p_{i}a_{i}+p_{i}b_{i}=a_{i}+b_{i}

as
(a_{i})_{i},(b_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}
and so indeed
(a_{i}+b_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}
.

Suppose
b\in\mathscr{B}

and
(a_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}
.
Then
b^{*}\bigl(\sum_{i}a_{i}^{*}a_{i}\bigr)b=\sum_{i}(a_{i}b)^{*}a_{i}b
as
x\mapsto b^{*}xb
is normal
by

and so
(a_{i}b)_{i}
is
\ell^{2}
.
Furthermore
p_{i}a_{i}b=a_{i}b
for any
i\in I
,
so
(a_{i}b)_{i}\in\ell^{2}
.
We have shown
\ell^{2}
is a right
\mathscr{B}
-module
with coordinatewise operations.

Next we will show
\langle(a_{i})_{i},(b_{i})_{i}\rangle\equiv\sum_{i}a_{i}^{*}b_{i}
defines a
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner product
on
\ell^{2}
.
First we have to show the sum converges.
Suppose
f
is any normal state on
\mathscr{B}
.
Pick
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

with
\sum_{i}a_{i}^{*}a_{i}\leqslant A

and
\sum_{i}b_{i}^{*}b_{i}\leqslant B
.
For any finite subset
S\subseteq I

we have

\Bigl|f\Bigl(\sum_{i\in S}a_{i}^{*}b_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr|\ \leqslant\ \sum_{i\in S}|[a_{i},b_{i}]_{f}|\ \leqslant\ \sum_{i\in S}\|a_{i}\|_{f}\|b_{i}\|_{f}\ \leqslant\ (AB)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where we used Cauchy–Schwarz for
\mathscr{B}
-valued inner products
in the second inequality and classic
Cauchy–Schwarz in the final inequality.
We have shown that
\sum_{i\in S}a_{i}^{*}b_{i}

is a norm-bounded net in
S
.
We claim it is ultraweakly Cauchy as well.
For now, pick any finite sets
S,T\subseteq I
.
Assume
f
is any normal state on
\mathscr{B}
.
We want to show that the following quantity vanishes
for sufficiently large
S\cap T
.

\Bigl|f\Bigl(\sum_{i\in S}a_{i}^{*}b_{i}-\sum_{i\in T}a_{i}^{*}b_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr|\ \leqslant\ \Bigl|\sum_{i\in S-T}f(a_{i}^{*}b_{i})\Bigr|\ +\ \Bigl|\sum_{i\in T-S}f(a_{i}^{*}b_{i})\Bigr|.

(7.2)

Note that
[(a_{i})_{i},(b_{i})_{i}]\equiv\sum_{i\in S-T}f(a_{i}^{*}b_{i})

is an inner product and so

\Bigl|\sum_{i\in S-T}f(a_{i}^{*}b_{i})\Bigr|\ \leqslant\ \Bigl(\sum_{i\in S-T}f(a_{i}^{*}a_{i})\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigl(\sum_{i\in S-T}f(b_{i}^{*}b_{i})\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(7.3)

The sum
\sum_{i}f(a_{i}^{*}a_{i})
converges
and so
\sum_{i\in S-T}f(a_{i}^{*}a_{i})

can be made arbitrarily small
by picking sufficiently large
S\cap T
.
And so (with a similar argument for the other factor),
we see that (
7.3
) vanishes,
which is the left term of (
7.2
).
The argument for the other term of (
7.2
) is the same.
Thus
\sum_{i\in S}a_{i}^{*}b_{i}
is ultraweakly Cauchy
and converges by
.
From the fact that
a\mapsto a^{*}
,

a\mapsto ab
and
(a,b)\mapsto a+b

are all ultraweakly continuous,
it follows readily that
\langle(a_{i})_{i},\rangle(b_{i})_{i}\rangle\equiv\sum_{i}a_{i}^{*}b_{i}
is an inner product on
\ell^{2}
.
We claim this inner product is definite.
Assume
0=\langle(a_{i})_{i},(a_{i})_{i}\rangle\equiv\sum_{i}a_{i}^{*}a_{i}
.
Then
a_{i}^{*}a_{i}=0
for each
i\in I
and so
a_{i}=0
,
which is to say
(a_{i})_{i}=0
. Indeed our inner product is definite.
Thus
\ell^{2}
is a pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.

Write
E\equiv\{\delta_{i};\ i\in I\}
,
where
(\delta_{i})_{j}=0
for
i\neq j

and
(\delta_{i})_{i}=p_{i}
.
Clearly
E
is an orthonormal set.
We claim it’s an orthonormal basis of
\ell^{2}
.
Assume
(a_{i})_{i}\in\ell^{2}
.
It is easy to see
\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{i}a_{i}=(a_{i})_{i}

and so
\sum_{\delta_{i}\in E}\delta_{i}\langle\delta_{i},(a_{i})_{i}\rangle=\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{i}a_{i}=(a_{i})_{i}
.
It remains to be shown that
\sum_{\delta_{i}\in E}\delta_{i}b_{\delta_{i}}

converges ultranorm for any
\ell^{2}
summable
(b_{i})_{\delta_{i}\in E}
,
which indeed it does to
(b_{\delta_{i}})_{i\in I}

as we already saw.
Thus
\ell^{2}
is self dual.

For the final part of the exercise, assume
X
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
over some von Neumanna algebra
\mathscr{B}
with orthonormal basis
E\subseteq X
.
Define
\vartheta\colon X\to\ell^{2}((\langle e,e\rangle)_{e\in E})

by
\vartheta(x)\equiv(\langle e,x\rangle)_{e}
.
Clearly
\vartheta
is
\mathscr{B}
-linear.
It also preserves the inner product:

\langle x,y\rangle=\langle x,\sum_{e}e\langle e,y\rangle\rangle=\sum_{e}\langle x,e\langle e,y\rangle\rangle=\sum_{e}\langle x,e\rangle\langle e,y\rangle=\langle\vartheta(x),\vartheta(y)\rangle
.
which entails it’s injective.
To show
\vartheta
is surjective,
let
(x_{e})_{e}\in\ell^{2}((\langle e,e\rangle)_{e\in E})

be given.
The family
(x_{e})_{e}
is
\ell^{2}

so
\sum_{e}eb_{e}
converges ultranorm.
Clearly
\vartheta(\sum_{e}eb_{e})=(\langle e,\sum_{e}eb_{e}\rangle)_{e}=(b_{e})_{e}
,
so
\vartheta
is indeed surjective.
It follows
\vartheta
is an isomorphism
X\cong\ell^{2}((\langle e,e\rangle)_{e\in E})
.

Solution to
2.5
.

Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X

for some von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{B}

with orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
.
Assume
(u_{i})_{i\in I}
is a family partial isometries
with
u_{i}u_{i}^{*}=\langle e_{i},e_{i}\rangle
.
We will show
(e_{i}u_{i})_{i\in I}
is an orthonormal basis
of
X
.
For brevity, write
d_{i}\equiv e_{i}u_{i}
.
To start, note
\langle d_{i},d_{j}\rangle=u_{i}^{*}\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle u_{j}
,
which is zero if
i\neq j
.
If
i=j
, then
\langle d_{i},d_{i}\rangle=u_{i}^{*}\langle e_{i},e_{i}\rangle u_{i}=u_{i}^{*}u_{i}
which is a projection.
So
(d_{i})_{i}
is orthonormal.
Hence
\sum_{i}d_{i}b_{i}
converges for any
\ell^{2}
-family
(d_{i})_{i}
.
It remains to be shown that
x=\sum_{i}d_{i}\langle d_{i},x\rangle
.
Note
\sum_{i}d_{i}\langle d_{i},x\rangle=\sum_{i}e_{i}\langle e_{i}u_{i}u_{i}^{*},x\rangle=\sum_{i}e_{i}\langle e_{i}\langle e_{i},e_{i}\rangle,x\rangle=\sum_{i}e_{i}\langle e_{i},x\rangle=x
,
so indeed
(d_{i})_{i}
is an orthonormal basis of
X
.

For the next part, assume
(p_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(q_{i})_{i\in I}

are projections with
p_{i}\sim q_{i}
.
Let
p_{i}
denote the partial projection with
u_{i}^{*}u_{i}=q_{i}

and
u_{i}u_{i}^{*}=p_{i}
.
Consider
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})
.
Define
((\delta_{i})_{i})\in\ell^{2}

by
(\delta_{i})_{j}=0
if
i\neq j

and
(\delta_{i})_{i}=p_{i}
.
In
2.5
we saw
(\delta_{i})_{i}
is an
orthonormal basis of
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})
.
Note
u_{i}u_{i}^{*}=p_{i}=\langle\delta_{i},\delta_{i}\rangle
.
By the previous
\delta_{i}u_{i}
is another orthonormal basis of
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})
.
By the second part of
2.5

we see
\ell^{2}((p_{i})_{i\in I})\cong\ell^{2}((\langle\delta_{i}u_{i},\delta_{i}u_{i}\rangle)_{i\in I})=\ell^{2}((u_{i}^{*}p_{i}u_{i})_{i\in I})=\ell^{2}((q_{i})_{i\in I})
,
as promised.

Solution to
2.5
.

Let
X
be a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module.
Assume
E\subseteq X
and
e_{1},e_{2}\in X

such that
E\cup\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
is an orthonormal basis
and
\langle e_{1},e_{1}\rangle+\langle e_{2},e_{2}\rangle\leqslant 1

We will show that
E^{\prime}\equiv E\cup\{e_{1}+e_{2}\}

is an orthonormal basis as well.
For brevity, write
p_{1}\equiv\langle e_{1},e_{1}\rangle

and
p_{2}\equiv\langle e_{2},e_{2}\rangle
.
Clearly
E
itself is an orthonormal set.
For any
e\in E
we have
\langle e,e_{1}+e_{2}\rangle=\langle e,e_{1}\rangle+\langle e,e_{2}\rangle=0

and so
E^{\prime}
is an orthogonal set.
By assumption
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are projections
with
p_{1}+p_{2}\leqslant 1

and so by

they are orthogonal and thus in particular
p_{1}+p_{2}

is again a projection.
Hence
\langle e_{1}+e_{2},e_{1}+e_{2}\rangle=p_{1}+p_{2}
is a projection.
Thus
E^{\prime}
is an orthonormal set.

Let
x\in X
be given.
By
2.2.2
we have
e_{2}=e_{2}p_{2}

and so
e_{2}\langle e_{1}+e_{2},x\rangle=e_{2}\langle(e_{1}+e_{2})p_{2},x\rangle=e_{2}\langle e_{2},x\rangle
.
Similarly
e_{1}\langle e_{1}+e_{2},x\rangle=e_{1}\langle e_{1},x\rangle
.
Thus
x=e_{1}\langle e_{1},x\rangle+e_{2}\langle e_{2},x\rangle+\sum_{e\in E}e\langle e,x\rangle=(e_{1}+e_{2})\langle e_{1}+e_{2},x\rangle+\sum_{e\in E}e\langle e,x\rangle
,
which shows the first condition on an orthonormal
set to be an orthonormal basis.
The second (and final) condition
holds automatically as
E^{\prime}
is an orthonormal set
and
X
is ultranorm complete. Thus
E^{\prime}
is indeed
an orthonormal basis.

The the last part of the exercise, assume
p,q\in\mathscr{B}

are projections with
p+q\leqslant 1
.
Clearly
\{p+q\}
is an orthonormal basis of
(p+q)\mathscr{B}

and so is
\{p,q\}
by the previous.
Hence by
2.5

we see
(p+q)\mathscr{B}\cong\ell^{2}(\{p,q\})=p\mathscr{B}\oplus q\mathscr{B}
, as promised.

Solution to
2.5.1
.

Assume
X
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{A}
-module
and
Y
is a self-dual Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
for von Neumann algebras
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
.

1.

Assume
x_{1},x_{2},x\in X
and
y_{1},y_{2},y\in Y
.
Then

\displaystyle\left|x_{1}\right>\!\left<x_{2}\right|\otimes\left|y_{1}\right>\!\left<y_{2}\right|\,x\otimes y

\displaystyle\ =\ (\left|x_{1}\right>\!\left<x_{2}\right|x)\otimes(\left|y_{1}\right>\!\left<y_{2}\right|y)

\displaystyle\ =\ (x_{1}\langle x_{2},x\rangle)\otimes(y_{1}\langle y_{2},y\rangle)

\displaystyle\ =\ (x_{1}\otimes y_{1})(\langle x_{2},x\rangle\otimes\langle y_{2},y\rangle)

\displaystyle\ =\ (x_{1}\otimes y_{1})\langle x_{2}\otimes y_{2},x\otimes y\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \left|x_{1}\otimes y_{1}\right>\!\left<x_{2}\otimes y_{2}\right|\,x\otimes y.

This is sufficient to show
that
\left|x_{1}\right>\!\left<x_{2}\right|\otimes\left|y_{1}\right>\!\left<y_{2}\right|=\left|x_{1}\otimes y_{1}\right>\!\left<x_{2}\otimes y_{2}\right|
,
either by appealing to the defining universal property of
X\otimes Y

or by the related property
that the
\mathscr{A}\odot\mathscr{B}
-linear
span of
\{x\otimes y;\ x\in X,y\in Y\}

is ultranorm dense in
X\otimes Y
.
(In fact the
\mathbb{C}
-linear span is already ultranorm dense.)

2.

Clearly, for any
x\in X
and
y\in Y

we have
(1\otimes 1)\,(x\otimes y)=1\,x\otimes y

and so by same token (as the conclusion of the previous point)
we see
1=1\otimes 1
.

3.

Assume
x\in X
,
y\in Y
,
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
and
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)
.
Then

(S\otimes T)(S^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime})\,x\otimes y\ =\ (SS^{\prime}x)\otimes(TT^{\prime}y)\\
\ =\ (SS^{\prime})\otimes(TT^{\prime})\,x\otimes y,

which is sufficient to show
(SS^{\prime})\otimes(TT^{\prime})=(S\otimes T)\otimes(S^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime})
(see the conclusion of the first point.)

4.

Assume
S\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(X)
,
T\in\mathscr{B}^{a}(Y)
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in X

and
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
.
Then

\displaystyle\langle(S\otimes T)^{*}\,x_{1}\otimes y_{1},\ x_{2}\otimes y_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle x_{1}\otimes y_{1},\ (S\otimes T)\,x_{2}\otimes y_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle x_{1}\otimes y_{1},\ (Sx_{2})\otimes(Ty_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle x_{1},Sx_{2}\rangle\otimes\langle y_{1},Ty_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle S^{*}x_{1},x_{2}\rangle\otimes\langle T^{*}y_{1},y_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\ =\ \langle(S^{*}\otimes T^{*})\,x_{1}\otimes y_{1},\ x_{2}\otimes y_{2}\rangle.

This is sufficient (see the conclusion of the first point)
to show that the vector functionals
for
(S\otimes T)^{*}x_{1}\otimes y_{1}

and
(S^{*}\otimes T^{*})x_{1}\otimes y_{1}
agree.
Hence
(S\otimes T)^{*}x_{1}\otimes y_{1}=(S^{*}\otimes T^{*})x_{1}\otimes y_{1}
.
In turn this is sufficient to show
(S\otimes T)^{*}=S^{*}\otimes T^{*}
,
as desired.

Solution to
2.6
.

Assume
\varphi\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
is an ncp-map between
von Neumann algebras.

1.

Assume
c\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}
is a filter
and that
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)
is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi
.
We will show that
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,c\mathop{\circ}h)
is a Paschke
dilation of
c\mathop{\circ}\varphi
.
To this end, assume
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
is some nmiu-map
and
h^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{C}
is any ncp-map
with
h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=c\mathop{\circ}\varphi
.
Assume
\varphi(1)\leqslant 1
.
(We will reduce the general to this one later on.)
Note
h^{\prime}(1)=h^{\prime}(\varrho^{\prime}(1))=c(\varphi(1))\leqslant c(1)

and so by the universal property of
c
,
there is a unique ncp-map map
h^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}

with
c\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime\prime}=h^{\prime}
.
Then
c\mathop{\circ}\varphi=h^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=c\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}
.
And so
\varphi=h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}

as
c
is injective, see
2.6
.
There is a unique ncp-map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}

with
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma=h^{\prime\prime}

and
\sigma\mathop{\circ}\varrho^{\prime}=\varrho
.
Then
h^{\prime}=c\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime\prime}=c\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\sigma
.
Assume
\sigma^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{P}

is any ncp-map with
\sigma^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varrho=\varrho

and
c\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}=h^{\prime}
.
Then
c\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}=h^{\prime}=c\mathop{\circ}h^{\prime\prime}

and so
h\mathop{\circ}\sigma^{\prime}=h^{\prime\prime}
.
Hence
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
by the uniqueness of
\sigma
.
Thus
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,c\mathop{\circ}h)
is a Paschke dilation of
c\mathop{\circ}\varphi
.

Now, in the general case, assume
\varphi(1)\not\leqslant 1
.
Then
\varphi(1)\neq 0
.
Define
\varphi^{\prime}\equiv\|\varphi(1)\|^{-1}\varphi
.
Then
\varphi^{\prime}(1)=\|\varphi(1)\|^{-1}\varphi(1)\leqslant 1
.
By
2.1

we know
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,\|\varphi(1)\|^{-1}h)

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi^{\prime}
.
Now we are in our previous case
and so
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,\|\varphi(1)\|^{-1}c\mathop{\circ}h)

is a Paschke dilation of
c\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{\prime}
.
Applying
2.1
once again,
we conclude
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,c\mathop{\circ}h)

is a Paschke dilation of
c\mathop{\circ}\varphi
as desired.

2.

Assume
c^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{B}
is a filter of
\varphi(1)
.
By the universal property of
c^{\prime}
,
there is a unique ncp-map
\varphi^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}

with
c^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
.
Then
c^{\prime}(\varphi^{\prime}(1))=\varphi(1)=c^{\prime}(1)

and so by injectivity of
c^{\prime}
(see
2.6
)
we get
\varphi^{\prime}(1)=1
—
that is to say:
\varphi^{\prime}
is unital.

For the final part of this exercise, assume
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,h)

is a Paschke dilation of
\varphi^{\prime}
.
By the previous point,
we know
(\mathscr{P},\varrho,c^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h)

is a Paschke dilation of
c^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi
,
as desired.

Solution to
2.6
.

Let
c\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{B}
be some filter for
b\in\mathscr{B}
.
By
2.6

we know
c_{b}\colon\left\lceil b\right\rceil\mathscr{B}\left\lceil b\right\rceil\to\mathscr{B}

given by
a\mapsto\sqrt{b}a\sqrt{b}

is also a filter for
b
.
By the universal property of
c
,
there is a unique ncp-map
\vartheta_{1}

with
c\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}=c_{b}
.
In the other direction, by the universal property of
c_{b}
,
there is a unique ncp-map
\vartheta_{2}

with
c_{b}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}=c
.
Hence
c\mathop{\circ}(\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2})=c_{b}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}=c\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}
.
Clearly
c(1)\leqslant c(1)

and so by the universal property of
c

the map
\mathrm{id}
is the unique ncp-map
with
c\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=c
, hence
\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}=\mathrm{id}
.
Reasoning similarly on the other side,
we see
\vartheta_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}=\mathrm{id}

and so
\vartheta_{2}
is an isomorphism.
Recall
c=c_{b}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}

and so it is sufficient to show
c_{b}
is injective.
To this end, let
a_{1},a_{2}\in\left\lceil b\right\rceil\mathscr{B}\left\lceil b\right\rceil
be given
with
c_{b}(a_{1})=c_{b}(a_{2})
;
i.e.
\sqrt{b}a_{1}\sqrt{b}=\sqrt{b}a_{2}\sqrt{b}
.
Then
a_{1}=a_{2}
by

and so
c_{b}
is indeed injective.

Solution to
2.6
.

Assume
\varrho\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

is a surjective nmiu-map between von Neumann algebras.
We already saw in
§
‣
2.1

that the kernel of an nmiu-map is an ultraweakly-closed
two-sided ideal and hence a principal ideal of a central projection
by
.
Write
z
for the central projection of
\mathscr{A}

with
\ker\varrho=(1-z)\mathscr{A}
;

h_{z}\colon\mathscr{A}\to z\mathscr{A}
for the map
h_{z}(a)=za

and
\varrho^{\prime}\colon z\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}

for the restriction of
\varrho
to
z\mathscr{A}
.
Clearly
\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h_{z}=\varrho
and so
\varrho^{\prime}
is surjective.
Furthermore
\varrho^{\prime}
is injective,
because for any
a\in z\mathscr{A}
with
\varrho(a)=0

we have
\varrho(a)=0
and so
a\in\ker\varrho=(1-z)\mathscr{A}
,
hence
a=0
.
It is easy to see that
\varrho^{\prime}
as a bijective miu-map
has an miu inverse and so it is an miu-isomorphism.
Consequently, it is an nmiu-isomorphism (as
miu-maps are order-preserving)
and in particular an ncp-isomorphism.
The map
h_{z}
is a standard corner for
z
(see
2.6
)
and so
\varrho=\varrho^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}h_{z}
is also a corner for
z

as
\varrho^{\prime}
is an ncp-isomoprhism.

Conversely, assume
h\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}

is a corner for a central projection
z\in\mathscr{A}
.
The map
h_{z}\colon\mathscr{A}\to z\mathscr{A}
given by
a\mapsto za

is another corner for
z
, see
2.6
.
By the universal property of
h
,
there is a unique ncp-map
\vartheta_{1}\colon z\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}

with
h\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}=\vartheta_{2}
.
On the other side, by the universal property of
h_{z}
,
there is a unique ncp-map
\vartheta_{2}\colon\mathscr{C}\to z\mathscr{A}

with
h_{z}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}=\vartheta_{1}
.
Again, by the universal property of
h
,
the map
\mathrm{id}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}

is the unique ncp-map
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}

with
h=h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}
.
Note
h=h_{z}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}=h\mathop{\circ}(\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2})
and so
\mathrm{id}=\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}
.
Similarly
\vartheta_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}=\mathrm{id}
.
Thus
\vartheta_{1}
is an ncp-isomorphism
and consequently an nmiu-isomoprhism by
.
Clearly
h_{z}
is a surjective nmiu-map
and thus
h=h_{z}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}

is a surjective nmiu-map as well.

Solution to
3.1
.

Reflexivity is easy:
a\ovee 0=0\ovee a=a

by the partial commutativity and zero axiom, so
a\leqslant a
.
For transitivity, assume
a\leqslant b
and
b\leqslant c
.
Pick
d,e\in M

with
a\ovee d=b
and
b\ovee e=c
.
Then by partial
associativity
c=b\ovee e=(a\ovee d)\ovee e=a\ovee(d\ovee e)

so
a\leqslant c
.

Solution to
3.1
.

Assume
E
and
F
are effect algebras.
We define
a\ovee b
for
a,b\in E\times F

whenever both
a_{1}\perp b_{1}
and
a_{2}\perp b_{2}

and in that case
a\ovee b\equiv(a_{1}\ovee b_{1},a_{2}\ovee b_{2})
.
Furthermore, define
1\equiv(1,1)
and
0\equiv(0,0)
.
We claim that these operations turn
E\times F
into an effect algebra.

We start with partial commutativity.
Assume
a,b\in E\times F
with
a\perp b
.
Then
a_{1}\perp b_{1}
and
a_{2}\perp b_{2}
,
hence
b_{1}\perp a_{1}
,
b_{2}\perp a_{2}

and
a\ovee b=(a_{1}\ovee b_{1},a_{2}\ovee b_{2})=(b_{1}\ovee a_{1},b_{2}\ovee a_{2})=b\ovee a
.

We continue with partial associativity.
Assume
a,b,c\in E\times F
with
a\perp b
and
a\ovee b\perp c
.
Then
a_{i}\perp b_{i}
,
a_{i}\ovee b_{i}\perp c_{i}
for
i=1,2

and so
b_{i}\perp c_{i}
and
(a_{i}\ovee b_{i})\ovee c_{i}=a_{i}\ovee(b_{i}\ovee c_{i})
. Consequently
(a\ovee b)\ovee c=a\ovee(b\ovee c)
.

Next the zero axiom: for any
a\in E\times F

we have
a_{1}\perp 0
,
a_{2}\perp 0
,
0\ovee a_{1}=a_{1}

and
0\ovee a_{2}=a_{2}
.
Thus
0\equiv(0,0)\perp(a_{1},a_{2})
and
0\ovee a=a
.

To prove the orthocomplement law, assume
a\in E\times F
.
Then for
a\equiv(a_{1}^{\perp},a_{2}^{\perp})

we have
a\perp a^{\perp}
and
a\ovee a^{\perp}=(1,1)\equiv 1
.
Furthermore, if
a\vee b=1
for some
b\in E\times F
,
then
b_{1}=a_{1}^{\perp}
and
b_{2}=a_{2}^{\perp}
, hence
b=a^{\perp}
.

Only the zero–one law remains. Asumme
a\perp 1
for some
a\in E\times F
.
Then
a_{1}\perp 1
and
a_{2}\perp 1
, hence
a_{1}=0
and
a_{2}=0
.
Thus
a=0
, as desired.
We have shown
E\times F
is an effect algebra with componentwise operations.

Write
\pi_{1}\colon E\times F\to E
and
\pi_{2}\colon E\times F\to F

for the maps given by
\pi_{1}(a_{1},a_{2})=a_{1}

and
\pi_{2}(a_{1},a_{2})=a_{2}
.
Clearly
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are additive and unital, hence
effect algebra homomorphisms.
We will show that
E\times F
with projections
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}

forms a categorical product of
E
and
F
in
\mathsf{EA}
.
To this end, assume
f_{1}\colon G\to E
and
f_{2}\colon G\to F

are effect algebra homomorphisms for some effect algebra
G
.
Define
g\colon G\to E\times F
by
g(a)=(f_{1}(a),f_{2}(a))
.
Clearly
g
is the unique map with
\pi_{i}\mathop{\circ}g=f_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
It is also an effect algebra homomorphism and
so
E\times F
is indeed the product of
E
and
F
.

Solution to
3.1
.

Let
E
be an effect algebra with any
a\in E
.
We will first show that
1^{\perp}=0
and
a^{\perp\perp}=a
.
By the orthocomplement law
1\ovee 1^{\perp}=1
,
so
1^{\perp}\perp 1
,
hence
1^{\perp}=0
by the zero–one law.
By the orthocomplement law
a\ovee a^{\perp}=1
.
Thus
a^{\perp}\ovee a=a\ovee a^{\perp}=1
by partial commutativity.
Hence
a=a^{\perp\perp}
by uniqueness of the orthocomplement.
Now, to prove the zero law,
note
a^{\perp}\ovee a^{\perp\perp}=1=(a^{\perp}\ovee a^{\perp\perp})\ovee(a^{\perp}\ovee a^{\perp\perp})^{\perp}=(a^{\perp}\ovee a^{\perp\perp})\ovee 0=a^{\perp}\ovee(a^{\perp\perp}\ovee 0)

by the orthocomplement law and partial associativity.
Thus by uniqueness of the orthocomplement
a^{\perp\perp}=a^{\perp\perp}\ovee 0
,
hence
0\ovee a=a
by partial commutativity and
a^{\perp\perp}=a
.

Solution to
3.1
.

We start with (D1). Assume
b\leqslant a
. Then
b\ovee c=a
for some
c
.
By definition
c=a\ominus b
.
Conversely, assume
a\ominus b
is defined.
Then
b\ovee(b\ominus a)=a
, hence
b\leqslant a
.
To prove (D2), assume
a\ominus b
is defined.
Then
a\ominus b\leqslant b\ovee(a\ominus b)=a
as desired.
For (D3) assume
a\ominus b
and
a\ominus(a\ominus b)

are defined.
Note
(a\ominus b)\ovee b=b\ovee(a\ominus b)=a

and so
b=a\ominus(a\ominus b)
.

(D4) is next. Assume
a\leqslant b\leqslant c
.
Then
(c\ominus b)\ovee(b\ominus a)\ovee a=c=(c\ominus a)\ovee a

and so by cancellation
c\ominus b\leqslant(c\ominus b)\ovee(b\ominus a)=c\ominus a
.
Note
((c\ominus a)\ominus(c\ominus b))\ovee(c\ominus b)\ovee a=c=(b\ominus a)\ovee a\ovee(c\ominus b)

and so by cancellation
(c\ominus a)\ominus(c\ominus b)=b\ominus a
.

For the final part of the exercise, assume
E
is a poset
with maximum element
1
and a partial operation
\ominus

satisfying (D1), (D2), (D3) and (D4).
We will show
E
is an effect algebra with
a\ovee b=c\Leftrightarrow a=c\ominus b
and
0=1\ominus 1
.

First we have to show that if
c_{1}\ominus b=c_{2}\ominus b

for some
b\leqslant c_{1},c_{2}
in
E
,
then
c_{1}=c_{2}

so that
\ovee
is at most single-valued.
By (D4)
we have
(1\ominus b)\ominus(1\ominus c_{1})=c_{1}\ominus b

and so
(1\ominus b)\ominus a=(1\ominus b)\ominus(c_{1}\ominus b)=(1\ominus b)\ominus((1\ominus b)\ominus(1\ominus c_{1}))=1\ominus c_{1}
by (D3).
Similarly
1\ominus c_{2}=(1\ominus b)\ominus a
,
so with another application of (D3) we
see
c_{1}=1\ominus(1\ominus c_{1})=1\ominus(1\ominus c_{2})=c_{2}
.

The first axiom we will prove is partial commutativity.
Assume
a,b\in E
with
a\perp b
.
By definition, there is some
c\geqslant b
with
c\ominus b=a
.
By (D3) we have
c\ominus a=c\ominus(c\ominus b)=b

and so
b\perp a

with
b\ovee a=c=a\ovee b
as desired.
Note that we have also shown that
a\leqslant a\ovee b

and
(a\ovee b)\ominus a=b

for
a\perp b
.

Next, to prove partial associativity, assume
a,b,c\in E

with
a\perp b
and
a\ovee b\perp c
.
Then
a\leqslant a\ovee b\leqslant(a\ovee b)\ovee c
and thus

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\ =\ (a\ovee b)\ominus a

\displaystyle\ \overset{\mathclap{\mathrm{(D4)}}}{=}\ \bigl(((a\ovee b)\ovee c)\ominus a\bigr)\,\ominus\,\bigl(((a\ovee b)\ovee c)\ominus(a\ovee b)\bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl(((a\ovee b)\ovee c)\ominus a\bigr)\,\ominus\,c,

which shows
b\perp c

with
b\ovee c=((a\ovee b)\ovee c)\ominus a
.
From that it immediately follows
that
b\ovee c\perp a
with
(b\ovee c)\ovee a=(a\ovee b)\ovee c

and so
a\perp b\ovee c

with
(a\ovee b)\ovee c=a\ovee(b\ovee c)

by partial commutativity.

We continue with the zero axiom.
Assume
a\in E
.
Clearly
a\leqslant 1\leqslant 1
and
so
(1\ominus a)\ominus(1\ominus 1)=1\ominus a
by (D4),
which shows
1\ominus a\perp 1\ominus 1\equiv 0

with
(1\ominus a)\ovee 0=1\ominus a
.
The zero law follows by partial commutativity.

Now we prove the orthocomplement law.
Assume
a\in E
.
By (D3) we have
1\ominus(1\ominus a)=a
,
which demonstrates
a\perp 1\ominus a\equiv a^{\perp}

with
a\ovee a^{\perp}=1
.
Assume
b\in E
with
a\ovee b=1
.
By definition, we have
a=1\ominus b
and so by (D3)
we see
b=1\ominus(1\ominus b)=1\ominus a\equiv a^{\perp}
.

To show the last axiom (zero–one), assume
a\in E
with
a\perp 1
.
Then
1\leqslant a\ovee 1\leqslant 1
and so
a\ovee 1=1
.
Hence
a=(a\ovee 1)\ominus 1=1\ominus 1\equiv 0
.
We have shown
E
is an effect algebra.

The conclude this exercise, we show that the original
order and
\ominus
coincide with
those defined for
E
as an effect algebra.
For clarity, write
\leqslant_{\mathrm{EA}}
and
\ominus_{\mathrm{EA}}

for the latter.

To start, assume
a\leqslant b
for some
a,b\in E
(as a D-poset).
Then
b=a\ovee(b\ominus a)

as trivially
b\ominus a=b\ominus a
.
Hence
a\leqslant_{\mathrm{EA}}b
.
To prove the converse, assume
a\leqslant_{\mathrm{EA}}b
.
Then
a\ovee c=b
for some
c\in E
.
Hence
a\leqslant a\ovee c\leqslant b
. Indeed
\leqslant=\leqslant_{\mathrm{EA}}
.

Assume
a,b\in E
.
The element
a\ominus_{\textrm{EA}}b
is defined
if and only if
b\leqslant a
,
which is precisely when
a\ominus b
is defined.
Furthermore
b\ovee(a\ominus_{\textrm{EA}}b)=a

and so
a\ominus_{\textrm{EA}}b=(b\ovee(a\ominus_{\textrm{EA}}b))\ominus b=a\ominus b
.

Solution to
3.1
.

Assume
f\colon E\to F
is an homomorphism between effect algebras.

1.

We have
0\perp 1
and so
f(0)\perp f(1)=1
,
hence
f(0)=0
by the zero–one law.

2.

Assume
a\leqslant b
in
E
.
Then
a\ovee c=b
for some
c\in E
.
Hence
f(b)=f(a\ovee c)=f(a)\ovee f(c)
,
which shows
f(a)\leqslant f(b)
.

3.

Assume
a\ominus b
is defined (i.e.
a\geqslant b
).
Recall
(a\ominus b)\ovee b=a

and so
f(a\ominus b)\ovee f(b)=f(a)
.
Hence
f(a\ominus b)=f(a)\ominus f(b)
.

4.

Assume
a\in E
.
In
3.1
we saw
1\ominus a=a^{\perp}

and so by the previous point
f(a^{\perp})=f(1\ominus a)=f(1)\ominus f(a)=1\ominus f(a)=f(a)^{\perp}
.

Solution to
3.1.1
.

Let
M
be an effect monoid with any
a\in M
.
Then
a\odot 1=a\odot(0\ovee 1)=(a\odot 0)\ovee(a\odot 1)
.
Hence by cancellation
a\odot 0=0
.
Similarly
0\odot a=0
.

Solution to
3.1.1
.

Assume
M
is an effect monoid with
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in M

such that
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}a_{i}=1
and
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}a_{i}\odot b_{i}=1
.
Note that for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n

we have
a_{i}\odot b_{i}\leqslant a_{i}

as
(a_{i}\odot b_{i})\ovee a_{i}\odot b_{i}^{\perp}=a_{i}\odot 1=a_{i}
.
Thus

a_{i}^{\perp}\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j\neq i}a_{j}\ \geqslant\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j\neq i}a_{j}\odot b_{j}\ =\ (a_{i}\odot b_{i})^{\perp}\ \geqslant a_{i}^{\perp}

and so
a_{i}^{\perp}=(a_{i}\odot b_{i})^{\perp}
,
hence
a_{i}=a_{i}\odot b_{i}
.

Solution to
3.2.1
.

Let
C
be an effectus in partial form.
Assume
f\colon X\to A
and
g\colon X\to B

are any arrows in
C
with
1\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}g
.

1.

Assume
[a,b]\colon A+B\to Y
is any map.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}a\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}g\geqslant 1\mathop{\circ}b\mathop{\circ}g

and so
1\mathop{\circ}a\mathop{\circ}f\perp 1\mathop{\circ}b\mathop{\circ}g
.
Hence
a\mathop{\circ}f\perp b\mathop{\circ}g

and so
[a,b]\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=[a,b]\mathop{\circ}((\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g))=([a,b]\mathop{\circ}(\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f))\ovee([a,b]\mathop{\circ}(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g))=(a\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(b\mathop{\circ}g)
.

2.

By the previous point and
3.2.1
,
we have
1\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=[1,1]\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=(1\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(1\mathop{\circ}g)
.

3.

Assume
k\colon A\to A^{\prime}
and
l\colon B\to B^{\prime}
are two maps in
C
.
Then by the first point of this exercise,
we have
(k+l)\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle\equiv[\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}k,\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}l]\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=(\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}k\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}l\mathop{\circ}g)\equiv\langle k\mathop{\circ}f,l\mathop{\circ}g\rangle
.

4.

Assume
k\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is some map in
C
.
Then by PCM-enrichment of
C
, we
get
\langle f,g\rangle\mathop{\circ}k\equiv((\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g))\mathop{\circ}k=(\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}k)\ovee(\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}g\mathop{\circ}k)\equiv\langle f\mathop{\circ}k,g\mathop{\circ}k\rangle
.

Solution to
3.2.2
.

Assume
h_{1},h_{2}\colon Q\to P

are two arrows with
m_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}=m_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}

and
m_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}=m_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}
.
Note
(f\mathop{\circ}m_{1})\mathop{\circ}h_{1}=g\mathop{\circ}m_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}=(g\mathop{\circ}m_{2})\mathop{\circ}h_{2}

and so by the universal property of a pullback
there is a unique map
h\colon Q\to P

with
m_{1}\mathop{\circ}h=m_{1}\mathop{\circ}h_{1}

and
m_{2}\mathop{\circ}h=m_{2}\mathop{\circ}h_{2}

and so
h_{1}=h_{2}=h
.

Solution to
3.2.2
.

For the first point,
assume the left and right inner squares are pullbacks.
To prove the outer square is a pullback,
assume
\alpha\colon S\to C
and
\beta\colon S\to X

are any maps with
g^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\beta=m\mathop{\circ}\alpha
.
As the right inner square is a pullback,
there is a unique map
\gamma\colon S\to B

with
g\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\alpha

and
l\mathop{\circ}\gamma=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\beta
.
By this last equality and the fact that the left inner square is
a pullback, there is a unique map
\delta\colon S\to A

with
f\mathop{\circ}\delta=\gamma

and
k\mathop{\circ}\delta=\beta
.
We will prove that this
\delta
is also the mediating map
for the outer square.
Clearly
g\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\delta g\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\alpha
.
Assume
\delta^{\prime}\colon S\to A

is some other map with
g\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\delta^{\prime}=\alpha

and
k\mathop{\circ}\delta^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\beta
.
Note that
g\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\delta^{\prime}=\alpha=g\mathop{\circ}\gamma

and
l\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\delta^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}k\mathop{\circ}\delta^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\beta=l\mathop{\circ}\gamma
.
Thus
f\mathop{\circ}\delta^{\prime}=\gamma
as
g
and
l
are jointly monic by
3.2.2
.
Consequently
\delta=\delta^{\prime}
and so the outer square is indeed a pullback.

For the second point, assume the outer square is a pullback
and that
l
and
g
are jointly monic.
We will prove that the left inner square is a pullback.
To this end, assume
\alpha\colon S\to B
and
\beta\colon S\to X

are maps with
l\mathop{\circ}\alpha=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\beta
.
Note
m\mathop{\circ}g\mathop{\circ}\alpha=g^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}l\mathop{\circ}\alpha=g^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\beta

and thus by the fact that the outer square is a pullback,
there is a unique
\gamma\colon S\to A

with
g\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\gamma=g\mathop{\circ}\alpha

and
k\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\beta
.
This will also be the unique mediating map
demonstrating that the left inner square is a pullback.
We have
l\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\gamma=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}k\mathop{\circ}\gamma=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\beta=l\mathop{\circ}\alpha

and so by the joint monicity of
l
and
g
,
we conclude
f\mathop{\circ}\gamma=\alpha
.
Assume there is some map
\gamma^{\prime}\colon S\to A

with
f\mathop{\circ}\gamma^{\prime}=\alpha
and
k\mathop{\circ}\gamma^{\prime}=\beta
.
Then clearly
g\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\gamma^{\prime}=g\mathop{\circ}\alpha

and so by the uniqueness of
\gamma

as a mediating map for the outer square,
we find
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma
.
Thus the left inner square is a pullback as well.

Solution to
3.2.2
.

Let
f\colon X\rightharpoonup Z
and
g\colon Y\rightharpoonup Z

be two partial maps in
C
;
that is
f\colon X\to Z+1
and
g\colon Y\to Z+1
.
Then the cotuple
[f,g]\colon X+Y\to Z+1

(with respect to the coproduct that we assumed to exist)
is also a partial map
X+Y\rightharpoonup Z
.
Note
[f,g]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{\kappa}_{1}=[[f,g],\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}=f

and
[f,g]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{\kappa}_{2}=[[f,g],\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}=g
.
Suppose
h\colon X+Y\rightharpoonup Z
is a partial map
with
h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{\kappa}_{1}=f

and
h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{\kappa}_{2}=g
.
Then
h\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}=h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{\kappa}_{1}=f

and
h\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{2}=h\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\hat{\kappa}_{2}=g
,
so
h=[f,g]
.
This shows
X+Y
is also a coproduct in
\Par C
with
coprojectors
\hat{\kappa_{1}}
and
\hat{\kappa_{2}}
.

To show
0
is an initial object of
\Par C
,
assume
X
is some object of
\Par C
, i.e. of
C
.
As
0
is initial in
C
, there
is a unique map
!\colon 0\to X+1
in
C

and so there is a unique map
!\colon 0\rightharpoonup X
in
\Par C
.

Solution to
3.2.2
.

We already know that the initial object
0
of
C

is also initial in
\Par C
.
We will show that it is also final in
\Par C
.
To this end, assume
X
is some object in
\Par C
, i.e. of
C
.
There is a unique map
!\colon X\to 1
in
C

and so there is a unique map
!\colon X\rightharpoonup 0
in
\Par C
.
Thus
0
is final in
\Par C
.

For any objects
X
and
Y
from
\Par C
,
the zero map
0\colon X\to Y
in
\Par C

is by definition equal to
i\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f
,
where
f\colon X\rightharpoonup 0
is the unique final map
and
i\colon 0\to\rightharpoonup Y
is the unique initial map.
Unfolding definitions, we see
0=i\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f=[i,\kappa_{2}]\mathop{\circ}f=\kappa_{2}\mathop{\circ}!
as desired.

Solution to
3.2.2
.

For the first point, assume
C
is an effectus in total form
together with a map
f\colon A\to 0
.
We will show that
f
is an isomorpism.
By initiality of
0
, we know
f\mathop{\circ}!_{A}=!_{0}=\mathrm{id}
.
By
3.2.2
, the following square is a pullback.

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{0}

As
\mathrm{id}_{0}\mathop{\circ}f=f\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}_{A}
,
there is a unique map
h\colon A\to 0

with
\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}h=f
and
!\mathop{\circ}h=\mathrm{id}
.
So
h=f
and
!\mathop{\circ}f=!\mathop{\circ}h=\mathrm{id}
.
Thus
f
is indeed an isomorphism with inverse
!
.

For the second point, assume
C
is an effectus in total and partial form.
Let
X
and
Y
be any two objects.
As
0
is final in an effectus in total form,
there are maps
!_{X}\colon X\to 0
and
!_{Y}\colon Y\to 0
.
As
0
is a strict initial object in an effectus in partial form,
both
!_{X}
and
!_{Y}
are isomorphisms.
Hence
!_{Y}\mathop{\circ}!_{X}^{-1}
is an isomorphism between
X
and
Y
.

Solution to
3.2.3
.

To show
{\mathsf{Rng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
is an effectus in total form,
we shown the dual axioms for
\mathsf{Rng}
.
Clearly, for any two unit rings
R
and
S
,
their cartesian product
R\times S
is a categorical product
with projectors
\pi_{1}(r,s)=r
and
\pi_{2}(r,s)=s
.
The integer ring
\mathbb{Z}
is the initial object of
\mathsf{Rng}
.
The zero ring (the unique unit ring with a single element
0=1
)
is the final object of
\mathsf{Rng}
.

To show the pushout diagrams corresponding to (
3.1
) hold,
assume we are given unit rings
R,S,T
with
unit-preserving homomorphisms
\alpha,\beta,\delta

that make the outer squares of the following diagrams commute.

\textstyle{T}
\textstyle{R\times S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\times\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times!}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\times S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times!}
\scriptstyle{!\times\mathrm{id}}

\textstyle{T}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{R\times S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!\times!}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}

We have to show that there are unique dashed arrows (as shown)
that make these diagrams commute.
We start with the left diagram.
Define
f\colon R\times S\to T

by
f(r,s)\equiv\alpha(r,0)+\beta(0,s)
.
Clearly
f
is additive and
f(0,0)=\alpha(0,0)+\beta(0,0)=0
.
By assumption
\alpha(n,m)=\beta(n,m)
for any
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}

and so
\alpha(1,0)=\beta(1,0)
in particular,
hence
f(1,1)=\alpha(1,0)+\beta(0,1)=\beta(1,1)=1
.
It remains to be shown that
f
is multiplicative.
First note that
\alpha(1,0)\beta(0,1)=\beta(1,0)\beta(0,1)=\beta(0,0)=0
.
Thus
\alpha(r,0)\beta(0,s)=\alpha(r,0)\alpha(1,0)\beta(0,1)\beta(0,s)=0

for any
r\in R
and
s\in S

and similarly
\beta(0,s)\alpha(r,0)=0
.
Hence
f(r,s)f(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\alpha(r,0)\alpha(r^{\prime},0)+\beta(0,s)\beta(0,s^{\prime})=\alpha(rr^{\prime},0)+\beta(0,ss^{\prime})=f(rr^{\prime},ss^{\prime})
.
We have shown
f
is a unit-preserving ring homomorphism.
Clearly
f(r,m)=\alpha(r,0)+\beta(0,m)=\alpha(r,0)+\alpha(0,m)=\alpha(r,m)

for any
r\in R
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}

hence
\alpha=f\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{id}\times!)
.
Similarly
\beta=f\mathop{\circ}(!\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id})
.
Assume
f^{\prime}\colon R\times S\to T
is any unit-preserving
ring homomorphism with
\alpha=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{id}\times!)

and
\beta=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}(!\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id})
.
Then clearly
f^{\prime}(r,0)=\alpha(r,0)
and
f^{\prime}(0,s)=\beta(0,s)
,
so
f^{\prime}(r,s)=f^{\prime}(r,0)+f^{\prime}(0,s)=\alpha(r,0)+\beta(0,s)=f(r,s)
.
This shows the left square above is indeed a pushout.

We continue with the diagram on the right.
By assumption
\delta(n,m)=n
for any
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}

hence
\delta(0,s)=\delta(0,s)\delta(0,1)=0
for any
s\in S
.
Thus
\delta(r,s)=\delta(r,0)+\delta(0,s)=\delta(r,0)

for any
r\in R
and
s\in S
.
Define
g\colon R\to T
by
g(r)=\delta(r,0)
.
Clearly
g
is additive, multiplicative and
g(0,0)=0
.
Furthermore
g(1)=\delta(1,0)=\delta(1,1)=1
,
so it is a unit-preserving ring homomorphism.
It is easy to see that
g\mathop{\circ}\pi_{1}=\delta
and that
g

is the unique such unit-preserving ring homomorphism.
We have shown the right diagram is a pushout too.

To show
{\mathsf{Rng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
is an effectus in total form,
it only remains to be shown that
\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2}\rangle,\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
are jointly epic.
So assume
f,g\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to R

are two unit-preserving ring homomorphisms
for which we have
f\mathop{\circ}\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2}\rangle=g\mathop{\circ}\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{2}\rangle

and
f\mathop{\circ}\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle=g\mathop{\circ}\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle
.
By the first equality
f(k,0,0)=g(k,0,0)

and by the second
f(0,l,0)=g(0,l,0)

for any
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In particular
f(0,0,1)=1-f(1,0,0)-f(0,1,0)=1-g(1,0,0)-g(0,1,0)=g(0,0,1)

and so
f(0,0,m)=mf(0,0,1)=mg(0,0,1)=g(0,0,m)

for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Putting it all together:
f(k,l,m)=f(k,0,0)=f(0,l,m)+f(0,0,m)=g(k,0,0)=g(0,l,m)+g(0,0,m)=g(k,l,m)
.
So
f=g
and so we have shown the required joint epicity.
We have shown
{\mathsf{Rng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
is indeed an effectus in total form.

We continue with the two additional points.
Let
p\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to R
be any predicate on
R
.
Then
p(1,0)\in R
with
p(1,0)^{2}=p(1,0^{2})=p(1,0)
and so
p(1,0)

is an idempotent.
Furthermore
p(0,1)=p(1,1)-p(1,0)=1-p(1,0)
,
so
p
is fixed by the idempotent
p(1,0)
.
Let
e\in R
be any idempotent.
It is easy to see that
p(n,m)\equiv ne+m(1-e)
is a unit-preserving
ring homomorphism with
p(1,0)=e
. Thus the predicates
on
R
correspond to its idempotents.
This also shows that the set of scalars of
{\mathsf{Rng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}

is the two-element effect monoid
2

as
\mathbb{Z}
has exactly two idempotents:
0
and
1
.

Assume
p\perp q
for two predicates
p,q\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to R

on
R
.
Then, by definition,
p\perp q
if
there is a
b\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to R

with
b(n,m,m)=p(n,m)

and
b(m,n,m)=q(n,m)
for all
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}

In particular
p(1,0)q(1,0)=b(1,0,0)b(0,1,0)=0
,
thus the idempotents corresponding to
p
and
q
are orthogonal.
Conversely, if
p(1,0)
and
q(1,0)
are orthogonal,
then
b(k,l,m)=kp(1,0)+lq(1,0)+m(1-p(1,0)-q(1,0))

defines a unit-presrving ring homomorphism
that shows
p\perp q
with
(p\ovee q)(1,0)=b(1,1,0)=p(1,0)+q(1,0)
.
From this it is also clear that
p^{\perp}(1,0)=1-p(1,0)
.

There are many examples to show unit-preserving ring homomorphisms
need not be fixed on their value on idempotents
(and thus that
{\mathsf{Rng}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
does not have separting predicates.)
For instance, let
R
denote the unit ring of continuous real-valued
functions on the unit interval
[0,1]
.
This ring has only two idempotents: the functions that are constant
0

and
1
, which are also the zero and unit element (respectively)
of the ring.
For every
x\in[0,1]

the map
\delta_{x}\colon R\to\mathbb{R}
given by
\delta_{x}(f)=f(x)

is a unit-preserving ring homomorphism.
Clearly
\delta_{x}(0)=0=\delta_{y}(0)
and
\delta_{x}(1)=1=\delta_{y}(1)

for any
x,y\in[0,1]
with
x\neq y
, but not
\delta_{x}=\delta_{y}
.

Finally, we treat the second and last point of the exercise.
Reasoning towards contradiction,
let
f\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
be any unit-preserving ring homomorphism.
Then
0=f(0)=f(1+1)=f(1)+f(1)=1+1=2
, which is absurd.
Thus there is no such homomorphism.
However, there are two unit-preserving ring
homomorphisms
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}

corresponding to the idempotents
0
and
1
in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Hence the states cannot be separating (for otherwise
there could at most be a single unit-preserving
ring homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.)

Solution to
3.2.3
.

We will first show that
\mathcal{D}_{M}
is a functor.
To start, clearly

\mathcal{D}_{M}(\mathrm{id})(p)(x)\ \equiv\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y;\mathrm{id}(y)=x}p(y)\ =\ p(x)

and so
\mathcal{D}_{M}(\mathrm{id})=\mathrm{id}
.
Let
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
be given.
Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{M}(g\mathop{\circ}f)(p)(z)

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;\ g(f(x))=z}p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y;\ g(y)=z}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;\ f(x)=y}p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y;\ g(y)=z}\bigl((\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(p)\bigr)(y)

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl(\,(\mathcal{D}_{M}g)\bigl((\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(p)\bigr)\,\bigr)(z)

and so
\mathcal{D}_{M}(g\mathop{\circ}f)=(\mathcal{D}_{M}g)\mathop{\circ}(\mathcal{D}_{M}f)
.

Next, we show that
\eta
is a natural transformation.
Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any map.
Then for any
x_{0}\in X
, we have

\displaystyle((\mathcal{D}_{M}f)\mathop{\circ}\eta_{X})(x_{0})

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;\ f(x)=y}(\eta_{X}(x_{0}))(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;\ \begin{subarray}{c}f(x)=y\\
x=x_{0}\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\ =\ (\eta_{Y}(f(x_{0})))

and so
\mathcal{D}_{M}f\mathop{\circ}\eta_{X}=\eta_{Y}\mathop{\circ}f
,
i.e.
\eta
is a natural transformation
\mathrm{id}\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{M}
.

To show
\mu
is a natural transformation,
assume
f\colon X\to Y
is some map,
y\in Y
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.
Then

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(\mu_{X}(\Phi))(y)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;\ f(x)=y}\mu_{X}(\Phi)(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;\ f(x)=y}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}\Phi(p)\odot p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;\ f(x)=y}\Phi(p)\odot p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}\Phi(p)\odot\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x;\ f(x)=y}p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}\Phi(p)\odot(\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(p)(y)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{q}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p;\ (\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(p)=q}\Phi(p)\odot q(y)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{q}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p;\ (\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(p)=q}\Phi(p)\Bigr)\odot q(y)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{q}(\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(\Phi)(q)\odot q(y)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mu_{Y}((\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}f)(\Phi))(y)

and so
(\mathcal{D}_{M}f)\mathop{\circ}\mu_{X}=\mu_{Y}\mathop{\circ}(\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}f)
,
hence
\mu
is a natural transformation
\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{M}
.
We continue with the monad laws. Assume
X
is a set,
x\in X

and
\aleph\in\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.
Then

\displaystyle\mu_{X}(\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{M}X}(\aleph))(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}(\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{M}X})(\aleph)(p)\odot p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{\Phi}\aleph(\Phi)\odot\Phi(p)\odot p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{\Phi}\aleph(\Phi)\odot\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}\Phi(p)\odot p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{\Phi}\aleph(\Phi)\odot\mu_{X}(\Phi)(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{\Phi;\ \mu_{X}(\Phi)=p}\aleph(\Phi)\odot p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{p}(\mathcal{D}_{M}\mu_{X})(\aleph)(p)\odot p(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mu_{X}((\mathcal{D}_{M}\mu_{X})(\aleph))(x)

and so
\mu\mathop{\circ}\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{M}}=\mu\mathop{\circ}(\mathcal{D}_{M})
.
For any
x\in X
and
p\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
, we have

\mu_{X}(\eta_{\mathcal{D}_{M}X}(p))(x)\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{q}\eta_{\mathcal{D}_{M}X}(p)(q)\odot q(x)\ =\ p(x)

and so
\mu\mathop{\circ}\eta_{\mathcal{D}_{M}}=\mathrm{id}
.
For the final monad law, assume
x\in X
and
p\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.
Then

\displaystyle\mu_{X}((\mathcal{D}_{M}\eta_{X})(p))(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{q}(\mathcal{D}_{M}\eta_{X})(p)(q)\odot q(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{q}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y;\ \eta_{X}(y)=q}p(y)\odot q(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y}p(y)\odot\eta_{X}(y)(x)

\displaystyle\ =\ p(x)

and so
\mu\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\eta=\mathrm{id}
.
We have shown
(\mathcal{D}_{M},\eta,\mu)
is a monad.

Our next project is to show that
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}
is an effectus
in total form.
It is easy to see that the coproduct
X+Y
from
\mathsf{Set}

is also a coproduct in
\mathcal{D}_{M}

with coprojectors
\eta\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{i}
,
where
\kappa_{i}
are the coprojectors for
X+Y
in
\mathsf{Set}
.
(In fact, for any category
C
and monad
T
,
the inclusion functor
K\colon C\to\Kl T

given by
KX=X
and
Kf=\eta\mathop{\circ}f

is a left adjoint and so
K
preserves colimits.)
The empty set is also the initial object of
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}
.
As
\mathcal{D}_{M}1\cong 1
,
the category
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}

has as final object the one-element set
1
.

Now we will show that a square such as that on the
left of (
3.1
) is a pullback.
So assume
\alpha\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+1)

and
\beta\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}(1+Y)

are maps with
(\hat{!}+\hat{\mathrm{id}})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\alpha=(\hat{\mathrm{id}}+\hat{!})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\beta
,
where
\mathop{\hat{\circ}}
denotes the composition in
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}

and
\hat{f}\equiv\eta\mathop{\circ}f
the Kleisli embedding.
Assume
z\in Z
.
By assumption
\alpha(z)(\kappa_{2}*)=((\hat{!}+\hat{\mathrm{id}})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\alpha)(z)(\kappa_{2}*)=((\hat{\mathrm{id}}+\hat{!})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\beta)(z)(\kappa_{2}*)=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y}\beta(z)(\kappa_{2}y)
,
where
*
is the unique element of
1
.
