We want to define
\delta\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)

by
\delta(z)(\kappa_{1}x)=\alpha(z)(\kappa_{1}x)

and
\delta(z)(\kappa_{2}y)=\beta(z)(\kappa_{2}y)
,
but first need to check the image of
\delta(z)
sums to
1
.
Indeed
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{w}\delta(z)(w)=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y}\beta(z)(\kappa_{2}y)\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\alpha(z)(\kappa_{1}x)=\alpha(z)(\kappa_{2}*)\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\alpha(z)(\kappa_{1}x)=1
.
Clearly
\alpha=(\hat{\mathrm{id}}+\hat{!})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta

and
\beta=(\hat{!}+\hat{\mathrm{id}})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta
.
Suppose
\delta^{\prime}\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)

is some map with
\alpha=(\hat{\mathrm{id}}+\hat{!})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta^{\prime}

and
\beta=(\hat{!}+\hat{\mathrm{id}})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta^{\prime}
.
Then
\delta^{\prime}(\kappa_{1}x)=((\hat{\mathrm{id}}+\hat{!})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta^{\prime})(\kappa_{1}x)=\alpha(\kappa_{1}x)
for any
x\in X

and similarly
\delta^{\prime}(\kappa_{2}y)=\beta(\kappa_{2}y)
for any
y\in Y
, so
\delta^{\prime}=\delta
.
We have shown that the square on the left of (
3.1
)
is indeed a pullback in
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}
.

To show that the square on the right of (
3.1
)
is also a pullback, assume
\alpha\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}1

and
\beta\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}(X+Y)

are maps with
(\hat{!}+\hat{!})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\beta=\hat{\kappa}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\alpha
.
Assume
z\in Z
and
y\in Y
.
Then
\beta(z)(\kappa_{2}y)\leqslant((\hat{!}+\hat{!})\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\beta)(z)(\kappa_{2}*)=(\hat{\kappa}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\alpha)(z)(\kappa_{2}*)=0
.
This allows us to define
\delta\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}X

by
\delta(z)(x)=\beta(\kappa_{1}x)

as
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\delta(z)(x)=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\beta(\kappa_{1}x)=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\beta(\kappa_{1}x)\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{y}\beta(\kappa_{2}y)=1
.
Clearly
\hat{\kappa}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta=\beta

and
\hat{!}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta=\alpha
.
Suppose
\delta^{\prime}\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}X
is some map
with
\hat{\kappa}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta^{\prime}=\beta

and
\hat{!}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta^{\prime}=\alpha
.
Then
\delta^{\prime}(z)(x)=(\hat{\kappa}_{1}\mathop{\hat{\circ}}\delta^{\prime})(\kappa_{1}x)=\beta(z)(\kappa_{1}x)=\delta(z)(x)

and so
\delta=\delta^{\prime}
.
We have shown that the square on the right of (
3.1
)
is a pullback in
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}
.

It only remains to be shown that the
maps
[\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{2}],[\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\colon 1+1+1\to\mathcal{D}_{M}(1+1)

are jointly monic in
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}
.
To this end,
assume
f_{1},f_{2}\colon Z\to\mathcal{D}_{M}(1+1+1)

are given with
[\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f_{1}=[\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f_{2}

and
[\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f_{1}=[\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f_{2}
.
Assume
z\in Z
.
Clearly

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)(\kappa_{1}*)

\displaystyle\ =\ ([\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f_{1})(z)(\kappa_{1}*)

\displaystyle\ =\ ([\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f_{2})(z)(\kappa_{1}*)

\displaystyle\ =\ f_{2}(z)(\kappa_{1}*)

and

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)(\kappa_{2}*)

\displaystyle\ =\ ([\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f_{1})(z)(\kappa_{1}*)

\displaystyle\ =\ ([\hat{\kappa}_{2},\hat{\kappa}_{1},\hat{\kappa}_{2}]\mathop{\hat{\circ}}f_{2})(z)(\kappa_{1}*)

\displaystyle\ =\ f_{2}(z)(\kappa_{2}*).

So

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)(\kappa_{3}*)

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl(f_{1}(z)(\kappa_{1}*)\ovee f_{1}(z)(\kappa_{2}*)\bigr)^{\perp}

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl(f_{2}(z)(\kappa_{1}*)\ovee f_{2}(z)(\kappa_{2}*)\bigr)^{\perp}

\displaystyle\ =\ f_{2}(z)(\kappa_{3}*),

hence
f_{1}=f_{2}
.
We have shown that
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}
is an effectus.

A scalar in
\Kl\mathcal{D}_{M}

corresponds to a map
\lambda\colon 1\to\mathcal{D}_{M}(1+1)
,
which corresponds in turn to an element of
M
.
Unfolding definitions, it is straight-forward to see
that the effect monoid structure defined on the scalars
is the same as that of
M
.

Solution to
3.2.4
.

We start with the surjectivity of
q
,
\mathcal{D}_{M}q

and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}q
.
By definition
q
is clearly surjective.
Pick a section
r\colon X/_{\sim}\to X
of
q
;
i.e.
q\mathop{\circ}r=\mathrm{id}
.
Then
\mathcal{D}_{M}r

and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}r

are sections of
\mathcal{D}_{M}q
and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}q

respectively, hence
\mathcal{D}_{M}q
and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}q

are surjective.

To prove the second point,
assume
\sim
is a congruence.
Assume
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
with
(\mathcal{D}_{M}q)(\varphi)=(\mathcal{D}_{M}q)(\psi)
.
By definition
\varphi\sim\psi
and
h(\varphi)\sim h(\psi)

by assumption that
\sim
is a congruence.
Thus
q\mathop{\circ}h(\varphi)=q\mathop{\circ}h(\psi)
.
Thus together with the surjectivity of
\mathcal{D}_{M}q
,
there is a unique map
h_{\sim}\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}X/_{\sim}\to X/_{\sim}
fixed by
h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}(\mathcal{D}_{M}q)=q\mathop{\circ}h
.
To prove the converse, assume there is a
map
h_{\sim}\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}X/_{\sim}\to X/_{\sim}
with
h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}(\mathcal{D}_{M}q)=q\mathop{\circ}h

and assume
\varphi\sim\psi

for some
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.
Then
q(h(\varphi))=h_{\sim}(\mathcal{D}_{M}q(\varphi))=h_{\sim}(\mathcal{D}_{M}q(\psi))=q(h(\psi))
and so
h(\varphi)\sim h(\psi)
,
which shows that
\sim
is a congruence.

For the third point, assume
\sim
is a congruence.
Then

\displaystyle h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}h_{\sim}

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}(h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}q)

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}(q\mathop{\circ}h)

by the second point

\displaystyle\ =\ q\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}h

idem

\displaystyle\ =\ q\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\mu

as
(X,h)
is a.conv.

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}q\mathop{\circ}\mu

by the second point

\displaystyle\ =\ h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mu\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}q

by naturality
\mu
.

Hence by surjectivity of
\mathcal{D}_{M}\mathcal{D}_{M}q
,
we find
h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}h_{\sim}=h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mu
.
Using naturality of
\eta
,
the second point
and the fact that
(X,h)
is an abstract convex set
(in that order),
we find
h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\eta\mathop{\circ}q=h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}q\mathop{\circ}\eta=q\mathop{\circ}h\mathop{\circ}\eta=q
and so
h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\eta=\mathrm{id}
by surjectivity of
q
.
We have shown
(X/_{\sim},h_{\sim})
is an abstract
M
-convex set.
The equality
q\mathop{\circ}h=h_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}q

from the second point shows that
q
is
an
M
-affine map.

Solution to
3.2.4
.

To show
\sim
is a congruence,
assume
\varphi\sim\psi
for
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.
Write
q\colon X\to X/_{\sim}

for the quotient map for
\sim

and
f_{\sim}\colon X/_{\sim}\to X
for
the unique map with
f_{\sim}\mathop{\circ}q=f
.
Note that
\mathcal{D}_{M}q(\varphi)=\mathcal{D}_{M}q(\psi)
and so
f(h(\varphi))=h(\mathcal{D}_{M}f(\varphi))=h(\mathcal{D}_{M}f_{\sim}(\mathcal{D}_{M}q(\varphi)))=h(\mathcal{D}_{M}f_{\sim}(\mathcal{D}_{M}q(\psi)))=f(h(\psi))

and so
h(\varphi)\sim h(\psi)
, which shows
\sim

is a congruence.

Solution to
3.2.4
.

Concerning the first point:
clearly
h(\eta(h(\psi)))=h(\psi)

and so
(\,\eta(h(\psi)),\,\psi\,)

is a derivation of
\eta(h(\psi))\approx\psi
.
Assume
\varphi\approx\psi
.
Then
\eta(h(\varphi))\approx\varphi\approx\psi\approx\eta(h(\psi))

and so
h(\varphi)\sim h(\psi)
.

We continue with the second point.
Suppose we are given
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{n},\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X

and
\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in M

with
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}=1
.
To cover the first base case in the definition
of a derivation, assume
h(\varphi)=h(\psi)
.
Then

\displaystyle(h\mathop{\circ}\mu)\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\psi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ (h\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}h)\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\psi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ h\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{h(\psi)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{h(\chi_{j})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ h\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{h(\varphi)}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{h(\chi_{j})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ (h\mathop{\circ}\mu)\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\varphi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\Bigr)

and so

\mu\bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\psi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\bigr)\approx\mu\bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\varphi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\bigr)
.
Next, for the other base case in the definition of a derivation,
assume
\varphi\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}^{m}\mu_{i}\ket{x_{i}}

and
\psi\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i}^{m}\mu_{i}\ket{y_{i}}

with
x_{i}\mathrel{R^{*}}y_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
.
Then

\displaystyle\mu\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\psi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{0}\odot\mu_{i}\ket{y_{i}}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\lambda_{j}\odot\chi_{j}(x)\ket{x}

\displaystyle\mu\Bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\varphi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{0}\odot\mu_{i}\ket{x_{i}}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x}\lambda_{j}\odot\chi_{j}(x)\ket{x}.

This shows that
\mu\bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\psi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\bigr)\approx\mu\bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\varphi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\bigr)
by the second base case of a derivation.
Thus by induction
\mu\bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\psi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\bigr)\approx\mu\bigl(\lambda_{0}\ket{\varphi}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\ket{\chi_{j}}\bigr)
whenever merely
\varphi\approx\psi
.

For the third point, we need some preparation.
Pick representatives
R\subseteq X
of
\sim
:
for every
x\in X
there is a unique
r_{x}\in R
with
r_{x}\sim x
.
By definition
\eta(r_{x})\approx\eta(x)
.
Let
\varphi\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{x_{i}}\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
given.
By the previous point

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\ =\ \mu\Bigl(\lambda_{1}\ket{\eta(x_{1})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=2}\lambda_{i}\ket{\eta(x_{i})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ \approx\ \mu\Bigl(\lambda_{1}\ket{\eta(r_{x_{1}})}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=2}\lambda_{i}\ket{\eta(x_{i})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ =\ \mu\Bigl(\lambda_{2}\ket{\eta(x_{2})}\ovee\lambda_{1}\ket{r_{x_{1}}}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=3}\lambda_{i}\ket{\eta(x_{i})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ \approx\ \mu\Bigl(\lambda_{2}\ket{\eta(r_{x_{2}})}\ovee\lambda_{1}\ket{r_{x_{1}}}\ovee\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=3}\lambda_{i}\ket{\eta(x_{i})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle\ \approx\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\ket{r_{x_{i}}}

\displaystyle\ \approx\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{r\in R}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x\sim r}\varphi(x)\Bigr)\ket{r}.

Now, assume
\varphi\sim\psi
.
Then
\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x\sim x_{0}}\varphi(x)=\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x\sim x_{0}}\psi(x)
for any
x_{0}\in X

and so

\varphi\ \approx\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{r\in R}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x\sim r}\varphi(x)\Bigr)\ket{r}\ =\ \mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{r\in R}\Bigl(\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits_{x\sim r}\psi(x)\Bigr)\ket{r}\ \approx\ \psi.

Clearly
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
X
.
To show it is a congruence, assume
\varphi\sim\psi
.
By the previous, we know
\varphi\approx\psi

and so
h(\varphi)\sim h(\psi)
by the first point,
which shows
\sim
is indeed a congruence.
Furthermore
R
is contained in
\sim
;
indeed, if
x\mathrel{R}y
,
then
(\eta(x),\eta(y))
is a derivation of
\eta(x)\approx\eta(y)

and so
x\sim y
.

To show
\sim
is the smallest congruence containing
R
,
assume
S\subseteq X^{2}
is some congruence of
X

with
R\subseteq S
.
It is sufficient to show that
\varphi\approx\psi

implies
h(\varphi)\mathrel{S}h(\psi)
.
Indeed if this is the case and
x\sim y
,
then by definition
\eta(x)\approx\eta(y)

and so
x=h(\eta(x))\mathrel{S}h(\eta(y))=y
.

We will prove that
\varphi\approx\psi

implies
h(\varphi)\mathrel{S}h(\psi)

by induction over the definition of
\approx
.
For the first base case in the definition of
\approx
,
assume
\varphi\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{x_{i}}

and
\psi\equiv\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\ovee$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\ovee$}}}{\ovee}{\ovee}}\displaylimits^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\ket{y_{i}}

for some
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n},y_{n})\in R^{*}
.
Then
x_{i}\mathrel{S}y_{i}

and so
\varphi\mathrel{S}\psi
,
hence
h(\varphi)\mathrel{S}h(\psi)
.
For the other base case, suppose
h(\varphi)=h(\psi)

for some
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}_{M}X
.
Then clearly
h(\varphi)\mathrel{S}h(\psi)
.
Thus, by induction
h(\varphi)\mathrel{S}h(\psi)

whenever
\varphi\approx\psi
.

Solution to
3.2.4
.

As the one element set is final in
\mathsf{Set}
,
there is a unique map
!\colon\mathcal{D}_{M}1\to 1
.
This turns
\mathcal{D}_{M}1
into an abstract
M
-convex set:

{!}\mathop{\circ}\mathcal{D}_{M}{!}={!}={!}\mathop{\circ}\mu

and
{!}\mathop{\circ}\eta={!}=\mathrm{id}
.
Note that there is a single formal
M
-convex combination over
1

and so
\mathcal{D}_{M}1\cong 1
.
As
\mathsf{AConv}_{M}
is the Eilenberg–Moore category
of the monad
\mathcal{D}_{M}
,
it follows that the functor
F\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{AConv}_{M}

given by
F(X)=(\mathcal{D}_{M}X,\mu)

and
Ff=\mathcal{D}_{M}f
is a left adjoint
and so preserves coproducts.
Hence
\mathcal{D}_{M}\{1,\ldots,n\}\cong F(n\cdot 1)\cong n\cdot F(1)\cong n\cdot 1
.

Solution to
3.2.4
.

Clearly
0\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}$}

and
0\odot 0=0
.
Thus, by uniqueness of

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}

,
we have
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}{{\reflectbox{$0$}}}$}=0
.
For any
a\in M
,
we have
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$1$}}}$}=1\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$1$}}}$}=a

and so in particular
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$1$}}}{{\reflectbox{$1$}}}$}=1
.
Next, note
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}

and
a\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}=a\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}=a

and so by uniqueness of

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}

,
we have
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}
.
For the final equation of the first point, assume
b\in M
.
As
a\odot b\leqslant a
we know

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a\odot b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}

is defined.
Note
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\odot b\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}

and
a\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\odot b=a\odot b
.
So by uniqueness of

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a\odot b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}

we see
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a\odot b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}$}\odot b
.

For the second point, suppose
a,b,c\in M
with
a\leqslant b\leqslant c
.
Note
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}

and
c\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}=b\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}=a
.
So by uniqueness of

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}

,
we get
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$b$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$a$}}}{{\reflectbox{$c$}}}$}
, as desired.

Solution to
3.2.4
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space.
Assume that the unit interval of
C(X)

is an effect divisoid.
To show
X
is basically disconnected,
assume
f\in C(X)
.
We have to show that
\overline{\supp f}
is open.
Note
\supp|f|=\supp f
and so, without loss of generality,
we may assume that
f\geqslant 0
.
By compactness
f
is bounded. Pick
B>0
with
B\geqslant f
.
Then
\supp B^{-1}f=\supp f
and
B^{-1}f\leqslant 1
,
so we may also assume without loss of generality, that
0\leqslant f\leqslant 1
.
If
\overline{\supp f}=X
, then we are done,
so assume
\overline{\supp f}\neq X
.
Pick any
y\notin\overline{\supp f}
.
By Urysohn’s lemma, there is a
g\in C(X)

with
g(y)=0
and
g(x)=1
for every
x\in\overline{\supp f}
.
Define
h\equiv\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\wedge(0\vee g)
.
Clearly
0\leqslant h\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\leqslant 1

and
h(y)\leqslant(0\vee g)(y)=0
.
Note that
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\equiv\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}

and so

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}

is zero–one valued: a characteristic function.
Pick any
x\in\supp f
.
Then
0<f(x)\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}(x)\in\{0,1\}
,
hence
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}(x)=1
.
By continuity
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}(x)=1

for every
x\in\overline{\supp f}

and so
h(x)=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}(x)\wedge g(x)=1\wedge 1=1
.
Hence
(f\odot h)(x)=f(x)h(x)=f(x)
for
x\in\overline{\supp f}

and
(f\odot h)(x)=f(x)h(x)=0=f(x)
whenever
x\notin\overline{\supp f}
.
Thus
f\odot h=f
.
By uniqueness of

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}

, we have
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}=h
.
Consequently
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}(y)=h(y)=0
.
Recall that
y
was an arbitrary element
y\notin\overline{\supp f}

and thus

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}

is the characteristic function of
\overline{\supp f}
,
which is thus open. Hence
X
is basically disconnected.

To prove the converse, assume
X
is basically disconnected.
Let
f,g\in C(X)
be given with
0\leqslant f\leqslant g\leqslant 1
.
Define
U_{n}\equiv\{x;\ g(x)>\frac{1}{n}\}
.
Note
U_{n}=\supp((g-\frac{1}{n})\vee 0)

and so
\overline{U_{n}}
is open as
X
is basically disconnected.
Define

h_{n}\ \equiv\ \begin{cases}\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}&x\in\overline{U_{n}}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

To show
h_{n}
is continuous, assume
there is a net
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
with
x_{\alpha}\to x

for some
x\in X
.
Suppose
x\in\overline{U_{n}}
.
As
\overline{U_{n}}
is open,
we know
x_{\alpha}\in U_{n}

for sufficiently large
\alpha

and so
h_{n}(x_{\alpha})\to h_{n}(x)

as
\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}

is continuous for
x\in\overline{U_{n}}

as then
g(x)\geqslant\frac{1}{n}>0
.
For the other case, suppose
x\notin\overline{U_{n}}
.
The set
X-\overline{U_{n}}
is open
and so
x_{\alpha}\notin\overline{U_{n}}
for sufficiently large
\alpha

and then
h_{n}(x_{\alpha})=0=h_{n}(x)
.
Thus
h_{n}
is continuous.

Clearly
0\leqslant h_{1}\leqslant h_{2}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant 1

and so we may define
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}\equiv\sup_{n}h_{n}

as
C(X)
is
\omega
-complete by basic disconnectedness of
X
.
Note
0\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}\leqslant 1
.

Suppose
f=g
and
x\in\supp f
.
Then
h_{n}(x)=1
for all
n>f(x)^{-1}

and so
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}(x)=1
.
Thus
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}(x)=1
for all
x\in\overline{\supp f}
.
Write
\chi
for the characteristic function of
\overline{\supp f}
.
We just saw
\chi\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}(x)
.
As
\overline{U_{n}}\subseteq\overline{\supp f}
and
h_{n}\leqslant 1
,
we have
h_{n}\leqslant\chi
for all
n
and
so
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\leqslant\chi
. Thus
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}=\chi
,
the characteristic function of
\overline{\supp f}
.
In particular
f\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}

and
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\equiv\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}$}
.

Let
f,g\in C(X)
be given with
0\leqslant f\leqslant g\leqslant 1

and
h_{1}\leqslant h_{2}\leqslant\ldots
as above.
We will show
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}
.
Assume
x\in X
.
Suppose
x\in\supp g
.
Then
h_{n}(x)=\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\leqslant 1=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)

for all
n>g(x)^{-1}

and so
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)
.
In particular
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)

for all
x\in\overline{\supp g}
.
For the other case, assume
x\notin\overline{\supp g}
.
Then
x\notin\overline{U_{n}}
and
so
h_{n}(x)=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
whence
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)=0\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)
.
Thus indeed
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}
.

In a similar way, it is easy to see
that
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)\leqslant\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}
for
x\in\supp g
.
To show equality, we define

k_{n}\ \equiv\ \begin{cases}\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}&x\in\overline{U_{n}}\\
1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

With similar reasoning as for
h_{n}
, we see that these
k_{n}
are continuous.
Furthermore
h_{m}\leqslant k_{n}
for all
m

and so
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}\leqslant k_{n}
for any
n
.
Pick any
x\in\supp g
.
Then
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)\leqslant k_{n}(x)=\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}

for
n>g(x)^{-1}

and so
\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)=\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}

for any
x\in\supp g
.
Thus
(g\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$})(x)=f(x)
for
x\in\supp g

and so
(g\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$})(x)=f(x)
for
x\in\overline{\supp g}

by continuity.
For the other case, assume
x\notin\overline{\supp g}
.
Then
g\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)=0=g(0)\geqslant f(0)
.
We have shown
g\odot\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}=f
.

Only uniquness of

\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}

remains.
So assume
h\in C(X)

with
0\leqslant h\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}

and
g\odot h=f
.
Assume
x\in\supp g
.
Then
g(x)h(x)=f(x)
and so
h(x)=\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)
.
By continuity
h(x)=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)
for all
x\in\overline{\supp g}
.
For the other case, assume
x\notin\overline{\supp g}
.
As both
h,\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}\leqslant\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}
,
we have
h(x)=0=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}(x)
.
We have shown
h=\reflectbox{$\nicefrac{{\reflectbox{$f$}}}{{\reflectbox{$g$}}}$}

and thus the unit interval of
C(X)

is an effect divisoid.

Solution to
3.3
.

Assume
C
is an effectus.

1.

Assume
\xi\colon X\to Y
is a quotient for
p

and
\vartheta\colon Y\to Z
is an isomorphism.
Note
1\mathop{\circ}\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi=1\mathop{\circ}\xi\leqslant p^{\perp}
.
To prove
\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi
is a quotient for
p
,
assume
f\colon X\to Y^{\prime}
is some map
with
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant p^{\perp}
.
As
\xi
is a quotient, there exists a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}

with
f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi=f
.
Clearly
f=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi=(f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta^{-1})\mathop{\circ}(\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi)
.
Assume
h\colon Z\to Y^{\prime}
is any map with
f=h\mathop{\circ}(\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi)
.
Then
h\mathop{\circ}\vartheta=f^{\prime}
by uniqueness of
f^{\prime}

and so
h=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta^{-1}
.
We have shown that
\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi
is a quotient of
p
as well.

2.

Assume
\xi_{1}\colon X\to Y_{1}

and
\xi_{2}\colon X\to Y_{2}

are quotients for
p
.
By the universal property of
\xi_{1}
,
there is a unique map
\vartheta_{1}\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{2}

with
\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
.
Similarly, there is a unique map
\vartheta_{2}\colon Y_{2}\to Y_{1}

with
\vartheta_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{2}=\xi_{1}
.
Note that
\xi_{1}=\vartheta_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{2}=\vartheta_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}
.
By the universal property of
\xi_{1}
again,
the map
\mathrm{id}\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{1}

is the unique map with
\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{1}=\xi_{1}

and so
\vartheta_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}=\mathrm{id}
.
Similarly
\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}=\mathrm{id}
.
Thus
\vartheta_{2}
is an isomorphism
with
\vartheta_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{2}=\xi_{1}
.
It is the unique such isomorphism by the universal property of
\xi_{2}
.

3.

Assume
f\colon X\to Y
is some map.
Clearly
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant 1=0^{\perp}
.
Furthermore
f
is itself the unique map
f^{\prime}
with
f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=f
.
Thus
\mathrm{id}
is a quotient for
0
.
Consequently any isomorphism is a quotient for
0
by the first point.

4.

Assume
X
is any object.
As
0
is a zero object,
there is a unique map
0_{X}\colon X\to 0
.
Clearly
1\mathop{\circ}0=0=1^{\perp}
.
Assume
f\colon X\to Y
is any map with
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant 1^{\perp}=0
.
Then
f=0
and so
f=0=0\mathop{\circ}0_{X}
.
If
f=h\mathop{\circ}0_{X}
for some
h\colon 0\to Y
,
then
h=0
and so any map into zero is indeed a quotient for
1
.

5.

Assume
\xi\colon X\to Y
is a quotient for
p
.
Clearly
1\mathop{\circ}p^{\perp}=p^{\perp}\leqslant p^{\perp}

and so there is a unique map
f\colon Y\colon 1

with
f\mathop{\circ}\xi=p^{\perp}
.
Then
p^{\perp}=1\mathop{\circ}p^{\perp}=1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\xi\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}\xi=p^{\perp}

and so
1\mathop{\circ}\xi=p^{\perp}
.

6.

Assume
f_{1}\mathop{\circ}\xi=f_{2}\mathop{\circ}\xi

for some quotient
\xi
of
p
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}f_{1}\mathop{\circ}\xi\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}\xi=p^{\perp}
.
Hence there is a unique
f
with
f\mathop{\circ}\xi=f_{1}\mathop{\circ}\xi
.
Both
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
fit the bill, hence
f_{1}=f=f_{2}
.
Thus
\xi
is epic.

Solution to
3.3
.

Let
C
be an effectus with quotients.
Assume
t^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi^{\prime}=t\mathop{\circ}\xi

for some quotient
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
and total maps
t,t^{\prime}
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}\xi=1\mathop{\circ}t\mathop{\circ}\xi=1\mathop{\circ}t^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi^{\prime}=1\mathop{\circ}\xi^{\prime}
.
Thus by
3.3
there is a unique isomorphism
\vartheta

with
\xi^{\prime}=\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi
.
Note
t\mathop{\circ}\xi=t^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi^{\prime}=t^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta\mathop{\circ}\xi

and so by epicity of
\xi

(see
3.3
),
we see
t=t^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta
, as desired.

Solution to
3.3
.

Assume
C
is an effectus with quotients.
We will show that
0
has a left adjoint by demonstrating the universal mapping property.
Pick for every object
(X,p)
in
\int\Pred_{\square}

a quotient
\xi_{p}\colon X\to X/_{p}
of
p
.
Note
(0^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p})^{\perp}=p

and so
\xi_{p}
is a map
(X,p)\to(X/_{p},0)
in
\int\Pred_{\square}
.
We will use these maps
\xi_{p}
as the components of the unit of the adjunction.
Let
f\colon(X,p)\to(Y,0)
be some map in
\int\Pred_{\square}
.
By definition of map
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant p^{\perp}

and so by the universal property of
\xi_{p}
,
there exists a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon X/_{p}\to Y

with
f=f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p}
in
C
.
Clearly also
(0f)\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p}=f
in
\int\Pred_{\square}
.
Let
g\colon X/_{p}\to Y
be some map with
(0g)\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p}=f

as well.
Then
g\mathop{\circ}\xi_{p}=f
and so
g^{\prime}=f^{\prime}
.
This shows that
0
has a left adjoint.

To prove the converse, assume
C
is an effectus
where
0\colon C\to\int\Pred_{\square}
has a left adjoint
Q\colon\int\Pred_{\square}\to C
.
Let
X
be some object in
C
with a predicate
p
.
Write
\eta\colon(X,p)\to 0Q(X,p)

for the
(X,p)
component of the unit of the adjunction
Q\dashv 0
.
By definition of maps in
\int\Pred_{\square}
,
we know
1\mathop{\circ}\eta\leqslant p^{\perp}
.
We will show that
\eta
is a quotientfor
p
.
To this end, assume
f\colon X\to Y
is some map in
C

with
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant p^{\perp}
.
Then
f\colon(X,p)\to(Y,0)
in
\int\Pred_{\square}
.
By the universal mapping property,
there is a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon Q(X,p)\to Y
in
C

with
(0f^{\prime})\mathop{\circ}\eta=f
, i.e.
f^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}\eta=f
.
This shows that
\eta
is indeed a quotient of
p
.
Hence
C
has quotients.

Solution to
3.4
.

Assume
C
is an effectus with comprehension.
We will show that
1
has a right adjoint by demonstrating
the dual of the universal mapping property.
Pick for every object
(X,p)
in
\int\Pred_{\square}

a comprehension
\pi_{p}\colon\{X|{\kern 0.86108pt}p\}\to X
.
Note that for any map
f
,
we have
1\mathop{\circ}f=p\mathop{\circ}f
if and only if
p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
Thus
(p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{p})^{\perp}=1
, which shows
that
\pi_{p}
is a map
(\{X|{\kern 0.86108pt}p\},1)\to(X,p)
in
\int\Pred_{\square}
.
We will use these maps as components for the counit of the adjunction.
Let
f\colon(Y,1)\to(X,p)
be some map in
C
.
By definition of maps in
\int\Pred_{\square}
,
we have
1\leqslant(p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}
,
viz.
1\mathop{\circ}f=p\mathop{\circ}f
.
Thus, by the universal property of
\pi_{p}
,
there is a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon Y\to\{X|{\kern 0.86108pt}p\}
in
C

with
\pi_{p}\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}=f
.
Hence
1f^{\prime}
is the unique map in
\int\Pred_{\square}

with
\pi_{p}\mathop{\circ}(1f^{\prime})=f
in
\int\Pred_{\square}
.
We have shown that
1
has a right adjoint.

To prove the converse, assume
C
is an effectus
where
1\colon C\to\int\Pred_{\square}

has a right adjoint
K\colon\int\Pred_{\square}\to C
.
Let
X
be some object of
C
with a predicate
p
.
Write
\varepsilon\colon 1K(X,p)\to(X,p)
for the
(X,p)
component of
the counit of the adjunction
1\dashv K
.
By definition of maps in
\int\Pred_{\square}
,
we know
1\leqslant(p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\varepsilon)^{\perp}
,
hence
1\mathop{\circ}\varepsilon=1\mathop{\circ}\varepsilon
.
We will show that
\varepsilon
is a comprehension for
p
.
To this end, assume
f\colon Y\to X
is some map in
C

with
1\mathop{\circ}f=p\mathop{\circ}f
.
Then
1=(p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}

and so
f
is a map
(Y,1)\to(X,p)

in
\int\Pred_{\square}
.
By the dual of the universal mapping property,
there is a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon Y\to K(X,p)
in
\int\Pred_{\square}

with
\varepsilon\mathop{\circ}(1f^{\prime})=f
,
i.e.
\varepsilon\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}=f
.
This shows that
\varepsilon
is indeed a comprehension for
p
.
Hence
C
has comprehension.

Solution to
3.4
.

Let
C
be an effectus.

1.

Assume
\pi\colon X\to Y
is a comprehension for
p

and
\vartheta\colon Z\to X
is an isomorphism.
We will show
\pi\mathop{\circ}\vartheta
is a comprehension for
p

as well.
To start, note
1\mathop{\circ}\pi\mathop{\circ}\vartheta=p\mathop{\circ}\pi\mathop{\circ}\vartheta
.
Assume
f\colon X^{\prime}\to Y
is any map with
1\mathop{\circ}f=p\mathop{\circ}f
.
Then, by the universal property of
\pi
, there is a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to X

with
\pi\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}=f
.
Then
f=(\pi\mathop{\circ}\vartheta)\mathop{\circ}(\vartheta^{-1}\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime})
.
Assume
g\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is a map with
g=(\pi\mathop{\circ}\vartheta)\mathop{\circ}g

as well.
Then
\vartheta\mathop{\circ}g=f^{\prime}
by uniqueness of
f^{\prime}
and so
g=\vartheta^{-1}\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}
.
We have shown that
\pi\mathop{\circ}\vartheta
is a comprehension for
p
as well.

2.

Assume
\pi_{1}\colon X_{1}\to Y
and
\pi_{2}\colon X_{2}\to Y

are comprehensions of
p
.
By the universal property of
\pi_{1}
, there is a unique map
\vartheta_{1}\colon X_{2}\to X_{1}

with
\pi_{2}=\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}
.
On the other side, by the universal property of
\pi_{2}
,
there is a unique map
\vartheta_{2}\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}

with
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}
.
Then
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}=\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}
.
By the universal property of
\pi_{1}
again, the map
\mathrm{id}\colon X_{1}\to X_{1}

is the unique map with
\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=\pi_{1}

and so
\vartheta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}=\mathrm{id}
.
Similarly
\vartheta_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{1}=\mathrm{id}
.
Thus
\vartheta_{2}
is an isomorphism with
\vartheta_{1}=\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\vartheta_{2}
.
It is the unique such isomorphism by the universal property of
\pi_{2}
.

3.

Assume
f\colon X\to Y
is some map. Trivially
1\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}f
and
1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}
.
The map
f
itself is the unique map
g
with
g\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}=f

and so
\mathrm{id}
is a comprehension for
1
.
Consequently any isomorphism is a comprehension for
1
by the first point.

4.

Assume
Y
is any object.
As
0
is a zero object, there is a unique map
0_{Y}\colon 0\to Y
.
Clearly
1\mathop{\circ}0_{Y}=0=0\mathop{\circ}0_{Y}
.
Assume
f\colon X\to Y
is any map with
1\mathop{\circ}f=0\mathop{\circ}f
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}f=0\mathop{\circ}f=0
and so
f=0
.
The zero map is the unique map
X\to 0

and for that map, we have
0_{Y}\mathop{\circ}0=0=f

and so
0_{Y}
is a comprehension for
0
.

5.

Assume
\pi\mathop{\circ}f_{1}=\pi\mathop{\circ}f_{2}
for some comprehension
\pi

of
p
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}\pi\mathop{\circ}f_{1}=p\mathop{\circ}\pi\mathop{\circ}f_{1}

and so by the universal property of
\pi
,
there is a unique map
f
with
\pi\mathop{\circ}f=\pi\mathop{\circ}f_{1}
.
Both
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
fit the bill, hence
f_{1}=f=f_{2}
.
Thus
\pi
is monic.

6.

Assume
\pi
is a comprehension of
p
.
Then
(p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi)\ovee(p\mathop{\circ}\pi)=(p^{\perp}\ovee p)\mathop{\circ}\pi=1\mathop{\circ}\pi=p\mathop{\circ}\pi
.
Thus
p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi=0
by cancellation.

Solution to
3.4
.

Let
C
be any effectus.
Assume
\pi\colon X\to Y
is a comprehension of
p
.
We will show
\pi
is a categorical kernel
p^{\perp}
.
To start, note
p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi=0
by
3.4
.
Now assume
f\colon X^{\prime}\to Y
is some map with
p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
Then
p\mathop{\circ}f=(p\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}f)=1\mathop{\circ}f

and so by the universal property of
\pi
,
there is a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
with
f=\pi\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}
.
Hence
\pi
is a kernel of
p^{\perp}
.

To prove the converse, assume
k\colon X\to Y
is a kernel
of
p^{\perp}\colon Y\to 1
.
We have
1\mathop{\circ}k=(p\mathop{\circ}p^{\perp})\mathop{\circ}k=(p\mathop{\circ}k)\ovee(p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}k)=p\mathop{\circ}k
.
Assume
f\colon X^{\prime}\to Y
is some map with
p\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}f
.
Then
(p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(p\mathop{\circ}f)=1\mathop{\circ}f=p\mathop{\circ}f

and so by cancellation
p^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}f=0
.
Hence by the universal property of
k

there is a unique map
f^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to X

with
k\mathop{\circ}f^{\prime}=f
.
This shows that
k
is a comprehension of
p
.

Solution to
3.4
.

We have
(\IM f)\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}g=1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}g

and so
\IM f\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant\IM f
.
Consequently
\IM f\mathop{\circ}\alpha\leqslant\IM f=\IM f\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\leqslant\IM f\mathop{\circ}\alpha
and so
\IM f\mathop{\circ}\alpha=\IM f
.

Solution to
3.4
.

Assume
\xi\colon X\to Y
is some quotient and
p
is a predicate on
Y

with
p\mathop{\circ}\xi=0
.
Then
p\mathop{\circ}\xi=0=0\mathop{\circ}\xi

and so
p=0
by epicity of
\xi
.
Hence
\xi
is faithful.

Solution to
3.4.1
.

Assume
\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor=p
.
Then
p=\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor\equiv\IM\pi
for some comprehension
\pi
of
p

and so
p
is sharp.
To prove the converse, assume
p
is sharp;
that is:
p=\IM f
for some
f
.
Pick any comprehension
\pi
of
p
.
Clearly
p\mathop{\circ}f=(\IM f)\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}f

and so there is a unique
g
with
f=\pi\mathop{\circ}g
.
By
3.4

we find
p=\IM f=\IM\pi\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant\IM\pi\equiv\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor\leqslant p

and so
p=\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor
.

Solution to
3.4.1
.

Note
\left\lceil p\right\rceil\leqslant\left\lceil q\right\rceil
whenever
p\leqslant q

and so
\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil\leqslant\left\lceil\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil\leqslant\left\lceil\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil\right\rceil=\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil

as
p\leqslant\left\lceil p\right\rceil

and
\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil
by
3.4.1
.
Thus
\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil=\left\lceil\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil
.

Solution to
3.4.1
.

To start, note
[\IM f,\IM g]\langle f,g\rangle=((\IM f)\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee((\IM g)\mathop{\circ}g)=(1\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(1\mathop{\circ}g)=1\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle

by
3.2.1
.
Assume
p\equiv[p_{1},p_{2}]
is some predicate with
p\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=1\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle
.
Then
(1\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(1\mathop{\circ}g)=1\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=p\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=(p_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(p_{2}\mathop{\circ}g)\leqslant(p_{1}\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(1\mathop{\circ}g)

and so by cancellation
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant p_{1}\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}f
,
hence
1\mathop{\circ}f=p_{1}\mathop{\circ}f
.
Thus
\IM f\leqslant p_{1}
.
Similarly
\IM g\leqslant p_{2}
.
Thus
[\IM f,\IM g]\leqslant[p_{1},p_{2}]=p
.
We have shown
\IM\langle f,g\rangle=[\IM f,\IM g]
.

Assume
[p,q]
is sharp.
That is, there is some
f
with
[p,q]=\IM f
.
Then
[p,q]=\IM f=\IM\langle\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f,\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}f\rangle=[\IM\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f,\IM\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}f]

and so
p=\IM\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}f

and
q=\IM\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\mathop{\circ}f
,
which shows
p
and
q
are sharp.

To prove the converse, assume
p
and
q
are sharp.
Pick
f
and
g
with
p=\IM f
and
g=\IM g
.
Note
f+g=\langle f\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1},g\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\rangle
.
We have
\IM f=\IM f\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\kappa_{1}\leqslant\IM f\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}\leqslant\IM f

and so
\IM f\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1}=\IM f
.
Similarly
\IM g\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}=\IM g
.
Thus
\IM f+g=\IM\langle f\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1},g\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}\rangle=[\IM f\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{1},\IM g\mathop{\circ}\mathord{\vartriangleright}_{2}]=[\IM f,\IM g]=[p,q]
, which shows
[p,q]
is sharp.

Solution to
3.4.1
.

Let
C
be an effectus with comprehension and images.
Assume
f\colon X\to Y
is a quotient of sharp
s
.
Let
\pi_{s}
denote a comprehension of
s
.
Clearly
1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=s^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=0
.
Assume
g\colon X\to Y^{\prime}
is some map with
g\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=0
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}g\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=0
and so
1\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant\IMperp\pi_{s}=s^{\perp}
.
Thus by the universal property of
f
as a quotient,
there is a unique map
g^{\prime}\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}

with
g=g^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}f
.
This shows
f
is a cokernel of
\pi_{s}
.

To prove the converse, assume
f
is a cokernel of
\pi_{s}
.
By assumption
f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=0
.
Thus
1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=0
.
Hence
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant\IMperp\pi_{s}=s^{\perp}
.
Let
g\colon X\to Y^{\prime}
be a map with
1\mathop{\circ}g\leqslant s^{\perp}
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}g\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\leqslant s^{\perp}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=0

and so
g\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}=0
.
Thus by the universal property of
f
as a cokernel,
there is a unique map
g^{\prime}\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}

with
g=g^{\prime}\mathop{\circ}f
.
This shows
f
is a quotient of
s
.

Solution to
3.5
.

Assume
s\leqslant t
.
Then
s\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant t\mathop{\circ}f
,
hence
(t\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\leqslant(s\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}
so
\left\lfloor(t\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\right\rfloor\leqslant\left\lfloor(s\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\right\rfloor
.
Thus
f^{\diamond}(s)\equiv\left\lceil s\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil\equiv\left\lfloor(s\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\right\rfloor^{\perp}\leqslant\left\lfloor(t\mathop{\circ}f)^{\perp}\right\rfloor^{\perp}\equiv f^{\diamond}(t)
.
This shows
f^{\diamond}
is order preserving.
Furthermore
t^{\perp}\leqslant s^{\perp}
,
so
f^{\diamond}(t^{\perp})\leqslant f^{\diamond}(s^{\perp})
,
hence
f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(s)\equiv f^{\diamond}(s^{\perp})^{\perp}\leqslant f^{\diamond}(t^{\perp})^{\perp}\equiv f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t)
,
which shows that
f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
is order preserving.

Solution to
3.5
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be some map in a
\diamond
-effectus.

1.

Assume
s\leqslant t
.
Clearly
f_{\diamond}(t)\leqslant f_{\diamond}(t)

so
s\leqslant t\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(f_{\diamond}(t))
,
hence
f_{\diamond}(s)\leqslant f_{\diamond}(t)
,
which shows that
f_{\diamond}
is order preserving.

2.

Assume
D\subseteq\SPred X
is a set of sharp predicates with a supremum
\sup D
.
We will show
f_{\diamond}(\sup D)
is the supremum of
f_{\diamond}(D)
.
For any
d\in D
, we have
d\leqslant\sup D

and so
f_{\diamond}(d)\leqslant f_{\diamond}(\sup D)
by the previous point.
Assume
x
is some sharp predicate of
Y

with
f_{\diamond}(d)\leqslant x
for all
d\in D
as well.
Then
d\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(x)
for all
d\in D

and so
\sup D\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(x)
.
Hence
f_{\diamond}(\sup D)\leqslant x
, which shows
that
\sup f_{\diamond}(D)=f_{\diamond}(\sup D)
.

3.

Assume
D\subseteq\SPred Y
is a set of sharp predicates with an infimum
\inf D
.
We will show
f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(\inf D)
is the infimum of

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(D)
.

, we have

and so

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(\inf D)\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(d)

by
3.5
.
Assume

is some sharp predicate of
X

with
x\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(d)
for all

as well.
Then
f_{\diamond}(x)\leqslant d
for all

and so

,
hence
x\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(\inf D)
.
This shows that
\inf f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(D)=f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(\inf D)
.

4.

For for any
D\subseteq\SPred Y
with supremum,
we have

\displaystyle f^{\diamond}(\sup D)

\displaystyle\ \equiv\ f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}((\sup D)^{\perp})^{\perp}

\displaystyle\ =\ f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(\inf_{d\in D}(d^{\perp}))^{\perp}

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigl(\inf_{d\in D}f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(d^{\perp})\bigr)^{\perp}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sup_{d\in D}f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(d^{\perp})^{\perp}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sup_{d\in D}f^{\diamond}(d)

and so
f^{\diamond}
preserves suprema.

5.

For any
s\in\SPred X

we have
f_{\diamond}(s)\leqslant f_{\diamond}(s)

and so
s\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(f_{\diamond}(s))
.
Similarly, for any
t\in\SPred Y

we have
f_{\diamond}(f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t))\leqslant t
.
Hence
f_{\diamond}(f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(f_{\diamond}(s)))\leqslant f_{\diamond}(s)\leqslant f_{\diamond}(f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(f_{\diamond}(s)))
,
so
f_{\diamond}=f_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}\mathop{\circ}f_{\diamond}
.

6.

We have
f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(f_{\diamond}(f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t)))\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(t)\leqslant f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}(f_{\diamond}(f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

and so
f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}=f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}\mathop{\circ}f_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}f^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 2.5 L 2.5 2.5 L 2.5 0 Z M 2.5 2.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
.

Solution to
3.5
.

Let
f,g\colon X\to Y
be some maps in a
\diamond
-effectus.
Assume
f^{\diamond}=g^{\diamond}
and
t\in\SPred X
.
Trivially
g_{\diamond}(t)\leqslant(g_{\diamond}(t)^{\perp})^{\perp}

and so
f^{\diamond}(g_{\diamond}(t)^{\perp})=g^{\diamond}(g_{\diamond}(t)^{\perp})\leqslant t^{\perp}
.
Thus
f_{\diamond}(t)\leqslant g_{\diamond}(t)^{\perp\perp}=g_{\diamond}(t)
.
Reasoning in the same way with
f
and
g
swapped,
we see
g_{\diamond}(t)\leqslant f_{\diamond}(t)
and so
f_{\diamond}=g_{\diamond}
.

To prove the converse, assume
f_{\diamond}=g_{\diamond}
and
s\in\SPred Y
.
Trivially
g^{\diamond}(s)\leqslant(g^{\diamond}(s)^{\perp})^{\perp}

and so
f_{\diamond}(g^{\diamond}(s)^{\perp})=g_{\diamond}(g^{\diamond}(s)^{\perp})\leqslant t^{\perp}
.
Thus
f^{\diamond}(t)\leqslant g^{\diamond}(t)^{\perp\perp}=g^{\diamond}(t)
.
Reasoning in the same way with
f
and
g
swapped,
we see
g^{\diamond}(t)\leqslant f^{\diamond}(t)
and so
f^{\diamond}=g^{\diamond}
.

Solution to
3.5
.

As
\xi_{\diamond}
is left adjoint to
\xi^{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.5pt\hbox to2.05pt{\vbox to2.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.12054pt\lower-0.12054pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hss}\phantom{\diamond}}
,
we have

.

.
