.

Solution to
3.5
.

For the first point, assume
f^{\diamond}=g_{\diamond}

for some
f:X\leftrightarrows Y:g
.
Trivially
g^{\diamond}(s)\leqslant(g^{\diamond}(s)^{\perp})^{\perp}
for any
for any
s\in\SPred X
and
so
f^{\diamond}(g^{\diamond}(s)^{\perp})=g_{\diamond}(g^{\diamond}(s)^{\perp})\leqslant s^{\perp}
.
Hence
f_{\diamond}(s)\leqslant g^{\diamond}(s)^{\perp\perp}=g^{\diamond}(s)
.
With essentially the same argument
(swapping both
f
and
g
and the up/down position of the diamonds),
we see
g^{\diamond}(s)\leqslant f_{\diamond}(s)
,
hence
g^{\diamond}=f_{\diamond}
.
Swapping
f
and
g
again, gives the argument for the converse.

For the second point, assume
f
and
g
are
\diamond
-adjoint.
Then
\IM f=f_{\diamond}(1)=g^{\diamond}(1)=\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}g\right\rceil
,
as desired.

Solution to
3.5
.

For the first point, assume
f
is
\diamond
-self-adjoint.
Then
(f\mathop{\circ}f)_{\diamond}=f_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}f_{\diamond}=f^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}f^{\diamond}=(f\mathop{\circ}f)^{\diamond}

and so
f\mathop{\circ}f
is
\diamond
-self-adjoint as well.

For the second point, assume
f
is
\diamond
-positive.
Then
f=g\mathop{\circ}g
for some
\diamond
-self-adjoint
g
.
Hence
f
is
\diamond
-self-adjoint by the previous point.

For the third point, assume
f
is
\diamond
-positive
and
f\mathop{\circ}f
is pure.
By the previous point
f
is
\diamond
-self-adjoint
and so
f\mathop{\circ}f
is
\diamond
-self-adjoint by the first point.
Thus
f\mathop{\circ}f
is
\diamond
-positive.

Solution to
3.5
.

Assume
f\colon X\to Y
is a sharp map and
p
is a predicate on
Y
.
Using
3.4.1

and
3.4.1

we see
\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil=\left\lceil\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil=\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f
.
For the converse assume
\left\lceil p\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f=\left\lceil p\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil

for every predicate
p
on
Y
.
Assume
s
is any sharp predicate on
Y
.
Then
\left\lceil s\mathop{\circ}f\right\rceil=\left\lceil s\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}f=s\mathop{\circ}f
and so
s\mathop{\circ}f

is sharp. Thus
f
is sharp.

Solution to
3.6
.

Note
1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}=p\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\equiv p\mathbin{\&}p
.
By
3.5
the map
\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}

is
\diamond
-positive
and thus
\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}=\mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathbin{\&}p}

by uniqueness of
\diamond
-positive maps.

Solution to
3.6
.

Assume
f\colon X\to Y
is a map in a
\&
-effectus
with
s\in\SPred Y
and
t\in\SPred X
.

Assume
\IM f\leqslant s
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}f=s\mathop{\circ}f
.
Thus there is a
g
with
f=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}g
.
Then
\mathrm{asrt}_{s}\mathop{\circ}f=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}g=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}g=f
.
For the converse, assume
\mathrm{asrt}_{s}\mathop{\circ}f=f
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{s}\mathop{\circ}f=s\mathop{\circ}f

and so
\IM f\leqslant s
.
We have shown the first equivalence.

For the second equivalence, assume
1\mathop{\circ}f\leqslant t
.
Then
f=h\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{t}
for a (unique) map
h
.
So
f\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t}=h\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{t}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{t}=h\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{t}=f
.
For the converse, assume
f=f\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t}
.
Then
1\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t}\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{t}=t
.

Solution to
3.6
.

Clearly
p\leqslant s
if and only if
1\mathop{\circ}p\leqslant s
.
Even more trivially,
s\mathbin{\&}p=p

if and only if
p\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{s}\equiv s\mathbin{\&}p=p
.
Thus the result follows by applying the second equivalence
of
3.6
with
f=p
and
t=s
.

Solution to
3.6
.

Let
f\colon X\to A
and
g\colon X\to B

be maps in a
\&
-effectus.
Assume
\langle f,g\rangle
is sharp.
Pick any sharp predicate
s
on
A
.
By
3.4.1
we know
[s,0]
is sharp
and so
[s,0]\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=(s\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(0\mathop{\circ}g)=s\mathop{\circ}f

is sharp as well.
This shows
f
is sharp.
With a similar argument we see
g
is sharp.
For the converse, assume
f
and
g
are sharp.
Assume
[s,t]
is some sharp predicate on
A+B
.
By
3.4.1
we know
s
and
t
are sharp.
Thus
s\mathop{\circ}f
and
t\mathop{\circ}g
are sharp.
Consequently
[s,t]\mathop{\circ}\langle f,g\rangle=(s\mathop{\circ}f)\ovee(t\mathop{\circ}g)

is sharp as well by
3.5
.
Thus
\langle f,g\rangle
is sharp, as desired.

Solution to
3.7
.

Assume
C
is a
\&
-effectus
where every predicate has a square root
and where
\pi_{s}
is
\diamond
-adjoint to
\zeta_{s}
.
Let
p
be a predicate and
s
be another predicate on the same object that is sharp.
Pick a predicate
q
with
q\mathbin{\&}q=p
.
The map
f\equiv\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}
is pure and
\diamond
-self-adjoint:

f^{\diamond}\ =\ (\zeta_{s})^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{asrt}_{q})^{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\pi_{s})^{\diamond}\ =\ (\pi_{s})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{asrt}_{q})_{\diamond}\mathop{\circ}(\zeta_{s})_{\diamond}\ =\ f_{\diamond}.

Thus
f\mathop{\circ}f
is
\diamond
-positive.
By
3.6
, we get
f\mathop{\circ}f=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{q\mathbin{\&}q}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}

and so
1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}f=1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}=p\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}
.
By uniqueness of
\diamond
-positive maps,
we get
\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}=f\mathop{\circ}f=\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}f\mathop{\circ}f}=\mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}}
.
Thus
\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}=\zeta_{s}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}=\zeta_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}}
.

Solution to
3.7
.

By
3.6
we have

\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\ =\ \pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}

and so

\mathrm{asrt}^{\dagger}_{p}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p}.

Next, note
\zeta_{1}=\pi_{1}=\mathrm{asrt}_{1}=\mathrm{id}

and so
\pi_{s}=\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1}
,
hence

\pi_{s}^{\dagger}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}\ =\ \zeta_{s}.

Also
\zeta_{s}=\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1}

and so

\zeta_{s}^{\dagger}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{id}^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1}\ =\ \pi_{s}.

Finally, note
\alpha=\mathrm{asrt}_{1}=\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1}

for any isomorphism
\alpha
and so

\alpha^{\dagger}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1}\ =\ \alpha^{-1}.

Solution to
3.7
.

Assume
h
is a pristine map in a
\&
-effectus.
By
3.6
we know there is an isomorphism
\alpha

with
h=\pi_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}h\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}
.
As
1\mathop{\circ}h
is sharp,
we have
\zeta_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}h\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}=\zeta_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1\mathop{\circ}h}=\zeta_{1\mathop{\circ}h}

and so
h=\pi_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1\mathop{\circ}h}
.

Solution to
3.7
.

Assume
h\equiv\pi_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1\mathop{\circ}h}

is a pristine map in a
\dagger^{\prime}
-effectus, cf.
3.7
.

1.

Clearly
h=\pi_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}h\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}

as
1\mathop{\circ}h
is sharp and by
3.6
.
Thus

h^{\dagger}\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil 1\mathop{\circ}h\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\IM h}\ =\ \pi_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\IM h}

using other equivalence of
3.6
.

2.

Note that by the previous point
1\mathop{\circ}h^{\dagger}=\IM h

and
\IM h^{\dagger}=1\mathop{\circ}h
, hence
by a second appeal to the previous point:

h^{\dagger\dagger}\ =\ \pi_{1\mathop{\circ}h^{\dagger}}\mathop{\circ}(\alpha^{-1})^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\IM h^{\dagger}}\ =\ \pi_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\ =\ h.

3.

By the first point
h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}h=\pi_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\IM h}\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1\mathop{\circ}h}=\pi_{1\mathop{\circ}h}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1\mathop{\circ}h}=\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}
as promised.

4.

Clearly
p\mathop{\circ}h^{\dagger}\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}h^{\dagger}=\IM h
.

5.

Assume
p\leqslant 1\mathop{\circ}h
.
Then
p\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}=p
by
3.6
and
so

\mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}h^{\dagger}}\mathop{\circ}h\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\textsf{\ref{pristine-asrt}}}}}{{=}}\ h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}h}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\text{pt.~3}}}}{{=}}\ h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1\mathop{\circ}h}}\ =\ h\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p},

as desired.

Solution to
3.7
.

The map
\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}

is a quotient for
\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}\right\rceil
:

\displaystyle 1\mathop{\circ}\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}

\displaystyle\ =\ \left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\right\rceil\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}

\displaystyle\ =\ \left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}\right\rceil

\displaystyle\ =\ \left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}\right\rceil.

Furthermore

\displaystyle\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}\right\rceil}

\displaystyle\qquad\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{dagger-iso-alpha}}}}{{=}}\ \alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{s}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\alpha^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\ =\ \mathrm{id}.

Thus
\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}

is the unique quotient corresponding to the
comprehension
\pi_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}\right\rceil}
.
Hence
\alpha\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil p\right\rceil}\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{s}=\zeta_{\left\lceil t\mathop{\circ}f^{\dagger}\right\rceil}
, as desired.

Solution to
3.7
.

Note that
\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1}=\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{1}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{1}

and so by definition of the dagger, we have
(\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1})^{\dagger}=\mathrm{asrt}_{1}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}(\psi^{-1})^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}=\psi\mathop{\circ}\zeta_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}
.
Furthermore
p\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}\leqslant 1=1\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1}
.
Thus we can apply the fourth point of
3.7
:

\displaystyle\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}(\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1})^{-1}}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}\pi_{t}\mathop{\circ}\psi\mathop{\circ}\zeta{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1}

\displaystyle\ =\ \mathrm{asrt}_{p\mathop{\circ}k}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{\left\lceil q\right\rceil}\mathop{\circ}\psi^{-1},

which is the desired equality.

Solution to
3.7.1
.

Assume
\mathscr{A}
is a commutative von Neumann algebra
with effect
a\in\mathscr{A}
.
The standard corner
h_{a}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\left\lceil a\right\rceil\mathscr{A}\left\lceil a\right\rceil

for
a
(see
2.6
)
is given by
b\mapsto\left\lceil a\right\rceil b\left\lceil a\right\rceil
.
If
\mathscr{A}
is commutative,
then
h_{a}(bc)=\left\lceil a\right\rceil bc\left\lceil a\right\rceil=\left\lceil a\right\rceil b\left\lceil a\right\rceil\left\lceil a\right\rceil c\left\lceil a\right\rceil=h_{a}(b)h_{a}(c)

and so
h_{a}
is an nmiu-map.
Every corner is a standard corner up-to an isomorphism,
thus every corner on a commutative von Neumann algebra
is an nmiu-map as well.
If
{\mathsf{CvN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
were to have dilations,
then any ncpu-map would be the composition of a corner and an nmiu-map.
We just saw that such corners are nmiu and so any ncp-map would be an nmiu-map
as well, which is absurd. Thus
{\mathsf{CvN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
does not have dilations
in the sense of
3.7.1
.

Solution to
3.8.1
.

We will first show that for any non-zero pure map
f\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}
,
there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
, an element
x\in\mathscr{H}
,
von Neumann algebra
\mathscr{C}
and isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{A}

with
f(\varphi(T,c))=\langle x,Tx\rangle
.
By definition of purity, we know
f=c\mathop{\circ}h

for some corner
h
and filter
c
for
f(1)
.
Clearly
c(1)=f(1)\neq 0
as
f\neq 0
and
so
c\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
is simply given by
c(1)\mathrm{id}
,
hence
f=f(1)h
for a corner
h\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}

of
p\in\mathscr{A}
.
As
p\mathscr{A}p\cong\mathbb{C}
both have only two projections
and so
p
is a mimimal projection.
In particular
0
is the only central element strictly below
p
,
thus
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}
is a factor.
Let
\varrho\colon\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
,
y\in\mathscr{H}

be a GNS-representation of the restriction of
h
to
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}

with
y\in\mathscr{H}
such that
h(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil a)=\langle y,\varrho(\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil a)y\rangle
.
This restriction of
h
is still a corner, hence pure
and so by
2.6
we know
\varrho
is surjective.
As
\left\lceil\!\!\left\lceil p\right\rceil\!\!\right\rceil\mathscr{A}
is a factor,
\varrho
must be injective as well.
Thus
\varrho
is an nmiu-isomorphism.
Hence
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}

defined by
\varphi(T,c)\equiv\varrho^{-1}(T)+c

is an nmiu-isomorphism as well.
Define
x\equiv\sqrt{f(1)}
. Then
f(\varphi(T,c))=f(1)h(\varrho^{-1}(T)+c)=f(1)h(\varrho^{-1}(T))=f(1)\langle y,\varrho(\varrho^{-1}(T))y\rangle=f(1)\langle y,Ty\rangle=\langle x,Tx\rangle
as desired.

We continue with the main task of this exercise.
Assume
(\Pure{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})^{\mathsf{op}}
has a
product
\mathbb{C}\xleftarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathbb{C}

of
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}
.
Note
\pi_{1}\neq 0

for otherwise
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}}=\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}h=0
,
where
h\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{A}
is the unique map given by the universal property
of the product for
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Similarly
\pi_{2}\neq 0
.

By assumption, there is a unqiue pure map
h\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{A}

with
\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}h=\mathrm{id}
and
\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}h=\mathrm{id}
.
With the reasoning at the start,
we know there is a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
, an element
z\in\mathscr{H}
,
a von Neumann algebras
\mathscr{C}

and an isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\mathscr{C}

with
h^{\dagger}(\varphi(T,c))=\langle z,Tz\rangle
.
Note that
T\mapsto\langle z,Tz\rangle

is the dagger of the standard filter
c_{\left|z\right>\!\left<z\right|}
.
Write
p\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})\oplus\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})

for the regular coprojection
(T,c)\mapsto T
,
which is corner with
p^{\dagger}(T)=(T,0)
.
Then
h^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}\varphi=c^{\dagger}_{\left|z\right>\!\left<z\right|}\mathop{\circ}p

and so
h=\varphi\mathop{\circ}p^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}c_{\left|z\right>\!\left<z\right|}
.
By definition of
h
, we have

\mathrm{id}\ =\ \pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}h=\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}p^{\dagger}\mathop{\circ}c_{\left|z\right>\!\left<z\right|}.

(7.4)

Reasoning the same way as we did for
f
at the start
of this exercise,
we know that
\left\lceil\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\right\rceil
is a minimal projection.
If
\left\lceil\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\right\rceil\leqslant(0,1)
,
then
\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}p^{\dagger}=0

and so
\mathrm{id}=0
by (
7.4
), which is absurd.
Thus
\left\lceil\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\right\rceil=(p_{1},0)

for some minimal projection
p_{1}
of
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
.
Thus
\pi_{1}(\varphi(T,c))=\langle x,Tx\rangle
for some
x\in\mathscr{H}
.
Similarly
\pi_{2}(\varphi(T,c))=\langle y,Ty\rangle
for some
y\in\mathscr{H}
.

Note that
\pi_{i}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{id}\oplus\vartheta)=\pi_{i}\mathop{\circ}\varphi

for any map
\vartheta\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
and
i=1,2
.
Hence
\mathrm{id}\mathop{\circ}\pi_{i}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}(\mathrm{id}\oplus\vartheta)\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}h

for
i=1,2
and so
(\mathrm{id}\oplus\vartheta)\mathop{\circ}\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}h=\varphi^{-1}\mathop{\circ}h

for every
\vartheta
by uniqueness of
h
,
which is absurd unless
\mathscr{C}=0
.

Suppose
\dim\mathscr{H}\geqslant 3
.
Then there is some non-zero
z_{0}\in\mathscr{H}

with
z_{0}\perp x
and
x_{0}\perp y
.
So
\pi_{i}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}c_{\left|z_{0}\right>\!\left<z_{0}\right|}=0
for
i=1,2
.
By the presumed universal property of
\mathscr{A}
,
there is a unique map
h\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{A}

with
\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}h=\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}c_{\left|z_{0}\right>\!\left<z_{0}\right|}

and
\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}h=\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\varphi\mathop{\circ}c_{\left|z_{0}\right>\!\left<z_{0}\right|}

Both
\varphi\mathop{\circ}c_{\left|z_{0}\right>\!\left<z_{0}\right|}
and
0
fit the bill,
so
\varphi\mathop{\circ}c_{\left|z_{0}\right>\!\left<z_{0}\right|}=0
,
which is absured. Thus
\dim\mathscr{H}\leqslant 2
.

Clearly
\dim\mathscr{H}\neq 0
, for otherwise there would be a unqiue pure map
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
, which is absurd.
If
\dim\mathscr{H}=1
, then
x=\lambda y
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\lambda\neq 0

and so
\pi_{1}=\lambda\pi_{2}
,
hence for any two pure maps
f_{1},f_{2}\colon\mathscr{B}\to\mathbb{C}
we would have
f_{1}=\lambda f_{2}
,
which is absurd.

Reducing clutter a bit,
we may assume without loss of generality
\mathscr{A}=M_{2}
,
\pi_{1}(T)=\langle x,Tx\rangle

and
\pi_{2}(T)=\langle y,Ty\rangle
for some
x,y\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Write
f_{1},f_{2}\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}

for the maps
f_{1}(\lambda,\mu)=\lambda

and
f_{2}(\lambda,\mu)=\mu
.
By the presumed universal property of
M_{2}
as product,
there is a unique pure map
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathscr{A}

with
\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}f=f_{1}
and
\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}f=f_{2}
.
From the definition of purity,
it follows that
f=c\mathop{\circ}h

for some corner
h\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil M_{2}\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil

and the standard filter
c\colon\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil M_{2}\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil\to M_{2}
.
Note
\left\lceil h\right\rceil\mathbb{C}^{2}\left\lceil h\right\rceil\cong\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil M_{2}\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil
.
The former is commutative, so
\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil\neq 1
.
If
\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil=0
, then
h=0
and so
f_{1}=0
, quod non.
Thus
\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil M_{2}\left\lceil h(1)\right\rceil\cong\mathbb{C}
and so
either
\left\lceil h\right\rceil=(1,0)

or
\left\lceil h\right\rceil=(0,1)
.
In the first case
1=f_{2}(0,1)=\pi_{2}(h(0,1))=\pi_{2}(0)=0
,
which is a contradiction.
The second case leads to a similar contradiction.
So there is no product of
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}
in
(\Pure{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})^{\mathsf{op}}
.

Solution to
3.8.1
.

Assume
\xi\colon\mathscr{C}\to M_{4}
is some filter
with
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\mathop{\circ}\xi=\xi
.
Note
\sigma^{*}=\sigma
.
Assume
c\in\mathscr{C}
.
Then
\xi(c)=\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}(\xi(c))=\sigma\xi(c)\sigma
.
Let
b\in M_{4}
be any element with
\sigma b\sigma=b
.
Then
(p_{\mathscr{S}}+p_{\mathscr{A}})b(p_{\mathscr{S}}+p_{\mathscr{A}})=b=\sigma b\sigma=(\sigma p_{\mathscr{S}}+\sigma p_{\mathscr{A}})b(p_{\mathscr{S}}\sigma+p_{\mathscr{A}}\sigma)=(p_{\mathscr{S}}-p_{\mathscr{A}})b(p_{\mathscr{S}}-p_{\mathscr{A}})

and so
p_{\mathscr{A}}bp_{\mathscr{S}}+p_{\mathscr{S}}bp_{\mathscr{A}}=0
.
As
p_{\mathscr{A}}
and
p_{\mathscr{S}}
have orthogonal ranges,
we see
p_{\mathscr{A}}bp_{\mathscr{S}}=0=p_{\mathscr{S}}bp_{\mathscr{A}}
.
Hence
b=p_{\mathscr{S}}bp_{\mathscr{S}}+p_{\mathscr{A}}bp_{\mathscr{A}}
.
Furthermore
bp_{\mathscr{S}}=p_{\mathscr{S}}bp_{\mathscr{S}}
.
If
b
is additionally self adjoint, then
p_{\mathscr{S}}b=p_{\mathscr{S}}b^{*}p_{\mathscr{S}}=p_{\mathscr{S}}bp_{\mathscr{S}}=p_{\mathscr{S}}b
.
Thus
p_{\mathscr{S}}\xi(c)=\xi(c)p_{\mathscr{S}}
for any
c\in\mathscr{C}

There is some isomorphism
\vartheta\colon\mathscr{C}\to\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil M_{4}\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil

with
\xi(c)=\sqrt{\xi(1)}\vartheta(c)\sqrt{\xi(1)}

and so
p_{\mathscr{S}}\sqrt{\xi(1)}b\sqrt{\xi(1)}=\sqrt{\xi(1)}b\sqrt{\xi(1)}p_{\mathscr{S}}

for any
b\in M_{4}
.
From this it follows (using the pseudoinverse of
\sqrt{\xi(1)}
)
that
p_{\mathscr{S}}\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil b\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil=\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil b\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil p_{\mathscr{S}}
.
Thus
\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil p_{\mathscr{S}}\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil
is central in
\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil M_{4}\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil
.
So either
\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil p_{\mathscr{S}}\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil=0

or
\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil p_{\mathscr{S}}\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil=\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil
.
In the first case, we have
\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil p_{\mathscr{S}}=0

hence
\xi(c)=p_{\mathscr{A}}\xi(c)p_{\mathscr{A}}+p_{\mathscr{S}}\xi(c)\left\lceil\xi(1)\right\rceil p_{\mathscr{S}}=p_{\mathscr{A}}\xi(c)p_{\mathscr{A}}\leqslant p_{\mathscr{A}}
for all
c\in\mathscr{C}
.
With similar reasoning we see that
\xi(c)\leqslant p_{\mathscr{S}}
for all
c\in\mathscr{C}

in the second case.

Assume there is an equalizer
e\colon E\to M_{4}

of
\mathrm{id}_{M_{4}}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}
in
(\Pure{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}})^{\mathsf{op}}
.
By definition of purity
e=c\mathop{\circ}h
for some filter
c
and comprehension
h
.
As
e
is supposed to be an equalizer of
\mathrm{id}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}
,
we have
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\mathop{\circ}c\mathop{\circ}h=\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\mathop{\circ}e=e=c\mathop{\circ}h
,
hence
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\mathop{\circ}c=c
by the surjectivity of the corner
h
.
We have just shown that then
either
c(b)\leqslant p_{\mathscr{A}}
for all
b
or
or
c(b)\leqslant p_{\mathscr{S}}
for all
b
.
Hence
h(e)\leqslant p_{\mathscr{A}}
for all
e\in E

or
h(e)\leqslant p_{\mathscr{S}}
for all
e\in E
.
In the first case, consider the map
\mathrm{ad}_{e^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{S}}}\colon M_{3}\to M_{4}
.
Clearly
\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma}\mathop{\circ}\mathrm{ad}_{e^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{S}}}=\mathrm{ad}_{e^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{S}}}

and so there must be a (unique) map
h\colon M_{3}\to E

with
e\mathop{\circ}h=\mathrm{ad}_{e^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{S}}}
.
However
p_{\mathscr{A}}\geqslant e(h(T))=\mathrm{ad}_{e^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{S}}}(T)\leqslant p_{\mathscr{S}}

for all
T\in M_{3}

and so
\mathrm{ad}_{e^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{S}}}=0
, quod non.
We reach a contradiction in the other case in a similar fashion.
Hence
\Pure{\mathsf{vN}}{{}^{\mathsf{op}}}
has no coequalizers.

Chapter 8

Errata

These are errata to the printed version of this thesis,
which have been corrected in this PDF.
Additional errata will be published
on the arXiv under
1803.01911
.

Erratum to
2.1
.

The second part of the exercise starts with
“Conclude that any quantum channel
\Phi\colon\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})\to\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
…”.
This should have read
“Conclude that any quantum channel
\Phi
from
\mathscr{H}
to itself …”

Erratum to
2.1
.

The third-to-last sentence reads
“Derive from the latter
that for each
y\in\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
and rank-one projector
e\in\mathscr{H}
,
there is a
y^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
U_{0}(e\otimes y)=e\otimes y^{\prime}
.”
This should have been
“Derive from the latter
that for each
y\in\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
and unit vector
e\in\mathscr{H}
,
there is a
y^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}^{\prime}

with
U_{0}(e\otimes y)=e\otimes y^{\prime}
.”.

Erratum to
2.2
.

A pre-Hilbert
\mathscr{B}
-module
X
is
self dual

if every bounded
\mathscr{B}
-linear
\tau\colon X\to\mathscr{B}

is of the form
\tau(x)=\langle t,x\rangle
for some
t\in X
.
In print the condition that
\tau
should be bounded
was accidentally lost when it was moved from
2.2.2
.

Erratum to
2.2.2
.

The assumption that the net
x_{\alpha}
is Cauchy in point 3
is superfluous.

Erratum to
2.2.3
.

The map
T
should be presumed to be bounded.

Erratum to
2.2.3
.

The C
∗
-algebra
\mathscr{B}
isn’t assumed to be a von Neumann algebra,
which it should have been (for otherwise
2.2.3
wouldn’t
be applicable.)

Erratum to
2.5
.

The displayed
inequality

|f(T-p_{S}Tp_{S})|\ \leqslant\ |f(\,(1-p_{S})T|+|p_{S}T(1-p_{S})\,)|

should have read

|f(T-p_{S}Tp_{S})|\ \leqslant\ |f(\,(1-p_{S})T\,)|+|f(\,p_{S}T(1-p_{S})\,)|
.

Erratum to
2.6
.

The standard filter
c_{b}
for
b\in\mathscr{B}

is a map “
\left\lceil b\right\rceil\mathscr{B}\left\lceil b\right\rceil\to\mathscr{B}
”
instead of a map “
\mathscr{B}\to\left\lceil b\right\rceil\mathscr{B}\left\lceil b\right\rceil
”.

Erratum to
3.3
.

Not every zero map is a quotient; only those into
0
.

Erratum to
3.4
.

Not every zero map is a comprehension; only those from
0
.

Erratum to
3.7
.

Also in the last two points of the exercise,
the map
h
should be presumed to be pristine.

Erratum to
3.8.1
.

The condition
“
f\mathop{\circ}\varphi=\langle x,(\,\cdot\,)x\rangle
”
is a typo.
What was meant is “
f(\varphi(T,c))=\langle x,Tx\rangle

for all
c\in\mathscr{C}

and
T\in\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{H})
”.
The same mistake was made writing
“
\pi_{1}\mathop{\circ}\varphi=\langle x,(\,\cdot\,)x\rangle
” and
“
\pi_{2}\mathop{\circ}\varphi=\langle y,(\,\cdot\,)y\rangle
”.
